### Third Journey — Knowledge of God

In the name of God, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful. We ask His help. Praise belongs to God,[^V06-1] who gives existence to all things through His own necessary existence and shines the light of His own reality on the realities of all that exists and on all that may exist. From the light of His being He brings into existence the lasting realities of the intelligences,[^V06-2] and from the showing of His attributes He brings the souls of the heavens into being. He makes the pages of the heavenly and earthly bodies a book of His words. He brings about the causes of motion[^V06-3] and the cycles of all that exists, so that His mercy may continue to reach us and His gifts and blessings may be renewed. Among all the forms of existence, He made the human soul ready to bear His trust and His messages. It reveals the wonders of His creation and the strange effects of His works. It carries the book of His signs and reads the clear and hidden meanings in the book He sent down. I thank Him for His abundant gifts, the wisdom and inspiration He gives, the fullness of His kindness, and the light of His proofs. I ask Him to bless Muhammad, His servant and teacher, the bearer of good news and warning, who was given words of great meaning,[^V06-4] sent with the truth to give good news and warn, and made, by God's leave, a caller to God and a shining lamp; and to bless his chosen, exalted family, who guard the secrets of revelation and certainty, carry the clear book of truth, and open the way to nearness to the Lord of all worlds.

This begins a new stage in wisdom and knowledge: looking closely at beings as they are[^V06-5] and establishing the reality of beings beyond matter and of divine things. This is called knowledge of God and divine wisdom. The greatest gift God gives to His creatures, and the noblest gift He grants to any servant, is what He calls in His illuminating Book “great good”: divine wisdom and knowledge of God. It is surely the greatest happiness and joy. By gaining it, a person reaches great honor and a high standing above all other ranks and forms of excellence.

Whoever receives a gift from God must give thanks and return kindness in keeping with that gift. So anyone whom God has shown mercy, taught from His own knowledge, and given strength in this field—so that he can walk through the land of realities, find light in the darkness of the lower worlds, rise on wings to the heights of the upper worlds, and see the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, as God says He showed Abraham—so that he might be among those who have certainty.

Such a person should hasten to thank God for His favor and generosity. He should make His kindness known, as God commands: “Speak of the blessings of your Lord.” He should also hope for more of God's kindness, as God promises: “If you give thanks, I will give you more.” He should explain these important ideas and uncover these lofty aims so that people may benefit and their lives to come may be set right.

In this field, we see the different ranks people may reach and the states they may attain in the life to come. If someone reaches them, that person has gained the fullest good and the widest perfection. If someone writes them down in a book, the reward will be great and the memory will be honored. Perhaps the book will become a garden inherited by the people who are mindful of God, and its author will be praised by those who come later. Guiding others who seek the truth is one of the greatest ways to draw near to the Lord of all worlds.[^V06-6] It is a mark of the messengers, may God's peace be upon our Prophet, his family, and all of them. The revealed books contain many words that show the honor of knowledge and teaching. Among the words revealed to our father Adam is this: “Those who discover knowledge are dearer to Me than those who build up the earth through crafts.”

Whoever discovers knowledge and writes it in a book is like Adam, the chosen one. The Prophet said to Mu'adh: “If God guides one person through you, it is better for you than the world and all it contains.” There are many such sayings, and recounting them all would take too long.

With God's help, we have set out the rules of this field, built its foundations, shown its path and proofs, and revived its teachings after they had faded. We have strengthened what had fallen into ruin. We have established what was true in the philosophers' views and rejected what was false. The early philosophers rarely went wrong in the foundations and main principles, because they followed the path of the prophets. The claims often attributed to them—that the world has always existed, that God has no power, that He does not know particular things, and that there is no bodily resurrection—are all lies told about them and grave falsehoods.[^V06-7]

Later philosophers, such as followers of the school of Aristotle and other recent thinkers, have made grave mistakes in divine questions and many natural questions. These are matters where people cannot excuse error and forgetfulness, if they are ready and able to follow the path toward the life to come. Some have gone astray and turned away from the road that leads to their final home.

A wise person does not follow a claim just because it is popular. If the truth opposes the majority, the wise person still accepts it. In every matter, the wise person asks not who said it but what was said. It is reported that our leader, the Commander of the Faithful, said: “You do not know the truth by knowing people. Know the truth, and then you will know its people.” In every question we have examined, our only aim has been to draw near to God and His highest realm by guiding a sincere seeker or refining a clear mind. If careful thinkers agree with us, that is what we hoped for. If they do not, the truth does not always fit minds clouded by inner illnesses that even healers of the soul cannot cure. The guiding Prophet was told by God: “You cannot guide everyone you love.”[^V06-8] When people seek wisdom in the wrong way, it only makes them more resistant and proud.

They gain nothing from it and reach no result. They also lose the chance to follow the examples found in revelation and the paths set out by sacred law. They return empty-handed,[^V06-9] covered in shame. This is great loss and painful deprivation. A wise person has no words or books for people like this, and no call or address for those like them. God says: “Even if you brought those who were given the Book every sign, they would not follow your direction.” He also says: “Even if they saw every sign, they would not believe in it.” How could they believe in what they cannot see when they are not ready to do so?

Wisdom and the light of knowledge have conditions and causes. They include an open heart,[^V06-10] a sound nature, good character, sound judgment, a sharp mind, quick understanding, and a taste for insight gained through direct spiritual experience. Along with all this, the heart must have a light from God[^V06-11] that keeps burning like a lamp. This light guides a person to wisdom, just as a lamp shows what is in a room.

If someone lacks these qualities, let alone God's light, that person should not tire themselves by seeking wisdom. Someone may have understanding but lack direct insight and the light that guides them. Such a person will not gain wisdom, even if they get some parts right and master some of its starting points. “If God gives no one light, that person has no light.”

Know, then, that this part of wisdom, which we are beginning, is its finest part: true faith in God, His signs, and the Last Day.[^V06-12] God points to this faith when He says, “The believers—all of them—believe in God, His angels, His Books, and His messengers,” and, “Whoever rejects God, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day has gone far astray.” This field contains two noble kinds of knowledge: knowledge of the beginning and knowledge of the return. Knowledge of the beginning includes knowing God, His attributes, His actions, and their effects. Knowledge of the return includes knowing the soul, the Resurrection, and prophecy.

God praises those who look and think about both. He praises those who look at the creation of the world and its wonders, which show the greatness and oneness of its maker: “They reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth and say, ‘Our Lord, You did not create this without purpose.’” He also praises those who look into their own souls and cleanse them of material coverings: “By the soul and the One who formed it, then showed it what is wrong and what is right: successful is the one who purifies it.”

“And lost is the one who buries it.” God condemns those who turn away from reflecting on His wisdom and signs, thinking about the wonders of His creation, and remembering Him. In many places in His noble Book, He says, for example, “If you ask them who created the heavens and earth, they will say, ‘God.’” He condemns them because they did not reach this knowledge through learning and thought, but only copied what they had heard and accepted it without examining it. He also says, “How many signs there are in the heavens and earth that they pass by and turn away from.” God condemns them for turning away from wisdom and reproaches them for refusing to seek the truth of things and the hidden secrets in the worlds of creation and command. He says, “I did not make them witnesses to the creation of the heavens and earth or to their own creation, nor do I take those who lead others astray as helpers.” This points out that they lack both kinds of knowledge and are led astray because of it. Those who mislead others have no share in the truth. They went astray and led others astray only after they failed to learn, turned away from knowledge, and grew arrogant. God did not wrong them; they wronged themselves.[^V06-13]

Reason and the Qur'an therefore agree: learning divine wisdom and knowing the human soul—that is, learning about the beginning and the return—lead to everlasting life. Rejecting this knowledge leads to lasting loss.

My brothers on this path, I have already offered you lights of wisdom, subtle sayings, basic ideas, general rules, principles for weighing claims,[^V06-14] and judgments of the mind. These are steps of great value and ranks on the road to God, which we travel through thought and inspiration. They are stairways to knowledge of God, to seeing the beauty and attributes of the One, to the company of the holy ones and the people of the kingdom and the highest realms. These are the aims of those who receive revelation and of those who uncover its deeper meanings. Their knowledge comes from the angels near to God and the noble recording angels.

These high and noble aims begin with God. He first placed them in the great Pen and the honored Tablet. He taught the Pen what it did not know, spoke to it through His words, inspired it with the clear signs, and guided it with His light. He then chose it and made it His representative on earth, fit for the upper world and a representative of His heavenly kingdom. This knowledge gives a person a vast realm, for it is the greatest elixir: it brings complete richness, the greatest happiness, the best lasting state, likeness to the highest good, and a character shaped by God's qualities.

In one of the revealed books, God says: “Child of Adam, I created you to live forever, and I am the Living who never dies. Obey what I command and avoid what I forbid, and I will make you like Me: living, never to die.” A saying from our Prophet also describes the people of Paradise. When an angel enters their presence, the angel gives them a letter from God after greeting them in His name. It says: “From the Living, the Self-Sustaining, who never dies, to the living, the self-sustaining, who never dies: I say to a thing, ‘Be,’ and it is. Today I have given you the power to say to a thing, ‘Be,’ and it is.”[^V06-15]

This is one of the stations a person can reach through wisdom and knowledge. Those who follow the Sufi path call it the station of “Be.”[^V06-16] It is said that during the expedition to Tabuk the Prophet said to Abu Dharr, “Be Abu Dharr,” and he became Abu Dharr. There is a station beyond this, called the station of being lost in God's oneness. God points to it in a sacred saying: “When I love someone, I become the hearing with which they hear and the sight with which they see.”[^V06-17]

During this journey, the proof will make this clear to you and teach you the knowledge that brings great joy and the highest rank to those who learn it and live by it. Understand it, take it in, and be happy through it. Share it only with those who will live by it. To live by it means to turn away from the world and prepare for the next life through mindfulness of God.

We have made this rough road easier, clearing away its thorns and stones and smoothing its hard places so that any of God's servants who wishes may find the way.[^V06-18] God is the giver of grace and guidance. In His hand are the keys to goodness and wisdom, and with Him are the stores of generosity and mercy. This part contains several arts.

### Part One — God’s nature and attributes

### First Discussion — The One Who Must Exist: His manner of existence, perfect oneness, and completeness

#### Chapter 1 — Establishing God's Existence and Coming to Know Him

Know[^V06-19] that[^V06-20] there are many paths to God, because He has many perfections and can be approached from many sides. Each person turns toward a different direction. Some paths, though, are firmer, nobler, and clearer than others. The strongest and noblest proof[^V06-21] does not rely on anything other than God Himself to connect its steps. The way to the goal is then the goal itself. This is the path of the people of truth:[^V06-22] they point to God as proof of God, then use His own being to show His attributes,[^V06-23] and His attributes to show His actions, one after another. Other people—such as theologians and natural philosophers—try to know God and His attributes through something else: for example, the possibility of an essence, the createdness of the world, or the body's motion. These are also signs of God's being and attributes, but this path is stronger and nobler.

The Qur'an points to both paths. God says, “We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that He is the Real.” He also says, “Is it not enough that your Lord is witness to everything?” The people who know God look at existence itself and understand that it is the source of everything. From there, they see that existence, in its own reality, must exist necessarily. Possibility, need, and dependence on a cause do not belong to existence in itself. They come from its limits and absences.

By considering what follows from necessary or possible existence,[^V06-24] they show that God's being and attributes are one. From His attributes they learn how His actions and effects follow. This is the path of the prophets, as God says: “Say, ‘This is my path: I call to God with clear insight.’” The argument is as follows. As we have explained, existence is one real, simple reality.[^V06-25] Its instances differ in themselves only by how complete or limited, strong or weak they are, or by features added to them, as happens among the members of a kind. The fullest existence is the one beyond which there can be nothing more complete: it depends on nothing else, and nothing greater can be imagined. Anything incomplete depends on something else and needs what completes it. We have already shown that completeness comes before lack, actuality before potential, and existence before nonexistence. We have also shown that the completeness of a thing is that thing together with what surpasses it. So existence is either independent of anything else,[^V06-26] or, by its very nature, in need of something else.

The first is the Necessary Being: existence in its purest form, beyond which nothing can be more complete, with no lack or imperfection mixed into it. The second is everything else: God's effects and works. Nothing else can stand without Him.[^V06-27] As we have already seen, existence itself has no lack. Lack belongs to it because it is an effect.[^V06-28] An effect cannot equal its cause in the fullness of existence. If existence were not brought into being and held under the power of a creator, it could not be limited in any way. Existence is simple and has no boundary or identity apart from being fully real and actual. If it were made up of parts or had an essence apart from existence, it would not be simple.

We have also shown that if an existence is caused, it is made to exist in a single, direct act. In its very being, it depends on the one who brings it into being. So it is now clear: existence is either complete in itself and necessary, or it depends in its very being on the Necessary Being. Either way, the Necessary Being's existence is independent of everything else. That is what we set out to prove.

This proof is extremely strong. It comes close to the path of the Illuminationists, who begin from the principle of light.[^V06-29] But later thinkers, because they overlook some of its steps or cannot grasp how existence can be stronger or weaker, sometimes object to it. At times they say it makes no sense for one reality to differ in perfection and lack while remaining the same reality, as if what makes things alike and what makes them different were one and the same.[^V06-30] At other times, they accept that existence differs in degree but deny that completeness belongs to existence itself, or that lack makes a thing dependent on something more complete. They argue: if existence itself made a thing necessary, then every existent would be necessary. If it made a thing possible and dependent, then every existent would be possible. If it did neither, both necessity and possibility would need causes outside existence.[^V06-31] Then even the Necessary Being would depend on a cause, which is impossible. Since all three conclusions are false, they say the claim that existence is one reality with degrees of completeness and lack must also be false.

I say that this objection comes from not understanding the point. The unity of existence is not like the unity of the human essence.[^V06-32] The unity of an essence is mental:[^V06-33] it belongs to general ideas after the mind sets aside the individual features of particular people. We divide that one idea into different cases by adding different limits: a person is one way or another. Existence, however, is not one general essence shared by everything. So it cannot be divided in the same way as other general ideas, which can exist in the mind and be shared by many things. The mind divides a general kind according to the forms of its members: a broader kind, a species, or an individual. But existence is not a broader kind, a species, or an individual under a broader kind.

The unity and sharing of existence among particular things is a different kind of unity. This objection therefore misses its mark. It would apply only if existence were a general nature. If someone instead means the general idea of “being,” and says that if this idea makes one instance necessary then all must be necessary, we answer: if “being” means the reality of existence, then our claim stands. If it means only the general idea we use when we say that something exists, that idea does not make anything necessary. It is like other features that depend on what they describe. No such feature is the Necessary Being, or even a separate thing in the world outside the mind. Someone may say that the idea of existence is identical with God but added to possible things. This means only that God, considered on His own, is what we point to when we say “exists,” while a possible thing is not, unless we consider it together with its cause.[^V06-34]

A correction and refinement. The Illuminationist writer raised a similar objection against his own path, which is based on light and darkness. His answer, in brief, is this: the common essence found in the perfect, middle, and deficient levels is a general idea in the mind. It is not something shared by them in the outside world. What exists outside the mind is not made of a common essence plus a degree of perfection. Each individual is different from the others. The perfect being is one thing, the deficient being another, and the one between them a third. So their features and effects may differ. The mistake comes from treating what exists in the mind as if it were what exists outside it.

I say that the truth is that a general nature does exist outside the mind, especially according to those who say existence is only an idea and that the essences of things—not their existence—are what is real. For them, it is the essence of a thing that exists outside the mind.[^V06-35] The general nature is not general there as it is in the mind, but it is something that the mind can recognize as shared by many things.

So the shared feature exists outside the mind, even though the mind is where it becomes a general idea. It is one essential meaning found in things that differ in perfection, lack, and other features. The mind can consider that shared meaning by itself, with or without the mental idea of oneness.[^V06-36] It then sees one of two things: either the shared nature itself requires a particular level or feature, in which case everything else falls outside its true instances; or it requires none of them, in which case each level and feature must come from something added to the shared nature.

This is why it is wrong to say that, when a general essence is shared, the very thing that makes instances different in perfection or lack is also what makes them the same. A general essence is capable of appearing in every instance and every part, and it has a common definition that applies to all of them. If that nature required completeness and strength, then all its instances would be complete and strong. If it required lack and weakness, then they would all be lacking and weak. If it required neither, then each would need some added feature to explain its level. So no essence can be more or less complete, stronger or weaker, except through added features.[^V06-37]

This objection does not apply to existence when existence has different degrees. Existence is not a general idea in the mind that can take different forms of being, as the human or horse essence can. Those essences have one shared definition, which becomes particular through added features, and which the mind can separate from each feature in turn. The reality of existence, as we have learned, is nothing but the particular identities of things. When existence varies in degree—stronger or weaker, earlier or later—it cannot be divided into a shared nature plus an added feature.[^V06-38] Its complete level cannot be broken down into a common part and something extra, because its identity is simple.

The weaker level is also just existence; its lack is a kind of absence. So the reality of existence in each thing is according to that thing's own level. The unity that gathers all these levels is neither the unity of a species nor the unity of a broader kind.[^V06-39] It is another kind of unity, known only to those who have reached full insight.

The Illuminationist writer would be right if, by the essence of light—which he regards as simple yet varying in strength and weakness—he meant the very reality of existence. But if he meant a general idea that can apply to many things, his claim cannot be made sound. The thinkers in Aristotle’s school say that stronger and weaker shades of black, heat, light, and similar qualities come from the different states of individual things, not from differences in a shared idea.[^V06-40] Their view is close to what we have said.

A brief reminder: the light of truth has dawned through the explanation you have heard, seeker. The reality of existence is simple. It has no separate essence and no part that defines or limits it. It is itself the Necessary Being, whose perfection has no limit. Any level less strong than this is not pure existence; it includes a lack. A thing's lack is not the thing itself. The lack of existence is absence. This absence does not belong to existence simply because it exists. It comes from its place at a lower level, after the first. So lack and need belong to later beings because they come later. The First Being has limitless perfection, beyond which nothing can be more complete. Lack and need arise from the giving of existence, but the gift also completes them: the later beings depend on the First, and their lack is made good by His fullness; their need is met by His independence.

This proof establishes God's existence and also establishes His oneness. Existence is one reality, and in its own nature it has no lack or division.[^V06-41][^V06-42] It also establishes His knowledge of Himself and of everything else, and His life, because knowledge is existence itself.[^V06-43] It establishes His power and will, since these follow from life and knowledge.[^V06-44] It also shows that His existence sustains everything. The strongest existence gives freely and acts on all that is below it. He is knowing, powerful, willing, living, sustaining, aware, and active. Since the levels that follow Him are ordered from weaker to stronger and from lower to nobler, His creating, bringing things into being, command, kingdom, and rule are also established.

This path is the surest, noblest, and most direct. A seeker does not need to know God, His attributes, or His actions by first proving something else, nor must the seeker rely on disproving circular causes or an endless chain of causes. God is known through Himself. “God bears witness that there is no god but Him.” Other things are known through Him: “Is it not enough that your Lord is witness to everything?”

For those who have reached full understanding, this path is enough to know the truth, its signs, and its works. But not everyone can draw many conclusions from one starting point. So teaching must also explain the other paths that lead to the truth, even if they do not lead to it as directly.

#### Chapter 2 — Other Paths to This Great Goal

One of these paths is the one we mentioned in the general study of existence and in first philosophy. There we explained the marks of the Necessary Being and His oneness. We said that, as a concept, “existent” divides into necessary and possible. A possible thing, in itself, is no more likely to exist than not to exist. It therefore needs something outside itself to make it exist.[^V06-45] If it made itself more likely to exist, then it would be necessary in itself, though we had assumed it was possible. The same applies if it made its own nonexistence more likely: it would then be impossible, though we had assumed it was possible. This is a contradiction. So the Necessary Being must exist. Since things exist, either one of them is necessary—in which case the point is admitted—or we must reach the Necessary Being, because the chain cannot continue without end, as we have already shown. A circle also leads to an endless chain, and is impossible; it would also make a thing come before itself, which is clearly impossible.

The fact that the cause is considered under different descriptions does not remove this problem. Different descriptions do not make the thing itself into two things. A circle still leads to an endless chain and makes a thing come before itself. This path comes closer than most to the path of the people of truth, but it is not the same path, despite what some have claimed. The people of truth consider the reality of existence. Here, the argument starts from the concept “existent.”[^V06-46] In brief, the idea of “existent” shows that it cannot be real unless the Necessary Being exists. If every existent were only possible, then nothing could exist at all. A possible thing either exists without a cause, which is obviously impossible, or it exists because of something else. If that other thing is also possible, then either the chain continues without end, forms a circle, or leads to the Necessary Being. The first two choices are impossible, and the third contradicts the assumption that every existent is possible. Yet it must be true, since it is impossible for all possible things to exist without the Necessary Being. This is what Avicenna called the path of the people of truth, and later thinkers followed him.

They were asked: “Did you not begin your proof with the existence of a possible thing, something you observe to have been absent before or after it existed?” They answered that the proof needs only one existent thing.[^V06-47] If it is necessary, that is the point they seek to prove; if it is possible, then, by the argument just given, it must depend on the Necessary Being.

Some then questioned whether this argument is truly a proof. They said there are only two kinds of proof: one that proceeds from a cause to its effect, and one that proceeds from an effect to its cause. God is not caused by anything; He is the cause of everything else. So any proof of His existence must proceed from an effect, and that kind of proof does not give certainty.[^V06-48] They answered that the proof does not begin by pointing to God's own existence, which is the cause of all things. It shows instead that one instance of the shared idea “existent” must be necessary. The fact that the concept “existent” includes a necessary instance is one of the states that follow from it.[^V06-49] So the proof moves from a feature of the shared concept to another feature that depends on the first.[^V06-50]

Another way they put it was this: they were not proving that the Necessary Being exists in Himself; they were proving that He belongs to the concept “existent.” Avicenna offers a similar example when he argues from the existence of something composed to the existence of what it is composed of. God's existence in Himself is the cause of everything else, but His belonging to this concept is an effect of it. A thing can be a cause in itself and an effect in relation to something else. That is the answer given by some careful thinkers.

But this answer is plainly strained.[^V06-51][^V06-52] As we said earlier, the Necessary Being is not proved in Himself,[^V06-53] but by something that follows from Him.[^V06-54] There is a proof that resembles one from a cause.[^V06-55]

Another path that thinkers have praised is supposed to work without disproving circular causes or an endless chain. The author of *The Conversations* gives it. Suppose that all existence consists only of possible things, whether their chain goes on without end or circles back on itself. Then all possible things together are like one possible thing:[^V06-56] they need a cause. That cause must be either the whole itself, one of its members, or something outside it. If the whole caused itself, it would have to come before itself. If one member caused it, that member would have to come before itself and its own causes.[^V06-57] If its cause were outside the whole, then, since everything in the whole is possible, what lies outside it can only be the Necessary Being. This argument does not work for us. Existence and oneness go together; in fact, existence itself is one. We have already learned[^V06-58] that a collection, simply as a collection, has no existence beyond the existences of its members. A collection of Zayd and Amr is just two people. It is not a third thing that exists outside the mind. So someone may say that all possible things have no cause other than the causes of their individual members. The cause of the collection is just the collection of causes of its members. Those causes are not outside the possible things, since some possible things cause others. So this alone does not require us to reach the Necessary Being.

Only if we also rule out an endless chain could this argument reach the Necessary Being. In that case it would not be a different path after all.

It is surprising that, when this path was challenged with the claim that a thing might cause itself, its defenders answered by saying that the whole made up of the Necessary Being and the first effect is itself possible because it depends on its members. They said it has no cause other than itself: its cause cannot be one of its parts, because it would depend on the rest of them,[^V06-59] and nothing lies outside it. So, they concluded, the whole must be its own cause. In answering, they accepted that a thing's complete cause can be the thing itself. They said that if “cause” means all the members on which the whole depends, then the cause is the whole itself.[^V06-60]

They said this is no problem, because the whole's dependence on each member does not mean that it depends on the whole, which would make it depend on itself. If “cause” means the acting cause, then one member—the Necessary Being, or something above the last effect and ultimately dependent on the Necessary Being—is its cause. Every member depends on the Necessary Being, so even the possible members of the collection have no effect on the whole apart from the Necessary Being's effect, whether directly or through others.

They did not see that this answer destroys the argument. Their proof depends on saying that the whole cannot be its own complete cause and that one part cannot act as the cause of the whole. The reply says both are possible. It simply gives away the point at issue and tears down the proof.[^V06-61]

All this confusion came from assuming that a whole made up of two or more things exists as something apart from the existence of each member. They decided that the whole made up of the Necessary Being and the first effect is a possible thing that needs a cause, then claimed that its complete cause is itself. They forgot that this makes no sense. Every possible thing that exists needs a complete acting cause. A whole made up of all its members can be the same as the whole only in numbers or amounts that the mind groups together by choice, such as an army or a number. Such collections have no reality outside the separate members.

One careful thinker among them answered the problem of a whole made up of the Necessary Being and the first effect by saying that we can consider more than one thing as a single group, or consider the things separately. “The group” names them when considered as one; “this and that” names them when considered separately. These can have different descriptions. A whole crowd cannot fit in a small house if everyone enters at once, but the people can fit if they enter one at a time. Knowing this, we can say that what makes the two things exist together is the two things considered separately. Since the whole depends on both parts, together they are enough to make it exist. So this and that are two causes that make the group exist.

If someone then asks about the two things considered separately, we answer that we do not accept that they are possible when taken that way. In that case they are two things: one necessary existent and one possible existent.

A contemporary thinker rejected this answer. In his example, what exists is the Necessary Being and its effect. Considered together, they form one group; considered separately, there are two. Surely both are real, he says, because a whole disappears only when one of its members disappears,[^V06-62] and every member is present. A possible thing must have a cause whether it is one or many, and whether we consider it as a whole or as separate parts. Taking things together or separately changes only how we look at them, not what they are. So the whole made up of the Necessary Being and the first effect exists, whether we consider it as one group or two things. Here “the whole” means the two things that bear the relation of being together, but not the relation itself. This whole exists, even if the relation and the fact that there are two are not themselves things that exist. The one exists in the same way: it exists, though the feature of oneness does not exist as a thing on its own.

If the two things that bear this relation exist, and the whole depends on them, it must have a cause. But there is no other thing that could be its cause. So, the contemporary thinker concludes, this answer does not solve the problem. We say that both replies miss the point. They fail to recognize what existence is: it is not a mental feature added to things, and a thing's existence is its own particular identity. A group does not become one thing in reality just because the mind treats two things as one. The mind's act of grouping them does not give them one nature with a separate rule of its own. What we take together from a human being and a stone is neither a human nor a stone, nor is it some third thing. The same is true of what we group together from a substance and an accident, or from the Necessary Being and a possible being. A number exists outside the mind only in the sense that its individual units exist; the mind can group them together, and in that sense the number has a kind of existence in thought.

The first reply goes wrong in several ways. It assumes that the same thing can be both cause and effect, different only in how we consider it. That is impossible: cause and effect are opposites and cannot belong to the same thing, even under two descriptions.[^V06-63][^V06-64] It also says that if the group made up of the Necessary Being and the first effect existed as one possible thing, then the Necessary Being would bring two things into being at the first level: the first effect and the group. But their own teaching says it brings one thing into being, not two. It further says that this combined being is not one of the kinds of existence in their own scheme. It is not the Necessary Being, an intellect, a soul, a body, or an accident. Yet everything that exists must belong to one of these kinds.[^V06-65]

At the end of his argument, the first thinker accidentally gives the right answer: if the two things are considered separately, they are two—a necessary being and a possible one. There is no need to group them as one real thing to make the objection apply. Nothing requires them to be one thing in the outside world, and their being together actually shows that they are two.[^V06-66][^V06-67][^V06-68]

The second thinker makes some of the same mistakes, and adds another. He argues that a group exists because its members exist, and that it would disappear only if one of its members disappeared. Neither point proves what he wants. The members' existence means that each exists in its own distinct way, for example as necessary or possible, substance or accident. So a group exists only through several distinct existences, with their different rules. And saying that a whole disappears when one of its members disappears does not show that every collection of separate things has one real existence of its own. It applies to a real whole that has one natural form, or could have one. It does not apply to every set of things the mind can group together.

The same mistake would make ordinary divisions useless. If we say that every thing is either necessary or possible, every substance either separate from matter or material, and every word either a noun, verb, or particle, these divisions would not cover everything unless two of the divisions could never each have a real existence of their own.

Among the paths to this goal that do not rely on disproving circular causes or an endless chain is one based on possibility. First, remember that a possible thing does not exist until its existence becomes necessary. And just as a possible thing does not make itself exist, it cannot make its own existence more likely. If it could, it would be enough to bring itself into being and would be necessary in itself, as we have already explained.[^V06-69]

The argument is this: no possible thing, whether it is one or many, or whether its members are ordered or equal in rank, can make anything exist necessarily. Whatever makes something necessary must make every way it could fail to exist impossible. But possible things cannot do this. The whole chain of possible causes and effects could cease to exist, even if the effect could not cease while its cause remained—as happens with features that cannot be separated from possible things. So all possible things, whether limited in number or endless, are like one possible thing: they cannot make the absence of their effect impossible, so they cannot make it necessary. For a possible thing to exist, its cause must therefore lie outside the chain of possible things and be the Necessary Being. This is what we wanted to show. This is the point Al-Farabi makes when he says:

“If a chain of beings existed without necessity, and its first member existed by itself as a possible thing, it would either bring itself into being, which is absurd, or be able to cease by itself, which is even more absurd.”

In other words, if a possible thing gives itself necessary existence and makes nonexistence impossible, then it would be its own cause, which is false because it would have to come before itself. If it does not make its existence necessary and its nonexistence impossible, then it could cease to exist on its own. That is even more clearly false. The first problem still applies: in some way, the thing would be the cause of itself. The second problem is that if it could cease to exist after it had come into being, then it could even more readily have failed to exist in the first place. So it cannot have come into being without something that made its existence necessary.

#### Chapter 3 — A Persian Proof, and Its Flaws

A Persian thinker offered these arguments for the claim that God exists. He said: “If all existing things were only possible, there would have to be a circle of causes. For, on that assumption, the existence of any thing would depend on something being brought into existence, while bringing anything into existence would itself depend on something already existing.”[^V06-70][^V06-71][^V06-72]

He offered another argument: “Existence, considered simply as existence, has no beginning. If it did, it would have to come before itself. This proves that the Necessary Being exists, as is clear with a little thought. This deserves to be called the path of the people of truth, who use the truth itself as their proof. Put another way, all existing things together cannot have a cause in themselves. This proves the Necessary Being. Or again: all existing things together cannot become sheer nothing, while all possible things together could become sheer nothing. This proves the Necessary Being.” That is his argument.[^V06-73]

I say that each of these arguments has a flaw. The first does not lead to the kind of circle that thinkers call impossible. A true circle requires the two ends to be the same individual thing. It is not enough for a human being to depend on a seed and for that seed to depend on a human being of the same kind. The mistake is to treat a whole kind as if it were one individual, and to confuse sameness of kind with sameness of the particular thing.

Muhammad al-Shahrastani made a similar mistake in his book *The Downfall of the Philosophers*. He says that if the world had no beginning in time, there would have to be a circle of causes. The circle between seed and human, or egg and chicken, is broken only if we choose one end as the beginning. Otherwise, each depends on the other, and neither would be more likely to exist than the other. Since they do exist, one must come first. He says the circle must therefore be broken by starting with one of them; in the human line, it is better to start with the more complete form.

The scholar Nasir al-Din al-Tusi replied: “You have done well in answering what ordinary people and children ask. But this is not a real circle; it is a circle only in words. If a thing depends on something that depends on another thing of the same kind, the result need not be that neither can exist. They may instead form an endless chain. If such a chain is impossible, then it must have a beginning. Here al-Shahrastani has confused a circle with an endless chain.”[^V06-74]

The second argument is also flawed. It leaves out a condition needed for a contradiction: the subject must be the same particular thing. A thing can come before or after itself in a general sense without contradiction, so long as it is not the same individual in both cases. As we explained at the start of this journey, first philosophy studies the most general kinds of things that exist, and the principles of existing things are also features of existence.[^V06-75] Avicenna says the same in *The Healing*.

As for the third argument, if “all existing things” means each one separately, we do not accept that they have no cause. On the assumption being discussed, each thing has a cause. If “all existing things” means the whole group as one, then, as we said, there is no group that exists apart from the many individual things. Just as there is no existence apart from the existences of its members, there is no need for a cause apart from the causes of its members.[^V06-76]

The fourth argument shows only that existence as existence differs from possibility as possibility.[^V06-77] It is impossible for something that exists, considered simply as existing, to become complete nonexistence. In that claim, the thing is already assumed to exist. But a possible thing, considered apart from its existence, can become nothing in itself. This does not mean that there is some existing thing that is not possible. So the conclusion does not follow from the difference between existence and possibility.

The better way to complete these arguments is to show that an endless chain of causes is impossible. Otherwise, the arguments do not work—unless one can first prove that all possible things form one possible being that needs a cause outside itself. That claim depends on proving that the whole has one unified form and a real nature of its own, unlike a number or a collection of separate things. We have already shown the problem with that claim.[^V06-78]

#### Chapter 4 — Other Arguments That Do Not Hold Up

The philosophers who study God have another path. They use two kinds of proof. One first shows that God exists, then proves that He is one. The other first proves that the Necessary Being must be one, then shows that bodies, their forms, and their features are many. None of these can be the Necessary Being, so they must be possible and must depend on something that makes them exist.

One way to prove that the bodily world is possible is to note that bodies of different kinds are clearly made up of parts. Each has the shared feature of being a body, and something that makes it one kind of body rather than another. A merely vague body cannot exist. It does not matter whether we accept matter that is more basic than body, or whether we accept a substantial form that gives a body its kind. Even those who reject such forms accept that bodies must have some definite shape or feature to exist at all. Bodies, then, are possible because they are made up of parts. Their forms and features are also possible because they need matter or something in which to exist. On any account, bodies depend on what makes them definite, and those causes depend on what they exist in. Souls also depend on bodies. Neither a body nor a soul can be the cause that makes the other exist, because that would make a thing come before itself. Nor can one body bring another into existence.[^V06-79][^V06-80]

As we have explained, a physical thing can affect another only through a shared position in space, and that relation cannot exist until both things exist. Also, the bodies cannot stretch back without end, as the proofs about the limits of size show. So the cause of all bodies must be something that is neither a body nor physical. That is what we wanted to show.

Natural philosophers have other paths, based on change. One[^V06-81] begins with motion itself. A moving thing does not start its own motion; it needs another mover. The chain of movers must end at something that does not move, so that there is no circle or endless chain. Because it does not change and has no unrealized potential, this unmoved mover must be the Necessary Being, or one of His aspects, as we explained when discussing the world's beginning.

Natural philosophers also show that the movements of the heavenly bodies come from souls, not from nature alone. A soul acts by choice. So those movements have a goal and aim at the good. They do not move from desire or anger, or to gain a benefit from anything below them, since lower things have no importance to them. Nor is one heavenly body aiming at another, or at the bodies or souls above or below it. If that were the goal, their movements would be alike and the number of bodies would not be limited. So the goal of these movements must be something beyond the physical world.

Nor is that goal a soul. If it exists necessarily, it is the one we seek; if not, it must depend on Him. This is the path of Abraham.

A related argument begins from the fact that changing things are plainly possible and have come to be, and that earthly elements are affected by the heavens.[^V06-82] The heavenly bodies cannot all be causes of one another. The one held within the others cannot cause the one that contains it, because it is smaller and lower. The containing body cannot cause what lies within it either, because that would make empty space possible.[^V06-83] The stars are nobler than other bodies, and the sun is nobler than the rest. Yet even the sun changes, with its rising and setting, so it needs a mover beyond itself. The mistaken ideas held by some ancient star-worshippers came from misunderstanding the heavenly bodies. This argument shows that they are not the highest goal. Something more complete lies beyond them and moves them, not by direct physical contact or change, but by drawing them through thought and giving them light. This is the Necessary Being, the maker of all.

This argument becomes complete through what we have established: every bodily nature, whether heavenly or earthly, changes in its very being. The heavenly bodies change not only in their features, positions, and arrangements, but in their substance and identity too. The one that changes them and keeps them in being by renewing them at every moment is neither a body nor physical. This is the sense of Abraham's story. As he watched the stars and other heavenly bodies disappear, he saw their very being renewed at each moment, while their lasting heavenly patterns and intelligible forms remained with God.

God says, “In this way We showed Abraham the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, so that he would be among those with certainty.” With his mind, Abraham traveled from this world, which fades and passes away, to the world of God. He said, “I do not love things that disappear. I turn my face toward the One who made the heavens and the earth, turning away from all falsehood. I am not one of those who associate others with God.”[^V06-84]

Natural philosophers also use a path based on knowledge of the soul. It is noble, though it does not reach as high as the path of the people of truth. Here, the seeker is also the way; on the first path, the goal itself is the way, which is nobler. The argument is this: the human soul is beyond the body, yet it begins when the body begins. At first, a soul cannot be distinguished from a body, and the soul cannot move from one body to another, as we will explain later. So the soul is possible and needs a cause that is neither a body nor physical. A body, simply as a body,[^V06-85][^V06-86][^V06-87] cannot cause a soul; otherwise, all bodies would have souls. Nor can a bodily power—another soul, a bodily form,[^V06-88] or a bodily feature—cause the soul. Such powers act only through a position in space, but there is no spatial position between them and something that has no place.

Also, nothing is brought into existence by something less noble than itself. The soul has a stronger and nobler kind of existence than the other powers of the body. Its maker must therefore be free of matter and body: God, either directly or through one of the beings of His command and word.

Two objections arise. First, if bodily powers can act or be acted on only through a position in space, then no body or bodily feature could receive existence from a cause beyond matter, since no spatial relation can connect a material thing and something beyond matter. Second, if the soul is beyond the body, then the body and its states should have no effect on it.

We answer the first objection by saying that bodies and matter themselves receive existence.[^V06-89]

They receive existence from causes beyond matter, but not through any spatial relation to those causes. So the supposed impossibility does not follow. As for the second objection, we say that the soul is not affected by the body as something separate from it, but through its connection to the body. We discuss this fully in our book *The Beginning and the Return*.

The theologians use a path close to the natural philosophers' argument from motion. Their path begins from the fact that things have come to be. They say that bodies are never free from motion or rest, and both have come to be. Anything that is never free from things that have come to be must itself have come to be. So all bodies began to exist, and everything that began to exist needs a maker. Their maker must be neither a body nor physical, to avoid a circle or an endless chain. That maker is God.[^V06-90][^V06-91]

This is also a good path. We have shown that changes in motion come from changes in the very being of the things that move. Anything that can change must be indefinite and able to receive new substantial forms; its features change because its substance changes. So the bodily world and everything in it fades and passes away at every moment and needs something beyond itself.

The familiar objection to the main premise—that something which is never free of changing things must itself have begun to exist—has already been answered by what we have explained. The other familiar objection says that each movement or moving thing could begin while its kind continues to exist. This objection also fails. A general kind does not exist as a lasting thing in itself, either for those who raise the objection or for us. An essence or idea does not exist on its own; it exists only through particular beings. No motion or moving thing remains the same, in its actual existence and identity, while passing through all these changes.

#### Chapter 5 — Why God's Existence Is Not Separate from What He Is

A common proof that God's existence is His very essence goes like this. If an essence has existence, it needs something to make it exist and count as real. Every feature that is not part of a thing's essence must be explained either by the thing itself or by something else. But an essence cannot cause its own existence or make existence necessary for itself. Whatever causes a thing to exist must already exist before it does. If an essence caused its own existence, it would have to exist before it existed, which is impossible. This differs from an essence requiring features other than existence, as a triangle requires three angles and four requires evenness. In those cases, the essence does not have to exist before the feature follows from it. So if God's existence were something added to His essence, it would need a cause outside Him. That is impossible.

We do not think this proof is complete. It also applies to the essences of possible things. Just as an acting cause must exist before its effect, the thing that receives or bears an effect must also be there first.[^V06-92]

If someone answers that there is no real receiving of existence by an essence, but only a difference in how the mind considers things—first thinking of an essence without considering existence, then describing it as existing—we can give the same answer if God is said to have an essence.[^V06-93] We have also shown that, for anything whose reality is existence, what is real is its existence, not its essence. If existence is brought about by another, then the essence follows from that act of bringing existence about. If existence is not brought about, then neither is the essence. In short, the proof confuses a feature of an essence with a feature of existence.[^V06-94][^V06-95] Saying that existence belongs to an essence means only that they differ in the mind's concepts while being one thing in reality.

The proof is sometimes given this way: if God's existence were something added to His essence, then, considered without that added existence, He would be neither existent nor nonexistent, as we explained when discussing essences. Anything like that is possible. If His essence made Him exist, that would be impossible, since something cannot exist before it exists. If something else made Him exist, He would be caused, not necessary. So He would be possible, not necessary.[^V06-96]

I say there is another option. His existence could be due to existence itself, just as prime matter has its form because of the form. The form makes matter real and makes it a bearer of that form; nothing else needs to do this.[^V06-97] Also, it is not true that every feature a thing has must depend on the thing in the same way. That does not apply to features the mind finds by analyzing an idea.[^V06-98]

Someone arguing in the usual way could also say that a cause is needed only for what is possible, as the common view holds. If it is possible for a thing to have a feature or not have it, then something must make it have that feature, as something must make a body white. But if having that feature is necessary or impossible, no cause is needed. The Necessary Being's existence is necessary, so it needs no cause. The source of this necessity is existence itself,[^V06-99] not an essence. When people say that the Necessary Being's essence requires existence, they mean only that it cannot fail to exist. They do not mean that its essence acts on existence. One careful thinker therefore says that God's attributes are not effects He produces.

People also say that, for us, necessity means that a thing requires existence. If God's essence requires existence, does that not make His essence the cause of His existence? The answer given by a later thinker is that there is no difference between causing and requiring, except in the words. If God's essence requires existence, then it causes its own existence. If “requiring” only means that it cannot fail to exist, then either this impossibility comes from the essence itself, which makes the essence a cause, or it comes from something else, which means God depends on that other thing and is not necessary.

I say someone can offer another answer: perhaps the impossibility of not existing comes from existence itself. Existence requires that God have an essence that exists through it, just as a species requires a broader kind.[^V06-100] The idea “animal” is different from the ideas “rational” and “human.” If we consider animal by itself, it may be rational or not, human or not. But if we consider what it means to be human as the essence, and what it means to be rational as its actual existence, then being an animal is necessary for being human. Saying that a human being requires animal nature means only that a human being, so long as it is human, is an animal. Likewise, saying that rational existence requires animal nature does not mean there are two existences or an act of causing between them. Being rational is itself being an animal, without one coming before the other.[^V06-101]

The broader kind and the difference are one in reality, though their meanings differ. If we considered animal without rationality and rationality without animal nature, then they would have different existences. But the mind invents that separation; it has no basis in reality. What these thinkers say would make sense only for those who treat existence as a mental idea and treat the essences of things as what truly exists. They say that existence is a general mental idea, not something real in itself. For them, the essence of a possible thing cannot be called existent unless something else makes it so; but God's essence, if it were present in the mind, would itself be enough for the mind to call it existent. The learned Dawani states this view directly: the shared general idea of existence is a second-order concept, not the real identity of any thing. The mind adds it to every thing, but in possible beings it arises from their being made by another, while in God it arises from His own being.

For that view, God exists through Himself. If the mind considers Him, it draws from Him the general idea of existence, unlike anything else. That is the end of the argument.

Their way of speaking shows that, for them, saying “existence is the same as God's essence” is only a special use of words.[^V06-102] They do not mean that God is a real instance of existence. Their claim applies only to the general mental idea of existence, not to the reality of existence that makes each thing real. On our view, each thing's existence is what is real in it. Existence is not added to one thing and identical to another in the same way. When we say that existence is added to a possible thing but identical with God, we mean that a possible thing is not real if we ignore the one who gives and sustains it.[^V06-103] A caused thing depends on what brings it into being. Its existence comes from a direct act of creation, and its cause upholds it. If we set aside its cause, its existence is no longer real. The Necessary Being, by contrast, exists through Himself, not through anything else. A possible thing cannot be complete without the Necessary Being. God's existence completes everything else; He is independent of all that exists beyond Him. In this sense, existence is added to what is possible but is the very reality of the Necessary Being. Consider this carefully; it deserves to be believed.

There is another way to understand the statement that possible things have an essence while the Necessary Being has none. Every being other than the Necessary Being can be considered at a level of reality where it does not yet exist through its own particular existence.[^V06-104][^V06-105] At that level, we can imagine its essence as not yet existing and as a known form. A body, simply as a body, does not exist at the level of the soul. The soul, simply as a soul, does not exist in the world of intellect. Likewise, something made by another does not have its own existence at the level of its maker. So its essence can be considered there before it has this particular existence. The first principles can know the forms of things before the things themselves exist.

The Necessary Being is different. He exists in Himself and at every level of existence. This does not mean He becomes different things at different times. It means that there is no part of the world where it is not true that God exists, and no aspect of existence in which He has no place.[^V06-106]

Here is another argument that the Necessary Being has no essence. If existence were added to an essence, that essence would have to belong to one of the basic kinds of thing. It would have to be a substance, not one of the features that depend on something else, because those features can exist only in a subject. But if it belonged to the kind of substance, it would need a further feature to distinguish it from other kinds of substance, and so it would need something to make it distinct.

Some kinds of substance clearly need such a distinguisher. And if a possibility belongs to any kind under a general class, then it also belongs to the class itself. If possibility did not apply to the general class, it could not apply to any kind beneath it. For example, if “animal” could not possibly be “stone,” then no kind of animal could be a stone. Whatever is impossible or necessary for the general class by itself also applies to its kinds.

So if the Necessary Being belonged to one of these classes, the class would make Him possible in some respect. He would no longer be necessary, which is impossible. Since the Necessary Being cannot belong to a class, He cannot have an essence in this sense. He is pure existence. This is what we wanted to show.

#### Chapter 6 — God's Oneness: He Has No Partner in Necessary Existence

We have already given a special proof from the highest level of insight. No one before us had noticed it. We set it out in the first part, on universal knowledge and the rules that govern existence. Here we will point to another noble proof that begins from much the same place.

The familiar proof goes like this. If there were more than one being that exists necessarily, each would have to differ from the other. Either each would differ through its own essence,[^V06-107] or through something added to its essence. If they differed through their own essences, then the concept “necessary being” would apply to each only as an attribute. But every attribute depends on what it describes. Each would then be the cause of its own necessary existence, which has already been shown impossible.

If they differed through something added to their essences, that added feature would either be caused by their essences or by something else. The first is impossible. If their essences were one, their individual identities would also be one, so there would be no plurality of either essence or identity—contrary to what we assumed. If their essences were different, necessary existence, or existence at its fullest strength, would be an attribute added to them. We have already shown that God's existence is not added to His essence. If something else caused their difference, each would depend on something else to be the individual it is. Anything that depends on another for its identity also depends on it for its existence. It would therefore be possible, not necessary.

The reply is that the claim “necessary existence is identical with God's essence” cannot mean that the familiar idea of necessary existence is His essence. No reasonable person would say that. If instead it means that God's essence is itself the source from which the concept of existence is drawn, while a possible thing's essence cannot by itself give rise to that concept, why could there not be two things, each of which is in itself the source of the concept and the reality it describes?

A defender of the proof might answer that what gives rise to the shared concept of existence must be a particular existence; and that existence, together with the identity it gives rise to, cannot be something added to the thing's essence.[^V06-108] So a particular, necessary existence is identical with its own individual reality and cannot be shared or multiplied. But this leads to the famous objection attributed to Ibn Kamuna: at first glance, the intellect does not rule out two simple identities, each of which cannot be divided into essence and existence,[^V06-109] each existing simply through itself and needing no cause. This is why people have said that the philosophers' argument here equivocates between a concept and a particular instance. When they said God's existence is identical with His essence, they meant the real existence that subsists through itself. But when they argued that existence cannot be shared because it is identical with His essence, they meant the concept of existence.

If they meant the particular existence that subsists through itself, their proof of oneness would not follow. There could be two particular existences, each subsisting through itself and distinguished by its own essence. Each would be a particular existence, distinct in itself, and its individual existence would be identical with its essence, just as the philosophers say when there is only one.[^V06-110][^V06-111]

I say that this objection has great force against later thinkers who treat existence as only a mental idea. For them, what all existents share is only a general concept drawn from them; the shared concept has no real instance, either in God or in possible things. When they call God's existence “particular,” that is only a special use of the word.

But on the view we have established, the general concept of existence has real instances. It relates to them as a broad feature relates to the particular things it describes. The objection is therefore much weaker and can be answered with a little care. Although the concept is drawn from an essence because of some feature, it is drawn from every particular existence because of what that existence is in itself. Its relation to particular existences is like the relation of meanings such as “humanity” to a human being or “animality” to an animal. We have seen that a shared meaning follows from what the things it is drawn from have in common. Its having one meaning follows from the unity of its sources, and its having many instances follows from their plurality.

For example, the one concept “humanity” is drawn from every human being, but not from a horse, a cow, or any other kind of thing. So the fact that one concept applies to several things means that what it describes is in some way shared by them, whether that shared feature is a broader kind or a species. If there were two beings that each existed necessarily, the concept of existence would be shared between them, as even the objector admits. The real existence from which the concept is drawn would also have to be shared in some way. They would therefore have to differ from each other in their basic essences. When two things share something essential, their difference must also be essential. Neither could then be simple, and this kind of composition is incompatible with necessary existence.

One issue remains. The feature that distinguishes two things can sometimes be the same feature they share, as when one thing has more or less of a quality than another. We see this in the reality of existence. Someone could therefore say that one necessary being differs from another because its existence is more complete and stronger, while both remain simple in themselves. At first glance, we have no proof that one necessary being cannot be less complete than another, even if we agree that it cannot be less complete than a possible being.

This objection can be answered by what we said earlier: any lack implies that a thing is caused. The reality of existence cannot be incomplete in itself, without a cause. Lack is not the same as existence; it is an absence. A thing cannot give rise to its own absence. Perfection, by contrast, strengthens what a thing already is. A longer line is longer through the nature of line itself. But we cannot say that a shorter line is shorter because of the nature of line; it is shorter because it lacks some length. Any line that is unlimited has nothing in it but the nature of line. A limited line has both line and something else: an endpoint or boundary that line itself does not require. So any existence that is limited in strength must have a cause, other than that particular existence, that sets and establishes its degree. Being caused is incompatible with necessary existence, which means existing eternally by itself. There cannot, then, be more than one necessary being.

This argument, in this form, does not work on the view that existence is only a mental idea. But we can restate it this way: the general concept of existence has one shared meaning among all existents. The source of that concept in possible things is not their essences by themselves, but their relation to the One who gives and sustains them. In the Necessary Being, the source of the concept is His essence itself. In reality, then, it is God's essence that is the source of the concept of existence in every case.[^V06-112] If there were more than one Necessary Being, each would, by its own essence, be an instance of this one shared meaning and the source from which it is drawn. Yet common sense tells us that one shared meaning cannot belong to completely different things only because they differ. There must be some common feature among them. So the two necessary beings would share an essential feature outside either one, and they would also have to differ through another feature of their essences, as a species differs from another species by its defining feature, or one individual differs from another by its individual mark. This composition is incompatible with necessary existence. This gives a sound basis for the proofs the philosophers offered.

One such proof appears in Avicenna's *Annotations*: “The existence of the Necessary Being is identical with His individual reality. So being existent is the same as being Himself. The Necessary Being does not exist for the sake of something else.” On our account, this cannot be challenged by asking why there could not be two things, each with its own particular existence identical to its own reality. If “being Himself” means that His particular existence is identical with Him, the same could be said of two things. If it means that His being existent in general is identical with Him, that is not accepted, since the shared meaning of existence requires the real source of existence to be shared as well.

Another proof says: if more than one Necessary Being existed, they would either have the same essence[^V06-113] or different essences. If they had the same essence, that essence could not by itself apply to many individuals; otherwise many things would exist without the one that makes them one.[^V06-114] If their essences differed, then necessary existence would be an attribute added to each. Every added attribute depends either on the thing that bears it or on something else. Both options fail. If it depended on its bearer alone, each would cause its own existence. If it depended on something else, the problem would be even worse. This proof too can face the familiar objection, despite the premise just explained.

Another proof comes from al-Farabi in *Fusus*: “Necessary existence cannot be divided so that it applies to many things that differ in number; otherwise it would be caused.”[^V06-115] The brief statement sums up the argument we have given.[^V06-116] It is a short proof that avoids Ibn Kamuna's objection, because that objection depends on treating existence as only a general concept, as the Illuminationist philosopher and most later thinkers did. Suhrawardi himself raised the objection directly in *al-Muṭāraḥāt* and hinted at it in *al-Talwīḥāt*. Ibn Kamuna, one of the commentators on Suhrawardi, later repeated it in one of his works, and it became known by his name. Because he strongly held that existence is only a mental idea and has no reality outside the mind, he wrote in one of his books: “The arguments they give show only that there cannot be more than one Necessary Being with the same essence. If their essences differ, another proof is needed, and I have not found one so far.”

#### Chapter 7 — A Later Thinker’s Proof of Oneness, and Its Problems

The learned Dawānī says in one of his letters and in his commentary on *The Luminous Temples* that we should begin with two points.

First, we cannot work out philosophical truths from everyday uses of words. Ordinary speech sometimes uses a word in a way that does not fit what reason shows. For example, people hear the word “knowledge” and think of knowing or learning, which they describe as a relation between things. But philosophical inquiry shows that the form of knowledge may itself be a substance, as when we know a substance,[^V06-117] or may exist in itself, as when immaterial beings know themselves, or may even exist necessarily, as when the Necessary Being knows Himself. In the same way, the defining features of substances are often expressed by words that sound like relations or added qualities. “Rational,” for example, is the defining feature of a human being; “sensing” and “moving by choice” define an animal. In truth, these are not relations or added qualities,[^V06-118] because any part of a substance must itself be a substance.

Second, a word formed from a quality does not always mean that the quality itself is present in the thing, even if ordinary speech suggests it does.[^V06-119] We call Zayd a blacksmith because iron is the material of his work, and call water “sun-heated” because it was warmed by the sun. Dawānī then says that existence, from which we form the word “existent,” might itself be something that exists through itself: the reality of God. Everything else would exist through its connection to Him. The word “existent” would then apply both to that reality and to other things through their relation to it. This broad concept would be a mental construct, one of the second-order ideas, and the first thing we know without proof.

You might ask: how can this reality exist if it is existence itself? And how can “existent” mean more than this one reality?

Dawānī replies that “existent” does not have to mean something separate from existence, as ordinary usage may suggest. In Persian, it means “is” or “exists.” If we suppose that existence stands by itself, separate from everything else, then it exists through itself and is both existence and existent. In the same way, if a knowing form exists by itself, it knows itself and is knowledge, knower, and known. If heat were separate from fire and existed by itself, it would be both hot and heat. Bahmanyār says in *The Book of Joy and Happiness* that a sensible form, if separated from the senses and existing by itself, would both sense and be sensed.

Dawānī also says that we know existence is different from what exists when we see that some things can exist and later cease to exist. If they were existence itself, they would have to exist necessarily. Since some existents are not necessary, existence must be something added to them.[^V06-120][^V06-121]

You might then ask how this broader meaning is possible. Dawānī answers that it may mean either existence itself or something connected to it in a particular way. The test is whether it is the source of effects. Or the general meaning may be what existence belongs to: either existence standing by itself, where it belongs to itself, or a mental relation that applies to its subjects, as with “universal” and “particular.” Even if “belongs to” is only a figurative way to speak in the first case, it does not follow that “existent” is figurative.[^V06-122]

In short, the existence from which we form “existent” is one reality that exists through itself. “Existent” applies both to it and to things related to it. If we read the philosophers this way, we avoid saying that existence as a concept is the first thing we know, while applying it to the self-subsisting reality only as a figure of speech or by a different use of the word. This also clears up the confusion that troubles people who study the question.

You might object that showing how the philosophers’ words could be read this way is not enough. We need proof that this is how things really are.
