Dawānī answers: proof has shown that the existence of the Necessary Being is identical with His essence, and it is clear that a general idea shared by many things cannot be identical with His essence.

You might then ask why there cannot be two distinct realities, each necessary in itself, with the concept of necessary existence applying to both only as an added quality. Dawānī says that the earlier and later points are enough to answer this. If the concept were an added quality to each, it would be caused either by each thing’s own essence—so that it would have to exist before itself—or by something else, which is worse. It has been established that anything whose necessity or existence is an added quality is possible, not necessary. So the Necessary Being is existence itself, existing in itself at its fullest strength. When we say that the Necessary Being is existent, we mean this. We do not mean that existence is a quality added to a subject. Dawānī says that al-Farabi and Avicenna also held that calling God “existent” is figurative, despite what the word may suggest in ordinary speech.

If this is understood, there cannot be two self-subsisting realities, each necessary in itself, since then necessary existence would be a shared attribute that belonged to both. We can put Dawānī’s point another way. We may begin with the shared meaning of existence[^V06-123] and, by examining it, discover that it is not shared as a quality added to different subjects. It is shared because different things are related to one reality. The existence to which all essences are related is something that exists through itself, not a quality added to something else, and is necessary in itself.[^V06-124]

At first, we might think that all blacksmiths share iron, and everything warmed by the sun shares the sun. Then we notice that they do not share iron or the sun as qualities present in them; rather, they are related to iron or to the sun. In the same way, what we thought was a shared quality is actually one reality that exists through itself, with other things related to it. There are not many suns or many kinds of iron behind these words.

Dawānī says that the familiar view—that existence is a quality added to essences—raises serious problems. If a thing has a quality, it must already exist in itself. But the subject here is unrestricted existence, and an essence has no existence before unrestricted existence is added to it. Some say that the essence is qualified by existence only in the mind. But this does not help: the question then becomes how it is qualified by mental existence. Making an exception for existence from the rule that a subject must exist before its quality is added to it is arbitrary, especially since leading philosophers have challenged that exception.

He adds that if existence is a quality of essence, and the agent’s effect is to make essence exist, then what the agent first produces must be the relation of essence to existence. But a relation requires the things it relates to exist first. So that relation cannot be the first thing produced. These are some of the problems that arise from saying that existence is a quality added to essences. On Dawānī’s account, none of them arise.

This is the view Dawānī supports. I say that, although this skilled thinker explained it at length, and most later scholars accepted or even admired it, it does not help with the question of oneness, even if all its premises are sound. Here are several objections. First, we do not accept that the word “blacksmith” is derived from the word “iron.” Iron is a basic noun, not a source from which another word is formed.[^V06-125][^V06-126] The same applies to “sun-heated.”

Second, even if using a derived word for something does not mean that its source quality is present in it, as Dawānī says, the source quality must at least be real in it.[^V06-127] His examples of the blacksmith and sun-heated water do not prove otherwise. Those uses may simply be figurative.[^V06-128] The source of the word could be “being ironlike” or “being sunlike,” not iron or the sun themselves. We might say the blacksmith has a share in iron because he works with it so much that its image fills his mind.[^V06-129] Iron itself cannot exist inside another thing, but an image of iron can exist in the mind. Likewise, we might figuratively call warmed water “sun-heated” because it seems to contain a share of the sun.[^V06-130] In short, as Dawānī himself says, we cannot discover the truth from such everyday uses of words.[^V06-131] Why rely on them here?

Third, a derived word is certainly a general concept,[^V06-132] and its source—whether the source is part of that concept or identical with it—must also be a general concept. Neither a part of a general concept nor the whole concept can be one particular thing. So the source of the word “existent” cannot be a particular reality that exists through itself.[^V06-133]

Fourth, people cannot form a derived word from a source whose meaning they do not know. But no scholar, or anyone else, knows God's reality in itself.[^V06-134] Yet people in every language use “existent,” “exists,” and similar words, and understand them without picturing God's sacred reality or a relation to it. Dawānī says that everyday speech can use a word in a way that conflicts with what reason shows. Even if that is true, it does not prove that “existent” is being used in that way here. The concepts of existence and existent are among the clearest and most familiar ideas. Dawānī's account would make them among the hardest to understand, since no one knows God's essence or any relation to something unknown.

Fifth, every derived word must have one source. It cannot have one source in one case and another in a second case.[^V06-135][^V06-136] No one has heard of a word being formed from two different sources in this way. So it makes no sense to say that “existent,” when applied to God, means existence, but when applied to other things means something related to existence.[^V06-137] Dawānī himself says the concept has one shared meaning. This is not like the word “black,” which may describe blackness itself or something that has blackness. Its meaning is the same in both cases: something in which blackness is present. In one case, blackness exists by itself; in the other, it is joined to something else. What makes either one black is simply the presence of blackness. The word “existent,” as Dawānī understands it, does not work this way.

Sixth, how did Dawānī learn that God's essence is pure existence after denying that existence has any reality outside the mind?[^V06-138] He says philosophers treat existence as a second-order idea with no real instance. How, then, did he learn that God's reality is an instance of existence? If he learned it because philosophers call God “existent,” then he is using an ordinary word to discover a truth, though he himself said that words used in ordinary speech do not reveal philosophical truths. It is strange that he spent so much effort proving a minor point about a derived word while leaving aside what matters here: proving that God is pure reality of existence, or pure existent. Unity can be proved only by showing that the shared concept of existence points to one simple reality. Since Dawānī denies that existence has any reality outside the mind, he is far from proving this.

His answer to the question, “How can this reality exist if it is existence itself?” also misses the point. It works only if the question is, “If God's essence is existence, how can He be existent?” But the real question is how God's essence can exist outside the mind if, according to Dawānī, existence is only a second-order mental idea. In that case, one might say that God is the real existent, not existence itself. That would reverse Dawānī's view, as another thinker of his time proposed: God is identical with the concept of existence.[^V06-139] We have already shown what is wrong with that view. The truth is that God's essence is identical with the real nature of existence, and He is the reality that makes the concept of “existent” apply.

Seventh, Dawānī's own words—“If we suppose that existence is separated from everything else and stands by itself, then it exists through itself”—plainly show that existence has one shared reality. Some instances stand by themselves, and others depend on something else.[^V06-140] This is possible only if existence has a real shared nature, not if it is merely an abstract concept, as we maintain. The mind cannot accept that a relation or concept formed from relations could exist through itself.

Eighth, Dawānī does not prove that the source of the concept “existent” is one reality. He says that “existent” applies both to one self-subsisting reality and to things related to it. But even if we accept this division, he has not shown that the first kind is one reality. For him, the shared concept has no real instance outside the mind. At most, he can say that God's essence exists by itself without a maker or a recipient. Someone could still suppose that there are two realities with this same feature.

Ninth, even if it were true that treating existence as a quality added to essences creates problems, that would not prove that possible things exist through their relation to one particular reality of existence. They might depend on existence in another way: they could be specific forms of existence that differ in strength, weakness, priority, and lateness, or they could be identical with the concept of “existent,” as some have held. Most problems with saying that essence is qualified by existence arise only if this relation is treated like a subject bearing an accident, or if we fail to distinguish a quality added to existence from a quality added to essence.[^V06-141] A quality added to existence requires a subject with an existence separate from that quality. A quality added to essence requires only that the essence exist, even if its existence is the same as the quality's. So existence, if it is real, need not be a quality added to essence; it may simply be the existing reality of essence.[^V06-142]

Tenth, it makes no sense to say that possible things exist by being related to one particular reality of existence. A relation cannot exist until both things it relates—the subject and what it is related to—already exist. Dawānī rejected the claim that the agent first produces the relation of essence to existence, because a relation depends on the things related. Yet here he says that the existence of everything is its relation to that one reality. Is this not a contradiction?

These are the main points. With God's help, we have made the way easier, removed the doubts, and cleared the argument of the confusion that clouded it.

I have examined this learned thinker's words because most people who study them accept and praise them. He and his followers seem to think[^V06-143] they have proved the special oneness known by the great mystics, and certainly the oneness of God that Muslims believe in. They do not see that treating existence as a mere idea closes the path to knowing God. The mystics' path is to see the light of existence flowing through all things, and to know that each thing exists by being joined to that light and receiving a level of it. If things existed only because they were empty of existence and stripped of it, then there would be no meaningful difference between what exists and what does not. Their path is the opposite of ours. By God's grace, each of us is guided toward the work we were made for.

Another problem arises in Dawānī’s discussion. He says that oneness can be proved this way: if there were more than one Necessary Being, the whole made up of them—before adding the idea of “two”—would be either necessary or possible.[^V06-144] It cannot be necessary, because this whole depends on each member, and dependence is incompatible with necessity. Nor can it be possible, because a possible thing needs a complete cause. That cause would have to be either the whole itself, which would make something cause itself and come before itself, or one of the members, which is impossible because the whole also depends on the other member.[^V06-145] Dawānī says the question is not about a complete cause, which, according to the common view, need not come before its effect.[^V06-146] So one could say that the complete cause is the whole itself, as when the Necessary Being and the first effect together form a whole.[^V06-147]

I have already shown why this proof fails. A collection that has no form or principle of unity over and above its members exists only through each member separately. Dawānī also argues that a multiple whole disappears if one of its members disappears, while every member here exists. We have seen why this is a fallacy.

He also argues that, according to the view that many things can proceed from the One, one thing may come from the Necessary Being, another from the first effect, and a third from the Necessary Being and the first effect together. Then there must be something that comes from their combination; otherwise, nothing could come from that combination. He claims that this is how many things can proceed from one real cause without relying on differences in the first effect, as people usually say.

I say that this account of how many things proceed from one was given by Suhrawardi, the Slain Master, in several books and followed by al-Tusi in his commentary on *Pointers* and in a separate treatise. But we do not accept it. Proof is what matters. We have another account of how multiplicity proceeds from the One, different both from this one and from the account that relies on mental distinctions. We will explain it later, God willing.

Another weak argument by some philosophers says: if there were two Necessary Beings, either they would have to exist together or they would not. If they had to exist together, they would be caused, as things that must exist together generally are. If they did not have to exist together, one could exist without the other, which would make a Necessary Being's absence possible. This argument is more like trickery than philosophy.[^V06-148] It confuses what is possible in itself with what is possible in relation to something else. Another version says that multiplicity is either necessary by the nature of necessary existence, possible in relation to it, or impossible in relation to it. If multiplicity were necessary, there would be many without the one; if possible, necessary existence could be removed; if impossible, that would be the correct conclusion. This too slips between possibility in itself and possibility in relation to another.[^V06-149]

There is another version of the argument. Sayyid al-Sadr al-Shirazi puts it this way: sometimes we mean by “existent” a thing that exists, such as a letter that is present or a person who is present. At other times, we mean pure existence, not a thing that has existence—as when we say “a present sky” or “a present person.”[^V06-150] The Necessary Being is pure existence; possible things are things that exist, meaning the mind can analyze them into an essence and the fact that it exists. Likewise, the concept of “necessary” is identical with the Necessary Being, because He cannot be analyzed into parts. If He could be divided, and A were necessary, reason would see that the general concept of necessity is not identical to A's essence and is not part of it. It would then be an added feature, which must be caused. So every thing with an essence is caused, and the Necessary Being has no essence. Existence in possible things is added to their essences, but the Necessary Being's existence is not.

After laying this out, he says that there cannot be more than one Necessary Being. If there were, the feature that makes one differ from the other would either be their own essence or something outside it. If they differed through their own essences, the Necessary Being would have an essence, which has already been shown impossible. If they differed through something outside their essences, each would need a cause to make it the particular one it is. That cause cannot be its essence, since the Necessary Being has no essence; it cannot be the individual itself, since nothing can cause its own individuality; it cannot be existence, necessity, or another general concept, because what is shared cannot make one individual different from another. If it were something else, the Necessary Being would be caused. This is the end of his argument.[^V06-151]

This approach is close to the truth if, instead of the general concept of “existent” or “necessary,” we use the real nature of existence itself. We must recognize that existence has a reality that is identical with its individual instances. Every existent has a particular instance of this reality, whether it also has an essence or not—just as whiteness is a real thing in the world: it is white in itself, and something else is white by having it. We have already explained how essence can be qualified by existence in a way that removes every doubt. The Necessary Being, then, is not only existence; He is the real nature of existence itself, not the general idea that exists only in the mind.

On this reading, we can answer the objections Dawānī raised against the argument of one of his contemporaries. Dawānī asks: if God is pure existence, is He identical with the shared concept of “existent”? That is plainly false, since God's essence cannot be a mental construct. If He is a particular instance of the concept, then His being existent must be either in Himself or because He is an instance of the concept. If it is in Himself, and He is an instance of the concept, then He would be existent twice—once through Himself and once through being an instance of the concept. If His existence comes only from being an instance of the concept, then He would not exist in Himself, no different from possible things.[^V06-152]

Dawānī also says that, on the first view, God would still be a subject of the shared concept. Then He would not differ from other things in that respect. And there would be no meaning to saying He cannot be analyzed into existence and essence, since He would have a particular instance under the shared concept. If Dawānī replies that God cannot be analyzed into His particular existence and the general concept of existent, we answer that a human being who exists can also be analyzed into the human being who is its essence and the general concept “existent,” which applies to it as an added quality. So the two cases would be alike: in both, the general concept would have an instance in the particular thing.

Dawānī further says that if God has no essence, either He has no reality at all, which is clearly false, or He has a reality that is not called an “essence” by convention. That would only be a dispute about words. Anyone who says God's reality is existence that subsists through itself means by it the same thing as “essence.” Dawānī also says that “God is a particular existent” is empty of meaning, since God's essence is beyond our understanding, and the concept “existent” applies to Him only as an added quality. In that respect, He would be no better than possible things. On our view, God's superiority is clear: His essence is a particular existence that is itself the source from which the concept is drawn. This is the full set of objections made by Dawānī, his contemporary.[^V06-153]

I say these objections can be answered on our account. As to the first: we can say that God's essence is identical with a particular existence. The fact that He is an instance of the general concept of existent does not mean that He exists twice, or that the mind can divide Him into two things. His existence comes from Himself, not from the addition of a share of the concept, just as a particular person, Zayd, is an instance of the general concept “human” without having two separate humanities, one particular and one universal.[^V06-154][^V06-155] To analyze something into two things means to find two features in it, each with its own basis and meaning, as when we analyze “existing human being” into humanity and existence. It does not mean analyzing a particular existence into itself and the general idea of existence. Every particular existence is called existent in itself, not because another quality has been added to it. Likewise, when the pure existent is called existent, this does not mean that it is made of two distinct things. The general concept simply points to that particular reality.

As to Dawānī's second point, the difference between the Necessary Being and possible things is that the Necessary Being has no two distinct features that would make it a composite, either in the world or in the mind. This does not mean that many concepts cannot apply to Him. He is the source of every quality of perfection through Himself. Saying that God cannot be analyzed into two things means that no aspect of Him is other than His being the Necessary Being. A possible thing can be analyzed in this way. A human being's humanity is not the same as its being necessary or existent. A human being can be imagined without existing, and existence can be imagined without being human, so these are different features. But for God, “this particular being” and “necessary being in general” refer to one true reality.

As to the third point, God's reality is one individual identity, which we call pure existence, pure existent, existence, individuality, or necessary being. It is not a general essence. An essence is what the mind imagines after setting aside existence and individuality; it can then be thought of as universal and shared. The reality of existence is itself a particular identity. The mind cannot picture it or know it except through direct awareness. That is what it means to say that God has no essence. This is not merely a choice of words; it is a philosophical truth.

So the statement “God is a particular existent” is meaningful, as long as we do not mean that He is identical with the general concept of existence or with one of its instances in the way a species is an instance of a broader kind.[^V06-156][^V06-157] Concepts such as existence, existent, individuality, individual, particular, and identity do not have individual instances in the way broader kinds and species do. They are ideas in the mind that point to particular things. The concepts “existence” and “existent” may apply directly to some things, with no further condition, or apply to others only because of an added condition. The first are existent in themselves; the second exist only through something else.

Since particular existences share the mental concept that points to them, they must share the same underlying kind of reality. Yet they also differ—by greater or lesser completeness, richness or poverty, priority or lateness, or by added qualities.[^V06-158] This is how we can show that Necessary Existence cannot be plural. These replies answer Dawānī’s objections, if we accept his claim that God is pure existent in the sense we have just explained. Some of his other statements and works, however, go against that reading.

Dawānī’s son, whom the author honors as a leading scholar, set out to answer these objections in another way. His reply to the first objection is that God is identical with the concept of “existent.” How could He not be, when “existent” is said of Him? To say that one thing *is* another is to judge that they are one in the respect being discussed. Every possible thing, too, is identical with this concept in that sense. This does not mean that the concept is the essence of any one thing.[^V06-159]

He says it is plainly wrong to call this concept merely mental. That an existent exists is self-evident. The philosophers say that the subject of metaphysics is unrestricted existence, and then say that its existence needs no proof because it is evident. So, he concludes, outside the mind the Necessary Being is identical with the concept of “existent”: the concept exists in the world through its individual instances, and in this way it can be identical with the Necessary Being.

His reply to the objection that God’s existence must come from Himself or from something else is also disputed. To understand his argument, we first need a distinction he sets out in his notes on *Tajrīd*: when we rightly describe a thing by an attribute, the feature belongs to the thing itself, although the mind can distinguish the thing from its description. Ask whether the thing, considered as it exists in the world, is identical with the attribute, and the answer is yes. Considered in the mind, the answer is no. They are one in external existence and distinct in thought. A relation between them is possible only when they are distinct; if there is no mental distinction, there can be no relation between the thing and the attribute.

With this distinction in place, he says that if the Necessary Being has no existence in the mind, there can be no mental distinction between Him and the concept of “existent.” So there can be no relation between them, and it makes no sense to ask whether His own essence or something else gives Him this relation. The same is not true of possible things. This, he says, answers the second objection and removes the far-fetched alternatives that sound unreasonable. A possible thing can be represented in the mind and divided into parts; the Necessary Being cannot.

His answer to the third objection is that an essence—the answer to the question “What is it?”—is what remains when the mind sets aside a thing’s attributes. Once the mind has set them aside, it can distinguish one from another, and it finds the essence bare of the attributes we say it has. In reality, however, the essence is joined to those attributes and one with them. The mind then asks what caused this state of affairs. The cause must be either the essence itself or something else. Since the essence cannot be the cause—nothing can exist before it exists—the philosophers conclude that anything with an essence is caused. That is the end of his argument.

I see several confusions in this reply. First, the claim that the idea of “what exists” must itself exist because it can be described by the word “existent” confuses two ways of speaking.[^V06-160] In one sense, an idea fits its own definition: the idea of existence is, of course, the idea of existence. But that does not mean the idea itself is an existing thing outside the mind.

Second, when the philosophers say that the subject of universal metaphysics is unrestricted existence,[^V06-161] they do not mean the universal concept in the mind. They mean things as they exist in reality, without restricting them to any particular nature or quantity. A person, for example, is a physical and natural existent, but is also an existent without that restriction. Metaphysics studies things in this latter respect. The thing that needs no proof because its existence is evident is an actual instance of unrestricted existence, not the universal concept, which exists only in the mind.

Third, his account of the difference between the Necessary Being and possible things cannot be relied on. God’s essence cannot be grasped by the mind and does not exist in the mind because He is pure existence, not because He is unintelligible.[^V06-162] A thing can be open to analysis if the mind could analyze it were it present there, even if it cannot actually be present in the mind. So the difference is not that the concept of unrestricted existence is identical with God but added to possible things. The true difference is this: a possible thing can be analyzed into two aspects—its existence and whatever else it is. The Necessary Being cannot. Every aspect of God is the same as His pure existence. He has no deficiency, while every possible thing has some deficiency distinct from its existence and necessity. A heavenly sphere, for example, is not pure existence: it is limited, depends on a place or region, and needs a cause and a mover. Its many negations and lacks correspond to perfections it does not have. These are not the same as its existence.

God, by contrast, is the pure reality of existence itself. The word “existent” and many other words for perfection apply to Him because of that one reality. He exists through His own concrete identity, whether or not we apply the general concept of unrestricted existence to Him. Applying that concept does not add a second, distinct aspect to Him. In the same way, calling Zayd “this existent” or “an existent in general” does not make him composite; what makes a person composite is being actual in some respects and only potentially something else, such as being potentially a writer or a scholar. The philosophers say that things with essences are caused because their essence or possibility is distinct from their actuality and existence. The first aspect needs a cause; the second may be independent of one, as it is in the Necessary Being.

**A final clarification.** It is right to say that God’s essence is pure existence, if by “existence” we mean the real nature of existence. Existence has a reality of its own: it exists through itself, while other things exist through it, just as whiteness is white in itself and other things are white through it. But the claim is false if “existence” means only the concept in the mind, the description rather than the reality it describes.

You might ask: how can God’s essence be identical with the real nature of existence if existence is an obvious concept, while God’s essence is unknown? As we have explained, existence is so clear and strong, and our ability to understand it so weak, that its very brightness hides it from us. God’s essence is, in itself, the fullest light and clarity.[^V06-163]

You might then say: if God’s essence is existence, either existence is His essence in the ordinary sense of the word, or it applies to Him only as an attribute, as the concept of “thing” does. In the first case, it must mean either the general concept of existence, which is obviously false, or some other meaning. But then God’s reality would be unlike anything we understand by the word “existence.” You would simply be naming that reality “existence,” as if you named a person “Existence.” The name would change nothing. This answer would amount to saying that the Necessary Being is not the existence we are discussing. It would also make Him a thing with an essence, and we have shown that anything with an essence is caused. In the second case, where existence applies to Him only as an attribute, it still needs a cause; indeed, it would not even guarantee that He exists. For this reason, most later philosophers have said that existence is not real.

I say this difficulty comes from failing to distinguish the real nature and individual instances of existence from the universal concept, and from misunderstanding what it means to say that this universal concept is an attribute of actual existents. It does not mean that the thing has one existence and the attribute has another, as a walking animal has existence and walking is added to it, or as laughter is added to a human being. The universal concept is a description and reflection of actual existences. Its relation to them is like the relation of “humanity” to human beings, or “animality” to animals: the concept “humanity” can be called identical with a human being because it reflects and describes that being’s nature, and it can also be called distinct because it is an abstract relation while the human being is a concrete substance.

Existence is not like possibility, which has no real thing corresponding to it; the concept of existence describes a reality. It is like blackness, a word that can mean the quality of being black or the particular quality that makes something black. If blackness were assumed to exist by itself, we could say that its essence is identical with being black. But if a body is described as black, we could not say that the body itself is identical with blackness—even though “black” is a general concept added in thought to all black things.

With this distinction in place, we can answer the question by choosing the first option: existence is God’s essence. As for the next question—whether that means the general concept we understand by the word “existence”—we say it means the real existence outside the mind, which this obvious concept describes. Every existent has a real nature of existence, just as every black thing has the reality of blackness. In some existents, existence is mixed with deficiencies and absences; in others, it is not. And just as shades of blackness vary in strength, existents vary in how fully they exist.[^V06-164]

We can also choose the second option in the first question, with one qualification. The general concept of existence is an attribute in the sense that, as a concept describing things, it has no existence of its own outside the mind. It describes the real nature of existence, which subsists through itself. The concept applies to that reality because that reality itself is what makes the concept true—not because another quality is present in it, as happens with ordinary attributes. So the objection that God would need a cause if existence were an attribute does not apply. This particular reality exists through itself, whether or not we apply the concepts “existence” and “existent” to it. What most philosophers call unreal is not these particular realities of existence, but the universal concept in the mind, which applies to them.

#### Chapter 8 — The Necessary Being Has No Partner in Divinity: The World Has One God

The earlier proofs[^V06-165] showed that the Necessary Being is one in Himself and has no partner in necessary existence. Now we want to show that the world has one God, with no partner in divinity. At first glance, it does not follow simply from there being one Necessary Being that there is one God of the world. We can reason as follows: once it is clear that the Necessary Being is one, and that everything else is possible in itself and depends on Him for its existence, then all things must ultimately depend on Him and receive their existence from Him.[^V06-166] The existence of everything, then, comes from one source: the Necessary Being in Himself. All things come from Him. His relation to everything else is like the relation the sun’s light would have to bodies that are dark by their own nature, if the light existed on its own.[^V06-167] The sun is bright in itself, and everything else shines by its light. If you see the sun light up a place, and then see another light arise from that illumination, you trace the second light back to the sun. You do the same with a third, a fourth, and so on, down to the faintest light. In the same way, all existences, however near or far they are from the One who is truly one, come from God.

Another proof, pointed to in the Qur'an and used by Aristotle, argues from the unity of the world to the unity of its God. It first shows that there cannot be another world alongside this one, whether that world had heavens, earths, and elements of the same kinds as ours or different kinds. If there were another world, its natural shape would be a sphere. If the two spheres did not surround one another, there would have to be empty space between them. But empty space is impossible, as we have already shown. So there cannot be a second world separate from ours. That is the outline of the argument. Let us look at each possibility more closely.

First suppose the two worlds had the same kinds of parts, each with its own heavens, earth, and other elements. Aristotle’s argument is that elements with the same nature must have the same natural places, movements, and directions of movement. So elements of the same kind in different worlds would be in different places, even though by nature they belong in one place. They would therefore be at rest by force. But a state imposed by force comes after a thing’s natural state. This would mean that these elements were once together as one and later separated. They would then always be separate and not separate at the same time—a contradiction.[^V06-169][^V06-170]

Also, what is held in place by force must eventually leave that state and return to where it naturally belongs.[^V06-171] The worlds would then come together again. So they would always be joined and not joined.

Someone might reply that there could be many earths, all sharing the nature of earth, while each had its own center in its own world. The general nature of earth would call for the center in general, and each particular earth for the center of its particular world. But bodies that are alike in kind must have places that are alike in kind too. If all these bodies were together in one place, their places would become one place. That they must have numerically different places is obvious; but nothing in their nature prevents them from coming together. If their nature required them always to remain apart, then no single continuous body of that kind could exist, which is absurd.[^V06-172][^V06-173]

For the other possibility—that another world might have bodies with different movements and qualities—Avicenna offers an argument in one of his treatises.[^V06-174] By reason, movement must be either circular or straight. Straight movement is either outward from the center, inward toward it, or across the center, with the body moving between opposite ends.[^V06-175] A body’s natural straight movement must be between ends that are naturally opposite, not merely opposite in relation to us. Aristotle explains this in Book Five of *Physics*, as do its commentators. From this we can see by reason that all the natural movements of bodies are either away from the center or toward it. As for sensible qualities, there can be no more than nineteen.[^V06-176] Aristotle explained this in Book Two of *On the Soul*, and its commentators Themistius and Alexander discussed it. I will not go through the full argument here, but will sketch part of it.

Nature does not move a thing into a higher kind until it has fully given it the features of the lower kind that comes first.[^V06-177] For example, a body cannot become a plant until it has all the qualities that belong to bodies as bodies. Nor can a thing become an animal until it has all the powers of plant life, such as nourishment, growth, and reproduction. Animal life includes sensation and movement, along with the bodily and plant powers. So before nature could bring a thing from the lowest kind of animal life into rational life, it would have to give it all the senses that perceive every kind of sensible thing. Nature has, in fact, produced rational beings. It must therefore have given them every sense in its fullest form, followed by the power of reason. A rational being can thus perceive everything that any animal perceives. So there is no sensible thing that a rational being cannot perceive.[^V06-178]

There can therefore be no sensible qualities beyond the sixteen perceived directly and the three perceived indirectly, such as movement, stillness, and shape.[^V06-179] No body can have a sensible quality beyond these. So there cannot be another world with sensible qualities different from ours. If we suppose several worlds, they must have the same nature and differ only as separate individuals. That is Aristotle’s argument.

Once we have ruled out several worlds—whether they differ in kind or only as individuals—we have shown that the world is one individual. Its unity is natural, not merely the result of putting parts together.[^V06-180] Its basic parts depend on one another. The great bodies in it depend on one another, as do those bodies and their qualities, and many other parts and their qualities. The impossibility of empty space, and the fact that bodies moving in straight lines cannot be without something that fixes the directions of their movement, show that earth and heaven depend on each other. An accident cannot exist on its own, and a substance cannot be without accidents; this too shows their dependence on each other.

As you know, such dependence and mutual connection[^V06-181] lead back to one cause. So the source that acts upon our world must be one. Every body and everything that depends on a body traces its existence to that single source, whose existence is shown by the ordered state of the heavens, the earth, and everything between them. The intellects and souls affirmed by the philosophers are either intermediate causes of these bodies or governing forms that direct them. The existence of separate beings that neither cause nor govern this world is not established—and, as will become clear, such beings do not exist.[^V06-182]

Every body, every bodily being, every soul, and every intellect therefore leads back to one source: God, the Self-Sustaining, Necessary Being. This is what the Qur'anic verse teaches: “If there were gods besides God in heaven and earth, both would fall into ruin.” The two realms may mean the whole of the heavens, including their intellects and souls, and the whole of the earth, including its unseen realm and its guardians.

“Ruin” may mean that the two realms would cease to exist altogether. The reasoning is this: if there were more than one god, the bodily world and everything connected to it would have to be multiple. But we have shown that this is false. So the claim that there are multiple gods is false as well. The next verse makes the point plain: “Then each god would have taken away what it created, and some would have risen above others.”

#### Chapter 9 — God Is Not Made of Parts, Either in Reality or in Thought

God is not made up of parts of any kind: real or mental parts such as matter and form; parts used to define a thing, such as broader kind and defining feature; or parts of size.[^V06-183] For anything composed of parts, the mind finds that the part exists in a more basic way than the whole does.[^V06-184] The whole depends on its part and is real only when the part is real, even if the part did not cause the whole. Anything that depends on something else in this way is necessary through that other thing, not necessary in itself. It is therefore possible in itself, which cannot be true of God.

This argument applies to every kind of part except parts of size. Such parts are not truly prior to the whole; we speak of them as parts by approximation and comparison.[^V06-185] So we need another proof that God has no parts of size. Earlier philosophers spent a long time arguing that the Necessary Being is immaterial and has no body, and they relied on the proof that dimensions cannot extend without limit.[^V06-186]

We have a clearer way to show this, based on a point mentioned several times already: anything extended, whether extension means space or time, has a weak kind of existence. Its existence is mixed with nonexistence, and its presence with absence. But God, may He be exalted, has the strongest possible existence, with no weakness or lack. We have also established that parts of size and the whole have the same nature.[^V06-187]

Suppose, as those who picture God as a body claim, that God had a part of size. That part would either be possible in itself or necessary in itself. If it were possible, it would have to differ in nature from the whole. If it were necessary, God would not be fully actual; He would exist only potentially. Both conclusions are impossible. So the original claim—that God has a part of size—is impossible. He is far above the imaginings of those who picture Him as a body or deny His reality altogether.

Another proof:[^V06-188] if God’s essence were made of parts, then either every part, or some parts, would be necessary in themselves, or none of the parts would be necessary and all would be possible. All three options are impossible.

The first option—that every part is necessary—goes against what we assumed: that God is one reality made of parts. A true unity cannot exist without its principle of unity, as we have explained. Any one reality made of parts must have a real dependence and connection between those parts. A specific kind of thing, or a particular individual, cannot be composed of things whose natures and identities are entirely separate and independent of one another. If God were made of two necessary beings, they would have to depend on one another in some way. But we have already shown that no kind of dependence between two necessary beings is possible. The argument would then amount to saying there are two separate simple Necessary Beings.

The second option is also impossible. The part that is necessary would exist on its own, independent of the other part and everything else. The other part, being possible, would depend on it. Then the supposedly necessary whole would depend on something else and exist only after it. It would depend on a possible part and, through that part, on something necessary. Dependence on anything else—even on another necessary being—and coming after it are both incompatible with being necessary in itself.[^V06-189]

The third option is even more plainly impossible: how could a necessary whole be made entirely of things that are only possible? If the whole existed, all its parts would still be possible in themselves. In the last two options, there is also a circle in our reasoning. When we compare the possible and necessary to existence, we say the necessary comes first: it exists, and so the possible exists. But when we compare the part and the whole, we say the part comes first: the part exists, and so the whole exists. That would make both the part and the whole prior to each other.[^V06-190][^V06-191]

Another proof: we have learned that God’s reality is pure existence, with no essence distinct from it. Anything with no distinct essence has no parts, either in thought or in reality. Let us make the reasoning clear. God has no parts in thought, and whatever has no parts in thought has no parts in reality. The reverse does not always hold: something may be simple in reality but have parts in thought.[^V06-192]

God has no parts in thought because if He had a broader kind and defining feature, the broader kind would depend on the defining feature—not for its meaning, but in order to exist as a definite thing.[^V06-193] That defining feature must be either pure existence or an essence distinct from existence. If it were pure existence, it would not make the broader kind exist; the broader kind would already be existence itself. If it were an essence other than existence, then God would have an essence, which we have already shown He does not.

Also, if God had a broader kind, He would belong to the category of substance and be one of its kinds. He would then share a most general kind with other kinds of substance. But those kinds are possible, and if a kind is possible, so is its broader kind. If the broader kind could not exist in itself at all, then none of its individual instances could exist. This would make God possible, which is impossible.

**A brief summary of this argument.** If God had the defining parts of a thing, all or some of them would have to be the reality of existence, or none would. In every case, it would be impossible to say that one of those supposed parts is the other.[^V06-194] That would contradict the assumption that God is composed of them.

#### Chapter 10 — God Cannot Be Divided into Different Kinds

The Necessary Being has no distinguishing feature that would make Him one kind of being, a smaller subdivision of a kind, or one individual among others.[^V06-195] None of these features would, by itself, make the thing divided into parts. A broader kind or species can be simple and have no parts.[^V06-196] But we have learned that the Necessary Being has no essence. Anything with a dividing defining feature, a feature that sorts it into a class, or a feature that makes it an individual must be a universal essence. What has no essence cannot be a broader kind or species. The Necessary Being is fully determined in Himself, with no defining feature, and is an individual through Himself, with no feature that makes Him an individual.

Another proof:[^V06-197] We have already discussed that existence has no distinguishing feature that divides it into kinds and no feature that makes it one individual. Such features do not define what a broader kind or species means; they make a broader kind exist in a definite form or a species identifiable to the senses or the mind.[^V06-198] So if existence had a defining feature or an individualizing feature, it would need something to establish its meaning and nature. The same is true of the Necessary Being, since He is the highest and strongest level of existence. He cannot be existence left indeterminate, like a broader kind awaiting its species or a species awaiting its individual instances. How mistaken are some ignorant mystics who claim that God is a natural universal broader kind and that all existing things are His individual instances or kinds!

They did not notice that if the Necessary Being’s simple reality were divided into kinds or individuals, it would have to multiply either through itself or through something else. If it multiplied through itself, there would be no single kind or individual in the universe: each individual would itself be something whose nature required multiplication. And the same would apply to each member of that multitude. There would be no one thing—and without one, there could be no many, since one is the starting point of many. So multiplying the Necessary Being through Himself would abolish His own reality.

If He multiplied through something else, He would have the potential to receive existence, distinct from His actual existence and necessary being. His essence would then be made of two parts: one like matter, the other like form. That is impossible.[^V06-199]

#### Chapter 11 — No Concept Makes God One of Several Beings

For two things to share any meaning at all is already a kind of oneness. As you know, oneness has several forms. Some are real[^V06-200] and some are not. Real oneness may be the very essence of the one thing itself: this is the true oneness that belongs to the Necessary Being. Or it may be something added to an essence, as when each possible essence is one. Non-real oneness belongs to several things that share some feature. We discussed these distinctions in the first journey, in the inquiry into oneness and multiplicity.

With this in mind, we say that the Necessary Being cannot have any kind of non-real oneness. No other being truly shares any meaning or concept with Him. He has no common broader kind, since He has no broader kind; no shared species, since He has no species; and no likeness, since He has no quality, as will become clear when we discuss attributes added to Him. He has no equal, since He is not described by quantity; no counterpart, since He is not described by position; no neighbor, since He has no place; and no being suited to Him through a relation. He does have relational attributes, but they all go back to one relation: His sustaining all things, as we will see. And since He alone acts and brings things into existence, as will become clear in the discussion of the oneness of His acts, no one shares with Him in this sustaining.

He has no place in which He dwells, as Christians claim. Since He is necessary through Himself and everything else is possible, there can be no union between Him and another, as some unlearned mystics claim. The affinities that some mystics assign to Him are all misleading fantasies. Far from the truth is their idea[^V06-201] that His relation to the whole world is like the soul's relation to the body. The soul's relation to the body is more like that of a craftsperson to a workshop and its tools[^V06-202] than that of a cause to its effect. Whatever acts only through God cannot be God. If the soul created its own body, it would no longer be a soul. The Creator's relation to what He creates is not like a soul's relation to its body, for God needs nothing.

There is another reason. The rational soul, considered as a soul, is immaterial in potentiality but material in actuality, like the other powers. It comes into being through the body, even though it survives in a spiritual state; it acts through the body, even though it is affected in a spiritual way. Bringing something into existence depends on existence itself. So whoever needs no matter in order to bring things into existence must also need no matter in order to exist. If the soul had no bodily effects, it would be a pure intellect. That is not the case. The Lord of the world, whose name is exalted, is wholly free from need and from coming into being.

The soul and body also form a natural kind and are joined together. Whatever is joined with something else has a connection to it that allows each to affect and be affected by the other. Whatever is affected by something else must have some lack and need something added to it to complete it. But the Necessary Being's existence is complete, indeed beyond all completeness. Nothing can add to it. So no being is truly akin to God. A relation is among the things farthest from a thing's own essence. Yet not a particle is without Him, and nothing in the heavens or on earth is hidden from Him. His essence is free of all things in essence, attribute, and relation, while all things depend on His presence.

#### Chapter 12 — God Is the Fullness of All Things, and All Things Return to Him

This is one of the difficult truths of divine philosophy. Only someone whom God has granted knowledge and wisdom from His own presence can readily grasp it. Yet a proof[^V06-203] establishes that every reality that is simple in itself is all existing things, apart from their deficiencies and absences. God is simple in every respect, so He is all existence, just as the whole of Him is existence.[^V06-204]

Here is the proof. If the simple divine reality were not all things, then what made it the thing it is would include both being one thing and not being another. Its reality would then be made up of two different aspects, at least in the mind's analysis.[^V06-205][^V06-206] But we have already established that this reality is simple in itself. That would be a contradiction. Suppose, then, that it is one thing and not another—for example, A and not B. The aspect by which it is A cannot be the very same aspect by which it is not B. If it were, the concepts “A” and “not B” would be one and the same. But existence and nonexistence cannot be one and the same. The first aspect would therefore differ from the second. So what is simple in reality must be all things.

Let me explain. If we say that a human being, for example, is not a horse, then the aspect by which a human is not a horse must either be the very same as the aspect by which the human is human, or a different one. If it were the same, then whenever we understood what a human being is, we would also understand “not a horse.” But that is not so: understanding what a human is does not necessarily include thinking that it is not a horse. Besides, this negation is not pure nothingness; it is a certain kind of existence.[^V06-207]

If we can truly say what something is while denying that it is something else, we can distinguish at least two aspects in it. Think of the thing and the description we deny of it. Whether we say “it is not that” or “that description does not belong to it,” what makes the thing what it is differs from what makes the denial true.[^V06-208] The difference may be in external reality, which would make the thing physically composite of matter and form; or it may exist only in thought, which would make it mentally composite of broader kind and defining feature, or essence and existence.

For example, when we say “Zayd is not a writer,” the picture of Zayd in the mind cannot be identical with the picture of “not a writer.” If it were, Zayd simply by being Zayd would be pure nonexistence. The subject of such a statement must instead include the image of Zayd and something else that makes “writer” absent from him, such as a capacity or potential. A fully actual thing cannot by itself be the absence of another thing unless it is composite: one aspect is actual, another is potential. This kind of composition comes from a lack in existence. In anything incomplete, the aspect of its lack differs from the aspect of its actual existence. Therefore, whatever is simple in reality must be the fullness of all things. Since the Necessary Being is simple in reality, He is the fullness of all things, in a more exalted and subtle way. Only deficiencies, possibilities, absences, and unrealized potentialities are denied of Him. And since He is the fullness of every thing, He is more truly that thing than it is itself. Hold fast to this and give thanks.

You might ask: does God not have negative attributes—for example, that He is not a body, substance, accident, quantity, or quality? All of these mean only that He is free of absences and deficiencies. To deny a denial is to affirm; to be free of a deficiency is a perfection of existence.

Know that none of these possible essences has unrestricted existence. Each has a restricted existence. By “unrestricted” I mean existence without a limiting absence; by “restricted,” I mean the opposite. To see what this means, consider a defined kind such as the human being, defined as “a rational animal.” In giving a definition, you must include all its essential meanings and leave nothing out. Otherwise your definition is not complete. A human being's definition must therefore exclude anything beyond “animal” and “rational.” If something existed that had these same meanings along with other meanings, such as being horse-like or celestial, it would not be a human being but a more complete kind of being. Here we mean a kind fully realized in existence, not just a concept fully defined.[^V06-209][^V06-210]

Some kinds are broad classifications rather than fully realized kinds. “Animal” and “growing body,” for example, each have a complete definition. Yet if we add another perfection to them, the broader name still applies to the whole: an animal that can sense is still an animal, and a human being is still an animal. A plant is different. Its existence as a kind is already complete, just as its definition is complete. Add sensation and it becomes an animal, not a plant; add rationality and it becomes human. A plant may be called a growing body, but it is not a stone or a mineral. So our point concerns an incomplete existence that becomes complete, not a broad concept to which another concept can be added. A fully realized kind cannot simply be said to be a more complete kind of thing.

The mystics use the terms “unrestricted existence” and “restricted existence” differently from the philosophers.[^V06-211] For them, unrestricted existence is not confined to any particular thing or boundary. Restricted existence is the opposite: a human being, a celestial body, a soul, or an intellect. Unrestricted existence is all things in a simpler way.[^V06-212] It is the source and perfection of every restricted existence. The source of a perfection possesses that perfection more fully than what receives it.[^V06-213] The source and giver of all things must contain all things in a higher and more complete way.

Consider intense blackness: it includes the weaker degrees of blackness beneath it, in a simpler way. A great length includes all shorter lengths as connected parts. A line ten cubits long includes lengths of one, two, or nine cubits, though it does not include their boundaries and endpoints once they are separated from the whole.[^V06-214] Those limits are not part of the nature of line itself, which is length as such. If an endless line existed, it would be more truly a line than any bounded line. The boundaries belong to limited and incomplete lines, not to line as such. The same is true of intense blackness and intense heat: each includes weaker degrees of itself.

The same is true of existence. The all-embracing divine reality, which has no greater completeness, encompasses all restricted, limited forms of existence. The limits bring absences and deficiencies that do not belong to unrestricted existence. The Qur'an points to this in the verse, “The heavens and the earth were joined together, then We split them.” The original joining points to the one, simple reality of existence; the splitting is its unfolding into heaven and earth, intellect and soul, sphere and angel. God also says, “We made every living thing from water.” What is the true water, if not His mercy, which embraces everything, and the flow of His generosity through every existing thing?[^V06-215]

Just as existence is one reality flowing through all things in degrees of greater and lesser completeness, so too are its real attributes—knowledge, power, will, and life—present throughout them, as existence is.[^V06-216] Those firmly grounded in knowledge understand how this is so. All existing things, even lifeless ones, are in their own way alive, aware, speaking in praise, and bearing witness to the existence of their Lord. They know their Creator. This is what we explained in the first journey. It is also what God points to when He says, “There is nothing that does not glorify Him with praise, though you do not understand their glorification.”[^V06-217] This deeper understanding of awareness is possible only for those free of the veils of body, position, and place.

### Station Two — A general inquiry into God’s attributes

#### Chapter 1 — The Kinds of Attributes

An attribute is either positive and affirming or negative and purifying. The Qur'an brings these together in the words, “Blessed is the name of your Lord, Possessor of majesty and honor.” Attributes of majesty describe what God is far above; attributes of honor describe the perfections with which His essence is adorned. The first kind denies deficiencies and absences. All these denials return to one: denying that God is possible in the way other things are. The second kind divides into attributes that describe His reality, such as knowledge[^V06-218] and life, and relational attributes, such as creating, providing, being first, and being a cause. All the attributes that describe His reality return to necessary existence—that is, existence at its fullest strength. All relational attributes return to one relation,[^V06-219] His sustaining all things.[^V06-220] This is the sound way to understand the matter; otherwise, multiplicity would enter His oneness. Exalted is God far above that.

More fully: although the Necessary Being is described by knowledge, power, will, and other attributes, as we will explain, these attributes exist in Him only as His essence exists through itself. Their meanings differ, but in God they are present through one existence. As Avicenna says in his *Annotations*, the First does not become many because His attributes are many. Properly understood, each of His attributes is the other: His power is His life, and His life is His power. They are one. He is alive because He is powerful, and powerful because He is alive. The same holds for His other attributes. Abu Talib al-Makki says that God's will is His power, and that whatever is known through one attribute is known through all of them, since there is no difference there. We will clarify this further and show where some have gone wrong.

All God's real attributes are one reality, not something added to His essence, though their meanings differ. Otherwise their names would be synonyms. His relational attributes are also different in meaning and do stand apart from His essence as concepts, but they all express one relation that follows from His essence.

Their being described as additional does not compromise His oneness. God's greatness and glory do not come from these relations, which come after His essence and after the things to which He is related. His greatness, beauty, and splendor come from the source of these relations, which is identical with His one essence. His essence is such that these attributes arise from it and these relations flow from it. In its complete oneness, His essence deserves the names knowledge, power, and life without becoming many in reality, in thought, or in respect. The aspect of His essence is the very same as the aspect of each attribute. As al-Farabi says: “He is all existence, all necessity, all knowledge, all power, and all life”—not that one part of Him is knowledge and another power, which would make Him composite, nor that one thing within Him is knowledge and another power, which would multiply His real attributes.

His relational attributes are likewise one in meaning and effect, though they are additional to His essence as concepts. His being the source is the same as His providing; both are the same as His generosity and kindness. The same is true of forgiveness, mercy, and approval. If their grounds and aspects differed, their sources would also have to multiply, though we have shown that these sources are identical with His essence. Shihab al-Din al-Suhrawardi writes that we must understand that different relations cannot attach to the Necessary Being in a way that gives Him different aspects. Rather, He has one relation—that of being the source—which supports all the others, such as providing and shaping. He also has one negation from which all other denials follow: the denial of possibility. This includes denying that He is a body or an accident, just as denying that a human being is lifeless includes denying that it is stone or clay. That is an excellent point, though his claim that negations never multiply needs qualification.[^V06-221] As we have said, denying positive realities simply because they are realities would multiply aspects in what is denied. What does not multiply aspects in its subject is denying a denial, such as denying possibility of the Necessary Being.[^V06-222] Denials of substance, accident, body, quality, quantity, and the like follow from that. But denying a higher level of existence to a lower one—for example, denying intellect to the soul or necessary existence to intellect—does multiply aspects.

A commentator explains that denying lifelessness of a human being rests on its being alive; denying that it is a tree rests on its ability to sense and move by choice; and denying that it is a horse rests on its rational nature. Such denials rely on several essential aspects, unlike the denials of God, which all follow from His one essence and the denial of possibility.

I say that this explanation is not sound. The rational nature of a human being is enough to ground the denials that it is lifeless, a tree, or a horse. Likewise, the Active Intellect, though simple, is distinct from all the possible things below it without needing several different essential aspects. The general rule is this: a thing's perfection in existence grounds the denial of deficiencies in it; its deficiencies and aspects of nonexistence ground the denial of perfections it lacks. If something is denied a positive feature simply as a positive feature, it must be composite, whether in reality or in thought: one aspect is positive and another is deficient. A human being is not a king; intellect is not necessary existence. In each case, the thing combines a positive aspect with a deficient one. But denying that a human being is lifeless or a tree requires no aspect beyond its own rational nature. Think carefully about this, and do not let it distract you from the earlier claim that what is simple in itself is the fullness of all existing things.

#### Chapter 2 — Four Ways of Describing Qualities, and Which Can Truly Be Said of God

Attributes are either perceptible or understood by the intellect. Each of these may either be identical with the thing described or distinct from it. This gives four cases: a body is extended; a body is black; an intellect knows itself; and a human being knows something.[^V06-223][^V06-224]

We say that God's attributes are not perceptible, for the senses cannot reach Him. How far astray are those who picture Him as a body or liken Him to created things! Nor are His attributes qualities added to Him, whether they are eternally attached to Him, as the Ashʿarites say, or separate from Him, as the Karramites say. If they were, His essence by itself would lack such perfections as knowledge, power, and life. He would be adorned by something other than Himself, and another thing would contribute to His completeness. That leads to an impossible circle:[^V06-225] His essence would have to be more complete than itself.[^V06-226] On the Illuminationist view, His essence would be brighter than itself because it is lit by the lights of its attributes. All of this is impossible.

Our natural judgment also tells us that an essence complete through itself is nobler and more perfect than one completed by something added to it. In short, because God is the source of the whole chain of existing goods and relations of light, there is no potentiality or possibility in Him. He has no aspect of possibility. He is wholly existence, without nonexistence; perfection without deficiency; actuality without potentiality; necessity without possibility; and good without evil. Because His existence is infinitely intense, His essence cannot be deficient or affected by anything. All His attributes of perfection are identical with His essence: each exists through the very existence of the Necessary Being. They are all necessary in themselves, without multiplying the Necessary Being. This is what al-Farabi means when he says that there must be existence in itself, knowledge in itself, power in itself, and will in itself, so that these can exist in other things only in a dependent way.[^V06-227] The same holds for every perfection of an existing thing, simply insofar as it exists.[^V06-228]

#### A Higher Insight

This points to a further truth: the realities of things may have one simple, all-gathering existence. One reality may have several modes of existence—some material, some immaterial; some possible, some necessary. Existence itself is one reality: some of it is substance, some accident; some necessary, some possible. These attributes are shared in meaning by God and possible things, but in God they are identical with His essence. For Him, then, they are necessary, since His existence is knowledge, power, will, and so on. Knowledge in an intellect that knows itself is that intellect; knowledge in a soul that knows itself is that soul. In other cases, knowledge is an accident, a mental quality distinct from power and will, which are also distinct mental qualities. Here, each has a different mode of existence and a different effect. In God, they are one existence and one reality.

Their effects are one as well. The effect of God's knowledge is also the effect of His power, will, and life. Whatever He knows is also what He can do and wills, and it lives through His life; it is brought into being by His act, without any difference in the grounds of these acts, except in their names and meanings. This is how we should understand the identity of God's attributes. Later thinkers, who held that existence is only a mental construct, misunderstood this. They said the attributes are identical in God because their meanings are one, or because what follows from His essence is the same as what follows from the attributes in other things. They had missed the reality of existence and its different degrees.[^V06-229] I will make this clearer.

#### Chapter 3 — The Later Thinkers’ Argument That God’s Attributes Are Not Separate from What He Is

They argue[^V06-230] that anything which is an attribute of something else[^V06-231] depends on that thing as its subject. Whatever depends on something else for its existence is not necessary in itself but possible in itself. So all attributes, whether they belong to the Necessary Being or to a possible being, are possible in themselves. How can both an attribute and the thing it describes be necessary in themselves? We have already shown that there cannot be several necessary beings.

Can a possible attribute belong to God? We say that He cannot have an attribute established in Him if that attribute is possible in itself. If it did, its cause and the source that gave it priority would have to be God, since there is no other necessary being. Nor could it come from things God has created, since He is not affected by His own creations. This much is clear.[^V06-232] God would then have to be both active and receptive, which is impossible. Every thing that acts and receives must do so in different respects, for three reasons.

First, an agent may act on something else, but a receiver can receive only in itself. The aspect from which something acts is therefore different from the aspect through which it receives. We have already established that God is simple.

Second, if the two aspects were one, then whatever acted through itself would receive through itself, and whatever received through itself would act through itself. Experience shows this is not so.[^V06-233][^V06-234]

Third, an agent brings about an effect, makes it necessary, and gives it existence, even if the effect also depends on other causes. A receiver does not bring about the effect or make it necessary. It only allows it, is ready for it, or is fit to receive it. The agent relates to its effect through necessity; the receiver relates to what it receives through possibility. Necessity removes potentiality, and a thing cannot undo what it requires of itself. These are therefore two different aspects. If God had an attribute established in Him, then He would have two aspects: one for acting, the other for receiving. Each would either be essential to Him, or one would be essential and the other added. In every case, the truly one would be composite. The last two options make this clear.[^V06-235]

If both aspects were essential, we could ask how they arise. They, too, would have to arise from different aspects. Either this continues without end, or it ends in two aspects that are essential parts of God's essence. Exalted is He far above that. This is the argument later thinkers give for the identity of God's real attributes. It has several problems.

#### Several Objections

First, the argument confuses receiving through passive change[^V06-236] with simply being described by an attribute. The proof may rule out the first, but it does not rule out the second. Someone could say that God's attributes are necessary consequences of His essence.[^V06-237] A thing's necessary consequences do not require a separate act that brings them about. They follow from the thing's existence: if the essence is brought into being, so are its necessary consequences; if the essence is not brought into being, neither are they. Perhaps this is what some theologians mean when they say that God's attributes are necessary through His essence.

Second, the argument also applies to God's relational attributes,[^V06-238] such as being the source and being a cause. If so, it would prove either that He has none of these attributes or that they are not additional to His essence. Both conclusions are false.[^V06-239]

The final part of the argument also has several problems. One is that it conflicts with some of God's real attributes. His knowledge is His power from one point of view, and His will from another. Power allows an act to occur; will makes it necessary. Through power, an act may or may not occur; through will, it must occur. If the possibility and necessity arose from different aspects, God's essence would have many aspects, even though the aspect of His essence is the same as the aspect of every one of His attributes of perfection. The same is true of each perfection: its aspect is the same as the aspect of the others. We will return to this.

Another objection denies that a receiver must relate to what it receives through a kind of possibility that conflicts with necessity. Why could it not relate to it through a broader sense of possibility, one that does not conflict with necessity? The reply may be that, simply as a receiver, it must be possible both to receive and not to receive the attribute. If it actually receives the attribute, that must be due to another aspect, not its capacity to receive. But this rule is not universal. It does not apply to things that necessarily have their essential consequences. Four, for example, cannot be even and also not even.

A third objection denies that necessity and possibility conflict here. The agent makes the effect necessary in itself; the receiver makes it possible to exist in something else. These are not necessarily incompatible.[^V06-240] The agent makes the effect exist, and the receiver does not remove this necessity; it allows the effect to be present in itself. So the two relations need not conflict.

In reply to the first objection,[^V06-241] someone might say that an effect needs an agent not only to exist in itself but also to exist in a receiver. The receiver, merely as receiver, does not bring about what it receives.[^V06-242] The agent gives it existence in the receiver, just as the agent gives it existence in itself. Also, the way a thing exists does not change. If it exists as an attribute of something else, then its existence in itself is simply its existence in that other thing. The existence of an accident, for example, is its existence in its subject; these are not two different aspects.

In reply to the second objection, necessity and the lack of necessity cannot belong to one thing in the same respect. It is no answer to say that the agent makes the effect necessary and the receiver does not remove that necessity. Although the receiver does not undo the necessity that comes from the agent, it is itself characterized by the absence of necessity that would come from its own side. One thing therefore cannot be both receiver and agent in the same respect. This is not a side issue about a finer point of the debate.[^V06-243] It states the very premise that was denied and supports it in a way that answers the objection.

I say that this argument may answer the objection, but it still does not establish the main point: that God's attributes are identical with His essence. It shows, at most, that the aspects of acting and receiving must differ when “receiving” means potentiality and readiness to be affected. It has not shown that there must be different aspects when “receiving” means simply being described by something additional to the essence. For this reason, Avicenna says in his *Annotations*:

> If the attributes and qualities are accidents of God's essence, then either they come from an external cause, in which case the Necessary Being would receive them—and the Necessary Being cannot receive anything, since receiving involves potentiality—or they come from His own essence, in which case He would be both receiver and agent. Unless, that is, these attributes and qualities are necessary consequences of His essence. Then His essence is not their subject because they exist in Him, but because they proceed from Him. There is a difference between saying that a body is white because whiteness comes to it from outside, and saying it is white because whiteness necessarily follows from it.
