If we understand the First in this way, there is no multiplicity in Him, and no receiver and agent as two distinct things. Every simple reality is such that its necessary consequences follow from it and are present in it as one and the same thing: it is both what they come from and what they belong to. The same is true of every simple thing. The simple reality gives rise to one thing, and that same thing is present in it. End of Avicenna's statement, abridged.

We have now seen that the aspects of receiving and acting do not make the essence or its attributes multiple, unless receiving means being passively affected. A thing need not be affected by what it contains. Simple realities can have necessary consequences without being changed by them.

Someone might object: we do not accept that a simple essence can have necessary consequences. The essences that cause such consequences are composite, so they may act from one aspect and receive from another. We reply, first, that every composite contains something simple, and even simple things have consequences: at the very least, they are one, possible in a broad sense, or intelligible. Second, a composite reality also has its own unity, even a group of ten as ten and five as five, and it has consequences that follow from it as a whole. These consequences cannot come from one of its parts alone, since they would then be present before the parts were joined. Nor can one part alone receive them. In a triangle, for example, neither the surface by itself nor the three sides by themselves has angles equal to two right angles. The whole triangle, as a whole, is both what has this property and what makes it true. So one thing, in one respect, can both receive a property and give rise to it. This is why Avicenna and other followers of Aristotle were not troubled by saying that God has intelligible forms in Himself that are additional to His essence: they did not think this made Him both agent and receiver in the sense at issue here. We will examine this later.

One of the later scholars says that an agent's making something necessary comes before its act, while the possibility of something being received comes before its reception. If what is truly one and without any multiplicity were both agent and receiver, then before either the act or the reception there would have to be two aspects: one from which it gives rise to the thing, and one that makes it fit to receive it.

I say that what matters here is whether being described by the attribute requires a distinct aspect, whether before or during the act of giving or receiving. That is the very question we have been discussing. If the argument worked, it would prove that the two aspects are distinct; otherwise it proves nothing. Many other arguments offered for this claim follow the same pattern. I have left them out because none adds anything to what we have already considered.

#### Chapter 4 — Why God's Perfections Are Not Separate from What He Is

I will offer several sound proofs for this great claim. The first[^V06-244] is this. If perfections such as knowledge and power were something added to God's existence, then at the level of His own existence His essence would not itself be what these perfections affirm. His essence, considered in itself, would be bare of their meaning. He would not, in Himself, know all things or be able to do whatever He wills. But that conclusion is false. His essence is the source of all goodness and perfection. How could He be deficient in Himself, then, and become complete through something else? That would mean something else has an effect on Him, so that He receives from another. It would also require Him to act in one respect and receive in another, which is impossible. The same is true of our first assumption. As for the necessary consequences of essences, they are not perfections added to an essence. Their following from an essence does not mean that the essence gains perfection from them. That would make the one essence both giver and receiver, in two different respects. These consequences are only ways we think and speak about an essence.[^V06-245][^V06-246][^V06-247]

The second proof is this. If these attributes were added to God's essence,[^V06-248] they would have to flow from His essence to His essence, and this would require some aspect of Him nobler than the Necessary Being as He already is. His essence would then be nobler than itself. For if His essence, just as it is, were enough to give rise to knowledge, then it would already be knowing in itself, so that its knowledge could give rise to another knowledge, as existence itself gives rise to existence. The same would apply to every perfection of existence. But this conclusion is impossible: deficiency and lowliness cannot belong to the same aspect as perfection and nobility. So the assumption is impossible as well. Nor can the attributes flow to Him from something else. That would make His effect nobler than its cause, which is even more impossible.

The third proof is the point we made earlier. Common sense tells us that a being whose perfection belongs to its very essence is better and more complete than one whose perfection is added to it. The first is beautiful through itself; the second is beautiful through its attributes. What is beautiful through itself is nobler than what is beautiful through something else, even if that something else is its own attributes. The Necessary Being must have the highest perfection, splendor, and beauty we can conceive. His essence is the source of all existence and the giver of every good and perfection. The giver must be nobler than what receives its gift. If His perfection, majesty, and beauty belonged not to His sacred reality itself but to additional things attached to it, then the whole made up of His essence and those additions would be nobler than His bare essence. But that whole would be an effect, so an effect would be nobler and more complete than its cause. That is plainly impossible.[^V06-249]

It follows that existence itself, and every perfection of existence and of existing things simply as existing, must lead back to existence in itself, knowledge in itself, power in itself, will in itself, life in itself, and every other perfection. Each must be necessary in itself, and all must be one reality, since there cannot be several Necessary Beings.

Our master and leader, the chief of those who know God and affirm His oneness, said something in one of his famous sermons that makes this point with great force. Peace and blessings be upon him and his family. He said:

> The first step in religion is to know Him. The perfection of knowing Him[^V06-250] is to affirm Him. The perfection of affirming Him is to declare His oneness. The perfection of declaring His oneness is to be sincere toward Him. The perfection of sincerity toward Him[^V06-251] is to deny attributes of Him, because every attribute bears witness that it is other than what it describes, and every thing described bears witness that it is other than the attribute. Whoever describes God has joined Him to something else; whoever joins Him to something else has made Him two; whoever makes Him two has divided Him; whoever divides Him does not know Him. Whoever does not know Him points to Him; whoever points to Him[^V06-252] sets a limit for Him; whoever sets a limit for Him counts Him. Whoever asks “Where is He?” encloses Him, and whoever asks “What is He upon?” leaves Him out of somewhere. [End of the Imam's words.]

Though brief, this noble passage contains many of the main questions of divine philosophy, together with their proofs. Let us uncover a few of its meanings and offer a glimpse of its light.

The words “The first step in religion is to know Him” mean that even some knowledge of God begins faith and certainty. Until we have formed an idea of something, we cannot affirm that it exists. That is why the question “What is it?” comes before the question “Does it exist?”, just as a simple idea comes before a more complex one.

The words “The perfection of knowing Him is to affirm Him” mean this. Whoever understands the meaning of the Necessary Being—that is, existence in its strongest form, with nothing more complete than it, on which all possible things and all limited forms of existence depend—will know that something necessary in itself must exist. Otherwise nothing in the world could exist at all. But that is plainly false, so the assumption is false as well. When we know the reality of existence fully, we know it through direct awareness: the reality known must be present in the very way it exists. This differs from other essences, which can be known by an idea that is not their actual existence.[^V06-253]

Existence does not exist as a second, mental copy of itself, as universal essences do. So whoever fully knows the reality of any existence must know what it is in itself and, if it has parts that make it what it is, must know those too. In any case, that person must know that the reality and source of existence is real, since “what is it?” and “is it?” are one question when asked about existence itself. To know any existence in its reality is to know that it exists, because existence is its own reality.[^V06-254][^V06-255]

So the perfect knowledge of God—the strongest, necessary existence—is itself the affirmation of Him. “The perfection of affirming Him is to declare His oneness” points to the proof that there cannot be more than one Necessary Being. The Necessary Being is pure existence, with no general category or individual limit mixed into it. Everything else is mixed with something beyond existence: a boundary, a restriction, a broad category, a lack, a weakness, a potential, or an incompleteness. Once we see that the Necessary Being is existence itself, we see that there cannot be several of Him. If there were, one reality would have to be two realities. That is impossible even to conceive properly, let alone to accept as real.[^V06-256][^V06-257]

To know God's essence—which is the same as affirming His existence—is therefore to witness His absolute oneness. As the Qur'an says: “God bears witness that there is no god but Him.” His essence bears witness to His oneness.

Why does the verse then add “and the angels and those who have knowledge”? Because every being other than God depends on Him so completely that none can be fully known without His presence and sustaining reality.

For something to be present to us, what gives it being must also be present in some way.[^V06-258] The truth of existence bears witness to its own oneness, and so does the existence of everything else. The verse calls these witnesses “angels and those who have knowledge” because everything that exists, in its own degree, has knowledge. Existence is knowledge, power, will, and the other attributes of existence, though these qualities are hidden in things whose existence is weak and mixed with absence and darkness. This is what God means when He says, “There is nothing that does not glorify Him with praise, but you do not understand their praise.”[^V06-259]

The words “The perfection of declaring His oneness is to be sincere toward Him” mean: to be sincere in declaring that nothing is added to Him and that there is no second Necessary Being. If anything else existed in this way, whether as an attribute or as another thing, He would not be simple in the fullest sense. We have already shown that what is simple in reality cannot lack any perfection of existence; only deficiencies and absences can be denied of it. For to deny an existence is one thing, and to affirm existence is another. If a real perfection were denied of His essence, His essence would be made of two aspects, though it is simple. That is impossible. “The perfection of sincerity toward Him is to deny attributes of Him” means to deny attributes that exist separately from His essence. His essence itself is the reality of every perfection and divine quality. His knowledge, power, will, life, hearing, and sight all exist through the one existence of His essence, even though their meanings differ. The perfection of existence is to gather many perfections in one existence.[^V06-260]

The words “because every attribute bears witness that it is other than what it describes, and every thing described bears witness that it is other than the attribute” give a proof against attributes that are added to God, whether they are eternal, as the Ashʿarites say, or come later. If an attribute exists separately from what it describes, then each of the two must be distinct from the other in one respect and share something with it in another, since both exist. But the respect in which they differ cannot be the same as the respect in which they share.[^V06-261] Otherwise one and the same thing would be both one and many. So each would have to be made up of a shared part and a distinct part. That would make the Necessary Being composite, though we have shown that He is simple. This is the meaning of the Imam's words, “Whoever describes God has joined Him to something else,” through “whoever does not know Him points to Him.” To describe God by an additional attribute is to join Him to another thing in existence.

If a second thing were joined to Him, God would be one of two. Anything treated as one of two is made of two parts: in one part it shares existence with the other, and in the other it differs from it. The Imam's words, which draw on the deepest knowledge of God, show how completely God is beyond possibility and composition. It follows that no other thing exists independently in the fullest sense. All possible things are flashes of His light and reflections of His radiance. As we have seen, perfect oneness means that the One is all things in His oneness. That is why the Imam continues: “Whoever points to Him sets a limit for Him,” and so on. To point to God in any way, whether by the senses or by the mind—to say “here,” “there,” or “like this”—would set a boundary around Him. And whoever gives Him a boundary counts Him as one unit among others. But His true oneness is not the numerical unity from which numbers and individual units begin; that would be impossible.[^V06-262]

So God cannot be contained in anything, and nothing can be outside His presence. He is not in earth or heaven, yet neither is without Him. A saying attributed to the Prophet describes this: “If you lowered a rope to the lowest earth, it would reach God.” This is why the Imam says, “Whoever asks ‘Where is He?’ encloses Him, and whoever asks ‘What is He upon?’ leaves Him out of somewhere.” The Qur'an says: “He is with you wherever you are,” and “There is no private meeting of three but He is their fourth, nor of five but He is their sixth,” and “We are nearer to him than his jugular vein.” A sacred report says, “I am his hearing, his sight, and his hand.” The Prophet also said that God is above all things and beneath all things; His greatness fills all things, so neither earth nor heaven, land nor sea, nor air is empty of Him. It is also reported that Moses asked: “Are You near, so that I may speak to You in private, or far, so that I must call to You? I hear Your voice, yet I do not see You. Where are You?” God replied: “I am behind you and before you, to your right and your left. I sit with whoever remembers Me, and I am with whoever calls on Me.” There are many more verses and reports of this kind.[^V06-263]

### A Further Explanation

We should clarify that denying attributes of God does not mean that He lacks them, which would leave us with no divine perfections at all. He bears every divine quality and every beautiful name, and His reality cannot be missing any perfection. The point is that all His qualities and attributes exist through one existence: the existence of His essence. Our own essential qualities, too, exist through one individual existence, but the Necessary Being has no essence distinct from existence and no possibility in Him. The divine world is vast beyond measure:[^V06-264] in His oneness, He is all.

The divine attributes are many in meaning and concept, but one in identity and existence. For every kind of possible thing, the Real has a divine attribute that is its source, beginning, and end. In every “day” He has a new affair of His essence and a new manifestation[^V06-265] in the divine realms and levels of self-sufficiency. The fully realized knowers of God witness these affairs, along with their names and attributes. They say that the first multiplicity in existence, and the boundary between God's own oneness and the many possible beings that appear as created forms of His attributes, is the multiplicity of His names and attributes. These names are not made or caused.[^V06-266] They are real through the reality of the Necessary Being, who is not made or caused. This is not the Muʿtazilite view that names and attributes are fixed things separate from existence. As we have already shown, nonexistent things are not things in themselves. Rather, these are many meanings understood within the hidden reality of God, yet one in existence, necessary and unmade. Still, considered in themselves, they have never tasted existence, because existence alone is what truly exists. In themselves, then, they are neither existent nor nonexistent. This is one of those truths that can be grasped only with great care.

There is one more point. Every perfection that things receive through existence belongs in itself to the source of existence, and belongs to other things through Him. So God is living, sustaining, knowing, powerful, and willing through His own essence, not through additional attributes. Otherwise, to give these perfections to anything He would need another life, power, knowledge, and will, since only one who has a perfection can give it. Once you understand this, you understand what is meant by saying that God's attributes are His essence. It does not mean, as people may first imagine, that the life, knowledge, and power that flow into created things are themselves God's essence. Nor does it mean, as some learned people have thought, that the universal concepts of these attributes have the same meaning as His essence. God's inner reality is unknown to others, while these attributes have distinct meanings. Rather, pure existence puts an end to all distinctions and concepts: in it, there is no attribute and subject, no name and named, no essence or concept—only the essence itself.

To say that God's attributes are identical with His essence means that His essence is such that these many meanings truly apply to it without any added quality inhering in Him. In this way of considering Him, there are attributes and a subject, knowledge and a knower, power and the powerful, existence and what exists. This is called the divine level, just as pure existence considered without any attribute is called the level of oneness and hidden identity. At the divine level, knowledge, power, will, and the other attributes are one, just as attribute and subject are one. The mind can distinguish them, though they are one in reality, as it can distinguish broader kind and difference within a simple intellect even though they are one there. In existence there is only the one essence. Considered another way, each attribute, name, and consequence is distinct from the others in countless ways. Understand this carefully, so that you fall neither into denying God's perfections nor into comparing Him to created things.[^V06-267]

#### Chapter 5 — How God's Qualities Are One Reality Though Their Meanings Differ

Many careful thinkers have assumed that saying God's attributes are identical with His essence means that their meanings and concepts are not distinct, but all go back to one meaning. This is a mistaken assumption. If it were true, the words “knowledge,” “power,” “will,” and “life” would all mean the same thing when applied to God. There would be no point in using one word after using another. That is plainly wrong and leads to denying God's perfections altogether. Rather, saying that His attributes are identical with His essence means that all these perfections exist through the one existence of His essence. God's essence is not separate from its attributes as if each were a distinct individual; nor is one of His attributes separate from the others in this way. He is powerful through His very essence and knows through His very essence: He knows His own essence, which is fully present to itself. He wills through a will that is His essence, indeed through His knowledge of the order of existence and of all created things as they ought to exist. We will explain this later.[^V06-268]

Each of these attributes gives rise to further attributes: God is wise, forgiving, creator, provider, kind, merciful, originator, restorer, shaper, giver of life, and giver of death. These follow from His power over everything that can exist. Not one possible thing or meaning enters existence in any respect except through His power and giving, with or without an intermediary. Other names—such as hearing, sight, awareness, and deep knowledge—follow from His knowledge. The same applies to all His limitless names and attributes, formed by combining these names and qualities,[^V06-269] just as kinds, groups, and individuals are formed from essential meanings such as broader kind and species, or from added features and properties. Some divine names are broad like broader kinds, some more specific, and some individual, such as “creator of Zayd” or “knower of ʿAmr.” Each name and attribute calls for a fitting place where its effect can appear.[^V06-270]

Every great attribute of God and every lofty name calls for a created thing that points to it, just as bodies point to souls, shadows to people, appearances to what lies within, and reflections to realities. The divine world is vast in the number of its meanings and names, though everything in it exists through one existence, simple in every way. This is one of the mysteries known only to those firmly grounded in knowledge. God created all things so that they would reveal His beautiful names and display His lofty attributes. Because He is all-subduing, He brought into being forms that show His power to subdue: Hell and its levels, its scorpions, snakes, punishments, and those bound in chains and fetters—devils, unbelievers, and all who do evil. Because He is merciful and forgiving, He brought into being places where mercy and forgiveness appear: the Throne and the angels of mercy around it, Paradise and its people—the near, the blessed, and the good. The same is true of His other names and the created forms that display them.

Look, too, at your own rational soul, created in the image of the Merciful and serving as God's proof to creation. Everything that comes from you—your words, states, movements and stillness, thoughts and imaginings—shows the names and qualities within you. When you love someone and feel close to them, that love moves you to praise and honor them, welcome them warmly, pray for them, rejoice, smile, and speak kindly. Had you not loved them, none of this would have appeared. These actions are signs of the love within you. If you hate someone, your words and actions show that too: you may insult, strike, criticize, avoid them, wish them gone, or want them punished. These actions reveal the hostility within you. The same applies to other qualities. God's names and attributes, then, are one with His essence in existence and identity, but distinct in meaning and concept.[^V06-271]

This also shows why the view held by many later thinkers—that existence is only a concept we draw from things, with no real identity outside the mind—is false. If existence were merely a concept, and differed only as the meanings and essences associated with it differed, then God's attributes would have to be many distinct things, one for each meaning.[^V06-272] That is a very bad conclusion. To avoid it, those thinkers said that the attributes all have one meaning, almost making “knowledge,” “power,” and the other words synonyms when used of God. We have already seen why this is wrong. The truth, as we have said many times, is that existence is what makes things real, and it varies in completeness and intensity. The more complete and stronger an existence is, the more meanings and perfections it embodies and the more effects and actions come from it. The more complete and noble it is, the more it gathers many attributes while remaining simple and one. The weaker and less complete it is, the fewer its attributes and the more open it is to division and opposition. Meanings that coexist in one powerful existence can become opposites in a weaker one. So opposing names—Guide and Misleader, Giver of Life and Giver of Death, Withholder and Bestower, First and Last, Forgiver and Subduer—appear in opposed beings and effects: guidance and error, angels and devils, life and death, spirits and bodies.[^V06-273]

### Third Discussion — God’s Knowledge

#### Chapter 1 — Foundations for This Inquiry

One point we have already established is that things exist outside us and also in the knowing power that perceives them. By “outside” here I mean the world of material bodies, which have a position and orientation. When philosophers tried to show that things outside us have another kind of existence—sometimes called mental existence—they began with this problem: we cannot affirm something of what is absolutely nonexistent. Yet we often make true claims about things that do not exist outside us. For example, we can say that a phoenix is an animal, or that a mountain of gold is a mountain. For a statement to be true of something, that thing must in some way exist. Since such things are not outside us, they must have another kind of existence: existence in knowledge, whether “knowledge” means that existence itself or the relation between the knower and what is known.

Knowledge, like existence, can mean either a real thing or an abstract relation we draw from it: the fact of knowing, from which we get the words “knower” and “known.” Knowledge is a kind of existence.[^V06-274] Strictly speaking, knowledge and existence are one reality. But when existence is weak, mixed with absence, and marked by limitations—as in material bodies and their changing conditions—it is hidden from the senses and from other powers of perception. Material bodies are hidden from one another in the same way: their parts do not all exist together in a single, present whole, and each part is absent from the others. In the same way, these bodies are hidden from other knowing powers. For one thing to be present to another, it must first be present to itself. So bodies and their material conditions do not exist as knowledge in this sense; they are not called knowers or known in themselves.

We still call them existent, because “existence” applies more broadly than “knowledge,”[^V06-275] or than other perfections such as power, will, and love. All of these are states of existence and of things insofar as they exist, but each name adds a relation beyond bare existence. Knowledge, for example, is the existence of one thing for another thing that exists in its own right. It is not like the existence of a form or an accident in matter. If every thing were a knower simply because it existed in itself, then every existent would be known to everyone. That is obviously false.

What we call “known” has two senses. In the first, a thing's existence in itself is the same as its existence for the knower; its real form is itself the form through which it is known. This is called “known in itself.”[^V06-276]

In the second sense, a thing's existence in itself differs from its existence for the knower, and its real form is not the same as the form through which it is known. This is called “known by means of something else.” When people say that knowledge is an image of a thing formed in the knower, they mean that the known thing is something outside the knowing power, such as the sky, earth, house, stone, tree, horse, human being, or any other material thing and its conditions. When they say that knowledge is the presence of a thing's form to the knower, they mean knowledge that is itself the known thing, not something different from it. In either case, what is truly known and directly revealed is a luminous, perceptive form, free of material veils and of the darkness of absence.

We have shown, then, that existence is of two kinds: perceptive existence and existence that is not perceptive. There are also two worlds: the unseen world and the visible world, the afterlife and this life. They are called unseen and visible in relation to our limited sight, not because of how they are in themselves. Concealment belongs to this worldly, material existence; presence and direct awareness belong to existence in the afterlife. Even there, degrees differ: each level is more free of this world and more distant from matter than the one below it, and so it is more manifest, present, and unified.

Many philosophers have thought that what prevents something from being known is its being joined to features beyond what it is that affect it, such as color, position, and shape. They often define knowledge as distinguishing a thing from others through a universal idea. So, they say, anything mixed with something else remains unknown as long as it is mixed with it. The known must either be separate from everything else or be mixed with other features that affect it and veil it.

The first kind is understood by reason. For example, the general idea of a human being applies to people who differ in height, size, position, place, and time. If the idea of humanity included a particular size, position, or time, it could not apply to people who differ in these ways. For the same reason, we can call both an ant and an elephant animals, despite their differences in incidental features.

The second kind is called sensible, whether it is seen, heard, smelled, tasted, touched, imagined, or supposed. Features that accompany a thing without affecting the way it exists are not an obstacle to understanding. For example, the presence or absence of blackness does not change the way movement exists. But a person's position and size do affect that person's particular existence: if they were removed, that individual person would no longer exist in the same way. So, according to this view, the mind can understand something only after separating it from incidental features. In the same way, what makes something perceivable is some degree of immateriality. What is free of matter but still joined to material features is sensed, provided it has some spatial relation to the material object. What is free of matter and its features except for size is imagined. What is free of all of them except for a non-spatial relation is supposed. What is fully free of them, and equally related to every individual, is understood by the intellect. This is the general account they give. But it is incomplete. It comes from too little knowledge of philosophy and too little awareness of the different strengths and weaknesses of existence.[^V06-277]

The fact that Zayd has a shape, position, color, and limbs is not what prevents him from being understood. These features can be present with Zayd outside the mind, and they can also be present with him in the mind.[^V06-278] The natural kinds made up of such parts, together with their essential features, would no longer be what they are if these were removed. A lion stripped of size, shape, head, eyes, paws, and belly would no longer be a lion or even an animal.

The mind can picture a person's size, shape, position, color, location, and other features, either separately or together.[^V06-279] So it is false to say that something can be understood only by removing its size, shape, and other features. Some of these belong to its essence; others complete or enrich it. Understanding them raises the same question as understanding the thing itself. The truer account is this: whether something is known or unknown, bright or dark, manifest or hidden, present or absent, depends on how strong or weak its existence is. The more firmly and completely something exists, the more it reveals itself, the more it appears, and the more it can contain and gather other things. The weaker and less complete its existence, the more hidden and dark it is, and the less it is present or manifest.

The strongest existence is the Necessary Being, wholly free of possibility, absence, evil, deficiency, and limitation. This is the divine world, in which all things exist through themselves as necessary, without multiplicity or possibility. The weakest existence belongs to natural bodies and their changing conditions. This is the world of coming to be and passing away, renewal and loss. The first is the source of life and manifestation, and the source of the lights of knowledge and what can be known. The second is the realm of death and darkness. Between these two ends are many levels. The closer a level is to the highest source, the stronger its manifestation and knowledge; the farther it is, the more hidden and less knowable it becomes.

Everything above this lowest, material world has a form of existence that is itself perceptive and luminous, and cannot be separated from life and awareness. This is not true of natural bodies when considered simply as they are, apart from their inward principles in soul and intellect. Those principles lie beyond this world even though they encompass these bodies, for what is lower depends on what is higher; without the higher, the lower would disappear. You already know the distinction between “body” as matter and “body” as a general kind. Life and awareness are outside the first meaning, when we speak of the animals in this material world, but not outside the second. Animal bodies and their corpses belong to this world; their souls and spirits belong to another.

We have said that knowledge can either be the known thing itself or something different from it. Knowledge of things outside us may be a mental image, as in intellectual knowledge of the external world. Such knowledge is always universal,[^V06-280] even if it is narrowed by a thousand qualifications. Or knowledge may itself be the real thing and its external form, as when we know ourselves and our essential qualities. Each person knows their own self through the very form by which they are that self, not through an additional image. We know ourselves as this particular person, not as something that could be shared by many. If this awareness came through an image in the mind, it would be universal, even if many universal features were combined to describe just one person. A universal concept is always something we point to as “it”; we point to ourselves as “I.” So our knowledge of ourselves is the same as our own personal existence and identity.[^V06-281]

Here is a subtle point that must be made clear.[^V06-282] Someone might object: the human soul[^V06-283] is certainly a definite substance, and every substance belongs to the category of substance. So “substance” must be its broader kind, and every broader kind has a difference that sets it apart. The soul would then be composed of a natural broader kind and a natural difference. But these two natures are universal concepts. Anything composed of universal concepts can be pointed to only as “it,” never as “I.”

You might answer that its broader kind and the feature distinguishing it are only two parts of what the soul is, and that some particular feature must be added to them to make this individual soul. Something made of a general kind together with an individual feature cannot apply to many people.

I reply: even if something made of several meanings and a further individual feature—such as existence—cannot apply to many, we can understand it only by understanding its universal components through general concepts and mental images. Anything understood that way is still “it” to us, not “I.” Yet we know from experience[^V06-284] that when we become aware of ourselves, we may be unaware of all general concepts and labels, even the ideas of “substance” or “rational.”[^V06-285] We do not point to any of these as “I.” All of them are absent to us except our simple identity. That identity, then, can be nothing but existence. Anything other than existence belongs to one of the categories and is made up of general concepts. Existence does not belong to a universal category, even though many of these concepts can be truly applied to it.

This also answers an objection[^V06-286] to the philosophers who say that the soul is immaterial. They argue that we can forget the body, other bodies, and their features without forgetting ourselves, so our self must be an immaterial substance distinct from all bodies and their features. Someone objects: “Often I know myself without thinking at all about the meaning of ‘immaterial substance.’ How, then, can I be identical with an immaterial substance?” The answer is that the soul has no essence or general definition distinct from its existence. If it is immaterial, it is even less likely to be composed of broader kind and difference. Or the reply is simply that forgetting the universal concept of immaterial substance supports the point: our simple identity is present to us, while the concept is not.

Just as the soul knows itself through its own form rather than through a second mental image, it also knows many of its perceptive and moving powers without a separate image. Here are several reasons. First, the soul acts on its own particular body and uses the particular powers that exist within it. For example, it uses the thinking power to divide particular ideas, combine them, and arrange the steps that connect premises to a conclusion. These are particular forms present in the soul's awareness. It turns them as it wishes, moving them forward and back, joining and separating them. What can be handled and rearranged in this way must be particular forms, present to the soul themselves—not vague, universal images in the mind.[^V06-287]

The same is true of the tool through which the soul performs this work, the thinking power, and of the powers of imagination and the body. The soul uses them, preserves them, and preserves the images within them. It sees these things directly, rather than through further images, which would multiply images without end. The soul therefore sees them with its own sight, not through another eye.

Second, if the soul knew these powers through mental images taken from them,[^V06-288] it would know them only through general ideas. But this is false: most people know many of their own perceptive images as particular things. Those who have reached a higher level also directly witness what carries these images, preserves them, records their actions, and guides the pens that write them.[^V06-289]

Third, we feel pain when our body is diseased or its continuity is broken. Pain is awareness of something that conflicts with us. The pain is not caused by another image of the bodily disruption or harmful condition being formed in that organ, in the soul, or somewhere else. What we perceive in this pain is the disruption itself or a condition in the organ.[^V06-290][^V06-291] A second image of that harmful thing may sometimes cause another kind of pain, but that is not this bodily pain.[^V06-292] So some things are perceived simply because their own reality is present to the soul, or to something directly connected with it.

Fourth, when we perceive something outside ourselves and our own powers, we perceive it through an image formed in the soul that corresponds to it. But the soul knows that image through the image itself, not through another matching image. Otherwise, an unlimited number of images would have to be present all at once.[^V06-293][^V06-294][^V06-295]

Fifth, a point drawn from the “Throne” discussions.[^V06-296] At the start of its development, the soul has no conceptual or judgmental knowledge. Using tools such as the senses is a voluntary act, not a merely natural one, so it must depend on knowledge of those tools. If all knowledge came from forming an image of what is known, then using a tool would require prior knowledge of the tool, which would itself require using the tool. This would either go in a circle or continue without end, both of which are impossible. So the soul's first knowledge must be its knowledge of itself, followed by knowledge of its powers and tools, the outward and inward senses. Both kinds of knowledge are direct: the thing known is present to the knower.

After these two kinds of knowledge, the soul begins to use its tools through an impulse that comes from itself. It does not first form a concept of using the tools or decide that doing so will be useful, as it does before other voluntary actions outside the body. This is another kind of willing: it is not based on deliberation, though it is never without awareness. Here, willing is itself awareness; in other voluntary actions, the soul first knows the action and judges it useful. The use of the soul's powers and senses arises from the soul itself, not from reflection. The soul, knowing and loving itself and its own activity from within, is drawn to use the tools it can use. It is moved to do so by its own nature, not by a further act of will or knowledge. Hold on to this precious point.[^V06-297][^V06-298]

Another sign is that an image may be present in a perceptive tool without the soul noticing it.[^V06-299] This happens when we are absorbed in thought, anger, desire, or what another sense is reporting. The soul must turn its attention toward the image. Perception is the soul's attention to and direct witnessing of what is perceived. This witnessing is particular, not a universal concept. So the soul must have immediate awareness that is not itself another image. We have shown in several ways that perception always requires a form to be present. But it requires an additional mental image beyond the object itself only when the object's existence is not itself luminous and perceptive, as with material bodies and their conditions, or when it is not present through its own perceptive existence to the power that knows it. It may be absent because it does not exist, because it has no perceptive form of existence, or because that form is not present to this particular power. Not every existent is present to every knower, and not every cognitive image is present to every power capable of knowing.[^V06-300]

For a knower and a known thing to be related, there must be an intrinsic connection between their existences. Whenever two things have a real unity and connection in their being, one knows the other unless something prevents it—such as a weakness in one of them, some lack, or a dark veil that hides it. That connection means one is present to the other and revealed to it. Sometimes the connection is between the knower and the thing itself, as when the soul knows itself, its qualities and powers, and the images preserved in its perceptive faculties. At other times, it is between the knower and an image that is distinct from the thing itself, as when the soul knows what lies beyond its own powers and senses. This is called acquired or new knowledge. Even here, what is truly known and directly revealed is the image present to the soul, not the thing outside it.

When we call an external thing “known,” we do so in a secondary sense. The same is true of the word “existent”: it can mean existence itself, or the essence that exists. Existence itself is what is real and definite; an essence, taken by itself, is indefinite. We have often said that knowledge is a kind of existence, indeed existence itself. So knowledge and existence work in the same way: whatever exists for itself is known to itself; whatever exists for something else is known to that other thing. Prime matter exists only potentially, so it does not exist for itself and does not know itself. The bodily form and specific form also do not exist for themselves, so they are not known to themselves, and matter does not know them either, as we have explained. True knowledge of a thing is its presence to the knower. This is the more complete kind of knowledge, compared with knowing it through an image that is separate from the thing.[^V06-301]

Anyone who says that knowing something other than oneself can only mean receiving an image of it is mistaken, because this denies the more complete kind of knowledge.[^V06-302] It would be right to say that knowledge of things whose external existence is not itself perceptive—such as natural bodies, their motions, and conditions—depends on an image distinct from them. But most people miss the point we have established:[^V06-303] these material, dark things are not present to anyone, even to the principles that give rise to them, except through cognitive lights connected to them. In reality, those lights are the complete reality of the things and the means by which they exist.[^V06-304]

We must also understand that an intelligible form, insofar as it is truly intelligible, has one and the same existence in itself and for the intellect that knows it; its intelligibility is not something added to it. The same is true of a sensible form, insofar as it is truly sensed: the sense-image present to a sensing substance has one existence in itself and for the sense that perceives it. It does not exist in one way in itself and another way for the perceiver. But what exists for something else is not understood or sensed in itself. Natural and lifeless forms exist for another, as do sight, hearing, and the other senses. That is why they do not perceive themselves: sight does not see sight, and hearing does not hear hearing. The soul perceives sight, what sight sees, and the act of seeing; it perceives hearing, what is heard, and the act of hearing, because these powers do not exist for themselves.[^V06-305]

If an image grasped by the mind existed independently, it would exist for itself and be intelligible to itself. It would therefore be intellect, knower, and known. Likewise, if a sense-image existed on its own, it would sense itself; it would be sense, sensing, and sensed. Some followers of Aristotle even say that if taste existed on its own, it would taste itself. This also answers the objection made by the leading follower of the Eastern philosophers: if knowledge were the same as presence, every lifeless thing would know itself and the qualities in it, since every lifeless thing has an essence and some features. The answer is that lifeless forms and similar things belong to matter. Their existence is for the matter, not for themselves. Whatever exists in another has the same existence as that other thing. So if an image or a quality is present in something, it is present in its underlying matter, not to itself. Something that is not present to itself cannot have anything present to it, whether it already exists in itself or not.

We have also shown that natural bodies and their conditions are, in themselves, mixed with absence and hiddenness, even before we consider the material veils around them. So what prevents a lifeless thing or any other material thing from being known in itself is not merely that it has matter or is joined to other things. Its very mode of existence prevents it from being known, whether by the intellect or the senses. We have already pointed out the weakness in the second argument.

### An Objection and a Clarification

Some people think the philosophers proved that every immaterial form is an intellective knower by combining two general claims in a way that would not prove the conclusion.[^V06-306] They say the claims were: “Every nonphysical form is immaterial,” and “Every form actually understood by something is immaterial”; from these, they conclude: “Every nonphysical form is actually understood.” But that does not follow from the two claims. This objection rests on a misunderstanding. The philosophers instead argued in one of two ways. They said either that every independently existing thing, free of matter, has its own form present to itself, so it is known and knows itself; or that everything free of matter is either fit to be understood or not. But nothing can be wholly unfit to be understood, since anything that exists can be understood in some way.[^V06-307]

The second possibility cannot apply to something already immaterial. Such a thing cannot be acted upon or changed, so nothing can happen to it that was not already the case. Whatever it can be in a general sense of possibility, it must be in actuality. Since it can be understood, it must always actually be understood. And because it cannot be understood by something else without existing for that other thing, it must be understood by itself. If it were only potentially understood by itself, that would contradict the claim that it is already actually understood. So it knows itself.[^V06-308]

Some difficulties remain, and we must clear them up. First, we may object that being aware of oneself is an external state, different from one's own essence as that essence is in itself. If so, the act of knowing oneself cannot be the very essence of the knower without some added attribute. Otherwise, the essence in itself would be the subject of the description “knowing.” But every thing is itself, and every essence, considered in itself, is only what it is. So for anything to know itself, there must be something beyond its essence that makes it a knower and known. How, then, can a separate immaterial being's knowledge of itself be its essence?

I reply that this argument confuses existence with essence. “Essence” can mean a thing's individual identity, or it can mean its general kind. When a substance exists independently, its knowledge is the very existence of that substance; I do not mean that the concept of its general kind, if it has one, means the same thing as “knowledge.” The individual identity of a separate being is itself the reality of knowledge, without anything added to it. As we have said many times, several different concepts can truly apply to one thing without requiring several different aspects in the thing itself.[^V06-309]

Second, the author of *Eastern Discussions* objected to the philosophers' claim that each separate being knows itself without anything added to its essence. He said: if that were true, anyone who understood a separate being would also understand that it knows itself. But proving that separate beings know themselves takes a further argument; showing that they exist does not prove this. We can see this in the case of God: someone who proves His existence by one line of reasoning cannot simply take that as proof of His knowledge.

The answer is this. A separate being's knowledge of itself is the very existence it has in itself, not something present in something else. No one else can receive that mode of existence. What a person grasps through a proof is only an image that matches the meaning “this exists.” In that case, the image exists for the person who proves it, not for the separate being itself. We have shown that whatever exists for something else is known only to that other thing, not to itself. So the image in a person's mind is understood by that person, not by the separate being. The separate being knows itself through another kind of existence, one that is its own. Therefore, grasping its image does not make us aware of its self-awareness. Only someone who knows it through direct, illuminating awareness—as some spiritual travelers do when they rise beyond their ordinary human state—would know it in that way.[^V06-310]

Third, many learned people think that when something knows itself, the knower and the known must differ in some respect. They compare this to someone treating their own illness: as healer, the person is active; as patient, the person is receptive. The self as healer has the skill to heal, while the self as patient is ready to receive treatment. It is surprising that they compare two things whose difference does not create any multiplicity, either in reality or in thought, with two things that must be distinct in external reality. Acting and being acted upon are different basic categories. A passive power requires bodily matter. When the soul receives a new mental state, it needs a bodily basis through which it can move from one state to another. If the soul were not connected to a material body, it could not gain new perfections by being affected.

In truth, the healer—the doctor or medicine that brings health—is a power above the soul's natural power, and certainly above the sick soul. The patient is someone afflicted by illness and deficiency, and is connected to the source of that harm: matter, or something joined to it. In this case, then, the subjects differ both in reality and in how we think of them. But the knower and the known do not require such a difference, as Avicenna says in several of his works.[^V06-311]

Fourth, most later thinkers believed that every relation implies opposition, because they had read that one of the four kinds of opposition is “opposition between related things.” So they judged that the relation of being a knower differs from and opposes the relation of being known. When they faced the problem of one essence being both knower and known in self-knowledge, and worried that opposites would be together, they replied that the knower and known differ only in thought. They failed to see that a difference made only in thought would not solve the problem if the opposition were real. Besides, if they said that the relations of knowing and being known exist only in the mind and not in reality, they would contradict proof and reason. This weak argument is why some early philosophers denied that God knows Himself.

The way to end this confusion is to see that two related concepts do not always have to differ in existence, much less oppose each other. The meaning of “knower” does not require that its existence differ in any way from the meaning of “known.” Nor does any concept with a different meaning, just because it differs in meaning, have to mark out another aspect in the thing. Many distinct concepts can truly apply to one undivided reality. Some relations really do involve opposition, such as being a cause and being caused, moving and being moved, being ready and what one is ready for, and being earlier and later. Their two sides cannot exist together because the realities they describe conflict. But this is not true of being a knower and being known, or of similar relations such as lover and beloved. Only the first group involves real opposition.[^V06-312][^V06-313][^V06-314]

With these points established, we can now examine God's knowledge of Himself and of everything else, how He knows things brought into being and things that come to be, and the levels of His knowledge—providence, decree, destiny, and the Book—in further chapters.

#### Chapter 2 — Proving That God Knows Himself

After recalling the points we have already established, you may feel that no further proof is needed to show that God knows Himself. Still, let us make the point clearer. The reality of knowledge[^V06-315] is the reality of existence, provided it is free of deficiencies and the veils of darkness. We have shown that whatever exists independently and is free of what accompanies it is present to itself. So it is understood by itself, and its knowing itself is nothing other than its own existence. This presence does not require a difference between what is present and what it is present to, either in reality or in the mind.

The stronger a thing's existence, the more fully it is present and the more free it is of deficiency and limitation. It is therefore more fully intellect, more fully intelligible, and more fully knowing itself. Since the Necessary Being is the source of all levels of existence—the intellectual and sensory, the created and the coming-to-be—He is at the highest level of strength and immateriality. His existence is unlimited in its intensity. Other things, even if they were unlimited in number or duration, would still not reach a level of intensity beyond which no further level could exist. The Necessary Being is infinitely beyond even an infinite degree of existence. His self-knowledge is therefore to other separate beings' self-knowledge as His existence is to theirs. The true existent knows Himself with the fullest knowledge, radiant and manifest beyond comparison. Just as the existence of possible things is gathered up and surpassed in His existence, as we have proved, so their knowledge is gathered up and surpassed in His knowledge of Himself.

We have also shown that God's existence is the reality of existence, from which no existent is excluded. In the same way, His knowledge of Himself is the reality of knowledge, from which no knowledge or known thing is absent. Recall what you have learned in first philosophy and general philosophy: whenever reason judges that something is a perfection for a being simply insofar as it exists, and that it entails no restriction to a lower kind, no change, no body, and no composition,[^V06-316] if this perfection is found in any existent, then it can also belong to the true existent. It must belong to Him; otherwise, He would have a possibility opposed to His necessary existence. We have already shown that the Necessary Being is necessary in every respect. Knowledge is plainly a perfection of anything that exists, and it entails no body, change, or limited possibility. Since many existents, such as intellects, possess it, it must belong to God by necessity.

How could anyone with sound judgment accept that the giver and source of a perfection lacks that perfection, making the receiver nobler than the giver and the one who benefits more honored than the one who gives? We have established that all possible things depend on God, whose essence is pure necessity and pure actuality. Among the things that depend on Him are knowing beings and cognitive forms. The giver of all things possesses every perfection fully, without being made multiple by them. Otherwise, He would lack the very perfection He gives. So the Necessary Being knows, and His knowledge is not something added to His essence, as we have shown.

#### Chapter 3 — God's Knowledge of Everything Other Than Himself

In general discussions of knowledge, we established that complete knowledge[^V06-317] of a complete cause—or of the aspect that makes it a cause—entails complete knowledge of its effect. Do not understand “the causal aspect” to mean the relation of causality itself,[^V06-318] which comes into being only after both cause and effect. It means what comes before the effect and makes the cause its cause,[^V06-319] the reality through which the effect comes to exist.

Every effect depends for its existence on something that gives it its own definite reality, without depending on anything else. Otherwise, it and that other thing together would be what gives rise to the effect. Here we are discussing what independently gives the effect its existence. So an independent cause must have its effect as one of its necessary consequences. Every effect follows from the specific cause that brings it about. Whenever that cause exists in its full identity, whether in thought or outside it, the particular effect follows, because it belongs to the cause's own reality.

The reverse is not true. An effect does not require its specific cause, for then the cause would itself be caused by its own effect. The effect only requires some cause, because it is possible and dependent. So whenever a specific cause is present, the particular effect is present; whenever an effect is present, some cause must be present, but not necessarily the specific cause. The existence of a cause proves with certainty that its particular effect exists. The existence of an effect proves with certainty that there is some cause, but only suggests which particular cause it has. That is why reasoning from an effect to a cause counts as a kind of demonstration only in one sense of the word.[^V06-320][^V06-321] Thus, knowing the complete cause, or the aspect by which it brings about its effect, leads to knowledge of the effect. Indeed, a thing with a cause can be known only through knowing its cause.[^V06-322]

With this general principle established, we can say: God knows Himself, and His essence is the cause of everything else in an ordered sequence. He directly gives rise to the first being; through it, to the second; through both, to the third; and so on, to every existent. He must therefore know all things in the most perfect order. His knowledge of everything other than Himself follows from His knowledge of Himself, just as the existence of all else follows from His existence.[^V06-323]

But how does He know things in a way that does not make His essence or real attributes multiple, or make Him both an agent and a receiver? Nor should His knowledge of things make Him compelled to act because of that knowledge.[^V06-324] Does He know things before they exist, after they exist, or along with them—so that He knows them only when they exist? If created things affect His knowledge, or bring about a state in Him that was not there before, then He would not be necessary in every respect. To find the right answer is among the highest forms of human perfection. Whoever attains this understanding becomes like the holy beings, even one of the company of the angels nearest to God. Because the question is difficult and subtle, many learned people have gone astray. Avicenna and his followers said that God's knowledge is something added to His essence. The leading Stoic philosopher and his followers denied that God knows things before bringing them into existence. If these two men, with all their intelligence, skill, and long study of this subject, fell into error, how much more likely are those who follow their own desires and innovations, or argue only to win disputes.

Because of this difficulty, some ancient philosophers denied that God knows any existent other than Himself and the attributes that are His essence. Some went further and denied that He knows anything at all. They thought knowledge was a relation between the knower and the known, and that there could be no such relation between a thing and itself. Or they thought knowledge was an image added to the known thing and equal to it, which would imply more than one Necessary Being. They reasoned that if God did not know Himself, He could not know anything else, because knowing something else depends on first knowing oneself. They went far astray and suffered a clear loss.

How ugly and wrong it is for a created being to claim that it can know the great realities of the heavenly realm and the subtle details of the spiritual world, call itself a philosopher and wise person, and then deny its wise and knowing Creator any knowledge of anything at all—the Creator who gave existence to those who know and lit their hearts with knowledge. Perhaps those ancient philosophers meant something other than their words seem to say, or perhaps they used “knowledge” in a special sense. They may have meant to deny God the kind of receptive knowledge humans have, or mental images divided into concepts and judgments, or something like these.[^V06-325] God knows best the hidden intentions of His servants.

#### Chapter 4 — A Survey of Views on God's Knowledge of Things

There are several well-known views on how God knows things. The first is held by followers of Aristotle's school, including al-Fārābī, Avicenna, Bahmanyār, Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Lawkarī, and many later thinkers. They say that the forms of possible things are present in God's essence as mental forms, as general forms.[^V06-326] The second view is that the actual existence of things—whether they are immaterial or material, simple or composite—is what makes God know them. This was the view of Shihāb al-Dīn Suhrawardī, the leading figure among the Illuminationists, and of those who followed him, including Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi, Ibn Kammūna, al-Shīrāzī, and Muḥammad al-Shahrazūrī, author of *The Divine Tree*.

The third view says that God is united with the intelligible forms. This view is attributed to Porphyry, one of the greatest students of Aristotle and a leading figure in Aristotle's school. The fourth is Plato's view: separate Forms, or intelligible Ideas, are divine knowledge through which God knows all beings. The fifth view belongs to the Muʿtazilites, who say that possible things exist as “things” before they exist in reality, and that God knows them eternally through this prior existence. A related view is attributed to the Sufis: things are established in God's knowledge before they exist in the world.[^V06-327]

The sixth view says that God's essence is one general knowledge of all possible things. Most later thinkers hold this view. They say God has two kinds of knowledge of things: a general knowledge that comes before them, and a detailed knowledge that accompanies them. The seventh view says that God knows the First Effect in detail and everything else in a general way. The First Effect, in turn, knows the second effect in detail and everything else generally, and so on down the chain of beings.

These are the views most often discussed. One can also lay out the possibilities in a more systematic way. Anyone who says that God knows existing things must decide whether this knowledge is separate from His essence. If it is separate, then either nonexistent things are already established—outside the mind, as the Muʿtazilites say, or in the mind, as some Sufi masters say—or they are not. In the latter case, the separate knowledge may consist of Forms that exist in themselves, apart from God and the things known. These are Plato's Forms. Or God's knowledge of things may be the things themselves: they are knowledge insofar as they are present to God and exist through Him, and known things insofar as they exist in themselves, with their changing matter, their succession, and their differences of time and place. On this view, God's knowledge does not change; the things known do.[^V06-328]

If knowledge is not separate from God's essence, it may still be something other than His essence, as al-Fārābī and Avicenna say; or it may be identical with His essence. In the latter case, God may be united with the intelligible forms, as Porphyry and some followers of Aristotle say, or His essence may be one general knowledge of everything other than Himself, or of everything except the First Effect. These are eight possible views. We will examine their strengths and weaknesses, correct what can be corrected, and then identify the view closest to the truth—the truth that comes from God, the Mighty and Generous.

#### Chapter 5 — Examining the Muʿtazilite View and a View Attributed to the Sufis

The Muʿtazilites say that a nonexistent thing is still a thing: before it exists, it is already distinct from other nonexistent things, and God knows future events eternally through this prior status.[^V06-329] To clear thinking, this is a weak and mistaken view. The books of theology and philosophy have already shown why something nonexistent cannot be a thing.

The Sufi masters have been reported to say something similar. Taken at face value, their words have the same flaw: something cannot be established apart from existence, whether in the world or in the mind,[^V06-330] whether it is never created or will exist later. To distinguish these cases in that way is arbitrary. Still, we think well of these great teachers. Their books contain subtle insights, profound discoveries, and difficult truths that agree with what God has opened to our hearts—truths about which we have no doubt, as surely as we do not doubt that the sun shines at midday. So we will give their words a sound and noble interpretation. Their apparent meaning is not, on close examination, any less implausible than the Muʿtazilite view.[^V06-331]

Among the passages at issue is one in the *Meccan Revelations*. Ibn Arabi says that, while possible things are nonexistent, they see and are seen, hear and are heard—not with ordinary sight or hearing, but through an abiding kind of seeing and hearing. God chooses whichever of these things He wills and addresses it with His command, expressed in Arabic as “Be.”[^V06-332] The thing hears the command and hastens to obey. It comes to be through God's word—or, in a sense, it is that very word. Even in their timeless nonexistence, possible things know the Necessary Being, praise Him, and glorify Him with an eternal praise, though none of them has an existing individual form.[^V06-333]

In *The Bezels of Wisdom*, Ibn Arabi says that knowledge follows what is known.[^V06-334] A person who is faithful in the way his own reality is fixed while he is nonexistent will appear in that form when he exists. God knows beforehand how he will be. That is why God says that He knows best who is guided. God also says, “My word does not change,” because His word about creation accords with His knowledge of it. God does not make people unbelievers and then demand what they cannot do. He brings them forth according to what He knows of them, and He knows them as they have made themselves to be. If this is injustice, then they have wronged themselves. God says, “We did not wrong them; they wronged themselves.” God only says to them what His own reality gives Him to say; He knows Himself as He is, including what He will say and what He will not say. He speaks only as He knows He will speak. The command comes from Him; whether they obey or refuse is theirs.[^V06-335][^V06-336]

Elsewhere Ibn Arabi says that God gives existence to things according to what their fixed realities call for. Read in their ordinary sense, these words suggest that essences exist in themselves before existence. In that respect, the Sufi view would be no different from the Muʿtazilite view. But reason shows that an essence cannot precede existence, not even in its own nature, much less as something independently real. It makes no difference whether this prior status is called “known existence” or “external existence.”[^V06-337]

We must, however, distinguish two ways in which many things may be present. They may all be present through one simple existence, or each may have its own distinct existence, matching its specific nature. When we say that a horse exists in the world or in the mind, we usually mean the horse's own distinct existence—the existence that makes it an actual horse, different from a human being, an elephant, or a cow.[^V06-338] A single, simple existence may contain the realities of many kinds in a higher, unified way, though those kinds differ in meaning. Such existence is not ordinarily called the existence of any one of those kinds.

On this distinction, what reason rules out is an essence wholly cut off from existence, whether the existence in question is distinct or simple. But there is no contradiction in saying that an essence is fixed before its own particular existence—the existence that makes it a definite thing, distinct from others. Careful inquiry and sound insight, as we will explain, require this distinction. So when the knowers say that fixed entities, while nonexistent, call for a certain outcome or have a certain character, they mean that these entities lack their own separate existence in the world. They do not mean that they lack all existence. God's existence encompasses them all, since they are implications of His names. His many names and attributes, beyond counting, exist through one simple existence. The fixed entities were never absolutely nonexistent before their particular forms appeared; what they lack from eternity is only that later, limited kind of existence. This is the deep difference between the Sufi way and the Muʿtazilite way.[^V06-339]

We have already offered a glimpse of this in discussing the divisions of God's names and attributes. The world of the names is vast. You will see that this approach comes close to an ancient view: before things exist, God's knowledge of them is a simple, eternal intellect. In its simplicity, eternity, and necessary existence, that intellect is all things in a higher, nobler, and purer way. Just as things have a natural existence in this world, an imaginal and particular existence in another world, and a universal intellectual existence above both, they also have a divine, name-based existence in the realm of lordship. The Sufis call this the World of the Names.

#### Chapter 6 — Plato’s Forms Apart from Matter, and the View Attributed to Porphyry

We have defended the Platonists' view of separate Ideas. We argued for it and strengthened its case in the discussion of universal knowledge in the First Journey. But whether these Ideas are what make up God's eternal and perfect knowledge, prior to everything else, is another matter. God's knowledge is eternal and necessary through Himself, while these Ideas exist after Him and after His knowledge of them.[^V06-340] How, then, can they themselves be His knowledge of things from all eternity?

The same problem arises if these separate Ideas are real beings rather than mental forms: how does God know them before they proceed from Him? Either that leads to an endless chain of forms, or it means God does not know many things until they exist and learns of them from those things. Both possibilities conflict with the principles we have established.[^V06-341]

As for the view attributed to Porphyry, Avicenna and many later philosophers have ridiculed and rejected it, as one can see in Avicenna's *Healing*, *Salvation*, and *Pointers*, in Suhrawardī's works, in Bahmanyār's *The Book of Attainment*, in al-Tusi, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, and others.[^V06-342] We have already discussed this question at length in the section on the unity of the intellect and the intelligible. Anyone who wants to understand the view and see its depth and subtlety should read that discussion. It shows how high the view's author stood in wisdom, how firmly grounded he was in knowledge, and how clear his inner vision was—provided the reader has the strength to enter these questions and think them through. Such insight is a gift from God.[^V06-343]

#### Chapter 7 — The View That Forms of Things Are Present in God's Essence

Avicenna's view, as it appears in many of his books, is this. Sometimes an idea understood by the mind comes from an object in the world. Through sight and careful observation, for example, we form an idea of the heavens, their arrangement, and their shapes. But sometimes the idea does not come from something already perceived. The reverse may happen. A builder first forms an image of a house in his imagination; that image then moves his body until the house is built. In that case, the house's existence in the mind does not come from its existence in the world. Its existence in the world follows its existence in the mind.

In the earlier discussion of mental qualities, we saw that knowledge can be active or receptive. Active knowledge is a cause of the thing known coming to exist in the world. Receptive knowledge comes from the thing already existing. A builder's knowledge of the house he plans is active knowledge, though it is not enough by itself to build the house: he also needs suitable material, tools, a particular place and time, and other conditions.

All things in this world stand in relation to God as a maker's work stands to its maker, or a wise author's book to its author—if that maker's or author's creative power were complete enough that merely conceiving the work brought it into existence. Things proceed from God because He knows them; He does not first find them existing and then know them. God's knowledge of things is like our plans, which we first work out in thought and then bring about. The intelligible form is in some way the cause of the thing's existence. There are, of course, many differences between our thinking and God's knowledge. We are incomplete makers. When we choose to act, we need desire, which arises after we imagine the act. We also need a power to move us, bodily tools such as muscles, tendons, hands, and feet, and external tools such as an axe, chisel, pen, or ink. We need material able to receive the form we intend to give it.

God, by contrast, is complete in existence and in bringing things into being. When He wills something, His command is “Be,” and it is. His command and word, which follow from His eternal essence, are His knowing of things. The existence of things in the world follows their intelligible forms, which are His word. These forms are God's complete words, which never run out.[^V06-344] The Qur'an points to this in a striking way: the outer world comes to be through God's word and intellectual command, which accords with His eternal will. We will explain the meaning of God's word and book later, in the Illuminationist way, God willing.

Another argument for saying that God knows things through forms present in His essence begins from this: God knows Himself, and His essence is the cause of things. Complete knowledge of a cause entails knowledge of its effect. So God's knowledge of His own essence would mean that He knows all things eternally. But things did not all exist in reality from eternity. If they also had no intellectual existence in Him, then He would not know them.[^V06-345]

Knowledge requires a connection between the knower and what is known. This connection may itself be the relation between them, or it may be a form that establishes the relation. A relation to sheer nonexistence is impossible, because a relation requires both sides to be present in some way. And if things were present in God as parts of His essence, that would make Him composite. So the argument concludes that before things exist in the world, they must exist as intellectual forms in God. These forms must either be separate from Him, which leads to Plato's separate Ideas, or be parts of His essence, which leads to composition. Both are impossible. So, the argument says, the forms must be added to His essence while present in it. This is the view under discussion.

An objection is raised against this reasoning. God's eternal power, too, is related to possible things. His power includes all created things,[^V06-346] which come from Him directly or through an intermediary. If the argument required the actual existence of everything God knows, then all created things would have to exist eternally. But created things cannot exist eternally. The proof would therefore fail.

One clever reply says that a relation needs only the assumed existence of its two sides, since the relation itself is only assumed. But the speaker recognizes that this would undermine the whole argument. So he adds that knowledge requires the known thing to be present and revealed to the knower, whereas sheer nonexistence has nothing that can be revealed.

I say: We will return to the original proof. As for the objection based on God's power, we can answer it in this way. Even if power, like knowledge, requires both what acts and what is acted upon, the objection does not succeed. The things God has power over need not exist externally from eternity. Just as it is enough for the things He knows to exist in their forms, it is enough for what He has power over to exist in its forms. These forms are known to Him and also proceed from Him.
