The other reply is extremely weak for several reasons.[^V06-347] First, it assumes that relations exist only because someone imagines them. But they have a basis in reality whether or not anyone thinks of them. Second, even if relations are not separate things in the world, we derive them from things, and they require the two things being related.[^V06-348] Third, that reply arbitrarily distinguishes God's eternal knowledge from His eternal power, although His real attributes are one reality and share one existence. We must not judge His power by our own. In us, power means the capacity and readiness to act;[^V06-349] in God, it means making something necessary and bringing it about. His knowledge and His power therefore stand in the same relation to possible things.

Avicenna gives another account in the divine philosophy of *The Healing*. God knows things, he says, but He cannot know them by receiving knowledge from them. If His essence were shaped by what He knows, then His essence would depend on those things and become composite, which is impossible. Nor can knowledge of things come to Him from outside, since that would introduce a new quality and change Him. The things themselves do not cause His knowledge.

God does not know changing individuals through separate images that multiply and change in His essence. Instead, He knows them as part of a universal order. This does not mean that He knows only general kinds and misses the individual things. The whole chain of causes includes individuals, times, and changes. God knows the causes and how they fit together, and thus knows what must follow from them, including particular events. So He knows individuals through a universal kind of knowledge, not through changing, sensory images.[^V06-350]

Anything that receives a new quality from outside must have a receptive capacity, and so must be composite: one aspect is actual, another is only potential. That cannot apply to the Necessary Being, who is simple in every respect.[^V06-351]

Nor can God know changing things as changing things, through a separate image for each one. Such images would be changing, perishable, and opposed to one another; God would then be a place where changes and opposites gather. Nor can He know them through sensory or imaginative forms, since these are particular and divided, and need bodily organs. God must know things in another way: intellectually, without change. He knows every thing in a universal way, yet nothing particular escapes Him—not even the weight of an atom in the heavens or earth. This is a remarkable truth, though difficult to grasp.[^V06-352]

How does this work? When God knows His own essence and knows that He is the source of everything that exists, He knows the first beings that come from Him and everything that follows from them. Nothing comes into existence unless something makes its existence necessary in some respect; we have already established this. The causes meet and interact until particular events result. God knows those causes, their connections, the outcomes that necessarily follow from them, the times between events, and the patterns that recur. He could not know the causes in full without knowing these consequences. In this way, He knows particular events through the general order of causes.

Avicenna also says: “Do not suppose that if there are many intelligible forms in God, their multiplicity makes them parts of His essence. How could they be parts, when they follow from His essence? His knowledge of His own essence is His essence itself. Through that knowledge He knows what comes after Him. His knowledge of those things follows from His knowledge of Himself. The forms that follow from His essence are present as intellectual objects, not as the images and impressions that arise in our souls.[^V06-353] His relation to them is the relation of their source to what proceeds from it, not of a subject that receives them. They follow in an order, one before another in the order of dependence, although there is no earlier or later among them in time. No movement from one act of thought to another occurs there.”

He continues: “The divine world therefore encompasses everything that exists or could exist. God's essence is related to these things as known things, not simply as separate things outside the mind. A further question remains.[^V06-354] When they exist as intelligible forms, are they present in the essence of the First as features that necessarily follow from Him? Or do they exist separately from Him and from other beings, as independent forms ordered within the divine domain?[^V06-355] Or do they exist within some intellect or soul? On this third view, when that intellect or soul knows the First, the forms become present within it. It is then the subject in which they are known. They are known to that intellect because they are within it, and known to God because they come from Him.”

“God knows from His own essence that He is the source of those intelligible things. Some proceed directly from Him, while others proceed through intermediaries. This must also determine the order of their existence, even if they are all present within one intellect. One comes before another because one causes another, not because of an interval of time.”

“But if the forms present in that other intellect[^V06-356][^V06-357] are themselves effects of the First, then they belong to what God knows Himself to produce. We face the difficulty described earlier. Either God's knowing a form brings it into existence, or the form's existence is itself His knowledge of it. If a further act of knowledge were needed, God would first have to know that He knows, and then know that He knows that—and so on without end. If their very existence is His knowledge of them, however, to say ‘They existed because He knew them’ amounts to saying ‘He knew them because He knew them,’ or ‘They existed because they existed.’”

“If these intelligible forms are made into parts of God's essence, multiplicity is introduced into Him. If they are features added to His essence, His essence seems to touch what is possible and thus to acquire a relation that does not belong to what is necessary in itself. If they are independent entities, we arrive at the separate Forms of Plato. And if they exist within another intellect, we encounter the difficulty just described.[^V06-358] You must therefore make every effort to resolve this problem while preserving the unity of God's essence.”

“Do not be troubled merely because the divine essence, when considered together with a particular relation,[^V06-359] can be described through something possible. Considered as the cause of Zayd's existence, it does not have the same description as it does considered in itself. In itself it is necessary. The divine world is indeed immensely vast. You must also distinguish a thing's giving rise to an image that can be known from its giving rise to a form that is intelligible in the very act of proceeding from it. God knows Himself as the source of every intelligible thing in that very respect, just as He is the source of every existing effect precisely as an existing effect. Think deeply about the principles already laid down and those still to come, so that the truth becomes clear to you.”

That is the end of Avicenna's explanation. Some of it is sound and valuable; some of it is uncertain. Everything in it that supports God's knowledge of things before their external existence, and the presence of their forms in divine knowledge before those things exist, is shared by two different views. One places the forms in God; the other affirms separate Platonic Forms.

Both accounts can accept that God's knowledge of His essence is the source of His knowledge of these forms; that their multiplicity follows from His undivided essence; that they proceed in an order of causes and effects;[^V06-360] and that their being known by Him is identical with their proceeding from Him.[^V06-361] The forms do not first become known and then exist, or first exist and then become known. They exist as known and are known as existing. Both accounts also accept that God's perfection lies in His essence being the source of these realities, not in His acquiring them as further attributes. What becomes uncertain is the attempt to explain all of this as forms impressed upon a receiving subject. We shall return to that problem.

It is remarkable that those who came after Avicenna and tried to refute this view have not managed to do so. Every objection they have raised comes from too little care in reading his words, a weakness in their own understanding, or too little study of the foundations of philosophy.

Abū al-Barakāt al-Baghdādī argues that if God learned things from them, then something other than God would help complete His essence. But the same reasoning would apply to God as the maker of things: if His making were complete only once the things were made, then those things would complete Him. That is plainly wrong; so, Abū al-Barakāt says, the claim that God learns from things is wrong too.

The reply is that “making” can refer to the relation between maker and made, which comes after both exist.

It can also refer to the source of that relation, which comes first. God's real power to create does not depend on the existence of His effects, because the effects depend on Him. Likewise, God's knowledge should be treated as the source of knowing, not as a relation that comes after the things known.[^V06-362]

Suhrawardī, the leader of the Illuminationist school, objects that this view makes God's essence a place for many qualities. Even if God is not changed by them, he says, the account still requires one aspect of God to act and another to receive. It also seems impossible for a being to be the subject of qualities without being described by them.

I agree that a single thing cannot both act and receive an effect when receiving means undergoing a new change. But this does not apply to every case in which a cause produces something and the result depends on that cause.[^V06-363] Suppose an effect exists only as a quality of the very cause that necessarily produces it. The act by which it comes from the cause is then the same connection by which it belongs to the cause. The cause produces just this kind of dependent existence. If the effect did not belong to it in that way, the cause would not be producing the effect we are speaking about. So if there is a problem with saying that the first things proceeding from God are qualities, the problem must lie elsewhere. It cannot simply be that producing and receiving are impossible in the same respect.

It is surprising that the critic accepts the necessary features of simple essences, while also saying that those essences themselves are the only causes of these features. Does that not make the same simple essence both the producer of its necessary feature and what the feature belongs to?

His question about how any reasonable person could accept this is aimed at Bahmanyār. In *The Attainment*, Bahmanyār says that God's essence, though it may be described as the subject of many qualities, is not changed by them and does not acquire new features. His point is that God is not affected by them. We often say that something “has a quality” when the quality changes the subject, as color and taste change a body, or acquired knowledge and power change a soul. But an intellect's own actions and what follows from its activity do not affect it in that way. Nor do the relations it has to things that depend on it. If “having a quality” merely means that something belongs to or follows from a subject, the wording is harmless and does not establish a defect. If it means that the subject becomes something it was not before, no such change follows in this case. The harmless meaning is possible; the harmful meaning is not required.[^V06-364]

Suhrawardī raises another objection. Even if God's self-knowledge is not an addition to His essence, one might say that His knowledge of what necessarily follows from Him comes *after* those consequences. If He knows a consequence because it follows from His nature, then that consequence must first follow from Him before He can know it. In that case, God's knowledge would depend on the consequence. It could not be the cause of the thing's existence, as the philosophers maintain.

I reply that this turns largely on the wording. When philosophers say that God's knowledge of Himself is the cause of His knowledge of what follows from Him, they do not mean that a consequence first exists on its own and is then discovered by God. They mean that God's knowledge of Himself, which is His own essence, gives rise to knowledge of what truly follows from that essence, even where the dependence of the consequence itself is mediated through that knowledge.[^V06-365] What follows from a consequence also follows, ultimately, from its first source.

The forms known within God follow from His essence, which is His self-knowledge. Things existing outside Him then follow from His knowledge of those things. They may therefore be called consequences of His consequences. In another respect they are the same consequences, because the form known and the thing existing outside the mind agree in what they are, while differing in their ways of existence—as known in thought and as existing in the world. So there is no contradiction between saying that God's self-knowledge is the cause of His knowledge of what follows from Him and saying that those things come to exist through His knowledge of them.[^V06-366]

Suhrawardī then anticipates this reply. He asks: if the forms known by God are present in Him before the separate things that follow from Him, and those things cannot exist without these forms, would God not produce them through His being together with the forms, rather than through His being alone?

I say that those who hold this view do accept that the forms are involved.[^V06-367] But this does not close the way to proving the existence of the first intellect. The argument that God is one in every respect, and that His first effect must therefore be one, is not our only proof. First, there are several ways to establish the first intellect and the existence of more than one intellect.[^V06-368]

Second, the many aspects that follow from God's being are forms of things existing outside Him. Not every one of these forms can account for just any effect. Each accounts for the thing that corresponds to it, in its proper order.[^V06-369]

Third, if proof shows that the forms of things are present in God's knowledge before He brings those things into existence,[^V06-370] their existence in the world must agree with how they exist in His knowledge. Their outward conditions and qualities must also agree with their known forms. Otherwise His eternal knowledge would not correspond to what it knows. It would be ignorance, which is impossible.

He then says: “Whether the first form precedes the separate effect or is neither before nor after it, its presence in God's essence would require two aspects in that essence. Nor can the absence of matter be the cause that brings the Necessary Being from an unestablished possibility into actually possessing a form. On that view, His essence would receive the form, while the absence of matter, either alone or together with His essence, would produce it. The absence would then be nobler than His essence: His essence would merely receive, while the absence would determine that the form comes to be and bring it into actuality. But a negative aspect cannot be nobler than the Necessary Being.”

I say: His claim that the first form would require two aspects in God, one for acting and another for receiving, merely repeats his earlier objection.[^V06-371] As for his claim about the absence of matter, he has either devised this answer on behalf of his opponents so that he can attack it, or found it in the words of their followers. It rests on a mistake: that whenever something follows from another thing, the first must have been capable of acquiring it, whether or not it follows necessarily and whether or not the first thing brings it about.

That is not so. A source that produces something does not truly lack it and then gain it, either in time or in the order of dependence. Otherwise the source would contain an unfulfilled possibility or capacity. Rather, a thing is more complete in its source than it is when considered in itself, as we have already shown. This is a valuable principle, understood by only a few.[^V06-372]

He then objects that the first form, together with the First, would produce two different effects: the first effect itself and another form. I reply, first, that his objection applies equally to the first intellect, which is said to cause both the second intellect and the outermost sphere.[^V06-373] More fundamentally, the first form is not one in the same absolute way as the Necessary Being. Everything other than God includes both perfection and limitation, and each aspect has its own effects: what is nobler gives rise to what is nobler, and what is lower gives rise to what is lower.

I say: you already know that, on this account, the forms do not add any perfection to His essence. His knowledge that truly belongs among His perfections is identical with His essence. These forms proceed from Him just as other existing things proceed from Him. They come from His essence, and their existence and their necessity depend on Him.[^V06-374] In relation to Him they are necessary and actual, not merely possible or waiting to become actual.

There is no contradiction in saying that something is possible when considered in relation to its own nature, yet necessary when considered in relation to its cause. The existence of each form is at once its existence in itself and its existence as something known to the Necessary Being. That does not make its existence merely possible in relation to Him. To call an existence *possible* means that it has a nature distinct from its existence and, when measured against that nature alone, could either exist or not exist. It does not mean that the existence is in a state of possibility in itself, in reality, or in relation to the cause from which it cannot be separated.[^V06-375]

In short, the presence of these forms does not remove any weakness or lack in the Necessary Being; nor does their absence mean that He has lost something. His perfection and glory consist in His being such that the forms proceed from Him. They do not consist in His acquiring those forms, or even in the forms' existence considered on its own. His perfection belongs to His essence, not to something else.

Avicenna makes this point repeatedly. In his *Annotations* he writes:

> Suppose the First understands Himself as the source of those things,[^V06-376] and suppose they then exist in Him. Does their existence in Him affect His understanding of them,[^V06-377] or does it not? If it does, their existence is the cause of His understanding them. Yet their existence is itself caused by His understanding them. We would then be saying that He understands them because He understands them, or that they come from Him because they come from Him.[^V06-378]

In another note Avicenna says:

> If the existence of those things as objects of thought[^V06-379] caused the First to understand them, while His understanding caused their existence, we would simply be saying that they came from Him because they came from Him. If His understanding caused their existence, but their existence then caused His understanding, we would be saying that He understood them because He understood them. Both accounts are impossible. The truth is that their being understood by Him is itself their existence as proceeding from Him.

He adds:

> The First does not learn about existing things from their existence,[^V06-380] for He gives them existence. He knows them as proceeding from Him. His knowledge of Himself is knowledge of them because they follow necessarily from Him. He brings them into existence as things already known, not as things that first exist and then become fit to be known.

Avicenna adds a further note:

> Someone may say that He knows things before they exist.[^V06-381] But this would seem to mean either that He knows them while they do not exist, or that He knows them only when they exist, so that He learns of them from their existence. That way of putting it is mistaken. His knowledge of them is their very existence, and their being objects of His knowledge is their being existent. He does not know things by first receiving them within Himself, as we know things when they become present to us. Their being present to Him is His knowledge of them, and His knowledge is simple.

He then says, in effect: “To claim that forms exist in the Necessary Being is a false claim and a poor belief. It would mean that something higher than His essence supplies Him with the forms, which is impossible. And if its defenders admit that one and the same thing, in the same respect, can both receive and act, they destroy many of their own important principles and accept numerous contradictions.” That is the end of his statement.

My reply is this. We know that the Necessary Being is the source of everything and the source of the necessity through which things exist. When something comes from Him through an intermediate cause, that cause too comes from Him. Every power to act therefore has its source in Him, and every excellence is a gift flowing from His excellence. Nothing can add anything to Him. How could the things He gives existence to increase a perfection that was not already His through His own essence?

We also know that the source of a feature and the subject to which it belongs need not always be distinct. Consider the features that follow necessarily from a thing's essence. In this discussion, to call something a *receiver* does not mean that it undergoes a change or is affected from outside. Nothing in this claim, properly understood, overturns a philosophical principle.

Of course, if someone claimed without qualification that one thing could act and receive an action in precisely the same respect, important rules would be undermined. The soul, for example, has been said to need different powers for receiving impressions and preserving them: common sensation and imagination, or estimation and memory. The power that senses receives and is affected; the power that preserves an impression acts. Natural philosophers likewise distinguish wetness from dryness, the capacity for movement from the power that moves something, and sensation from movement.

The same mistake would affect the account of bodies as composed of matter and form. A body, as a body, already has the definite reality of an extended substance, but it is also capable of receiving other states. Its form explains what it already is; its matter explains its capacity to become something else.

But the proof that God's attributes are identical with His essence does not rest solely on saying that, if they were added to Him, one and the same being would have to act and receive. We gave a different argument in the discussion of the divine attributes. God, after all, has many ordered relations to created things,[^V06-382] and these do not force us to posit separate sides in Him, one acting and one receiving.

In short, despite the long history of objections, I do not see that the critics have yet shown the claim that intelligible forms exist in the Necessary Being to be a corrupt belief or an incoherent position. There remains the argument that I shall present, with the help of God, the Mighty and Wise.

Another determined opponent of these forms was Nasir al-Din al-Tusi, in his commentary on Avicenna's *Pointers and Reminders*. This is surprising, since at the start of his sections on nature and theology he had promised not to disagree with Avicenna's beliefs. I wish he had kept that promise here too. It would have served him better until God opened his heart to what is right.

Al-Tusi set out what he regarded as the problems with saying that forms exist within God's essence. He wrote:

“To say that the necessary consequences of the First exist within His essence is to say that one and the same being both acts and receives. It is to say that the First has attributes that are neither relations nor negations.[^V06-383] It makes Him a subject[^V06-384] in which His many possible effects exist—far exalted is He above that. It also means that the first effect is not separate from His essence, and that He brings nothing distinct from Himself into existence except through things that reside within Him.

“Such claims conflict with what appears to be the teaching of the philosophers. Earlier thinkers denied knowledge to God.[^V06-385] Plato held that intelligible forms exist independently; the followers of Aristotle held that the knower and the known become one; and the Mu'tazilites held that things which do not exist nevertheless have an established reality. They took on these impossible positions in order to avoid the implications just listed.”

I say that the first objection—that God would both act and receive—works only if receiving means undergoing a new change,[^V06-386] or if what is received adds a perfection God did not already have.[^V06-387] If 'receiving' merely means that something necessarily follows from His essence, no contradiction has been shown. Anyone who means something more must prove it.

Avicenna says in his *Annotations*: “The One who must exist cannot receive something that makes Him what He is, since receiving in this sense involves a capacity not yet fulfilled. Nor can we simply say that qualities arise in Him from His own essence and make Him a receiver as well as an agent. There is, however, a different case: qualities that necessarily follow from His essence. They are present in Him because they proceed from Him; He does not acquire them from elsewhere. Consider the difference between saying a body is white because whiteness comes to it from outside, and saying, if this were possible for a body, that it is white because whiteness follows from its own nature.[^V06-388] On this understanding, the First and what necessarily follows from Him do not introduce several things into His essence. There is no separate receiver and agent. In the very respect in which He is called a receiver, He is the agent.[^V06-389] The same holds for every simple reality: what follows from it and is present in it does not divide it. In a thing that has parts, by contrast, what something proceeds from can differ from the part in which it is present.”

Avicenna adds that this applies to everything that necessarily follows from a simple reality. He writes: “There is no readiness in simple things. Readiness means that something can come to be in a thing through another cause after it was absent, and the readiness must exist before that reception. The human soul[^V06-390] cannot both produce intellectual forms and receive them after lacking them, because that would require an earlier capacity. But something whose nature is always accompanied by what it understands does not need an unrealized capacity. What receives an intellectual form cannot, in the same respect, be the source that produces it, when neither activity was there before. Such a change presupposes a capacity.”

What we have now shown should be clear, though most people do not readily see it. When a property belongs to a thing, that does not by itself mean that the thing is made of several parts. Such a composition arises only where one of two conditions holds: either there is a capacity that comes before the property in time, or there is a possibility that comes before it by nature.

The first kind creates real composition in the outside world. Think of a body becoming white or a moving thing beginning to move. The form, or what plays the role of form, is the source of the action. Matter, or what plays the role of matter, receives it and undergoes the change. Here the two sides really are distinct.

The second kind produces a distinction in thought and meaning, not two separate things outside the mind. When existence is said to belong to an essence, for example, existence is what makes it actual, while the essence considered in itself is open to the possibility of existing. These are different aspects we can understand, not necessarily separate parts of the thing.

A feature that follows necessarily from an essence is different from both cases. The essence has that feature because of what it is, not because the feature first completes it. Considered in itself, it is not merely able to receive that feature; it makes the feature necessary. We cannot therefore divide it into a possible receiver and a necessary giver. Nor can we divide it into something that acts and something that is acted upon.

The second alleged difficulty—that God would acquire further real attributes—does not follow either. It would follow only if the intelligible forms completed His essence or added something to His existence. But God's existence is unlimited in its intensity, while the forms that necessarily follow from Him are outpourings of His own being.

This explains the statement that, although God's essence may be described as the place where those forms are present, He does not gain attributes or perfections from them. His greatness does not come from acquiring knowledge of things through an addition to Himself.[^V06-391] His greatness lies in His own essence, from which things come forth as known realities. It belongs to Him through what He is, not through the forms that follow from Him.

Bahmanyār says the same: although the forms are in God, He is not changed or perfected by them. His being Necessary in Himself is the same as His being the source of what follows from Him. His perfection lies in giving rise to these forms, not in being their receptacle.[^V06-392]

In short, God has these necessary consequences through His essence, not because things or images make Him have them. His knowledge of things does not come from those things or their mental forms; it comes from His own essence.[^V06-393] This point is subtle and difficult, but once understood, it clarifies how God may know things before creating them, whether one thinks the known forms exist in the mind or as separate Ideas. If one has not grasped this point, both accounts seem to make God potentially dependent on something else.

Avicenna repeatedly says that God's knowing Himself is the very existence of things from Him; and that the existence of things from Him is their being known by Him.[^V06-394][^V06-395] His relation to them is a simple intellectual relation, not a relation of a form to matter, or of an accident to a subject.[^V06-396]

I find it surprising that Avicenna, after examining the question in this way, so strongly rejected the view attributed to Porphyry: that the intellect becomes one with what it understands. Yet the account he gives here and elsewhere seems to support that very view. The existence of these forms from God is itself their being known by Him. Their relation to Him is a simple relation, not the relation of one independently existing thing to another. So we cannot imagine the knower and these understood forms as separate in existence, or even as capable of being separated. There must be some kind of union between them, although its nature is difficult to grasp without direct insight.[^V06-397]

Another note says: “The existence of these forms from God is itself His knowledge of them. His knowledge that their existence necessarily follows from Him is the source of that existence. He does not need a second act of knowledge to know that He is their source.”

Another: “That these forms exist from Him is the same as His knowing Himself to be their source. Their being known is their existing, and their existing is their being known.”

Another: “If God first knew His essence and only afterwards knew that His essence is the source of existing things, He would have to know His essence twice.”

Another: “The First knows His essence as it really is. He knows it as the source of things, with the things that follow from it present in one simple act of knowledge. He does not first know Himself and only then discover that He is the source of things. Knowing the forms and giving them existence are one. We must not judge God's knowledge by the way we know. We discover causes and effects through comparison and reasoning. First we know that something exists, then we establish that it causes something else, and then we may reflect on our own earlier knowledge. Each can require a further act of thought. God's first and simple act of knowledge does not work in that way.”

Another: “God necessarily knows His own essence and knows it as the source of beings. Existing things are therefore known by Him, and in this account they are not outside His essence,[^V06-398] because His essence is their source. He is the one who knows and what is known. This does not hold for other knowers, who know things outside themselves.” Avicenna gives other explanations along the same lines.

In a further note he says: “The First's greatness and glory do not consist in His acquiring knowledge of things. They consist in things flowing from Him as things already known. His greatness and glory belong to His essence, not to the forms that follow from Him. The same applies to creation: His excellence lies in His being the creator, not in His receiving some added perfection from the fact that the things have been created.”

Now consider the third objection: that these forms would introduce multiplicity into God. Such multiplicity follows from His essence in an order of cause and effect—first, second, third, and so on to the last effect.[^V06-399] It does not divide His essence or damage its unity. The unity of a source is not destroyed because many things come from it.

Avicenna answers this objection in several places in the *Annotations*. The order in question is an order of dependence, not of time. Many existing things proceed from God in a sequence of causes and effects, without making His essence composite. In the same way, the many distinct things known by Him depend on Him in an order that gathers them all into one source. Their plurality remains, but His essence contains them in undivided unity.

Al-Fārābī expresses the same thought: “The Necessary Being is the source of every outpouring, and His essence is wholly present to itself.”[^V06-400] He has everything without having a plurality of parts; He reaches everything through Himself.[^V06-401] His knowledge of all things follows from His essence,[^V06-402] while His knowledge of Himself is identical with His essence. In relation to Him, all things come together in one. He is all in unity.

The fourth alleged difficulty is that the First Effect would not be separate from God's essence. If *not separate* means that it exists in God as a feature belongs to its subject,[^V06-403] that is exactly what is being disputed. It cannot be used as an argument against those who say that God's knowledge consists of forms present in His essence, though distinct from Him.

Perhaps the objection means something else: the form of the First Effect would have to be identical with the Necessary Being. Every effect, it is said, must be known before it proceeds from its cause. If the form of the First Effect were not identical with God, another form would have to come before it so that He could know it. That form would require yet another, and the series would go on without end, contrary to the original assumption.

The answer has been given in the works of al-Fārābī and Avicenna, and in those of their followers, including Bahmanyār's *The Attainment*. An external thing whose existence is not the same as its being known must be preceded by knowledge of it. But this is not true of a form whose very existence from God *is* its being known by Him. Its existence is itself intelligible, so it needs no other act of knowledge before it can proceed from Him. Another form would add nothing.

Avicenna writes in his *Annotations*: “Everything that comes from the Necessary Being comes through His knowledge of it. The forms known by Him are such that their existence from Him and His knowledge of them are one and the same. Neither comes before the other, and the two are not separate. His knowing them is not something other than their existence from Him. Considered as existing, they are known; considered as known, they exist. In the same way, the Creator's existence is the same as His knowledge of Himself.”

He continues: “The forms known by God must be such that their existence from Him is itself His knowledge of them. Otherwise, if other known forms caused those forms to exist, the same question would arise about the additional forms, and the chain would never end. They would have to be known before they existed; or they would be known because they existed, so that their existence caused their being known and their being known caused their existence. Then their being known would cause itself, and their existence would cause itself.”[^V06-404]

In another note he writes: “If these things that necessarily follow from God began to exist only after He knew them, then they would already have to exist when He knew them. A thing must exist in some way to be known. Its existence would therefore come before its being known, and the same problem would repeat without end. Their being known by Him must therefore be identical with their existence from Him.”

Elsewhere he makes the point again: “Either the forms come from God after being known, in which case they already have to exist in order to be known, or they exist and are then known. But since they are themselves things that follow from God, knowing them through another form would require another form in its turn. That process too would continue without end.”

Avicenna gives another argument in his *Annotations*: “Suppose someone says that the things necessarily following from God exist because He knows them. But the knowledge said to bring them about would itself be one of the things that follow from Him. We must then ask why that knowledge exists and through what it exists. If the answer is that it exists because He knows it, the same question returns without end.”

He puts the point once more: “The forms known by God necessarily follow from His essence. If their existence depends on His knowing them, that knowing must also follow from His essence. We must then account for the existence of that knowledge by another act of knowing, and another after that, without end.”

A final objection says that this view makes God bring about things distinct from Himself only through forms that exist in Him, rather than directly through His essence. But this is not an argument; it is simply an expression of disbelief. Someone could object in the same way to the view that the world of separate intellects comes before the bodily world, or that the world of God's command comes before the world of creation. Such a person might say that God would then make bodies only through intellects, or create things only through His command, rather than directly.

My first answer is that we must follow proof, not what someone finds surprising. My second is that the higher world comes first and is more closely related to its source. In the same way, the forms that necessarily follow from God endure through His enduring existence, not because He has to keep them in being through a further act.[^V06-405] They exist through His existence, not through a separate act of making; they are necessary through His necessity, not through an additional act that makes them necessary. They are therefore higher and closer to Him than things that exist apart from Him. That makes them more, not less, fitting as the means through which He brings those distinct things into existence.

Among the later critics of divine forms was al-Khafarī. In one of his writings, he quotes Anaximenes of Miletus: every created thing appears in the form it had in its creator's knowledge, and the forms in God's knowledge are without limit.[^V06-406][^V06-407] The philosopher then says there are only two options: either God creates what He knows, or He creates things He does not know. The second is an unacceptable claim. If He creates what He knows, the forms are eternal with Him, without multiplying His essence or changing when the known things change.[^V06-408][^V06-409]

Al-Khafarī objects, first, that Anaximenes never explains whether these forms come from God's prior knowledge. If they do, then God's own simple knowledge is already enough to know the things that exist, so why would He need further forms? If the forms do not come from prior knowledge, then God creates things He does not know, which the argument itself rejects. I reply that God's simple knowledge brings forth the forms that necessarily follow from His essence, but that knowledge by itself does not bring forth external things whose realities are distinct from Him. God's knowledge of the forms is identical with His bringing them about; no further prior knowledge is needed for forms whose being known is their existence from Him.

Al-Khafarī raises a second objection. The forms, he says, must be either substances or accidental properties. If they are substances, they must be real beings in their own right. God would then need further forms in order to know them, and the same difficulty would arise about those further forms. If, on the other hand, they are accidental properties, God would be both their cause and the subject in which they exist. Saying that God causes them but does not receive them, because He is not affected by them, would be to treat them as substances after all.

Al-Khafarī adds that knowing through such forms could not count as God's own essential perfection, because on that account His knowledge would follow the outpouring of the forms. If the knowledge that comes before the existence of external things were nothing but the outpouring of forms that make those things known, God would have no complete knowledge belonging to His essence independently of His acts. Yet we have already shown that He does have such knowledge. That is the objection.

Several parts of this argument need examination. First, Al-Khafarī claims that God's knowledge, identical with His essence, is sufficient by itself to produce things. But the philosophers he criticizes hold that this knowledge necessarily brings forth intelligible forms, which follow from God's essence. It is not by itself sufficient to bring into existence things that are separate from God's own reality. His knowledge of an external thing is not that external thing's own existence,[^V06-410] and the relation by which He gives it existence is not the same as the relation by which He knows it. If the two were identical, external things would be forms joined to Him, not beings distinct from Him.

We have already shown that God's knowledge of the forms present in His essence is the same as His bringing them forth. Where knowledge and creation are one, and the known thing is the very effect that comes into being, there is no need for an earlier, separate act of detailed knowledge before that effect can proceed through knowledge, will, and choice. Al-Khafarī therefore cannot fairly say that this account has God producing things He does not know.

More precisely, on the view we are developing, such forms are necessary consequences of His essence, so it is misleading to speak of their existence as if it were produced by a new act of making.[^V06-411] A thing does not stand in the same cause-and-effect relation to the features that necessarily follow from what it is. Its being that thing already includes its having those features.

Second, the objection that the forms must be either substances or accidents confuses different meanings of these terms. It treats a substance as the mind understands it as though it must be an independently existing substance outside the mind. It also assumes that something cannot be a substance in what it means while being an accidental property in how it exists. But the proposed choice is useful only if the two possibilities really exclude each other.[^V06-412]

We can therefore say that the forms are substances in their intelligible meanings but accidental properties in their actual way of existing.[^V06-413] Their being known requires no second forms. Looked at as existing, they are present through God's essence, but that essence is not altered or affected by them, as we have already explained.

Al-Khafarī argues that God's knowledge of the forms is not itself a perfection, because it follows their coming forth from Him. But this is wrong. We have explained repeatedly that God's knowing these forms *is* their coming forth, not something that happens afterwards. If he means instead that the forms themselves do not add perfection to God,[^V06-414] who has denied that? Who has ever claimed that the things which necessarily follow from the complete divine essence are further perfections that complete it? The philosophers who affirm these forms say plainly that God's greatness and perfection do not consist in the forms existing in Him. His greatness lies in His essence being such that these things come forth from it already known, and in His knowing them through knowing Himself.

It is also important to see that His knowledge of the forms is, in this account, His knowledge of separate external things,[^V06-415] although the existence of those external things is not the existence of the forms. These philosophers maintain that external things are known through forms whose existence is the existence of knowledge itself. Some later thinkers and followers of the Stoics, including Suhrawardī and Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi, take a different view.

They regard external things, including bodies,[^V06-416] as capable of being directly related to divine knowledge. But they seem to overlook a familiar principle: for a thing known directly, its existence as that known thing, its being known, and its existence for the knower are one reality. The same applies to an object directly sensed: its existence as sensed and its presence to the one sensing it are not two separate things.

Material things with definite positions in space do not, just by existing as material things, exist as intellectual objects or as sensible forms in the knower. This, at least, is what our own investigation has established. How then could material forms, before being separated from matter by thought,[^V06-417] be knowledge and objects of knowledge, or intellect and objects of intellect, simply as they are?

Nor is Al-Khafarī's final objection sound. He says that if God's earlier knowledge consisted only in the coming forth of distinct forms, God would have no knowledge that was a perfection of His essence. But the philosophers do not limit divine knowledge to forms that follow from the essence.

Consider Avicenna's words near the beginning of his discussion of God's relation to intelligible things in the *Metaphysics* of *The Healing*: “When the First is called an intellect, this is meant in the simple sense explained in the book on the soul. It does not mean that different, ordered images arise within Him as they arise in our souls. He therefore understands all things at once, without becoming many in His substance or receiving images as parts of His own reality. Their forms flow from Him as intelligible forms. He has a better claim to be called intellect than the forms flowing from His knowing, since He knows His own essence, knows it as the source of everything, and so knows everything from Himself.”

Avicenna's references to the First as *simple intellect*, to His being more truly intellect than the forms that follow from Him, and to His knowing everything from His essence all point to a single, complete knowledge belonging to God Himself. Yet it is extraordinarily difficult to understand what that simple intellect really means. I strongly believe—in fact, I am certain—that the thinkers most famous for their skill and learning have not fully grasped it, despite claiming to have established it. I shall return to this, God willing.

These are some of the objections made against placing intelligible forms in God's essence, together with the answers, corrections, and further arguments that have occurred to us.

#### Chapter 8 — The True Account, and Problems with the View That Forms Are Imprinted in God's Essence

We do not disagree with the supporters of imprinted forms on all of their premises. We agree that the forms are necessary consequences of God's essence and are present in Him, not separate from Him. We disagree about calling them accidents and saying that their existence is mental. If they had not explicitly called them accidents, we could have read their view in the way we think correct: as eternal intelligible realities, like Plato's Forms. It is possible that calling them accidents was the later philosophers' addition, since the earliest accounts of these Ideas, such as those attributed to Anaximenes, do not describe them that way.

Here are the reasons we reject that view as it is usually stated. First, the necessary consequences of things fall into three kinds: those that follow in the mind, such as the idea of being a species in the idea of a human; those that follow in the world, such as heat from fire or motion from the heavens; and those that follow from an essence itself. A consequence of an essence follows it in both ways of existing: if the essence exists in the mind, its consequence exists mentally; if the essence exists in the world, its consequence exists in the world. It cannot exist externally while its essence exists only mentally, or the other way around.[^V06-418]

So if the consequences of God are consequences that exist in the world, they must have external existence, just as their source does.[^V06-419] The same follows if they belong to God's essence, because His essence and existence are one. These consequences must therefore be real, self-subsisting beings, not mental images or accidents. This rules out the usual view that mental images are imprinted in God.[^V06-420] But then a question remains: how can beings that stand on their own be necessary consequences of God and inseparable from Him?

Second, the philosophers say that complete knowledge of a cause gives complete knowledge of its effect, and that we know something with a cause only through knowing its cause. “Complete knowledge of the cause” does not mean merely knowing the cause's definition, unless its definition alone explains the effect. Nor does it mean knowing that it is a cause, or knowing the relation of causation. Those would give knowledge of cause and effect together. The point of this principle is to show that God knows everything through knowing Himself. So the relevant knowledge is of the specific reality that makes the cause bring about its effect—and that is a particular way of existing.[^V06-421]

We have shown that causes and effects are relations between existences, not between abstract essences. We have also shown that one cannot know a way of existing merely through an image or likeness: it must be present to the knower. This can happen through direct union or through the cause's encompassing the effect in existence.[^V06-422] God's knowledge of Himself is His own existence, and He is the cause of all other existences. So His knowledge of Himself entails His knowledge of those existences, which must be identical with the existences themselves.[^V06-423] In that sense, what He brings into being is what He knows in detail.

We call these existences God's knowledge because they are necessary consequences of His knowledge of Himself. Someone may instead call them God's knowledge in respect of their existence, and His creations in respect of their distinct meanings. That distinction may help keep God's knowledge separate from His act of creating.[^V06-424] We have also argued that acquired, image-based knowledge does not exist in a separate intellect that is free from matter and sense. A separate being knows through direct presence, not by receiving mental concepts.

Third[^V06-425], philosophers on both sides accept the principle that what proceeds first from the First Source is nobler and stronger in existence than what proceeds later. But if God's knowledge were made up of accidents imprinted in Him, those forms would be lower and weaker than the substances and things that follow them. The things known would then be more real and more complete than the knowledge through which they come to be. This is plainly wrong.

Al-Ṭūsī makes a different argument. God's existence, he says, is the cause of everything else, and God's knowledge of Himself is the cause of His knowledge of everything else. Yet God's existence and His self-knowledge are one, without any difference between them. We should therefore regard the two results—the existence of what proceeds from Him and His knowledge of that result—as one, without a real difference.[^V06-426]

I do not think this settles the issue. A cause can have a greater unity than its effect, since the effect has a weaker and less complete existence. Think of an intellect that brings forth a heavenly sphere through one aspect of its nature and another intellect through the necessity of its own existence. The cause is one thing in reality, but the two results are distinct things. In the same way, God is utterly one and simple, while the first intellect that proceeds from Him can be considered under two distinct aspects. A cause and its effect do not have to match in how many aspects or parts we can distinguish in them. What is impossible is for an effect to have a more complete unity than the single cause from which it proceeds.

Anyone who has studied the works of the divine philosopher Shihāb al-Dīn Suhrawardī will see that the distinguished commentator on Avicenna's *Pointers* says much the same thing here, perhaps taking it from Suhrawardī in a shorter, more polished form. Suhrawardī applied his principle even to bodies and bodily things: their actual presence is enough for God to know them through His illuminating light, without any extra forms. The commentator did not stop there. He based God's knowledge of these things on their forms being present in higher intellects or souls. Each view has something in its favor. I shall show how the First Reality knows these particular material things in a way that, to my knowledge, has not been established outside this book.

**An insight through illumination.** If you reflect carefully on what has been said, we will see that the divine forms are neither mental objects nor external accidents. They are simple, graded existences, untouched by possibility. They are necessary consequences of God's essence, but not in the ordinary sense of an accident inhering in a subject or an effect produced later.[^V06-427] They belong to the levels of divinity and the realm of lordship. They exist through one existence and remain through one permanence. The world, by contrast, is everything other than God: bodies, their forms and natural powers, their qualities and matter—all of which, as we have proved, come to be and change. Nothing in the world is eternal. Only God, His decree, His knowledge, His command, and His complete word are eternal. Understand this, receive it as a gift, and thank your Lord for guiding you to the truth of divine oneness.

The Aristotelians and Platonists, if they studied each other's views carefully, might find that they lead to the same account. Before that careful study, however, each view has a flaw that the other does not. The Aristotelian view makes the divine forms weak mental accidents, while the created things that follow them are stronger, more fully formed, and more real. We have seen why this is wrong. If we understand the forms instead as real existences, not mental images, they approach the Platonic Forms in this respect.

The flaw in the Platonic view is that it places God's knowledge in things outside His essence. Yet anything that exists outside God must come from Him, and must therefore be preceded by His knowledge. If God knows these Forms by other external Forms, the same question comes back without end. Or we can say they are divine forms joined to Him, necessary consequences that are not separately created but exist through His existence. On that reading, there is no need for separate Forms beyond them. If the Platonic view is freed from this flaw, it comes close to the account we have given. Perhaps Plato and Aristotle shared one view, and the differences arose from distortions in transmission, errors of interpretation, or the limits of readers who could not reach the depth of their thought. The question is distant, lofty, and subtle; minds grow tired trying to understand it, hearts are startled by it, and eyes struggle to bear its light.[^V06-428]

Al-Fārābī tried to reconcile the two schools in his book *The Reconciliation of the Views of the Two Sages*. His account is close to the truth and to what we have explained. In brief, he says that God's reality is wholly unlike anything else—not in a merely different degree, but in a nobler and higher way. When we use familiar words to describe God, such as “existent” or “living,” we must remember that their meaning in God is far above what those words mean for created things. God's existence is not like the existence of anything beneath Him; His life is not like theirs.

Once this is understood, it becomes easier to grasp what Plato, Aristotle, and their followers meant. God is living and wills this world and everything in it. So the forms of what He wills to bring about must be present in Him, though not as images or shapes in a place. His essence does not change, so what belongs to His realm is also lasting and unchanged. If the living, willing Creator had no forms or effects of things in Himself, what would He create, and what pattern would guide His creation? To deny that He creates according to knowledge would make His acts random, without purpose. This is a deeply mistaken conclusion. The philosophers' words about divine Forms should be understood in this way, not as shapes standing in places outside this world.[^V06-429][^V06-430]

Aristotle explains in his books on nature why the idea of many worlds existing alongside this one leads to contradictions. We should think carefully about the path just described when reading divine philosophy. It is highly useful and essential to the whole inquiry. Neglecting it causes serious harm. This supports the account we have given. We can now return to the remaining views on God's knowledge.

#### Chapter 9 — The View That God's Knowledge of All Things Is One

One view holds that God's essence is a single, general knowledge of all things before He brings them into existence. Many later philosophers held this view. They argued that God knows Himself, and His essence is the source from which all things come. He must therefore know all things through knowledge that belongs to His essence and comes before their existence. Otherwise, He would know them by their own essences, rather than through His own. In that case, He would lack knowledge of what is other than Himself, which would mean He lacked a perfection. They therefore said that God's knowledge of the things He creates is His being the source of those things, each distinct from the others. Since the source that makes a thing distinct is knowledge of it, God's essence is knowledge of everything other than Himself.

This argument assumes that, if something can be said of every member of a group, the statement can also be turned around without becoming false.[^V06-431][^V06-432] Some also say that knowledge of other things is contained within God's knowledge of Himself. If His knowledge of Himself is His essence, and His essence causes everything else to exist, then His knowledge of everything else is contained in His essence. It follows that His knowledge causes what comes from Him, so His knowledge is active knowledge. But when asked what it means for one kind of knowledge to be contained in another, they cannot explain it.

Know that the idea that God's essence is a single, simple intellect that is all things is true, subtle, and difficult. Its difficulty has kept even the leading philosophers, including Avicenna, from understanding it fully. To grasp it requires both deep reasoning and a clear inner vision. Without these, a thinker cannot see the realities of existence. Most of these philosophers focus on universal concepts—the subject matter of their sciences—rather than on existence itself.[^V06-433] So when they reach a question like this, their explanations falter. They ask: How can one simple thing, utterly one and undivided, be the mental forms of many different things? This seems to undermine their own rule: to know a thing, the mind must have a form that matches it and shares its nature. Yet God has no nature distinct from existence, much less many different natures.[^V06-434]

They also ask how God's knowledge alone can distinguish things, when those things do not yet exist at all.[^V06-435] Is this not like distinguishing things that are sheer nonexistence, as the Muʿtazilites claim?[^V06-436]

One reply is that a thing can be distinguished either by its particular mental form or by what gives it its own character.[^V06-437] What gives a thing its character also distinguishes it from everything else, through the very way it gives rise to that thing and its qualities. Just as a mental form becomes knowledge of a thing when it is present to the mind, so too can what makes a thing distinct be knowledge of it when present to the knower. Since one source gives rise to the whole world as it is in reality, it can distinguish every particle of existence from every other. There is no reason to rule out that this one source, when present to a knower, could be knowledge of each thing. All things would then be known through this one reality and, in this field—which is like mental existence—would be one.

Some later philosophers object that a cause differs from its effect and has a different existence.[^V06-438] So the cause's presence is not the effect's presence. Unless a thing itself is present to the knower, it cannot be known simply because it has a cause that distinguishes it. It is also a mistake to compare a cause with a mental form. A mental form is either the same in nature as the known thing, as the sounder account holds, or a likeness of it, as the weaker account says. A cause is neither the thing it causes nor an image that represents it.[^V06-439] Comparing cause and form is like comparing two different things while ignoring the difference between them.

As for the second objection, some answer that God's essence is a general knowledge of things: He knows each thing with all its own features, but not by distinguishing it from the others.[^V06-440] Knowledge and distinction are two different matters; the first does not require the second. The objection is that we know by direct insight that one reality, wholly different from many other realities, cannot know each of them in all its particular features—even if they are not distinguished from one another in the knower's awareness.

What earlier philosophers handed down on this subject—how God knows things through His essence—is the idea that knowledge of everything is contained in His knowledge of Himself, as knowledge of the features of humanity is contained in knowing human nature.[^V06-441] This is very general, though it hints at the truth we have described. We will return to it later and explain it more fully, if God wills. They sometimes offer a more detailed example by dividing human knowledge into three kinds.

The first kind consists of particular ideas and mental forms that arise one after another over time, as we move from one thought to the next. Imagination also takes part in this kind of thinking. The act of understanding is joined in some way with an image in the imagination.[^V06-442] For example, when we see something, an image of it also appears in the common sense. The two acts of perception come together, and we cannot tell which one belongs to sight and which to the common sense except by reasoning from evidence.

The second kind is a lasting ability formed by repeated learning and perception. Through it, we can call up the ideas we learned earlier whenever we wish, without having to learn them again. Yet those ideas are not always present to the mind. While the soul is occupied with guiding the body, it cannot think of everything at once, partly because imagination is involved. So all the ideas it has learned are not present to it together. It has a simple ability to call up whichever one it wants, whenever it wants, without strain. This simple state is related to every idea it could bring to mind as a readiness is related to something not yet gained; it is not like an active power producing an effect. In this state, a person is not thinking those ideas now, and the ideas themselves are not present. The person has them only as a near possibility and so knows them potentially.

The third kind arises when someone is given many questions at once. The person first grasps the answers as a whole, then works them out one by one until the answers fill the listener's ears and the written pages. At first, the person knows with certainty that the answers are all there, though their order has not yet been worked out in the mind. Then the person begins to explain them, drawing on the simple, unified understanding already present. This one simple knowledge produces the detailed answers and is nobler than they are.

They compare God's knowledge of the things that proceed from Him—and the way all things are contained in His knowledge—to this third kind. The difference is that in a human being, this simple, productive state is a learned ability added to the soul, while in God it is His very essence. When critics point out that the third kind is still only potential knowledge, though close to being actual, they reply that the person is already certain of the answers and can explain them whenever they wish. This points to something already known. It is impossible to be actually certain that a thing is present unless one knows it in some way. The questions are therefore known through this simple understanding and can be made known in another way. This simple knowledge is a state that comes to the soul from a source that moves it from potential understanding to actual understanding. Through this state, the soul forms thoughts and gains detailed knowledge. The simple state is the soul's intellectual power, like the active intellect; the details belong to the soul insofar as it is a soul. That is their account.

The problem with this account[^V06-443] is that, on its surface, it shows only that the person answering knows they can give an answer that will meet the question. They do not know the answer's actual content. The answer has a reality and nature of its own, as well as a feature by which it answers the question. The answer's reality is unknown, while the fact that it answers the question is known. This is a state between full, actual knowledge—where the details are known and distinguished from one another—and sheer potential knowledge, where detailed information is stored as an acquired ability. It is a state between the two.[^V06-444]

How can one thing, especially something utterly one and simple like God's exalted essence, know many things with different natures and distinct identities? It cannot know them directly as separate things, or we would have to distinguish things that do not exist, as already explained. Perhaps they are known only indirectly: the one simple reality is known directly, and the ideas that follow from it are known through it. For example, we know individual human beings through the general description that applies to them, and branches through their root. This differs from knowing the parts of a definition through the thing defined, since the definition and the thing defined are one in reality but different in how we think about them, as explained in our discussion of essence. Here, the difference between general and detailed knowledge lies only in how the mind knows, not in the thing known. Even if we accept this for the human example they gave, how can God's sacred essence stand to the possible things He knows as a definition stands to what it defines?[^V06-445][^V06-446] There is a great secret here. Keep examining it.

#### Chapter 10

Another view says that, at the level of His essence, God has detailed knowledge of some possible things—the First Intellect—and general knowledge of all the possible things that follow it.[^V06-447][^V06-448] He knows each effect in detail through His knowledge of the thing that comes before it, before He brings that effect into existence. In this way, He knows each later thing through the one before it, all the way to the last things in the chain. His detailed knowledge of all possible things need not be at one level; it can belong to a series of different levels. At each level, He knows the next thing in detail and knows the things that come later in a general way. On this account, God knows each effect before creating it, so His knowledge is active.

In addition to the objections already raised,[^V06-449] this view faces three more. First, it makes God's detailed knowledge of most things depend on something other than Himself. Second, it seems to make His knowledge change, moving from one known thing to another, as human knowledge does. Third, it implies that God lacks knowledge of many things at many stages of reality and that much remains hidden from Him. Reason clearly rules out this last result.

One reply to the first objection is that God's detailed knowledge of something distinct from His essence can depend on another thing without creating a problem, as long as that thing itself depends on God. Some things He brings into being depend on others, and those in turn depend on others, until the chain reaches what depends directly on Him. This does not mean God depends on anything. In truth, all things depend on Him, not on one another.

To the second objection, they reply that this is not a change over time but an order of dependence in the mind. Moving from one known thing to another is impossible for God only if it happens over time. If one thing comes before another in its very nature, without a passage of time, there is no impossibility. The same is said of the view that forms of things are present in God in an ordered chain of causes and effects, and of the views held by some theologians[^V06-450] and Abū al-Barakāt. A related view is held by some later philosophers:[^V06-451] God's knowledge of Himself is identical with His essence, while His knowledge of possible things consists in the First Intellect and the forms present in it. They chose this view to avoid the problems that follow from saying that many forms of things exist in God's essence. Someone deeply grounded in transcendent wisdom might be able to defend this view.[^V06-452] But it does not fit their usual principles. The First Intellect and all that it contains are originated in their very being, while God's knowledge is eternal because it is identical with His essence. How, then, can they be the same?

Do not accept the claim that some of God's knowledge is eternal and some of it comes to be. That is like saying that some of His power is eternal and some comes to be, or that part of His essence is eternal and part comes to be. The reality of His knowledge must be one; through that one knowledge, He knows everything, as we have explained near the beginning of this divine journey. Another objection is that God's knowledge of the First Intellect must exist before the First Intellect itself. God cannot create and give existence to what He does not know. So His knowledge of the First Intellect must be prior to it, and therefore cannot be the First Intellect. The Intellect's nature must also differ from the reality of knowledge. As we have explained, knowledge itself must exist necessarily, since its reality is existence itself. A thing's nature, by contrast, is never necessary existence in itself.[^V06-453] How, then, can the nature of the First Intellect be God's knowledge?

Our discussion has shown that God knows all things through the same knowledge by which He knows Himself, not through anything else. The First Intellect is called a substance because it has an abstract nature and a kind of existence that is added to that nature. Knowledge, especially God's knowledge, is not like that. Like existence, it is neither a substance nor an accident. These deeply grounded philosophers call the First Intellect a substance because God's necessary reality flows through it.[^V06-454] In their view, it can therefore be God's knowledge of things, though it cannot be so in the view of others.

Also, saying that the First Intellect and everything in it are God's knowledge of things undermines God's prior care for all things. That care cannot simply mean that the First Intellect is present to Him. Presence is a quality of what is present,[^V06-455] in this case the First Intellect; God's knowledge is His own quality and must be distinct from it. Presence also follows what is present,[^V06-456] and the First Intellect follows God as an effect follows its cause. But God's knowledge, with all His perfections, is prior by its very nature to every existent thing. Presence, then, cannot explain His knowledge.

**A teaching from illumination, with a further point.** Anyone who is fair-minded will see that the one who brings things into being, gives them their reality, and brings them forth through what follows necessarily from His essence knows them—with their true natures and what follows from them—before creating them. This is especially clear because He brings them into being in an ordered arrangement, the finest possible order. Otherwise, He could not give them existence. His knowledge must therefore be distinct from them and prior to them. Anyone who tries to prove God's knowledge of things by appealing to something He creates, such as an intellect or a soul, or who says that His detailed knowledge comes after His essence, has failed to see the matter clearly. In our view, the one firmly grounded in wisdom knows that God knows every thing, in all its detail, through His essence, before all that follows from Him and all that exists outside Him, without any difference of aspect in His essence. This is God's gift, which He grants to whichever of His servants He wills. God is rich in grace.[^V06-457]

#### Chapter 11 — The View That God Knows Things Through the Light That Reveals Them

Know that the master of the Illuminationist school explained God's knowledge through the principle of illumination. I followed him in this for a time, until the truth came to me and God showed me its proof. The principle is this: God knows Himself because He is light to Himself.[^V06-458] He knows the things that come from Him because they are made visible to Him by His light. They are visible either in themselves, as external substances and accidents, or through what they affect—the places where things become perceptible. Some of these are lasting, such as the intellects or souls that guide the heavenly bodies; others are not lasting, such as the animal powers of reason, imagination, and sense.[^V06-459]

On this view, God's knowledge of things is the relation between His light and what it reveals.[^V06-460] The Necessary Being needs no forms to know things. His light and power reach everywhere; nothing can hide one thing from Him while another remains visible. His knowledge and His sight are one.[^V06-461] His knowledge is understood as sight, not sight as a kind of knowledge. His light is also His power, since light gives of itself. So God's knowledge of things is the same as bringing them into being, just as their existence from Him is the same as their being present to Him. He has an active relation to everything. Through this relation, all other relations that rightly apply to Him—knowing, willing, and the rest—are established. In truth, they are all one, as we have explained. This is his view of God's knowledge. God knows without any forms at all, through direct presence. He says that every act of perception also depends on the perceiver's luminous power and on the thing's being present in the light. The soul perceives its body, its imagination and judgment, its senses, and everything it can act on—including its bodily parts and powers—through a commanding, illuminating relation. The stronger its control and illumination, the more fully it perceives them. It does not perceive them through extra forms that settle in the soul. Any form that settles in the soul is universal, even if we give it a thousand limits: it still applies to many things and can be shared. If the soul perceived its own judgment, imagination, and other powers through such forms, it would perceive only a universal body and universal powers, not its own particular body and powers or the particular actions it directs. But our direct awareness tells us otherwise. Every person perceives their own particular body and powers. The soul also uses its thinking power to separate and combine particular images, draw general ideas from individual cases, and reach conclusions from premises. Yet a particular power cannot directly see itself,[^V06-462] because something fixed in a bodily place is present to that place, not to itself, as we explained earlier. Imagination can even deny that it exists and deny the other inner powers, while still accepting their effects.

If imagination, which leads the other bodily powers, cannot perceive itself or the other inner powers, neither can the other particular senses and faculties. It is the rational soul that perceives these particular powers, the individual images present in them, and the general ideas drawn from those images. It does so by illuminating and controlling them, while being light to itself. Anything that is light to itself and has power over something else perceives both itself and that other thing. It perceives itself simply because it is light: light necessarily makes things visible; indeed, light is visibility itself. It perceives the other thing through its relation to it. The more luminous and powerful a thing is, the more fully it perceives itself and what is other than it. God is the most luminous of all beings, and creation from nothing is the highest kind of power.

The soul has only the power to move its natural body and a limited kind of influence over it, as explained elsewhere. If it created the body and its powers, its perception of them would be complete. But it does not. Souls differ in their light and power: the stronger their light and control over the body, the stronger their perception, because the body is more fully present to them. If a soul had power over another body as it does over its own, it too would perceive that body through the relation of illumination and command, without having to receive its form and be affected by it. Reception is a kind of lack; command is a kind of perfection and honor.
