We need forms to know some things—the heavens, stars, and other distant objects—because they are absent from us, so we make their forms present to ourselves. Those forms are what we actually know, since they are under our control. If those things were present to us as our tools and bodily parts are, we would not need forms to perceive them. So it is established that the soul is not absent from itself, its powers, the images within those powers, or its bodily body. It is light to itself and has some influence over these things. The Necessary Being, pure light at the highest degree, has the relation of creation to everything else—the highest kind of influence and making—and the greatest power and command. He therefore knows Himself, and all that is other than Him: intellects, bodies, their powers, and whatever appears to them or is formed within them. He knows them simply through the relation by which He brings them into being. His knowledge of Himself adds nothing to His essence, and His knowledge of things adds nothing to His relation to them.

We have also shown that every perfection belonging to a being simply because it exists belongs to God in the fullest way. If the soul can know through illumination, God must be even more able to do so. He knows Himself without anything added to Him, and knows what is other than Himself through His relation of illumination. If He needed an extra light to perceive Himself, His essence would be less luminous than that added light. If He knew things through forms present in His essence, He would both receive and produce them, which leads to the same problems already mentioned.

He says that this is enough to explain knowledge because sight depends only on the relation by which an external thing appears to the eye.[^V06-463] If someone rejects the idea that sight occurs when images of objects settle in the eye's lens or when rays leave the eye, they must admit that seeing happens simply when a lit object faces the eye. This creates an illuminating relation between the soul and the object, and nothing more. So God's relation to every visible thing is sight and perception. These many relations do not multiply His essence, and their coming to be does not change Him, as explained elsewhere. Nothing in the heavens or earth, not even an atom's weight, escapes Him. This is the path taken by this great teacher. The learned al-Tusi and others followed him, and those who came after them judged it sound. When al-Tusi, the commentator on Avicenna's *Pointers*, saw that the view of forms present in God's essence faced many objections he could not resolve, he tried another way to defend God's knowledge. He said that, just as an intellect needs no form besides itself to know itself, it needs no form besides the forms of its own effects to know those effects.[^V06-464] Consider your own experience: you understand an image you form or bring to mind. That image comes from you, though not from you alone; something outside you also plays a part. Still, you know the image itself, not another image of it, since otherwise images would multiply without end. If you can know what comes from you with the help of something else in this way, imagine how an intellect knows what comes from it alone, without anything else taking part. Not every known thing must exist inside the knower as a form. You know yourself, though you are not a container holding yourself. Being a container is one condition for receiving knowledge, but if a thing reaches you in another way, you can know it without its being contained in you. So the effects of an intellect that produces them by its own nature are present to it without being inside it.[^V06-465] It knows them through themselves.

With that in mind, consider this. The Necessary Being has no difference between His essence and His knowledge of Himself. In the same way, there is no difference between the First Effect's existence and God's understanding of it. God's knowledge of Himself causes His knowledge of the First Effect, just as His essence causes the First Effect. If you say the causes are one, then say the effects are one too. The First Effect's existence is therefore God's understanding of it, with no new form entering God's essence. You know that every immaterial being knows itself and other immaterial beings.[^V06-466] Since intellectual substances know beings that are not their effects through forms present in them, and every existent thing is an effect of God, the forms of all beings—universal and particular, exactly as they are—are present in these intellects. The First, Necessary Being knows these intellects and their forms, not through other forms but through the intellects and forms themselves. In this way, He knows existence as it is.[^V06-467] No atom in the heavens or earth escapes Him, and no impossible result follows. Once you grasp and develop this principle, you will see how God's knowledge encompasses all beings, universal and particular.[^V06-468] This is God's gift to whichever of His servants He wills. That is what this learned man says. If you consider it carefully, you will find that it is almost the same as the Illuminationist account, with only a small difference. Everyone who examined it accepted it; no one criticized it. I, however, find several problems with it. We have already mentioned some; the rest remain. There is no harm in repeating them, since a reminder can make the point clearer.

There are several objections to this view. First, it does away with God's prior care for all possible things,[^V06-469] which is shown by the remarkable order of the world. The answer that the order of this world is only a shadow of the noble relations and perfect arrangement among the separate intellects is not enough. This view gives those intellects many different levels, with higher and lower chains, intellectual forms, and relations. The beings in this world are said to follow their heavenly patterns: their forms follow the forms above them, and their relations follow those of the higher world. But even if the order here follows the order of that world, what gives that world its order? Unless God's own realm contains the best possible order and the most complete unity, we face a circle or an endless chain. Aristotle made this point in *Theology* with great clarity.

Second, knowledge is not simply a relation, whether it is called illumination or anything else.[^V06-470] Some knowledge matches reality,[^V06-471] and some does not; the latter is called ignorance. Some knowledge is simple understanding, and some is judgment. A relation cannot be divided into these kinds. The reply that logic divides only the knowledge of embodied minds—not the knowledge separate beings have of themselves or of things they know simply through presence—does not work.[^V06-472] Knowledge is one meaning and one reality. It cannot be a relation in some cases and a form in others. Every division into kinds depends on adding different features to something common; the common thing is shared among those kinds. But a relation has no independent meaning. This is clear with only a little thought.

Third, as we have explained elsewhere,[^V06-473] knowledge takes one of four forms: understanding, imagination, judgment, or sense-perception. There is no knowledge outside these kinds. On this view, either God and separate intellects must sense physical bodies and images in the outside world and the forms within our particular faculties,[^V06-474] or there must be a fifth kind of knowledge beyond the four. This objection does not apply to those who say that God or an immaterial being knows external and particular things through forms. The four kinds of knowledge are classified according to what is known in itself, not what is known only through something else.[^V06-475] This condition applies to every such classification, even when it is not stated. So, as we have explained,[^V06-476] every knower perceives material things through their perceptible forms. An immaterial being perceives them through intellectual forms. Every material, changing image has a living, perceiving form. This form is its lasting face before God and is what is known in itself. The ordinary image is also known, but through that living form.[^V06-477] Hold on to this point; it is very useful.

Fourth, a relation comes into existence after its two sides do.[^V06-478] So God's highest attribute would depend on His creatures. Fifth, we have shown in many places in this book that no natural body, material form, or bodily state can be known in itself.[^V06-479] None of them is fully present to anything except through its perceptible or intellectual likeness. That is why philosophers say such things are known through forms drawn from them, not through the things themselves. How, then, could this great teacher, al-Tusi, and those who agreed with them[^V06-480] say that material beings can be perceived without perceptible forms?

Sixth, his claim that seeing happens when a lit object faces the eye does not follow. The correct account is this: when an object faces the eye, the soul brings its visible form into being in another world. This form is joined to the soul as an intellectual form is joined to God—not by being inside the soul.[^V06-481]

Seventh, he says that imagination does not consist in forms settling in a part of the brain. Instead, the soul looks at images in the imaginal world. Yet those images are not effects of the soul, so the soul cannot control or command them.[^V06-482] This undoes the rule he gave: that a thing knows itself by being light and knows another by having power over it. By power he means causation. There can be no control without causing, and no control over what is not caused. So his account of the soul's knowledge of imagined forms falls apart.

Eighth, this view implies that God does not know anything other than Himself at the level of His essence, either in detail or in a general way.[^V06-483] He rejects general knowledge of other things in *The Temptations*. But this conclusion is impossible. How can anyone with clear insight say that the one who brings things into being and gives them their nature has no knowledge of them? Philosophers say that even the natural powers moving matter act toward ends built into their activity. An end exists after an action but comes before it in the intention. So they must have some kind of awareness of the ends they bring about. What, then, of the creator of all things? Could He bring them into being at random? These are the objections to this view.

The teacher and his followers rejected the idea of divine forms because they thought it meant that things settled inside God's essence and inside His knowledge, which is His essence. You now know that this does not follow unless one is blocked from the truth and assumes that the forms are separate from God and are accidents lodged in Him. If we understand them as the same as Him in reality and existence, but different through their limits and qualifications, there is no subject and no accident. There is one existence, differing in fullness and incompleteness, hiddenness and appearance. On the true account, the real order of things is God's knowledge, which contains the forms of all things, universal and particular, beginningless and originated. It is right to say that the real order is this divine knowledge, in which all things exist as they are. The knowers also say that things have natural, imaginal, intellectual, and divine existence. Of these, divine existence is most truly what we mean by reality. This does not mean that nonexistent things exist absolutely. The impossibility of a nonexistent thing's being established means that it is wholly cut off from existence, not that it lacks a separate existence in the world while still being realized in God's existence and appearing there.[^V06-484] More will be said about this.

If you ask how knowledge can follow what is known, when this knowledge is God's essence, we answer: this divine knowledge, like power, will, and other relational attributes, is a reality that can be considered in two ways. First, it can be considered without any difference from the One's essence. In this respect, it is an attribute of God and depends on nothing else. Things depend on it, since their existences in the world proceed from it. This is why God's knowledge is called active. Second, it can be considered in relation to things. In this respect, it follows them and takes on their number as they multiply. We will explain this fully when we discuss how God knows things according to the way of those who see the truth directly. It is better to return to the question of whether knowledge follows what is known, or the known thing follows knowledge, there.[^V06-485]

#### Chapter 12 — The Clear Truth About God's Knowledge Before All Things

This level of knowledge is called the unseen, as in God's words: “With Him are the keys of the unseen; none knows them but He.”[^V06-486] The keys are the detailed forms of things; the unseen is the level of God's pure essence, which comes before those details.[^V06-487] The purpose of this chapter is to show how God knows all things in this one, simple level—the hidden reality behind every hidden thing, simpler than anything else. We can explain this in two ways. The first follows the ancient philosophers: God is a simple intellect. The second follows the fully realized mystics: God has names and attributes in His essence.[^V06-488] These are not things that come after His essence; their relation to it is like the relation of a possible thing's nature to its existence, even though God has no nature separate from existence. Both ways are subtle and difficult. To understand them from a human teacher requires a sharp mind and a clear heart. How much more so when trying to discover them through the world of the spirit.

To explain the first way, we need to establish several principles.

**First principle.** Every particular existent is the real ground for certain general meanings at its own level of existence. Outside God, philosophers call these meanings an “essence”; mystics call them a “fixed entity.” There may be one such meaning or many. A single meaning is called a simple essence. Several meanings may all exist through one existence, as with blackness; this is called a simple thing in the world. Or they may exist through several existences, as in the essence of an animal: its broader kind comes from its matter—a living, growing body—and its defining feature comes from its form—the power to sense.

The matter and form must be united in some way in existence. They cannot be like a stone placed next to a human being; otherwise, the essence would not be one. The form gives the thing its unity, not the matter, because the form is more fully realized than matter and gives matter its reality, as explained elsewhere. The form is the source of unity because, for example, the form “sensitive” is itself the ground for all the meanings that apply to an animal, including “living body.” It is not that the meaning “living body” is a part of the essence. If it were, the supposed defining feature would itself be a species, and the supposed broader kind would be a broader kind of that species, while the defining feature would also be both a dividing and a defining part.[^V06-489] Nor is the broader kind meaning an added feature that belongs to the form. The basic form, in its own reality, is the ground for the broader kind meaning. The familiar claim that the broader kind is an “accident” of the defining feature does not mean it is an external feature, like a person's possible ability to laugh or write. It means only that the broader kind concept is not part of the meaning of the form, since the form itself has no essence separate from its existence. What are called basic forms—which match the forms predicated in logic—are each a particular kind of external existence.[^V06-490] Existence has no definition or essence of its own. So when a broader kind meaning applies to this kind of existence, it is not an external feature with an existence of its own. The broader kind meaning is present within the existence of the form. The difference between broader kind and form appears only when we analyze them in thought. This is what philosophers mean when they say the broader kind is an “accident” of the form: the broader kind concept and the form concept differ, but both exist through the one existence of the form.

The point is that several distinct meanings, which in other things may exist through several existences, can exist through one existence. The final form of a human being, the rational soul, is an example. Through the soul's one existence, we find the meanings “substance,” “receptive,” “physical body,” “growing body,” “sensitive,” and “rational.” Some of these meanings also exist through other forms in other kinds of being. “Substance,” “receptive,” “physical body,” “growing body,” and “sensitive” all exist through one form in a nonrational animal. Some of these meanings exist through one form in a plant, and some through one form in an inanimate thing. “Substance,” “receptive to dimensions,” and “physical by nature” exist through one form in an elemental body. “Substance with dimensions” exists through one form in an extended body. The meaning “substance” also applies to matter itself. We see, then, that many things which exist through different existences can also exist through one existence.

**Second principle.** The stronger and more complete a thing's existence, the more meanings it can encompass and the more perfections of other things it can gather within its simplicity.[^V06-491] We see this in the stages by which forms become more complete. A series of forms successively appears in matter as it becomes ready for each new form. The final form performs all the functions of the earlier forms, because its stronger existence contains the principles of all their actions while remaining one.

**Third principle.** A thing's having a general meaning does not mean that its special existence is the same as the existence of that meaning. A thing's own existence is the existence through which it is distinct from other meanings outside its definition. A human being's existence is not merely the existence of an animal, even though it includes the meaning of “animal.” Nor is an animal's existence the existence of a plant, even though it includes the meaning of “plant.”

Do not assume that, just as a human being has a further perfection beyond animal life,[^V06-492] every kind of animal must also have a further, higher perfection added to animal life. The defining feature of kinds below human beings need not be an additional perfection in their existence. Sometimes the actual strength of a general meaning prevents a thing from receiving another perfection. As we explained in our discussion of essence, one general meaning can serve as the meaning of a species or of a broader kind. This difference is not simply a matter of taking the same meaning “without a condition,” so that it is a broader kind, or “with a condition,” so that it is a species. That is a correct way to describe the concepts, but the real basis of the difference lies in the strength and weakness of existence.

One meaning may have a weak existence and another a strong one. The weaker existence can be absorbed into the stronger, moving from its own limited existence to the existence of what is more complete. For example, a growing body may exist fully as a plant, with strong powers of nourishment, growth, and reproduction, as in a tree. It is then complete as a plant and cannot pass into another kind with the added power of sensation. By contrast, a growing body that exists in a more delicate form, within animal matter, can develop into a higher kind. What is a definite species in plant life becomes an incomplete, general meaning in animal life, ready to take on a more complete form. The same applies to animals in relation to human beings, and to each broader kind in relation to the species below it. So the whole reality of one thing can form part of the reality of another.

**Fourth principle.** Whenever a perfection of existence is present in something, its source must possess that perfection in a higher and more complete way. This is clear in the words of the teacher of the philosophers in Aristotle’s school in *Theology* and is supported by reason, sound insight, and direct experience. Every aspect of existence in an effect depends on the cause that gives it existence, and ultimately on the first cause. The cause contains all good things, without the limitations, deficiencies, and absences that belong to effects at lower levels.

With these principles established, we can say that God is the source from which all realities and essences flow.[^V06-493] His essence must therefore, in its simplicity and unity, be all things.[^V06-494] We have proved in our discussion of intellect and the intelligible that a truly simple existence must be all things. To see that proof, return to that discussion. Since God's existence is the existence of all things in this way, whoever understands that existence understands all things. That existence is itself intellect, knower, and known. The Necessary Being knows Himself through Himself; His knowledge of Himself is knowledge of everything else. His knowledge of Himself comes before the existence of everything else, so His knowledge of everything else also comes before those things. We have shown that God knows all things through His essence, before anything other than Him exists, whether the things are intellectual forms present in His essence or forms separate from it. This is complete, detailed knowledge in one respect and general knowledge in another. The known things are many and distinct in meaning, but all exist through one simple existence. In this divine view, everything appears without multiplicity: everything is one within unity.

If you object that this would give the Necessary Being an essence, so He would not be pure existence, remember that reason has proved that God is pure existence without an essence, since anything that has an essence depends on a cause. We have already answered this objection. By “essence” here we mean a thing's limited nature, which includes certain things and excludes many others.[^V06-495] To say that a thing has an essence, or an existence added to its essence, means that it needs something else in order to exist and does not exist at every level of reality. There must therefore be some level of reality at which it does not yet have its own particular existence, even though its nature can exist there as a possibility. Every possible thing has such a level. This is what we mean when we say that a possible thing has an essence and that its existence is added to it.

God, by contrast, has no limited boundary in existence and no restricted nature that excludes many things. There is no level of reality at which He does not exist. To say that He is pure existence means that no nature is found in Him apart from existence and its fullness. All His aspects are one: necessary existence. His existence has no limit, since He is beyond measure in strength and perfection. Each level of His existence is beyond measure in the effects and actions that proceed from it. No place or thing is empty of Him—land or sky, sea or shore, heaven or earth. If His existence had an end, there would be a boundary: existence on one side and something other than existence on the other. He would then not be truly one and would have a limited nature. Everything returns to God.

**A further explanation.** God's knowledge of things at the level of His essence does not consist of forms added to Him and different from Him. Rather, many unlimited meanings share the rule of necessary existence, without God's existence becoming the particular existence of any one of them or of all of them.[^V06-496] As we said in the principles above, God is the manifestation of each meaning. There is a difference between existence itself belonging to a particular nature and existence revealing that nature. The existence belonging to a nature is the existence that distinguishes it from everything else. Think of the human soul: through its one existence, many kinds of meaning apply to it, but it does not become those kinds in the limited way they exist elsewhere. It reveals them and makes their qualities known.

Consider another example. In a quality such as blackness or heat, degrees of strength and weakness are different kinds, according to the philosophers. Yet one continuous change from weak to strong passes through infinitely many degrees, each of which can be understood as a different kind of that quality. There is only one continuous existence through all these degrees. The process of growth can reach a final, complete state that includes all the weaker states, just as a long line contains all shorter lines while remaining one line. Strong blackness—whether it develops gradually or appears at once—contains the meaning of weaker blackness, while existing as one. The reason in all such cases is that the existence particular to one thing is not the same as the existence through which that thing appears or is represented. The many kinds included in strong blackness and in a single process of change each have a particular existence when they appear separately. The same is true when the human reality includes many essences. This is not a case of something existing only as a possibility, as some suppose. Those meanings are actual, in a higher and more complete way than their separate existences. “Possible” here means that they do not exist through their own particular forms, but through one existence that is their source and completion. The divine knowledge of things has the same kind of unity: all things are actually present through God's single, all-encompassing existence.

The truth is that possible kinds remain distinct only when each comes to exist through its own particular existence, when its own qualities apply to it and its own effects follow from it. Before that, all things have a shared, unified existence. This is another kind of existence, higher and nobler than intellectual, imaginal, or external existence. It is not a mere possibility. If we use the word “potential” here,[^V06-497] it does not mean a readiness or possibility, whether near or distant. It means only that things do not yet exist through their separate forms but instead exist together through the existence of their source and completion. This does not mean that nonexistent things exist. It would mean that they were nonexistent only if they were cut off both from existence of their own and from the existence of their source and completion.

If, as we have shown, God understands all things as they are through one simple act of understanding, why must we also establish intellectual forms, whether they are present in Him or separate from Him?[^V06-498] And if His essence reveals realities that have different existences in the world, what proof is there for the First Intellect from the principle that the highest source is one in every respect, without different aspects? Have we not just established many meanings in God's essence?

We answer: intellectual forms follow from His knowledge of Himself, which entails knowledge of His immediate effect; knowing that effect entails knowing the effect that follows from it, and so on.[^V06-499] Since God's essence causes things through their existence, knowledge of the cause entails knowledge of its effects as effects. These things must be known in the order in which they proceed, one after another. This is different from knowing them through the higher, simple unity in which they are not considered as effects.[^V06-500] As for why the First Effect must be one and simple, even though God manifests all possible meanings, many titles do not undermine the unity of the thing they describe. The different aspects that make a thing composite are those that differ in existence, not merely in the effects or meanings that apply to it. The first kind includes potentiality and actuality, before and after.

The first kind also includes cause and effect, mover and moved. The second kind includes knowledge and power, knower and known, existence and individuation, unity and existence, and similar aspects. One reality can be the ground for these different meanings without anything else being added to it. In God, there is no aspect that differs from necessary existence. Everything that can be said of Him is said because He is necessary existence—not because of possibility, composition, limitation, dependence, delay, or weakness.

You may ask: Why, then, do philosophers say that something made up of a broader kind and a feature distinguishing it has parts, even in the mind? A human being, for example, is an animal that can reason; blackness is a color that darkens the sight. The answer is that, in natural things, these different meanings correspond to different ways of existing. Animal life can exist at a lower level where it cannot yet be joined with reason. The same living thing may first exist at that level, unable to reason, and then change into a stronger, more complete kind of existence in which it can reason. Because these ways of existing can be separated, the being that has them is made up of distinguishable aspects and powers. This is why philosophers studying nature distinguish several powers in the soul, including powers that can oppose one another.

Anyone who wants to understand divine wisdom[^V06-501] must grasp that beings exist at different levels, and that existence has different realms—divine, intellectual, psychic, and natural. Some are more complete and noble; others are lower and less complete. Each realm has qualities and consequences that fit it. The higher and stronger a realm is, the closer its beings are to unity and wholeness. The lower and weaker it is, the more they tend toward multiplicity, separation, and opposition. Many opposing natures in the natural world—the lowest of the worlds—are not opposed in the world of the soul. Black and white, hot and cold, are opposites in physical existence, but they can both exist in one imagined form. Likewise, things that differ in the soul's world exist together in the world of intellect. As the teacher of the philosophers says in *Theology*: “The human being perceived by the senses is an image of the intellectual human being. The intellectual human being is spiritual, and all its parts are spiritual. The place of the eye is no different from the place of the hand, and the parts are not in separate places. They are all in one place.” If this is so, what do you think of the divine realm, where all things are united? There, all things are one, and through its unity it is all things, without any difference of aspect that would divide it.

This is supported by another passage in *Theology*: “In intellect, all perfections are always present, not present sometimes and absent at other times. They are always there. Though they are eternal in intellect, it receives them from the First Cause. The First Cause has these perfections in a higher way. It is not a container for them; it is all the perfections. They flow from it without being divided or moved, or resting in any place. It is a simple reality from which particular realities and perfections spring. They exist in every moment through a kind of power of the moment.”[^V06-502][^V06-503] The intellect receives these perfections more fully than the soul; the soul more fully than the heavenly bodies; and the heavenly bodies more fully than the bodies that come to be and pass away. The more intermediaries stand between an effect and the First Cause, the less fully it receives from that cause.

Elsewhere, *Theology* says: “The pure One is the cause of all things. He is not like any of them. He is their beginning, but He is not the things themselves. All things are in Him, and He is not in any of them. They all flow from Him; through Him they are established and sustained, and to Him they return. If someone asks how all things can flow from the simple One, who contains no duality or multiplicity, I answer: He is perfectly one and simple and contains none of these things. Because He is perfectly one, all things flow from Him. Since He has no identity of His own, all identities flow from Him.”

The text continues: “In brief, because He was none of the things, I saw all things come from Him. Though all things flow from Him, the first identity to flow from Him, without an intermediary, is the identity of intellect. Through the identity of intellect, all the identities in the higher and lower worlds flow from Him.”

It also says: “All things are in the First Intellect because the First Agent first brought intellect into being. He made it with many forms and placed in each form all things suited to it. He brought the form and its states into being together, not one after another. He created the intellectual human being with all its suitable qualities at once, not some qualities first and others later, as happens with the human being perceived by the senses.”

Another passage says that natural things depend on one another. When one passes away, it returns to what is above it, rising through the heavenly bodies, then the soul, then the intellect. All things are fixed in intellect, and intellect is fixed in the First Cause. The First Cause is the beginning and end of all things; from Him they come and to Him they return, as we have often said. He also says: “All things are from intellect, and intellect is all things. When intellect exists, things exist; if things did not exist, intellect would not exist.”[^V06-504][^V06-505] Intellect is all things because it contains all their qualities, and each quality in it brings about the thing that belongs to it. Every quality in intellect matches something else.

These statements clearly say that all things are in God, without undermining His unity. The statement “He is not in any of the things” does not conflict with our account. What is denied is that things are in Him with the particular existence that makes each thing distinct from the others. What is affirmed is that they exist in the one, divine existence that is the source and completion of every thing. A thing is more fully itself when it exists through its completion than when it exists in an incomplete form. An animal is more fully animal when it exists in a human, rational form than when it exists without reason. A human being exists more fully in the intellectual world than in the world of the senses, because the intellectual human being is the complete form of the sensory one. The same is true of the divine human being—the human reality present in the divine realm—and of everything else. In the divine realm, each thing is more fully itself than it is in the lower realm, because divine existence completes every existent thing, just as existence itself completes every essence.

This is the fullest account of this question, and perhaps no book has explained it before. Reader, understand its value and place it with the other rare insights in this book.

The second way—the way of the fully realized mystics—is close to the way of the firmly grounded philosophers. Here is how it works. Every being has names and attributes that follow from its existence. By “names” we do not mean words such as “knowing” or “powerful”; in this usage, those are names of names.[^V06-506] Nor do we mean by “attributes” qualities added to God's essence, as though they were accidents.[^V06-507] We mean general concepts, including the meanings of essences. Philosophers also use “attribute” to include an essence.[^V06-508] For example, in logic a descriptive phrase means a general concept that can be predicated of a subject, whether it names something essential or accidental. In *Theology*, the claim that intellect contains the attributes of all things means that it contains their essences, in contrast to their particular existences.

In this usage, “what follows necessarily” includes both essential and nonessential features. The difference between a name and an attribute in mystical language is like a distinction philosophers make between “one thing”—for example, one line—and “oneness” itself.[^V06-509] It is a difference in how we consider the matter, like the difference between a simple and a composite description.

Everything that exists in its own right can truly be described in many ways, if we set aside features that come to it, whether necessarily or only for a time. These descriptions refer to one existing reality. What belongs to its nature can be described in several general ways, but the thing itself has one individual existence. Anyone with insight can see that the more complete and strong an existence is, the more perfections it has and the more concepts can describe it. At every level of perfection, it has particular effects that flow from it. Each effect gives rise to a meaningful concept that applies to its source. Any concept that applies to something because of an aspect in its own reality belongs to it in the way that essences and essential qualities do: they exist through the existence of that thing. Whoever knows that single existence as it is knows everything that can truly be said of it in the same act of understanding, without needing another act of knowledge for each one.

Since God's simple essence contains every good and perfection, and is the source of every action, good, and perfection, every perfection found in something He creates can be understood as a concept that applies to Him. A single essence can be described by many concepts with different meanings.[^V06-510] In this usage, the essence considered together with one of these concepts is called a “name.” The concept itself is called an “attribute.” All these names and attributes are fixed in God's essence before anything comes from Him. “Before” here means that they are present in Him as His essence is present to itself, but only indirectly. The same is true of the relations and connections between the names and attributes and the things through which they appear. These are what the mystics call “fixed entities,” which, they say, have never tasted existence. They mean that the things do not exist through themselves; existence is not an added quality in them, nor something they possess as a separate reality. They are not accidents belonging to existence, and existence is not an accident belonging to them.[^V06-511] They are not made by existence or caused by it. They are not self-existing, eternal things either. They are fixed from eternity through the uncaused existence of the One, just as a possible thing's nature is fixed through the existence God gives it—not as something made in itself, but as something made through the existence of the thing. These names, attributes, and all that they refer to[^V06-512] are fixed entities, not created separately. Though they do not have their own particular existences from eternity, they are all one with God's necessary existence. That is enough to show they were never absolutely nonexistent, without accepting the Muʿtazilite claim that nonexistent things are real.

With this distinction established, we can say that God's knowledge of Himself is His existence. The fixed entities exist through His existence,[^V06-513] so they are also known through one act of knowing—His knowledge of Himself. Though many in meaning, they are known through one knowledge, just as they exist through one existence. For God and His knowledge are one. His knowledge of all possible things is established in His essence before they exist. It is active knowledge: the cause of their existence in the world.

God's knowledge of Himself is His existence, and that same existence is knowledge of things. It is also the cause of their existence outside Him. Their stages of existence begin with intellectual forms, continue as imaginal forms, then become natural forms, and finally take on external material existence. This is the lowest stage. God knows them through one existence before bringing them into being,[^V06-514] and knows them again after their creation. Through the same knowledge, He knows them both before and after their coming to be.[^V06-515] With one vision, He sees them in their original unity and later in their multiplicity. This is the soundest account of the mystics' view, made consistent with the philosophers' principles of reasoning. Yet because the mystics devote themselves to spiritual practice and discipline rather than debate and formal reasoning, they may not be able to explain their insights in a teaching form, or may not take care to present them as proofs. Their words therefore often invite objections. Only someone who understands their purpose through both strong reasoning and long spiritual practice can improve and clarify them.

**A reminder and further explanation.** As we explained earlier, when we say that God's attributes are identical with His essence, we mean that His essence and existence are beyond all attributes, limits, and concepts—even the concepts of “essence,” “existence,” and “reality.” No name or description points to Him as He is in Himself. This is the level of absolute oneness, the unseen behind all unseen. When we consider the meanings of the names and attributes—which in one respect are also identical with Him—we speak of the level of divinity and unity. There, distinctions appear: knowledge differs from power, and power from will; the attributes multiply, then the names multiply, and their forms and manifestations are distinguished. The divine realities exist through one divine existence, but differ in meaning, just as intellectual forms and Platonic realities, which correspond to them and depend on them, differ through their particular existences. There is the level of gathering, and the absolute oneness is the level of gathering all gatherings. At this level there is distinction. As these realities descend into the forms of the soul, they become more divided. That division continues until they reach a level where existing carries the possibility of ceasing to exist, and being carries the possibility of decay. What remains in being does so through continual change, and what is joined can come apart. Unity now includes multiplicity, as in number, or the capacity to become many, as in continuous size.

In brief, real kinds such as human beings, horses, animals, trees, stones, gold, silver, earth, air, fire, the heavens, the sun, and the moon each have different ways and levels of existence.[^V06-516] The higher and nobler the level, the earlier its existence, the stronger its unity, the more it contains all that is below it, the greater its wholeness, and the clearer its light and effects. This continues until existence reaches a level free from every lack, even the possibility of nonexistence. There, things that can destroy one another are reconciled, opposites embrace, and many things become one. All are present through one existence and known through one knowledge. The Prophet Muhammad is reported to have said of this level: “I have a time with God when neither a near angel nor a sent prophet can contain me.” The word “time” is used rather than “station” to distinguish the rank of prophethood from the rank of spiritual friendship. A claim to prophethood would not fit that station; the claim to a moment does.

**A teaching point.** One reality can have different degrees of existence: natural, psychic, intellectual, and divine. Knowledge, for example, is one reality. It can be an accident when a soul knows something other than itself; it can be a spiritual substance when the soul knows itself; it can be an intellectual substance when an intellect knows itself; and it can be neither substance nor accident but something beyond both, as with God's knowledge of Himself and all things.[^V06-517] Power is similar. In animals it can be an accident, a quality of the soul. In intellects it can be a substance, since their power is their existence, even if it is not their essence. As you know, the existence of a substance is itself substantial. In God, power is necessary existence and reaches every thing.

Once you see that one meaning, such as knowledge or power, can have different levels of existence, some stronger and nobler than others, apply this to all realities. A human being, for example, exists as a mental form when the mind imagines the human nature; as a physical body in the natural world; as a spiritual body in the next world; as an intellectual form, as Plato taught and we have explained; and as a divine reality in God's knowledge. The same is true of other things.

This also shows what is wrong with the common view held by Aristotle’s followers, stated clearly by Bahmanyār in *The Book of Attainment*: the mental forms in God's knowledge are accidents that exist in His essence. On this view, the human being, the heavens and earth, the stars, the elements, and everything that comes from them would all be accidents. Their existence would be among the lowest forms of existence. These philosophers say that God's essence is the subject in which these things exist, in their weakest and lowest form. Yet a thing that is near something noble and high should itself have a level of nobility and height. How could these celebrated philosophers make God a subject for things in their lowest form? They have missed the truth in this matter.

**A quotation that supports the point.** The realized knower Muḥyī al-Dīn Ibn Arabi says in chapter 377 of *The Meccan Revelations*:

> Know, may God support you, that it is impossible for there to be something known whose judgment is not its own essence but another intelligible thing.[^V06-518] If there were, no single thing would be one in reality. There would be no unity behind the many. Everything would be composite; the least kind of composition would be a thing together with what is said of it. So a unity with no multiplicity is impossible.
>
> Know, however, that the essential composition[^V06-519] required by the Necessary Being does not have the flaw that thinkers imagine. That flaw belongs to possible things, whose different kinds of composition[^V06-520] require something to determine them.[^V06-521] For example, a thing that can take one particular shape rather than another needs something beyond its own capacity to determine the shape.[^V06-522] But the Necessary Being's essential composition is not of this kind. We have never found an intellect that could prove that no judgment can be made about Him. The greatest thinkers, who have gained fame through reason but have no share in faith, have called Him pure intellect, yet still judged Him to be a cause. They did not make His oneness complete when they called Him a cause. Other thinkers called Him by relations,[^V06-523] saying that He is knowing and powerful through knowledge and power. Still others said that He has added attributes[^V06-524] called life, knowledge, power, will, speech, hearing, and sight. Through these attributes, He is called living, knowing, powerful, willing, speaking, hearing, and seeing. All the divine names, in their meanings, fall under these eternal attributes.

The purpose of quoting Ibn Arabi is to show that, through inner insight, he saw that many meanings and concepts do not undermine the unity of existence. The fact that concepts differ in their simplest meaning does not require them to differ in real existence. Many essential predicates do not undermine perfect unity. By “possible composition” he means that things which have no composition in their simple divine existence may become externally or mentally composite when they exist in a limited, possible form, as when a broader kind and a defining feature make up an essence. This is the point we established earlier.

He says elsewhere, explaining the words “We gave him wisdom and a clear way of speaking”: “Possible things are distinct in themselves while they do not exist. God knows them as they are in themselves, sees them, and commands them to come into being. They then exist in the world through His command. Nothing is general in God's knowledge, just as nothing is general in the fixed entities. Everything is detailed in itself and in God's knowledge. Generality arises only in us and in our way of knowing. Whoever sees detail within what appears general—whether through knowledge, direct sight, or insight—has been given wisdom and the ability to speak clearly. This belongs only to the prophets, messengers, and their true heirs. Philosophers' wisdom, by contrast, is borrowed: they do not see detail within what is general.”[^V06-525]

I say: his words show that it is a great insight to know that, in His essence, God contains the detailed realities of all possible things. Only those fully grounded in knowledge can grasp it. Praise be to God, who has guided us to this. Without His guidance, we would never have found the way. It is possible that God's words, “A Book whose verses are made firm, then set out in detail by One who is wise and all-aware,” point to these two levels of knowledge. The same is true of “We gave him wisdom and a clear way of speaking.” “Wisdom” refers to the verses: the possible things, which are signs of God, exist together in a complete, unified way. They are a book because their meanings are gathered together. Their being set out in detail refers to their separate existence as possibilities and their clear expression in words.

For these two levels of knowledge, God's book is called both the Qur'an and the Criterion. The Qur'an is wisdom: simple intellect and general knowledge, in the philosophers' terms. The Criterion is the book set out in detail and clear speech: detailed intellect and the soul's knowledge as it moves from one form to another. The other books sent down to the prophets are not called the Qur'an, nor are they called God's speech, for this subtle reason. Their knowledge came from the heavenly pages of the spiritual world, according to the rank they held there. But the final Messenger sometimes received knowledge directly from God, in the moment expressed by “I have a time with God,” without Gabriel or any other near angel. God knows best.[^V06-526]

#### Chapter 13 — The Levels of God's Knowledge of Things

God's care and decree[^V06-527] are also called the Mother of the Book and destiny, and the Book of Erasure and Confirmation, as in God's words: “God erases what He wills and confirms what He wills, and with Him is the Mother of the Book.” Their place is the Tablet and the Pen. The Tablet receives and is affected; in this account, it has two levels. The Pen acts and preserves. The Illuminationists deny God's prior care, while the Aristotle’s followers, including Avicenna and those who follow him, affirm it—but they treat it as forms added to God's essence like accidents.

You now know the problem with that view. The truth is that God's prior care is His knowledge of things[^V06-528] at the level of His essence,[^V06-529] free of possibility and composition. It is His existence itself, in which beings in the possible world are revealed in the most complete order, an order that leads to their existence in the world and matches it fully. He does not arrive at this through deliberation or planning. This knowledge is simple, necessary in itself, and present in His essence.[^V06-530] It gives rise to detailed intellectual and psychic knowledge, not as things contained in Him but as things that proceed from Him.

Aristotle’s followers define decree[^V06-531] as the intellectual forms of all beings, which flow from God at once, without time, through creation, because those forms are part of the world and are separate from Him.[^V06-532] In our view, they are forms of knowledge that necessarily follow His essence, without being created or produced through an act. They are not parts of the world, since they contain no aspect of nonexistence or real possibility. God's decree is His knowledge in forms that are eternal in themselves and remain through God's permanence, as we have explained.

Destiny[^V06-533] means that the forms of things exist in the psychic world of the heavens in particular, individual shapes,[^V06-534] matching the things in their external matter. These forms depend on their causes, follow necessarily from them, and belong to specific times and places. God's decree encompasses them, just as His care encompasses His decree.

As for the Tablet and the Pen, here is how to understand them. The first thing to flow from the exalted Creator is a sacred, luminous, and splendid substance. Through it, other sacred substances come into being, each lower in nobility, perfection, and light according to its distance from God. From these, through their dependence, lack, and weaker light, come psychic and natural beings: the souls of the heavens, the heavenly bodies, and the elements and compounds they contain. These all come into being and change over time, like written words made from intelligible words. The commanding lights, by contrast, are free of time and change. Their levels of nobility and light differ, yet they are so closely joined that they seem to form one existence. In truth, they are one and many,[^V06-535] as we have proved elsewhere. So they may be named with a single word, such as “the Pen,” in the Qur'an's “Nūn. By the Pen and what they write.”[^V06-536] The verse uses the plural form for rational beings even though it names one Pen, to point to its united wholeness. The same is true of “the Spirit,” as in “The Spirit is from the command of my Lord” and “His Word, which He gave to Mary, and a Spirit from Him”; of “the Command,” as in “Our command is but one”; and of “the Word,” as explained earlier.[^V06-537]

At other times, these realities are named in the plural: “the complete words of God,” as in the Prophet's prayer, “I seek refuge in the complete words of God from the evil of what He has created”; “the keys,” as in “With Him are the keys of the unseen”; or “the treasuries,” as in “There is nothing whose treasuries are not with Us.” They receive different names according to how we consider them. They are called the Pen because they shape forms of knowledge in the receptive powers of souls and bodies, forms that change and pass away. They are called the Command because they affect what lies below them, as a spoken command informs the one addressed. They are commanding lights that affect what lies below them through God's power. Their existence comes from God and depends on Him; in the unity of God they are nothing apart from Him. Their power over other things is a reflection of God's power, and their light—which adds nothing to their own being—is a flash of His face and beauty. They cast shadows that stretch across external time and space, even though, as we have shown, these lights themselves are beyond time and space.

God gives them the forms and realities of things, and also the secondary qualities and perfections that help things overcome their weaknesses. In this respect, or because they lead things toward their perfections and preserve them once they reach them, these realities are called the World of Power. They reflect God's quality of overwhelming power; they are His decree and command, His speech and complete words, the keys to His mercy, the treasuries of His knowledge and generosity, and the all-seeing eyes mentioned in “Build the Ark under Our eyes and by Our revelation.” All these names describe aspects of the divine Pen.

The Preserved Tablet is the universal soul of the heavens, especially the outermost sphere. Everything that happens or will happen in the world is written and fixed in the heavenly souls, which know what follows from their movements, as we explained in our discussion of bodies and will explain again when we discuss first principles and ends. Just as a pen makes visible marks on a physical tablet, the Active Intellect writes ordered forms of knowledge in the universal soul. These forms are universal and known through their causes. The universal soul is the place where they are written. It is called the Preserved Tablet because its forms are continually preserved in God's treasuries in a simple, intellectual way, or because it is united with the Active Intellect. It is not called preserved because of its own psychic existence. As we have explained, every soul, nature, or power connected to natural bodies comes to be and changes as long as it exists through this created existence.

Particular forms, each with its own shape and measured qualities at a particular time, are then imprinted in the souls that receive the heavenly motions. They resemble the forms that appear in external matter. Since these images are particular, they change and follow one another, each with its own individual features. This differs from the Preserved Tablet, where the forms remain ordered and stable like universal principles. These souls are the Tablets of Destiny, containing erasure and confirmation. Their world is the world of universal imagination and images, and each is a clear book, as God says: “No grain in the darkness of the earth, no fresh thing or dry thing, but it is in a clear Book.” The first is the Preserved Tablet; change in it is called the Mother of the Book. The second is the Book of Erasure and Confirmation, as in: “God erases what He wills and confirms what He wills, and with Him is the Mother of the Book.”

Are material things, in their dark, formless matter, also a level of knowledge, as some suppose? The truth is that this is a false idea. We have proved in several places that this existence is not a form of perception. It is a mistake to claim that the particular forms in external matter are the final level of God's knowledge or that universal matter, which contains them, is the “Book of Existence.” Anyone who says this seems to have forgotten what philosophy teaches: all knowledge and perception involve some separation from matter. These forms are immersed in matter, mixed with nonexistence and darkness, and change from one moment to the next. It would make sense to say they are known indirectly through mental forms that match them. To know them, we must have other forms of perception connected to them in some way.

**Further explanation.** These higher worlds are divine books and pages. The hand of Mercy first writes them with a luminous pen in the Preserved Tablet as intellectual signs. Only those purified of the veils of darkness and the heaviness of bodily things can touch their secrets. God says of the Qur'an: “It is a noble Qur'an in a hidden book; none touches it but the purified, a sending-down from the Lord of the worlds.” Then the noble recording angels write them in honored, exalted, and purified pages, “in the hands of messengers, noble and righteous.” These books are the sources of all divine books. Their branches include every conscious being and power: souls, animal powers, imagination, and all the other senses and faculties. The complete human being is a book that gathers all these books, since it is a copy of the greater world. In spirit and intellect, the human being is an intellectual book. In the heart—the rational soul[^V06-538]—it is the Preserved Tablet. In the animal soul, especially imagination, it is the Book of Erasure and Confirmation.

The Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī, pointed to this when he said: “You are the clear Book whose signs reveal what is hidden.” Know that when the human soul is perfected and reaches its full development, it rises through its stages and changes until it reaches the higher world. It then becomes a divine book, as in God's words: “The record of the righteous is in ʿIlliyyūn.” But if the soul loses its way, follows its desires and ignorance, and burns with passion, its imaginative power turns it into a devilish book filled with lies, false arguments, and nonsense. A book of this kind should be cast into the Fire, as God says: “The record of the wicked is in Sijjīn.”

The perceptions of other animals[^V06-539] are their books and records of their actions. In this world, they are consumed by the fire of nature. To those who see beneath the surface, nature's power over bodies—especially plants and animals—to change, melt, digest, and break them down is a burning fire. It changes them, separates them, and refines their parts into powers and spirits, as ordinary fire melts and changes things. In the next world, the fire of anger, envy, hatred, hostility, and resentment burns within hard hearts and disobedient souls. Those who see the forms of the next world know these as real fires and flames, already hidden within the souls of the wicked and consuming them. They erase the false beliefs and blameworthy views written in the soul's book. This is how we should understand the claim that the books of souls are in the Fire, not as those do who cling to the outward meaning of every image and can see only the senses. God says: “We give these examples to people, but only those who know understand them.”

Truth-seeker, do not imagine God's Pen and Tablet as inert tools: a pen made of reed or iron, and a smooth tablet made of wood or emerald, like those used by people. Just as God's essence and attributes cannot be measured against created beings, His Pen, Tablet, Book, and writing are unlike theirs. If you look at the meanings of these words and remove details that do not belong to their core meaning, you will see that such features as reed, wood, or ink are not part of the basic meaning. Writing means giving form to realities, whatever the form may be. A tablet means a thing able to receive that form, whether it is a visible body, an unseen body, or something that is not a body at all, such as a spirit or soul.

Once you understand this, take these words in the way that fits the divine realm, not in the way that fits created things. Stand firm, follow guidance, and do not follow desire.

**An example.** The complete human nature is created in the image of the All-Merciful and contains levels of knowledge that mirror God's. An action begins hidden in the simple intellect, concealed so deeply that it is barely noticed. It then descends to the heart and soul, where the person calls it to mind through thought. At this level, the person forms general concepts and major premises for reasoning about a particular matter; this gives rise to the decision to act. Next, the action descends to imagination, where it takes a particular, individual form. This is where we form particular ideas and minor premises. Together with the general premises, they produce a particular judgment and a firm intention to act.

Then the body's limbs move to bring the intention into the world. In the same way, events in the natural world pass through four levels. The first is like general knowledge; the second is like the forms of decree, located in the rational soul and written by the simple intellect; the third is like the images in the sky, located in imagination; and the fourth is like forms that come to be in material things. The first level comes only through general knowledge and will. The second comes through particular knowledge and will joined to the general. Their agreement or conflict produces a particular judgment, which leads to a firm resolve to show the action. The limbs and tools then move, and the action appears.

The movement of the limbs through nerves is like the movement of the heavens. The rational mind's rule in the brain is like the rule of the universal spirit in the Throne. The appearance of a person's inner heart in the physical heart is like the appearance of the universal soul in the sphere of the sun, since the sun is the world's heart as the heart is the body's. The Throne is like the brain.[^V06-540] God surrounds and encompasses everything. Of these four levels, the first three belong to knowledge because their existence is through forms. The last is not itself a level of knowledge, as some suppose, but is known indirectly. Its existence also follows what is known in itself, as we have already explained. Keep this distinction in mind.

**A final explanation.** A saying reports that God has seventy thousand veils of light and darkness. If He were to uncover them, the splendor of His face would burn everything reached by His sight.[^V06-541] They burn and overwhelm because the veils here are the ordered effects between God and this world. A distant effect has a weak kind of existence. If it were placed at the level of a nearby effect, its weak existence would collapse and its identity would disappear. Anyone who tried to see God's noble face without these veils, or before passing from one level of existence to the next—until they had passed through all or some of the veils and reached the level of the first veil or one near it—would meet the same fate as the mountain when God appeared to Moses. That is why Gabriel said, when the Messenger asked why he did not go beyond his known place: “If I came a finger's breadth closer, I would burn.”

The veils of light are the separate intellects, ranked according to their degrees of light. They are pure light, unmixed with the darkness of nonexistence, because they are not in time. The veils of darkness are souls and natural powers. They are in time, and everything temporal includes some nonexistence in its way of being. Its light is therefore not pure. All these veils are levels of God's detailed knowledge. God brought them into being even though He is complete, luminous, and free of need for the worlds. He did so to complete the existence of the things that follow from His names and attributes—the fixed entities and possible essences. As we have said, these have two modes of existence. In the first, all are gathered in one necessary existence. Strictly speaking, this is not the separate existence of any one of them, or of all of them; it is the existence of their unity and shared source, and is the place where they appear. In the second, each has its own particular existence. Through His all-encompassing care and wide mercy, God gives them their particular forms of existence and completes their existence in knowledge with their existence in the world. He does not seek an end outside Himself in doing so, since all these things lead back to His attributes,[^V06-542] and His attributes are His essence. This is why He says in a report to one of the prophets: “I was a hidden treasure and was not known, so I created the world that I might be known.” This detailed knowledge, which belongs to those who know God and His names and attributes, reaches its fullness only through the separate existence of things. The levels of knowledge about God are completed by this existence; God Himself does not become more complete through it.

Then God revealed Himself to Himself through the lights of His face, and the lost spirits appeared in a hidden realm that no one can uncover.[^V06-543] They are called “the lost” because each one is so absorbed in God that it does not know of any existence other than Him. It is consumed by God in God, unaware of itself. It has no self apart from the self that exists through God, since God's oneness rules over its existence. If it has no existence separate from God, it knows nothing except God through God. It knows no other thing, since knowledge follows existence.

The next thing God brought into being, after these spirits, through another act of revelation, was a simple, universal essence. Though simple, it contains all the intellects, just as some say the outermost sphere contains all the spheres.[^V06-544] We have proved this by reason, and it is true.[^V06-545] It has many faces, but their number does not multiply its essence. It depends in its existence on its creator, as we explained near the beginning of the book. God calls it the Real, the Pen, the Command, and the Spirit; a report calls it the Intellect. We have mentioned most of these names. God says: “We created the heavens and the earth and all that is between them only in truth.” It is the keeper and guardian, the knower of the unseen, through the knowledge of its creator. It knows itself through knowing its creator, and knows other things through knowing itself. This is the meaning of the Prophet's saying: “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.”

This is God's greatest Throne, which He established over. After it comes the Preserved Tablet, which, as we said, is the universal soul and the source of all particular souls and powers of perception. God brought it into being as the second level in the world. It is among the noble angels and God's messengers to creation. The first level, by contrast, never leaves the divine realm and does not descend into the world of possibility. As we explained, the world of possibility is limited to creation, not the divine command. This Tablet is what God means when He says: “We wrote for him on the Tablets an instruction and an explanation of all things.” It is where the books are sent down. It was the first book written by the Intellect, with detailed marks and letters that unfold in time. God commanded the Pen to write upon this Tablet, through to the Day of Resurrection, all that He had decided and decreed. This Tablet receives what the Intellect sends to it in detail, after the Intellect receives it from God in a simple, unified way. The soul is where things become detailed because it exists in time.

Some people who see realities clothed in images say that the Pen has three hundred and sixty teeth as a pen, three hundred and sixty faces as a spirit, three hundred and sixty tongues as an intellect, and three hundred and sixty rivers as knowledge—the water of life and the spring of life. These rivers draw from three hundred and sixty seas of knowledge.[^V06-546] They are not touched by sleep. They are the gathered meanings of God's words, which never run out. The Qur'an gives this example: “If all the trees on earth were pens, and the sea, with seven more seas added to it, were ink, God's words would not be exhausted.”

The noble being that serves as a Tablet for what is above it is also a Pen for what is below it. The same is true of every power that perceives or is perceived: in one respect it is a Tablet, in another a Pen. From these two noble angels, the Pen and the Tablet, come all their offspring, male and female. The higher Pen gives them spiritual forms. This giving is natural for the Pen, while God wills it, just as our actions are willed by us but follow from our will by nature. The soul has two connections: a luminous one toward the noble Intellect, and a dark one toward matter. Through the first, it receives stable intellectual forms from the Intellect. Through the second, it writes changing natural forms into matter. The soul itself is green, made from a mixture of light and darkness, and is called the Emerald Book.

After this, God created the universal sphere and the physical Throne. He established Himself upon it as the All-Merciful, in a way that fits His mercy toward creation, though this is a lower kind of establishment than the two earlier levels that fit His essence and attributes. The sphere's unending circular motion is an image of the universal soul's longing for God. At every moment, the soul finds something new from Him that it did not find in the moment before. Time comes from this motion, and place comes from the shape it receives—a revolving sphere, which is the finest shape. Its motion is the swiftest, and all the other spheres move by following it.[^V06-547] Each has its own motion, led by its own soul and drawn toward its own beloved, in obedience to God, who turns the mill, begins and ends its course, and is its final destination. Because it is so subtle, this universal sphere is like a boundary between the world of perceptible forms—the world of souls and the afterlife—and the world of material bodies, nature, and this life. It is the ground of the next world, though it has been called this world. Some say that the sphere of the fixed stars, called the Footstool, is the ceiling of Hell on its inner side and the ground of Paradise on its outer side.[^V06-548] The Prophet is reported to have said: “The Footstool is the ground of Paradise, and the Throne of the All-Merciful is its roof.” Perhaps the Footstool in religious language is this outermost physical sphere, and the Throne of the All-Merciful is its counterpart in the imaginal world, the universal imaginal sphere. God knows best.

In short, every mercy God opens for His creation in this world enters through the highest heaven and the gates of the heavens. Anyone who enters Paradise or rises to God must pass through the heavenly realm and travel its reaches until reaching the Lote Tree of the Boundary, then leave that world through its gate.

The words that descend from God come to this boundary as one, without division. Beyond it, they divide in the world into unseen and seen, form and meaning, this life and the next. This is why the Footstool is said to be the place of two feet that extend from it. One reaches toward Paradise, as in God's words, “They will have a firm place of truth with their Lord”; the other is the Foot of the Mighty, mentioned in the Prophet's saying that God will place His foot in Hell, which will then say, “Enough, my Lord.” One foot gives the people of Paradise their firm place in Paradise; the other gives the people of Hell their firm place in Hell. The records of human actions reach this boundary. The records of their knowledge may pass beyond it and reach God, as in “To Him good words ascend, and He raises up good deeds.”

God then created the remaining spheres, one above another down to the elements, and arranged them according to the subtlety and density of the higher beings that guide them. The whole world is like a single jewel with layers that grow denser as they descend. The higher a level is, the more subtle and luminous it is; the lower, the denser and darker. On one side, the order reaches the Light of Lights; on the other, the lowest of the low. All these levels are stages on the way to God. They are, in one respect, levels of His knowledge; in another, signs of His attributes, signs of His beauty and majesty, lights of His face, rays of His perfection, and shadows of His power.

**A point about illumination.** Know that the visible world is like a shell beside the higher world, like a body beside its spirit, or like darkness beside light. Each level of the higher world, whether closer to us or farther above us, stands in a similar relation to the level above it.

We can see something like this in visible light. Light may be received from another light, and the nearer it is to the source, the stronger and clearer it is. Imagine moonlight entering through an opening in a house and falling on a mirror fixed to a wall. The mirror reflects it onto the opposite wall, and the wall reflects it onto the floor. The light on the floor depends on the light on the wall; that depends on the mirror, and the mirror on the moon. The moon, in turn, receives its light from the sun. These four reflected lights have different degrees of brightness. Each has its place in the order through which the light reaches us.

In the same way, people who see with insight have learned, both through reasoning and through direct spiritual discovery, that the lights of the higher world—whether of intellects or souls—come in an ordered succession from the Light of Lights. The light closest to that first, limitless Light is strongest. The nearer it is, the fuller its light and the greater its knowledge of itself and its Lord. The farther away it is, the less complete its light, the less it knows, and the weaker its existence, until the order reaches the world of shadows and then darkness. All these lights are levels of God's knowledge of Himself and places where His kindness and mercy are shown. Understand this, if God wills.

**A consequence.** Once you know that light is existence, since existence makes things appear, and that levels of light are the same as levels of existence, know that the deepest darkness is hidden nonexistence. We call something dark when the eye cannot reach it, even if it exists in itself. What exists neither for itself nor for anything else is even more deserving of the name “darkness.” Nothing in existence can be absolutely dark, or it would be sheer nonexistence. What can exist is a mixture: the light of existence joined with the darkness of possibility and the ways it can fail to exist. Bodies and their states are like this. Their existence is limited by time and place, so when they exist in one boundary, they do not exist in the others.

### Station Four — God’s power

#### Chapter 1 — What “power” means

Power has two well-known definitions.[^V06-549] The first is that an agent can either act or refrain from acting. The second is that an agent, by its nature, acts if it wills and does not act if it does not will. Theologians use the first definition; philosophers use the second.

Some distinguished later thinkers say that the two definitions go together, both in meaning and in reality. Anyone who accepts the second, they say, must also accept the first. For if an agent is such that it acts when it wills and does not act when it does not will, then, considered apart from whether it wills, both action and inaction are possible for it by its own nature. It must act when its will is necessary, and refrain when the absence of will is necessary. The fact that it acts continually because its will is continual does not rule out its ability not to act if it did not will. The same reasoning applies in reverse.

I say that this argument is confused. For the first definition to hold, both acting and refraining must be possible for the agent in itself.[^V06-550] Philosophers have shown that this cannot apply to the Necessary Being. God cannot fail to exist: His existence is necessary, and there is nothing in Him waiting to become fully realized.

These shortcomings are allowed only by those who treat God's attributes as additions to His essence, as the Ashʿarites do, or who treat the motives for His creating as something separate from Him. On that view, if we set those additions aside, God's essence could will or not will, act or not act. But those who affirm God's oneness and free Him of every trace of plurality and possibility say that His will to give and bring things into being is His very essence. There is no difference between His essence and His will, either in reality or in thought. His essence is His will, and His will is His essence; there is no separate aspect that limits Him or explains His action.

So the conditional statement “If He wills, He acts” does not conflict with the fact that His willing is eternally necessary. Likewise, “If He does not will, He does not act” does not conflict with the impossibility of God not willing, or with the eternal necessity of the opposite. The second definition, then, is true for philosophers, but the first is not.

The two definitions can go together only when an agent's will is something added to its essence. But God—exalted is His name—is complete and beyond all completeness. Whatever He does, He does through His simple, true essence, not through a will added to Him or through some passing aim, whether joined to Him or separate.
