Through His will, knowledge, contentment, and wisdom—which are all His essence—He gives good, brings the world into order, and creates with wisdom. This is the fullest form of power[^V06-551] and the finest way to create and give existence.

This does not mean that God is forced to act, as fire is forced to burn, water to cool, or the sun to give light. Those sources do not know or will what they do, and have no independence. They serve God's command. The same is true of all created beings that seem to choose: their motives and reasons compel them in their willing. Their desires make them act, and by acting they gain a further perfection.

Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī claims that philosophers and theologians do not really disagree about what power means when it is attributed to God. Their dispute, he says, is only about words. The disagreement between the two definitions is verbal, not a difference in meaning. Their real disagreement concerns whether the world is eternal or began to exist. The theologians allowed that, if the world were eternal, it could be caused by an eternal cause.[^V06-552] But they rejected an eternal cause and effect, not for the reason that an eternal thing cannot depend on an agent.[^V06-553] Rather, they argued that whoever brings the world into existence must be capable of choosing.[^V06-554] Philosophers, by contrast, agree that an eternal thing cannot be an act of an agent who chooses. So, Rāzī concludes, both sides agree that an eternal thing cannot depend on a choosing agent, though it may depend on a cause that necessarily produces its effect. There is no real disagreement between them. That is Rāzī's account in his *Commentary on the Book of Hints*.

The learned commentator Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi rightly says that this is a settlement the two sides never accepted. The theologians begin their books by arguing that the world began, without first discussing its maker or whether that maker chooses. Only after claiming that the world began do they say it must have a maker, and that this maker must choose: if the maker acted by necessity, the world would be eternal, which they have already rejected. So they did not base the world's beginning on God's choice; they based their claim that God chooses on the world's beginning.

Nor do all theologians agree that an eternal cause cannot have an eternal effect. Some Muʿtazilites who accept God's “states” say so openly.[^V06-555] The Ashʿarites, too, affirm eight eternal attributes alongside the First Principle. They must either count God as nine eternal realities, or treat the attributes as effects caused by God's essence. Even if they avoid saying this openly, their view leaves them no other choice. So theologians do not all agree that an eternal cause and effect are impossible.

Nor do philosophers say that an eternal act cannot come from an agent who chooses. They say that an eternal act can come only from an eternal agent who is fully able to act, and that such an agent cannot have a non-eternal act. Since they hold that the world is an eternal act, they trace it to an eternal agent who is fully able to act. They discuss this in their study of nature,[^V06-556] and also in their study of divine things, where they argue that the First Principle is eternal and fully able to act.

Philosophers do not say that God lacks power or choice.[^V06-557] They say that His power and choice do not divide His essence into many parts, and that His way of acting is unlike the choice of animals and unlike the compelled action of bodies governed by nature. That is the end of al-Tusi's argument. May God raise his rank.

#### Chapter 2 — Our power is an ability we may exercise; God’s power is always fully realized

Our souls, and the souls of other animals, are constrained in what they do and how they move. Their actions and movements are directed, like the actions and movements of nature, by aims and motives outside them. In this respect, our soul is like nature: both are directed in what they do. The difference is that the soul is aware of its aims and motives, while nature is not.

In the fullest sense, free action belongs only to the Necessary Being. Other beings that choose are constrained while appearing to choose. The souls that move the heavens act because of motives and beloved objects that direct them, as we will explain. The movements of the heavens and stars are directed, though they are not natural movements. Natural movement happens by necessity, without choice, awareness, or consent. And when something is compelled to move one way, it cannot at that same moment be compelled to move the opposite way.[^V06-558] A heavenly mover, however, aims at a position that it also leaves behind. We will explain this clearly in its proper place.

The souls of animals on earth, too, act and move because of aims and motives, not through power alone. Every chooser other than the First Necessary Being is constrained in its choice[^V06-559] and compelled in its actions. In us, power is only the ability, readiness, and potential to act. In the fullest sense, then, free choice belongs only to the Real.

Avicenna says in his *Annotations*[^V06-560] that, for the Muʿtazilites, choice comes from a motive or cause, and choosing because of a motive is a kind of compulsion. God's choice and action, by contrast, do not come from a motive.

Elsewhere he says that the Necessary Being is necessity itself: His existence is necessary through Himself, and every attribute of His is fully actual, with no potential or readiness in it. If we call Him a chooser or capable, we mean that He has always been so in actuality. We do not mean what people usually mean by those words. In ordinary usage, a chooser has only the potential to choose and needs something to tip the balance and bring that choice into action. The motive may come from within or from outside. So we choose as if compelled. God, by contrast, has no motive beyond His own essence and goodness. He was never a potential chooser who later became an actual one. He has always chosen in actuality. This means that no one forced Him to act: He acted from His own essence and goodness, not because of another motive. There were no two competing powers within Him, as there are in us.

We do not mean that God has the ordinary kind of power we have. Our power is potential: nothing comes from it until one option is favored over another. We can act in opposite ways. If our power alone could produce an act without anything favoring one side, one person could perform two opposite acts at once. That is impossible. Our power is potential; God is free of all potential. If we call Him powerful, we mean that He always acts in full actuality.

When we examine our own power, we find that it means: if we will and nothing prevents us, we act. But our willing is not always actual. We too have the power to will.[^V06-561] So our willing is potential, and our power takes different forms. In the soul, it is the ability to will; in the limbs, it is the ability to move. If we attributed power to God in the ordinary human sense, His action would be potential. Something would remain in Him that had not become actual, and He would not be complete.

Potential and possibility belong to material things. God is full actuality. The active intellects are like Him in their power and choice: they seek the true good, not an imagined good, and they have no competing desire. There are not two powers within them pulling in different directions, as there are in us. God's glory is that acts proceed from Him; the glory of these intellects is to make their actions resemble His. That is the end of Avicenna's words.

If you say that this account makes the world eternal and unable ever to pass away, we reply: anyone who reflects carefully on what we have said about the existence and coming to be of natural things—and of everything whose existence depends on bodily matter, such as the forms, souls, and powers of the heavens and elements—will see that God's true power is eternal while its effects arise and renew themselves over time. There is no conflict between an eternal act of bringing things into being and an effect whose existence consists only in renewal, passing away, change, and decay. That is the nature of everything in the world of nature, as we have explained.

As for the separate forms that are images of God's names and belong to His eternal decree, they are not external acts. They are divine attributes, veils of light, and canopies of majesty. They are not called “the world” or “other than God.” We will explain this more fully later.

**A clarification and addition.** Anyone who seeks the truth must set aside partisanship and imitation, reject false guides, and return to wisdom and the realm of spirit. They must learn that the difference between a free agent and an agent that acts by necessity is not what many people assume, people whose view is clouded by inherited beliefs and the words of their teachers.

Since God is the source of everything He wills, His will is necessary, just as His essence is, because it is one with His undivided essence. God cannot fail to exist or be what He is in any respect. So God's will is not an intention to create—not even an intention to create everything in general or to bring the first and noblest being into existence. An intention cannot remain after its aim has been reached.[^V06-562][^V06-563] It follows that God's will is not an intention.[^V06-564]

To say that God wills means that He understands Himself and the good order of everything that comes from Him.[^V06-565] He knows that this order necessarily flows from Him and does not conflict with His essence.[^V06-566] His essence contains every good and perfection, as we have said many times. The most complete created order follows from the highest, necessary order that is one with God's knowledge and will. God's knowledge that things flow from Him and do not conflict with His essence is His will and approval. This is His will,[^V06-567] free of every flaw and possibility.[^V06-568] It conflicts with defining God's power as the possibility of acting or refraining,[^V06-569] contrary to what some people assume when they have not thought deeply about wisdom and direct understanding.

If you reflect on this, you will see that the difference between one who wills and one who does not will—whether in us or in God—is what we have described. As long as a will bears the same relation to doing something and not doing it, it cannot make one more likely than the other. Once it is decisive, the action follows. In God, true, decisive will is necessary in itself and eternal. In everyone else, will is mixed with possibility and weakness. At most, their action is necessary because of something else.

So the common claim that a free agent can either act or not act, while an agent acting by necessity cannot refrain, is false. You have learned that when a will is equally related to both sides, it is not decisive. No action can follow from it, unless one denies that things cause other things, as the Ashʿarites do. Once one side is favored, the will brings about the action, and in that respect it is no different from other causes that make their effects necessary.

The real difference is this: a willing agent knows what it brings about, and the action does not conflict with its own nature. An agent without will does not know what it brings about, like natural powers. Or, if it is aware of what it does, the act may go against what it wants, as when someone is forced to act. Even then, the person wills the act.[^V06-570]

The fact that God knows in advance that an act will occur does not mean that He lacks will or power. If God knows that a particular act will happen at a particular time, then its not happening would make His knowledge false. That is impossible. So the act's not happening is impossible, and its happening is necessary.[^V06-571] Yet God wills the act and has power over it. The possibility that it might not happen, or that God might refrain, is not a condition for its being within His power or being willed. Anyone who thinks an agent must sometimes will not to act, or that this must be possible, is mistaken.[^V06-572]

An agent acts as an agent only when its act occurs. At that moment, it is false to say that it willed not to act. So we do not call God an agent because He has actually chosen not to act. We call Him an agent because this remains true: if He willed not to act, He would not act.[^V06-573] The “if” statement remains true even though the situation it imagines is impossible. In the same way, “If the Creator did not exist, the world would not exist” is true, although it is impossible for the Creator not to exist. As we have explained, God's will is His essence. So the truth of the conditional does not depend on its premise being possible. No one can insist that God must be able to will not to act in order to be powerful.[^V06-574]

A fully active cause cannot have all the conditions for acting and still fail to produce its effect. So the difference between a free agent and a cause that acts by necessity is not whether it can refrain from acting. It is whether knowledge and will take part in bringing about its action. As you will see, every created being that chooses is constrained in its choice and compelled in its willing.

#### Chapter 3 — Answering an objection

Some people accept the definition of power as the ability both to act and to refrain, but try to avoid saying that God has this kind of possibility. They say that this possibility belongs to the thing acted upon, since the effect is possible in itself. This is a weak argument.[^V06-575] If it were right, every cause that acts by necessity would count as powerful, and there would be no difference between a necessary cause and an agent who chooses. Every effect is possible in itself when we consider only what it is. If, instead, they mean that the effect is really able to happen or is ready to come into being over time, we have already shown why that cannot apply to an agent that is fully active.

Most Ashʿarite theologians base their account of God's power on denying that causes produce effects by necessity. They say that a mere preference, short of necessity, is enough for a choice to produce an act. God's will by itself, they say, can produce the act without any motive or cause beyond it. The will can attach to either side, and one will can be preferred to another without any further reason.[^V06-576] As you have seen, this claim makes certainty impossible. It would allow a conclusion not to follow from a sound proof, even when its premises are certain.

We have already explained that a thing's possibility is what makes it need a cause. If it were necessary or impossible, it would not need one. So the effect's possibility—not its beginning in time or its having once been absent—is what makes it need a cause.

A cause makes its effect necessary. If something remains only possible, we cannot say that it must either happen or fail to happen.[^V06-577] One side becomes actual only when a complete cause makes it necessary. A mere preference, whether it belongs to the thing itself or comes from a cause, does not remove the possibility.[^V06-578] It does not explain why the act occurs. The question of what made it happen, and what made happening more likely than not happening, remains open until we reach a complete cause that makes it necessary. If we do not reach such a cause, the question remains, and each preference would need another preference to explain it. This leads to an endless chain of preferences, which is impossible.

Even if all these preferences are considered together, they are no stronger than the first one. The opposite could still happen. If the opposite could not happen, the preferred side would already be necessary, contrary to the claim that it is only preferred. That is the difference between necessity and preference: necessary causes can lead back to the first necessary source, from which all necessity and existence come. But a first preference cannot be the source of all later preferences, because it does not make even its own act necessary.[^V06-579] The light of truth has now appeared and driven away this darkness.[^V06-580] Possibility is what makes a thing need a complete, necessitating cause. Nothing short of the true agent can provide what is needed.

#### Chapter 4 — Another reason one will cannot choose either side of every possibility

A will is related to what it wills as knowledge is related to what is known,[^V06-581] and as existence is related to the thing that exists.[^V06-582] Its relation to two opposite or incompatible things cannot be equal. Through the will, one side becomes necessary and the other impossible. How could its relation to both be the same? That would be like saying that the very same existence that makes something black could also be the existence that makes it white, or that the mental form of a human being could also be the mental form of a horse.

It is just as clear that the existence of Zayd cannot be the existence of ʿAmr, or that knowing one of them is the same as knowing the other. The particular identity of our knowledge of Zayd is tied to Zayd. If that knowledge were directed to someone else, it would become a different act of knowledge. The same is true of the will. It takes its particular identity from what is willed, whether that thing is real or imagined, considered generally or in detail. The will for each act, like our knowledge of it, is made particular by being directed to that act. To suppose that it is directed to another act would be to suppose that it had become something else.

Know that this does not conflict with saying that God's will—which is one with His true, undivided essence—can be directed to all possible things that exist. His simple knowledge, too, includes every general and particular thing that has existed or will exist. This is because there is a difference between a human will, which is one limited act among many and is directed to one particular option, and God's simple, true will, which even the minds of most philosophers cannot fully grasp.[^V06-583] We are speaking here about those who claim that God's will is one numbered thing that could equally attach to an act or its opposite.

What we have said still applies to every will, in God and in created beings: each will is one with what is willed, just as each act of knowledge is one with what is known, and each existence is one with the thing that exists. In truth, the will itself is what is willed, knowledge itself is what is known, and existence itself is what exists. This holds in reality, though not in the accidental way we speak of them. There is a great secret here for those who understand.[^V06-584]

#### Chapter 5 — The theologians' views on what makes God will the world's creation

The careful commentator on *The Aims of the Book of Hints* says that theologians who believe the world began fall into three groups. The first group, mostly the early Muʿtazilites and those like them, say that something other than the agent determined the time when the world began. They call this a preference, not a necessity, and say that its purpose was to benefit the world.

The second group says that the world's beginning at that time was necessary. The world could not have begun at another time, they say, because there was no time before that one. This is the view of Abū al-Qāsim al-Kaʿbī and his followers. The third group refuse to say that an outside cause determined the time, because they do not want God's action to depend on another cause. They say that the world’s existence does not depend on time or anything besides the agent, who is not questioned about what He does. Or they admit that a choice was made but deny that it needed a cause beyond the agent. They say a free agent can choose one of two equally available options without anything that makes one more likely. Their familiar example is someone who is thirsty and sees two equally placed cups of water: that person will still choose one. This is the view of Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī, his followers, and other later theologians.

With the principles we have established, you can refute these views. They lead to denying God's action or to treating Him as a body made of parts—claims far from the truth. A preference that is not necessary cannot determine God's action, whether it comes from something separate from Him, as the early Muʿtazilites say,[^V06-585] or from His will alone, as the Ashʿarites say. You have seen that a possible thing needs a cause that makes its existence necessary.[^V06-586]

The claim that an outside cause made God act is also wrong, whether the cause is a benefit to the world or the choice of that particular time, as al-Kaʿbī says.[^V06-587] Consider the supposed benefit to the world. Was bringing that benefit about more fitting for God than not bringing it about? If neither option was more fitting, why would He choose one and leave the other? But if bringing the benefit was more fitting, then God would gain something through His action and become more complete because of what He gave to another. God is far above needing to gain a perfection from anything else. He is the final source and end of all things.

As for denying that God's action has any reason, and using the saying “He is not questioned about what He does,” there is a subtle mistake here.[^V06-588]

There are two different questions. One asks what makes God an agent at all; no cause can explain His essence, and no one denies that He acts through His own essence. The other asks what explains this particular act and makes it happen in this way rather than another. God's knowledge of the most complete order is the reason He brings the world into being. His essence is both the source of the act and its final aim.[^V06-589]

We have established that there is a reason for God's acts, whether we mean the things He creates or His creating them. But this does not mean that something outside Him causes His agency or serves as its final aim. And even though God acts fully through His own essence, with no added will, waiting motive, or hoped-for cause, it does not follow that the world is eternal or that everything besides Him has always existed. We have discussed this in the First Journey and will return to it, if God wills. This is a subtle and elegant path. The great Muslim philosophers had reached this insight before us,[^V06-590] and the Muslim mystics reached it through direct insight and proof,[^V06-591] while also following revealed teachings and submitting to the guidance of those who speak truthfully. Praise be to God, who has favored us over many of His creatures.

#### Chapter 6 — Clearing up some misunderstandings

You may say: If God's necessity is eternal, then the world's not coming into being must be impossible in itself.[^V06-592] That would make the world's coming into being necessary in itself, which conflicts with its being possible in itself.

The answer follows from what we established in our discussion of existence. Beings with no internal parts, and all things that exist only because God gives them existence, may have to exist as long as He continues to give them existence. But their existence is not necessary through themselves; it depends on His eternal giving. It is not a necessity they have apart from their connection to God's complete existence. Logic distinguishes this lasting, eternal necessity from necessity in itself.[^V06-593]

A great thinker tried to answer this difficulty by distinguishing a thing's existence “in itself” from its existence “for another.” The first, he says, is not the same as a thing's coming from, or existing for, something else. The latter is understood only by reference to that other thing. So, he concludes, the world's existence may be possible in itself while its coming from God is necessary.[^V06-594]

But the truth, as many accomplished thinkers have held, is that a caused thing's existence in itself is the very same existence it has for its cause. It is also the very same existence as its coming from that cause. There is no difference in the thing itself. So if it is necessary under one description, it is necessary under the other too. The difference is in how we describe it, not in the reality. And the distinction between what is understood by reference to something else and what is not does not apply here to the thing that is created in its very existence. Its existence is never self-standing; it is always tied to its cause, unlike its essence, as we have already explained. The further objection—that anything understood only in relation to another must belong to the category of relation—has already been answered.

Now consider the surprising argument of this great thinker. After accepting that God's power means that He can bring things into being, he tries to avoid making possibility part of God's essence. He asks: If the most complete order of the world must follow from God's knowledge and will, and His knowledge and will are His own sacred essence, which is free of multiplicity, how can His essence also be open to creating or not creating? How can the power to do either belong to the very same essence that is necessary in itself?[^V06-595]

His answer is that this power does not require a possibility in God. It rests on the possible thing itself. A human being's ability to act or not act comes from the person's own potential and incompleteness. But God's power over every possible thing comes from the possibility of the thing He creates, since He is free of every kind of lack and possibility. The created thing is possible in itself, so God can bring it into being or not bring it into being, even though He necessarily wills it through His knowledge and will. He calls this the highest form of power.

I have already shown what is wrong with this answer. A result's being possible in itself does not make its cause powerful. Heat is possible in itself, but that does not make fire a choosing agent. Otherwise every cause and natural power would count as choosing freely in everything it does.

The strongest answer someone who defines God's power as the ability either to create or not create can offer is this: God's power stands to His will as weakness stands to strength, or as what is incomplete stands to what is complete. The weaker may be folded into the stronger without making them two separate aspects of a thing. For example, when we compare a strong existence with a weak one, our minds may analyze the stronger as though it were several times the weaker, or the weaker together with something more. That is only a way of thinking about it; it does not mean the stronger existence is actually made of parts.

This is the best that can be said for that view. Even so, it remains clouded to anyone who sees the majesty of God's oneness: He is fully realized, with no unrealized capacity; necessary, with no possibility of not existing; complete, with no lack. Al-Tusi says in his *Commentary on the Treatise on Knowledge* that the distinction between knowledge and power arises only in possible beings. The mind takes the first source as resembling them and describes Him as knowing and powerful. To declare God's glory beyond such descriptions is to say: “Glory to your Lord, the Lord of might, above what they describe.” Al-Tusi then explains that, for philosophers, God's will is His knowledge of the whole order in its most complete form. Since His knowledge and power are one reality that brings possible things into being in the best order, knowledge, power, and will are one in Him, though our minds consider them under different descriptions.

Al-Tusi adds that, among causes, an action follows when its causes are present and cannot occur when they are absent. Someone who looks only to the first causes and sees that these are not within the agent's control concludes that the action is compelled.

That conclusion is not fully right, because the nearest cause of an action is the agent's power and will. Someone who looks only to that nearest cause concludes that the action is freely chosen. That conclusion is not fully right either, because the action depends on other causes that were not chosen or willed. The truth is what some have said: “Neither compulsion nor complete independence, but something between the two.”[^V06-596]

In God's case, if we make His will and power two separate things, the same problem returns, and we have no way to resolve it.[^V06-597] But God's acts do not arise from a collection of causes. He is the source of all multiplicity. In Him there is neither choice as we experience it nor compulsion by something else. Al-Tusi means “compulsion” here in the sense of an agent being forced. His point is that God's power, knowledge, and will are one. Through them the best order necessarily follows from Him; they do not merely make it possible for that order to follow. Possibility belongs to the essences of possible things, not to God's necessary power.

The argument can now be stated clearly. When an agent's knowledge and will are what bring about an action, that agent acts by choice, whether knowledge and will are one thing or several, and whether they are identical with the agent's essence, as in God, or distinct from it, as in created beings. In ordinary speech and in careful philosophical writing, we do not call such an agent unchosen or say that its action is forced, even if the action must follow from its knowledge and will.

We have established that the Sustainer of all and God of the world brings about the best and most complete order through knowledge that is His own essence—knowledge that embraces every intelligible and every sensible thing in the highest way. He therefore acts through knowledge and will in the fullest possible way. The fact that the action necessarily follows from His will does not weaken that will; it confirms it. The strongest cause is one that necessarily brings about its effect, and the strongest will is one that necessarily brings about what it wills. God's own simple essence gives good and brings about the best order without limit.

As we have learned in our discussion of God's knowledge, His simple essence contains all existing things in the most complete and sacred way. They exist through His divine existence and are known through His all-merciful knowledge. So the things that flow from His essence follow from His necessary, intellectual existence and from the complete order He knows. They do not follow as light follows a lamp or heat follows what heats it—God is far above that. Rather, as Avicenna explains in *The Healing*, God knows[^V06-598] the order of good in existence, knows that it must flow[^V06-599] from Him, and knows that this knowledge[^V06-600] brings existence forth in the order[^V06-601] He understands as good.

This flow of goodness and order does not conflict with His nature as the giver of existence. It agrees with that nature.[^V06-602] It follows from the goodness of His essence and His perfect generosity, which are not things added to Him but are His very essence.

So God's created things are willed by Him, and the order that comes from Him is pleasing to Him. This does not mean that He first knows them and then becomes pleased with them. His knowing them is itself His being pleased with them, and His choosing them.

This makes clear that God's knowing and willing are one, with no difference either in His essence or in reality. His will is His knowledge of the most complete order. That same knowledge is the reason and aim of His choice. The aim is not something in the created world, beneath Him. As we explained when discussing final causes, a higher being does not act for the sake of a lower one. The soul, for example, does not govern the body for the body's sake. It does so for a perfection beyond both body and soul: to gain perfection and draw near to the active source. Likewise, the nutritive power takes in food, digests it, and makes it like the body—not for the food's sake, or even for the body's benefit, but to preserve its own living form and follow the higher power above it.[^V06-603] Fire does not burn wood in order to produce ash or charcoal; it burns by acting according to its nature and preserving its form.

If no active cause, high or low, has an aim or motive in something beneath it, how could this be true of God's all-conquering power, which is above all things? God has no lack or weakness. Other active powers all have some shortcoming, but that shortcoming is made good by something above them—by the first nature of the higher intellects, or by the further development of the heavenly souls and what follows from them.

#### Chapter 7 — What will and aversion mean

We have explained in several places in this book that one essence may exist in different ways and pass through different stages.[^V06-604] Things that differ in meaning can belong to one existence and one individual. In a single person, for example, the near and distant kinds and the successive differences that make up the idea of a human being are all present in one individual—Zayd, say. They have one existence and one identity. They are all one self, pointed to by the words “this” and “you.” These meanings are gathered in the person's simple inner reality, which is the source and form of them all. The final difference that completes the human being holds together what came before it and gives it a definite form.[^V06-605]

Things whose meanings differ may also exist separately, in different materials, or even in opposition to one another in one realm. Yet those same meanings may come together in a different realm as one simple, subtle, and higher existence, without clashing or crowding one another. We have discussed this in our account of mental existence and elsewhere in this book.

With that in mind, we say that God is truly real and exists. His perfect attributes differ from the attributes of everything else, just as His existence differs from the existence of all other things. His existence is necessary through Himself; the existence of everything else depends on Him. He needs nothing, while everything else depends on Him and is held up by Him. Nothing resembles Him in His essence or in His attributes—in His existence or in the perfections of His existence, such as knowledge, power, and will. Yet “existence” has a shared meaning when we speak of Him and of created things. The same is true of knowledge, power, and the other qualities of whatever exists. Existence is one reality, as we established at the beginning of this book, but it is necessary in God, possible in created things, substantial in substances, and dependent in qualities. The same pattern applies to the other perfections of existence. Knowledge, will, and the rest ultimately return to existence, as we have said.

Most people, even the very intelligent, cannot grasp how knowledge, power, and will run through all that exists—even stones and lifeless things—as existence does. But by God's grace and the light He has given us, we are led to see knowledge, will, and power in everything where we see existence, each according to its own level.

In animals and in human beings insofar as we are animals, will and aversion are states of the soul, like other inner states. We know their individual instances directly, as we know pleasure and pain: when we will or dislike something, that state is present to us. But it is hard to define what will is in general.[^V06-606]

The same is true of knowledge. Every soul knows directly that it is knowing, because its knowledge is present to it. Yet defining knowledge in general is difficult. It may have no separate essence apart from existence itself; it is existence under one description, and differs from existence only in meaning, not in reality.[^V06-607] That is why it is hard to define inner states that we know directly. It is also hard to separate the experience of will from other inner states that accompany it—our thought about an action, our judgment that it is useful, our inclination toward it, and our desire for it. These experiences overlap, so the mind struggles to isolate what will is in itself.[^V06-608] This is why theologians have disagreed about the meaning of will and aversion.

The Ashʿarites say that will is an attribute that selects one of two possible actions. They say it differs from knowledge and power. Power makes either action possible at any time, and allows action or restraint equally; knowledge that an action will happen follows the action itself, since otherwise action would depend on knowledge and cause a circle. They also say that will differs from life, speech, hearing, and sight. I have already explained the problems with this view, including their claim that knowledge of an event must follow the event itself. If they mean that all knowledge follows what is known, that is plainly false; some things happen because they are known.[^V06-609][^V06-610]

Most Muʿtazilites treat will and aversion as kinds of awareness. They define will as believing that something is beneficial, and aversion as believing that it is harmful.[^V06-611] Their thought is that power is equally related to acting and not acting, until the mind judges that one option is useful and so favors it. But we often believe an action will benefit us and still do not will or do it. We may also choose something that we believe will harm us. So another group of Muʿtazilites says that will is an inclination that follows the belief in benefit—a desire of the soul to obtain something. Yet we often feel such an inclination without willing to act, as when we want something forbidden. Others define will as a strong desire to get what is wanted. But we sometimes act without a strong desire, as in routine actions like moving our limbs or cracking our fingers, and in pointless or random actions, or when taking bitter medicine.[^V06-612]

The opposite can happen too: a strong desire may not lead to action because there is no will, as when a devout person with strong appetites avoids forbidden pleasures. This is why some say will differs from desire. Will, they say, is resolve: a chosen inclination, while desire is a natural inclination. That account also faces a difficulty. If resolve and will are themselves chosen actions, they would require another act of will, and so on without end. The answer is that this endless chain follows only if every act of will must always be chosen. But will can arise by choice or without it; we will explain this later.

Some careful thinkers say that an animal may will something by using an earlier act of will. A person weighing the best course of action considers each option in thought, one at a time, in order to see which is sound and which is harmful. The option that seems best then becomes the one the person wills. This is something the person has chosen, through the ability and will to think, along with earlier knowledge. Some of that knowledge is also gained by choice and will, but the process does not go on forever; it stops at causes that were not themselves chosen.

But the same point applies to desire. We can deliberately consider the benefits and pleasures of something, again and again, until we come to want it and long for it. So this does not establish a difference between will and desire.

Another intelligent writer says that, in us, will may be an inner state that produces action and differs from desire, which is the soul's longing for what it wants. His first reason is that desire does not always lead to action, even when it becomes very strong. A person may deeply want a forbidden pleasure and still avoid it. Conversely, an action may happen with little desire. His second reason is that desire can reach toward two opposite actions, such as moving in one direction to meet a loved one and in another to overpower an enemy. Will, he says, cannot choose two opposites at once. Some even say willing one of two opposites means disliking the other. His third reason is that we sometimes know directly that someone wills an action, even when we cannot tell that they desire it. When we see someone take bitter medicine, for example, we know they chose it, but we do not know that they longed for it.[^V06-613]

I say that these thinkers have missed several points. Their main mistake is to assume that will has exactly the same meaning wherever it appears, just as they assume the same of existence. But will in each thing follows the kind of existence it has. Existence ranges from necessity to possibility, from independence to need, from simplicity to complexity, from clarity to obscurity, and from freedom from lack and ignorance to being mixed with them. Some kinds of existence are pure good and have no evil in them. In other kinds, evil is hidden within what is good, or follows from it only in how we think about it. In still other kinds, evil is real and inseparable from the way things exist: this is so with possibilities and capacities that have not yet become actual. The more existence falls toward the margins and depths of multiplicity, the more what is already real is mixed with what has yet to develop, what is one with what is many, and what is definite with what is still uncertain. Yet “existence” still has one general meaning throughout.[^V06-614]

Will and love follow existence in the same way. Whatever exists has some share in being loved and desired; more existence brings more delight and a greater good. What is complete in every way is loved and desired for itself. The good that follows from it is loved only because it follows from that being. Something incomplete is also loved for the existence it has, and it desires what will complete it. Any further good that follows is desired only in a secondary way. So what we call will, love, desire, longing, or inclination runs through all things just as existence does, though people may not use these names for every level of it. The meaning may be hidden from most people, or its signs may not be obvious. In our account, even bodily form is one level of knowledge and awareness, though we reserve the name “knowledge” for forms free of the darkness and lack that make ignorance possible.[^V06-615]

Will and love are one reality, like knowledge. In God, they are His very essence and the source of His action. In other beings, will may be an added state, distinct both from the power to act and from the motive for acting. In a person, for some actions, these three are separate: for example, the ability to walk or write; the will to do it; and the expected benefit that gives us a reason to do it—or the harm that gives us a reason not to. In God, they are one, and all are His single essence. In us, however, these three may also be one in certain inner actions, such as the body's natural work in feeding, growing, and reproducing.[^V06-616]

A human being is created in the image of the All-Merciful. So every deliberate outward action begins within us and passes through the powers that lie between the soul and the organs that carry out the action. The powers, and the places where they work—in the body's subtle spirits and its organs—are like the worlds of spirit and body within the greater human being. In this inner spiritual realm, some powers are higher, such as practical reason, estimation, and imagination;[^V06-617][^V06-618] others are lower, such as memory, common sense, appetite, anger, and the powers that move the limbs.[^V06-619]

The body's material realm also has levels. Some parts are finer and more subtle than others. The author compares the body's subtle spirits, in their different degrees of clarity, to the heavens and their varying purity; these spirits are the seat of the animal and natural powers. The organs themselves, simple and composite, are like the elements and the things formed from them. A full account would take us too far afield. The point is that when we decide to act, we first know what we are to do and judge it beneficial; then we will and resolve to do it, desire it, and finally feel our limbs move toward it. In truth, these four—knowledge, will, desire, and bodily inclination—are one meaning appearing in four different realms, each in a form suited to its level.

When love exists in the realm of intellect, it appears as thought and judgment. In the soul, it appears as desire. In the body, it appears as inclination. Once you see this, you can understand both what is right and what is mistaken in the various definitions of will. Each definition fits one level but not every level. The fact that will, power, and motive can be separate in us does not prevent them from being one in God. Our power is only a capacity; God's power is complete and fully active. In us, power means we may act or refrain; in God, it is His knowledge of the best order, which follows from Him by necessity. Keep this in mind, my dear friend. Follow the truth, not your own desires, lest they lead you away from God's path. God is the source of all help.

#### Chapter 8 — Answering objections about God's will

Some people object that God's will cannot be identical with His knowledge. God knows everything, they say, but He does not will everything: He does not will evil, injustice, disbelief, or other shameful acts. Since His knowledge reaches everything but His will does not, will must differ from knowledge. And since His knowledge is His essence, His will must be an attribute added to His essence. Some of our Imami teachers have relied on this argument to prove that God's will is not identical with His essence.

The answer is that God's outpouring of existence extends to everything He knows to be good within the order of existence. Nothing in the world of possibility opposes His essence, which is His knowledge, and there is nothing He does not approve in the sense relevant here. The kinds of good differ. Some are pure good, with no real evil mixed in; any possibility of evil in them is only a way of thinking about their limitations, hidden beneath the brightness of God's necessary existence. Other things are good but carry some real evil with them. In these cases, good prevails and evil is overcome. Such things must still come from the all-generous source, who chooses all that is good: leaving them out would mean losing a great good for the sake of a small evil. A wise agent does not abandon a great good because a little harm comes with it. Pure evil, evil that prevails, and evil equal to the good do not occur in this world. God does not will or permit any of these to enter existence. He wills good in itself. The rare evils that follow from it are willed only as consequences of that good, not as evils in themselves.[^V06-620][^V06-621]

The verse “God does not approve disbelief for His servants,” and verses like it, mean that God does not approve disbelief in itself, as an evil.[^V06-622] Another way to answer is to compare will with hearing and sight. Knowledge directed toward good things is called will; when it is directed toward what can be heard, it is hearing, and when toward what can be seen, it is sight. In the same way, power directed toward sounds and words is called speech. This does not mean that will is something separate from knowledge. God knows every possible thing, wills every possible good, hears everything audible, sees everything visible, and has power over everything. In relation to certain sounds and words, He speaks. This is another way to resolve the objection, though the first answer is stronger.[^V06-623]

A second objection is that God brings events into being at one particular time, neither before nor after it. Something must select that time. It cannot be power, whose relation to all times is equal, nor knowledge, for the reason just given. So, the objector concludes, the selector must be will, distinct from power and knowledge.

The answer can be given by turning the objection back on itself or by resolving it directly. First, we can ask what selects this will rather than its not existing, or selects its being directed to this time rather than another. If the will is eternal, then its object seems to come later than the will. If it begins in time, we must ask what makes it begin at this time rather than another, and then what selects that cause, without end. The direct answer is that will is one, like knowledge and power, but relates to things in their order as cause and effect. Whatever makes it possible for one agent, with one essence and one attribute, to bring about many different things at different times also makes it possible for one simple will to relate to many different things at different times. We have explained how this happens elsewhere.[^V06-624]

Some say that will itself selects each event for the time at which it occurs and has no power to select a different time. No further reason is needed, they say. But the same could be said of power. If God must will the event at this time and cannot will otherwise, then He acts by necessity, not by choice. And if this particular time has a special property that makes God's will fit only this event, then why not say that the time also makes His power fit it?

One might object that all times are alike in their parts, so one time cannot be preferred over another unless it contains some special wisdom or benefit. Since an act that follows wisdom and benefit must be willed, will must be what selects the time. But the same question remains: Why does one moment, rather than another equal moment, contain the wisdom that makes the event happen then? God's wisdom does not follow a chosen moment; rather, His knowledge and will determine why that moment and all other things have their own features and effects.[^V06-625]

There is nothing impossible about knowledge itself causing things to come into being. Consider someone walking along a narrow wall. If the person imagines falling, that thought may cause the fall. People also speak of a strong will or imagination affecting others, and of the “evil eye,” whose effects are reported in scripture and tradition. If weak human knowledge can sometimes affect what it knows, then God's eternal knowledge—the knowledge of the One who brought the world out of nothing—can surely be the cause of what He knows.

A later thinker compares this with our own minds. Through study and repeated attention, we gain a simple, stable knowledge that allows many detailed ideas to appear in the mind. These ideas remain present while we attend to them, and disappear from awareness when we forget them. In the same way, God's simple and eternal knowledge of possible things, which is His essence, gives rise to them in detail outside Himself. They are present to Him, never hidden from Him, because their existence from Him is also their presence to Him. In truth, possible things are forms of knowledge for God, held in existence through their links to Him. If those links were cut for even an instant, nothing of them would remain.

We have explained that what exists is existence, and that existence is identical with knowledge. So the links that things have to God in their existence are also their links to Him in knowledge. In God, knowledge is also will. Things are therefore not only existences and existing things that come from Him; they are also His knowledge and what He knows, and what He wills and what is willed. They arise from His necessary existence, eternal knowledge, and everlasting will in an ordered sequence. Unity can give rise to many levels in an order, just as the many numbers arise from unity through orderly repetition. Unity is the farthest thing from multiplicity, yet multiplicity arises from it. Consider this carefully.

A third objection is that, if God wills the creation of the world, He must will it either at all times or at one particular time. If He wills it at all times, the world and every event in it must be eternal. But the world and everything in it began to exist; and if the world already existed, it would make no sense to intend to bring it into existence. If God wills the world at one particular time, that time did not exist eternally. It came into being through God's will, so either He willed its creation at every time, making it eternal, or He willed it at a particular time. Then we must ask when He willed that time, and the question repeats without end.

The answer is that God's eternal will brought the particular time itself into being after it did not exist. It did not bring that time into being at some other time. God's eternal will brought the whole world into being, down to each of its parts and smallest details, in their particular places and times. He willed each time and place in its own identity, not in another time or place. An event's being tied to a certain time depends on that time, but the time's own identity does not depend on some other time. Its identity is part of what it is, and it needs only its creator to bring it into being. The same is true of the identity of places. The objection that one cannot intend to bring into being what already exists only applies if “intention” is used in its ordinary human sense, not in the sense of eternal will used by those who understand this matter.[^V06-626][^V06-627]

A fourth objection says that God must will the world through either an eternal will or a will that begins in time. If His will is eternal, then, once the world exists, it cannot fail to exist; God would be a cause that acts by necessity, not by choice. And if His will remains directed toward creating the world, this seems to mean that He keeps trying to create something already created. If it does not remain, then an eternal thing has disappeared. If the will begins in time, we face an endless chain of further wills, or a cycle of causes.

The answer is that God's will is eternal, and the fact that the thing willed cannot fail to follow does not make Him a cause that acts by compulsion. A cause is compelled when its will plays no part in the action. What is compelled is something whose action is imposed on it; it is not simply an action that necessarily follows from its cause.[^V06-628] As people say, “Necessity through choice does not oppose choice; it confirms it.”

Nor does God's continuing will to give the world existence mean that He tries to create it all over again. A possible thing depends on its maker to preserve it as well as to bring it into being. Since possibility and need belong to it at every moment, it needs an eternal creator—and, in this changing world, a creator bringing it into being at every moment. We have shown that this changing world is renewed in its existence over time and is sustained by God, the eternal and all-knowing Sustainer.

A further objection says that God's will to create the world must be either eternal or new. If it is eternal, it either remains after the world has passed away, in which case it seems to will an existing thing at a time when it does not exist, or it relates only to the world's past existence, which makes no sense. If the will disappears, then something eternal has vanished. If the will is new, it must be caused by another will, leading to an endless chain. And if God changes from one state to another, He would be a body, made of parts and powers, as those claim who imagine Him as a subject in which successive intentions arise.

This and similar objections come from comparing God's will to ours and imagining it as a decision or resolve to create. They also assume that things that occur in time are present to God only after being absent, or exist for Him only after not existing. This is not so. From eternity, God knows the whole chain of existence and the complete order, the relations among its parts, its beginning and end, and every stage in between. He knows that this precise order should come from Him and be realized in the world. This eternal knowledge, one with His essence, is His will to bring into existence all that has come into being and to send the light of existence to every thing. It brings them all out of nonexistence and hiddenness into existence and open appearance. This knowledge and will are one eternal reality. They do not change or pass from one state to another, either in themselves or in their relation to what they bring about.

Another objection says that God's will makes the action necessary; otherwise, the action would not happen. Once the will is directed toward it, the action cannot fail. So the world must follow from God's essence as heat follows fire or light follows the sun, and God would be a cause that acts by necessity rather than by choice. His will would be only a name, not a real attribute.

We answer, as we have repeatedly said, that God's essence is His will and approval.[^V06-629] Will is truly the source from which action comes. This is the highest form of action through will. There is also a difference between what is necessary from God and what is imposed on God. God's essence is simple and is identical with His will, but it can also be considered in relation to the identities of possible things. Through His attributes and names, it bears relations to the distinct essences of things in His all-embracing knowledge, which is one and simple in one respect, yet detailed in another. Considered as existence, His essence has no composition of any kind—not in the mind, as in the analysis of broader kind and difference or essence and existence, and not in reality, as in the combination of matter and form. But when we consider the meanings of His attributes and names, the divine realm contains a great many relations. They do not divide God's oneness or simplicity.[^V06-630]

When God's essence is considered together with these specific relations—which are called the “treasuries” of things, as in the verse “There is nothing whose treasures are not with Us”—He wills each kind of possible thing, and each individual in its number, time, and place. Each thing comes into being in its own time and place. If we consider God's essence without these specifying relations, no one created thing is distinguished from another, and no particular possible thing must follow. The action becomes necessary only when these relations are considered. So it is true that God brings things about through the specific ways His will relates to them, and each particular will is connected to a particular possible thing.[^V06-631]

Some reports from our Imami leaders, preserved by the leading hadith scholars, say that will and “mashiyya” come into being and belong to God's acts, not to His essence. At first glance, this seems to conflict with our claim that His will is His essence. Our teacher and guide answers that “will” can mean the act of bringing things into being, the relation by which God creates them. It can also mean the created thing itself, which has come into being through that act.[^V06-632][^V06-633]

Knowledge of created things has several levels. At its final level, those things exist outside the mind and are disclosed, not hidden, to God. In one respect, their own connected identities are His knowledge of them and what He knows; but His knowing them is not identical with their identities. His knowledge as the one who knows is His eternal essence. His knowledge as what is known is the created things themselves.

The same applies to will. At its final level, God's will is the actual existence of things. They are “will” in the sense that they are willed by Him, not in the sense that they are the act of His willing. The source of will, approval, and the power to specify things is God's own essence. This is a stronger account of choice than one in which God's will and approval arise from something added to Him. That is the substance of what our teacher says.

There is a great secret of divine knowledge that I will hint at here. A person with insight may see that the existence of things outside us belongs to God's knowledge and will—not only as what He knows and what He wills, but as the very act by which He knows and wills them. Anyone who understands the principles explained earlier can reach this insight.

#### Chapter 9 — Further support from reports of the Imams

As we explained earlier, when we want to do something or set out to obtain it, we first picture the action and judge that it will bring us some benefit. This judgment may be uncertain, imagined, or well founded. We may think the action will bring us a real good, or only an apparent one—something material, fame, praise, or honor that benefits our own being or one of our powers. From this thought and judgment, desire arises. When desire grows stronger, the power of will is stirred, and we resolve to act. In a human being, this will comes from a power higher than animal desire, which divides into appetite and anger: it comes from practical reason.[^V06-634]

In other beings, the state of that power grows stronger in the way we have described. In both cases, will and desire set the powers in the muscles in motion. The muscles contract or extend, the nerves and limbs move, and the intended action takes place. In our outward actions, these starting points are separate. But God is far above multiplicity, outside motives, being acted upon, or taking delight in something beyond Himself. He is the end of every aim and desire. He has no desire,[^V06-635] no love of anything other than Himself, and no purpose of obtaining something He lacks. Rather, He delights in Himself for His own sake. From this delight, all other good things and joys follow through His active giving and creation—not through being acted upon or acquiring something new.

Many Qur'anic verses point to this for those who reflect on them. God says, “God is self-sufficient, and you are the needy.” He is independent in every way of what is other than Him; everything else depends on Him in its essence, attributes, and actions. If His action and giving involved a separate purpose, desire, wish for obedience, or need for praise, He would not be fully independent of everything else. God also says, “To Him all things return,” and “To God all things come back.” These and many other verses show that He is the end of everything. His action has no end or purpose beyond His sacred essence. If He had a will added to His essence, or an outside motive that tipped Him toward action, He would not be the end of all things. These verses and others show that His will for things is identical with His knowledge of them, and both are His essence.

Reports from our Imami leaders, preserved in *The Book of Knowledge* and other hadith collections, include a sound report from Ṣafwān ibn Yaḥyā. He says: “I asked Abū al-Ḥasan to explain the difference between God's will and the will of created beings. He said: ‘A created being's will is an inner resolve, followed by the action that comes from it. God's will is His bringing things into being, and nothing else. He does not consider, worry, or think things over; these are the attributes of created beings. God's will is the act itself. He says to a thing, “Be,” and it is, without words, speech, resolve, thought, or any how—just as there is no how for Him.’”[^V06-636]

By “inner resolve,” the report may mean that we picture an action, judge it beneficial, feel desire rise from our appetites, and then let that desire grow until we form a firm resolve called will. These are the stages of our deliberate actions. God is far above all of this. Our chosen actions pass through many states between our essence and what we do: some involve knowing, some deliberation, some appetite and anger, and some the bodily actions of pulling, pushing, attracting, or repelling. God's action, by contrast, follows directly from His essence. Nothing stands between Him and His action—not an added attribute or state, not an external motive, desire, intention, or any other change. His acts do, however, follow one another in an order, from the highest beings to those below them.

Hishām ibn al-Ḥakam relates a story about a skeptic who asked Imām al-Ṣādiq whether God feels approval and anger. The Imam answered: “Yes, but not as created beings do. Approval is a state that enters a created being and moves it from one state to another. A created being is ‘hollow’ and made up of parts; things enter into it. Nothing enters our Creator, for He is one, one in essence and one in meaning. His approval is His reward, and His anger is His punishment. Nothing enters Him to stir Him or move Him from one state to another, for that is the way of weak and needy creatures.”[^V06-637][^V06-638] Al-Ṣadūq relates a similar report in *The Book of Divine Unity*: approval and anger are not states that enter God, for He is one in essence and meaning.[^V06-639]

Calling a created being “hollow” is a striking and apt way to contrast it with God, who is *al-Ṣamad*—the One who needs nothing and to whom all turn.[^V06-640] Every possible thing is made up of essence and existence. Essence by itself is like nothing; existence gives it form and definition, as though it surrounded an empty sphere and gave it boundaries. Every possible existence is also mixed with absences and shortcomings, so it has gaps corresponding to what it lacks.[^V06-641] The farther existence moves from the source of good and generosity, the more lack and weakness it contains. It becomes, as it were, more hollow, thinner, and less substantial. Natural things are also composed of matter, which is what a thing may become, and form, which makes it actual. Form gives matter its identity, since matter by itself is indeterminate. In that sense, every material thing is hollow.

A thing is open to outside influences and passing states because it contains potential and possibility, along with absences or limitations. It therefore needs something to complete it—to bring it from absence to existence, from potential to actuality, from lack to fullness. God has placed in every created thing a movement toward the perfection and completeness suited to it, drawing it nearer to Him. For the beings nearest to God this takes the form of intellectual love; for higher souls, a longing of the soul; for lower souls, animal desire; and for natural things, change of position, size, quality, or place. Everything other than God has some lack or need and must be completed by something else. In this sense it is “hollow.” Only God is without any hollow or composition, whether from lack or from completion: He is the One, the Self-Sufficient.[^V06-642]

Reports from our Imami leaders on this subject are numerous. I will cite only a few, so that the account does not become too long. Anyone who wants to read the rest can consult the hadith collections. Among other scholars who affirm God's oneness, Abū ʿAlī says in his notes on *The Healing* that all existing things come from God's essence and follow from it, so they do not oppose Him. Because He loves His own essence, all things that come from Him are willed for His sake. He does not will them for an aim beyond Himself, but because they follow from His essence. He does not will them simply for their own sake. If you love someone, you love the things that come from that person for that person's sake.

We will something because we desire or enjoy it, not because of the thing's own essence. If desire and pleasure could know themselves and produce their own actions, then they would will things for their own sake, since those things would come from them. But will belongs only to a being that is aware of itself.

The Necessary Being is complete—indeed, beyond complete—so He does not act for an aim outside Himself. He does not need to recognize something as good for Him, desire it, and then bring it about. His will is His knowledge that a thing is good in itself and must exist in a particular way to be truly good. No further will is needed to make it exist. His knowledge of the possible things arranged in the best order is itself the cause that brings them into being in that order. He does not first know what follows from His essence and then become pleased with it. Since these things come from what His essence requires, their coming from Him is itself His approval of them. Their existence does not conflict with Him; it fits His nature. Whatever comes from an agent in this way, and is known by that agent as its own act, is willed by it because it accords with its nature. So all things are willed by the Necessary Being, without His acting for a purpose beyond Himself.

This is like loving something for the sake of a person: the person is what you love in the fullest sense. The ultimate beloved is God Himself. We desire things because we need them, but the Necessary Being wills them in the way just described. He does not long for them, because He has no need of them. Purpose, in the sense of a motive for seeking something, involves desire: “Why did he seek it?” “Because he wanted it.” Where there is no desire, there is no such purpose. God has no aim in obtaining something or in the benefit that follows from obtaining it. An action itself can be an aim, or its benefit can be an aim: walking may be an aim, or exercise may be; building may be an aim, or the shelter it provides.

If someone understood the perfection that is the Necessary Being and then arranged everything below Him according to that model, so that all things were in the best order, the real aim would be the Necessary Being Himself, who is perfection.[^V06-643][^V06-644] If He is both the source of action and its perfection, then He is both the agent and the end. The same is true if we build a house according to a perfection we have understood: that perfection is our aim. If the perfection itself were also the source of the building, then the source and aim would be one.

In us, a similar action may occur when we picture something and recognize it as useful or right. Unless some other factor favors a different course or blocks it, this thought moves our appetites. There is no further act of will between the thought and the appetite's movement; the thought itself is enough. God's will is similar: His understanding of things in the way described is the cause of their existence.[^V06-645]

He does not need desire or a longing for what He does. We need desire and the power of longing so that we can use our limbs to obtain what suits us. Our limbs act only after desire. God needs no such desire or instruments. His knowledge of the whole order of existence, of the best way things should be, and of the best arrangement is providence itself. If we were to arrange existing things, we would first understand the best order, then arrange them according to it. When the order and perfection are the agent itself, and all things issue from that perfection, providence is present. It is God's will, and His will is His knowledge, because agent and aim are one.

Providence is God's understanding of how each thing should be: how a human being's parts must be arranged, or how the heavens must move, for each to be complete and for the good order to be present. This knowledge is not followed by desire or by a further purpose. God knows that what He knows accords with His own beloved essence. To say that He created the world for an aim below Himself—to make the world or the perfections that come with it His purpose—would mean seeking a perfection He would not have if He had not created the world. That does not befit the One who is necessary in every respect.[^V06-646]

Avicenna concludes that God's will is His knowledge and providence, and is not something that comes into being. He also notes that when God conceives a thing, existence follows from that conception. When we conceive something, desire follows; when we desire it, our limbs move to obtain it.[^V06-647] Power means that an action follows from will, with no added condition. In God, power is His knowledge: when He knows and conceives a thing, its existence follows. In us, power belongs to the moving faculty, not to the faculty that knows. God's power is not a mere capacity that may or may not be used; His action follows from will alone, without needing to consider whether He wills or does not will. His power is not like ours: our power is only a capacity, while His is fully active. Otherwise, He would contain unrealized possibility, which is impossible. And if His power were not identical with His will and knowledge, His attributes would multiply. So His power and will both return to His knowledge. Our will follows a purpose; God's only purpose is His own essence.[^V06-648]

Avicenna also says that when things come from God's essence without an aim beyond Himself, their coming is His approval; He does not create them and only afterward approve them. God cannot have power as mere possibility. When He acts, He wills; when He does not act, it is because He does not will. His power, as such, is not about a possible choice between the two.[^V06-649]

Wisdom means knowing the Necessary Being, the First, as He is. No created intellect knows Him as He knows Himself; He is truly wise. Philosophers also use “wisdom” to mean complete knowledge and perfect action. In understanding, complete knowledge means defining a thing if it has a definition, and knowing it through its causes if it has causes. A thing without a cause is known through itself. God, whose existence depends on nothing, cannot be defined through anything else and is known through Himself: He needs nothing else in order to be understood and has no cause.[^V06-650][^V06-651]

Wisdom also means acting with perfect order. A perfect act gives each thing everything it needs to exist and continue existing, as far as it can.[^V06-652] If its possibility lies in matter, this means giving it what its matter is ready to receive. If its possibility does not lie in matter, as with the separate intellects, it receives what its own nature allows. Different degrees of possibility explain why beings differ in their perfection and shortcomings. If possibility differs by kind, the beings differ by kind; if it differs among individuals, their degrees of completeness differ. Absolute perfection is existence without possibility, being without nonbeing, action without potential, and truth without falsehood. Everything that comes after the First is less complete, because everything other than Him is possible in itself. The degrees among later kinds and individuals follow from their different capacities. Each separate intellect is more complete than the one below it; together they are more complete than material things; and the heavens are more complete than the lower world of the elements. “More complete” here means prior in its own existence, such that the next being cannot exist without it.

These degrees of possibility also explain the presence of evil. Evil is a kind of absence, just as good is existence. The more possibility and potentiality a thing has, the more it can lack what would make it complete. That is the end of Avicenna's discussion.

Al-Tusi, one of the most careful Imami scholars, examines this question in *The Refutation of the Book of Conclusions*. He quotes the Ashʿarite author of *The Book of Conclusions*: “God must not act for an aim, contrary to the Muʿtazilites and most jurists. Anyone who acts for an aim gains some perfection through that act; one who is completed by something else is incomplete in himself. And any proposed aim is possible, so God could bring it about directly. Using an action as a means would then be pointless. We cannot say that the aim is impossible to achieve without that means, because the only suitable aim is to bring pleasure to the servant, and God can do that without any means.”[^V06-653]

The opposing side argues that an act with no aim is pointless, and that a wise agent cannot act pointlessly. The response is: if “pointless” means only “without an aim,” this argument simply repeats itself. If it means something else, that meaning must be explained.

Al-Tusi then clarifies the Muʿtazilite view. They say that a wise agent always acts for a purpose that motivates the action; otherwise, one option would be chosen without a reason. Jurists say, for example, that the law of retaliation is meant to deter people from murder. Later jurists use this aim when deciding what the law permits or forbids in cases where scripture gives no explicit ruling.[^V06-654] Some who affirm aims say that incomplete things are guided toward their perfection. Some perfections cannot be reached without this process, just as a body cannot move from one place to another without being moved; the movement itself serves its aim. So some aims cannot be reached without the particular actions that lead to them. It is not true in every case that God can provide the benefit directly: a person cannot receive the reward for work without doing the work.

Nor is every action without a further purpose pointless. It is pointless only when it is the kind of action that a choosing agent would normally perform for a purpose, but performs for no reason at all.[^V06-655] The claim that any agent who acts for an aim is perfected by that aim borrows a rule from the philosophers and applies it incorrectly. Philosophers do not deny that things are guided toward their perfections. If they did, the study of the body's organs, nature, astronomy, and every other branch of natural philosophy would be pointless, and final causes would have no place. They say instead that things come from their source in the best way possible. God does not first create them incomplete and then complete them through a second act. He creates them with an inbuilt longing toward their perfection, without later needing to make a separate effort to complete them.

The Sunnis, meanwhile, say that God does whatever He wills, and that His actions cannot be judged good or bad. On this view, many incomplete beings are brought to an end before they reach their perfection, and many things in motion are moved away from the ends toward which their movement tends. God is not asked why or how He acts. That is the end of al-Tusi's argument.[^V06-656]

**Completion and clarification**

The disagreement between philosophers and Ashʿarites is about whether things have causes and aims. Philosophers say every action and movement has a cause and an aim. The Ashʿarites deny cause and effect and deny that any agent—including God—must act because of a reason or motive. They define power in this way so they can say that God may choose either option without any reason favoring it. This view, as it is usually presented, is far from wisdom and knowledge. Perhaps the earliest Ashʿarite teachers meant only to deny that God acts for a further aim beyond Himself.

The disagreement between philosophers and the Muʿtazilites is narrower: does God's action as a whole have an aim beyond His own essence? Philosophers say it does not. God does not act in order to give a benefit, reward, or other good to someone else. Yet these aims and benefits follow from His actions. The Muʿtazilites say that the overall aim of His action is a benefit that returns to His servants. As for particular actions, each has a cause and aim for the agent; the agent gains a perfection through that action, either in time, as in agents in the natural world, or in a way not bound by time, as in beings above nature.

As we explained earlier, an action’s ultimate goal is also, in its full reality, what sets the agent’s action in motion. It is the first source behind the action: it brings it into being.
