It is also the aim when the agent has it in view. These are one thing in reality but differ in how we consider them. In the same way, the benefit that follows from an action and the end toward which it moves are one thing in reality, though we describe them differently. The goodness and completeness that the action brings about are its benefit when we see what follows from it, and its aim when we see what the action is directed toward.[^V06-657]

A choosing agent's actions may have aims and benefits that follow one another in a chain, until they reach the final aim. That final aim is where the chain begins and ends. In God's action, all four—the goal, benefit, end, and aim—are one: His own essence. They all return to His perfect knowledge of the good order and His true will to bring about good in itself.

The greater world—the complete human being—is brought about by God, and God is its beginning and end. Each part of the order of existence has its own nearby aim and its own nearby cause, just as each part of a person's body has a nearby cause and function. In the human body, the soul is the cause and aim of the whole person. Each particular act of the body's organs has a particular power of the soul behind it—attraction, expulsion, nourishment, growth, reproduction, and many others.[^V06-658] Their actions all return to the soul, outwardly and inwardly, as their source and end.

A created agent, because its own being is incomplete, acts for something beyond itself that completes it. God, whose essence is complete—indeed, beyond complete—has no goal or aim beyond Himself. This does not mean that His action has no aim at all: His aim is His sacred essence. Philosophers deny only that God's action as a whole has a final aim other than Himself. They call Him the aim of all aims, the end of all desires and longings, because He is the cause of causes. They do not deny that other, nearer aims and perfections follow from His actions. This differs from the Ashʿarite view, which closes the door on all explanations by causes, and the Muʿtazilite view, which gives God's action as a whole an aim beyond Himself. Both views miss the truth, which preserves the oneness of existence as the knowers of God have understood it.

We have already proved this from the point of view of God as the source and agent of existence. Here we prove it from the point of view of God as its final end. The perfection of each thing is itself, and the completion of that perfection is another perfection, continuing through the levels of being until all reach the highest perfection and complete existence. This avoids an endless chain or a circle. “Everything perishes except His face.” In this way we understand the great resurrection and the words, “Whose is the kingdom today? God's, the One, the All-Conquering.” We also understand what the people of direct insight call passing away into God and abiding in Him, and what Porphyry calls the union of the knower and the object of knowledge. The Prophet—peace be upon him—said: “I have a time with God in which no near angel or messenger prophet can share.”[^V06-659]

#### Chapter 10 — God's wisdom, care, guidance, and generosity

You have learned that wisdom is the best knowledge of what is known and the most exact action in what is made. The Necessary Being knows everything through Himself, its causes and sources, and brings about the most complete order for a true end that follows from His own essence. In this sense, He is wise in His knowledge and perfect in His making and action. He is perfectly wise.

As we have seen, this very knowledge causes things to exist and is His will to bring them into existence. He does not look to anything lower than Himself as the aim of creation, nor to an end that does not belong to His essence. This is what God's care for the order of things means. Guidance is the movement by which a thing is led to a second perfection, one it does not need simply in order to exist or continue existing. God gives every thing the perfection it needs to exist and endure, and also gives it a further perfection beyond that. The Qur'an points to this when it says: “Our Lord is He who gave each thing its form, then guided it.” Creation gives the first perfection; guidance gives the second. God also says: “He created me, and He guides me.”

Generosity means giving good without receiving anything in return. Giving can take two forms: exchange and generosity. In an exchange, you give something and receive something back. The return may be a material thing, a good reputation, joy, a prayer, a new perfection, or freedom from a flaw. In short, if the giver wants something that can be gained only by giving, the act is really an exchange—even if most people use that word only when material things are traded. Those who think clearly know that true generosity has no return and no aim. Any act done for a benefit or purpose is an exchange. Only God, who depends on nothing, acts without seeking anything beyond Himself. Only He is generous in the fullest sense; every other act of generosity comes from Him.

#### Chapter 11 — His will extends to all actions

This is one of the subtle and difficult questions, and few people have found their way to its heart. Opinions differ, schools of thought divide, and people’s minds are left confused.

One group, including the Muʿtazilites and those who follow them, says that God created human beings and gave them the power to act, then left the choice to them. On this view, people create their actions independently, according to their own wishes and abilities. God wants them to believe and obey, and dislikes their disbelief and disobedience. They say this view explains the purpose of commands and prohibitions, the promise of reward and threat of punishment, and how God is free of blame for creating and willing shameful acts and evils.[^V06-660][^V06-661] But they overlook the partners they have, in effect, assigned to God.[^V06-662]

The view that every person creates their own actions independently is even more extreme than the claim that idols or the stars intercede with God. It also means that what the King of kings wants may fail to happen in His own kingdom, while what He dislikes may still happen there. That would be a serious flaw in His rule and dominion. Exalted is God above such a thing.

Another group, including the Ashʿarites and their followers, says that everything that comes into existence does so directly by God’s will. This includes both independent beings and qualities that depend on other things, such as people’s actions, wishes, desires, movements, obedience, and disobedience. They say that God wills whatever happens and does not will what does not happen. As the familiar saying goes, everything that occurs in the world is by God’s decree and measure. The Prophet is also reported to have said, “What God wills happens, and what He does not will does not happen.” No one creates or affects anything in existence but God. He does what He wills and judges as He chooses; no cause governs His actions, and nothing can turn back His decree. “He is not questioned about what He does, but they will be questioned.” Reason, they say, has no standing to call God’s actions good or bad: everything He does is good because it comes from Him. The causes we observe—the heavens, stars, and their positions, or people and animals acting and moving—are connected to events only in appearance, not in reality. They are not truly causes and do not contribute to anything’s existence. God’s custom is simply to create a cause first and then create its effect afterward. In truth, the effects come from Him directly. They say this protects God’s power from any shortcoming or need to use something else as a means. The two groups have argued against one another, citing conflicting arguments and even verses of the Qur'an and reports from the Prophet, whose surface meanings seem to point in different directions.

A third group, the philosophers and the most learned among our Imāmī scholars, says that things differ in how they can receive existence.[^V06-663] Some can exist only after another thing exists, as a quality cannot exist without a substance. God’s power is perfect: He gives existence to possible things in an ordered sequence, according to their different capacities. Some things come from Him without an intermediate cause; others come through one cause or many. They enter existence only after the things that cause them are already there. God is the cause of causes, with no cause before Him. This does not show a limit in His power. The limit is in what can receive existence. Nor is there any need or dependence in this: the intermediate cause also comes from Him, so God depends on no one in bringing anything into existence.

There is no doubt that the world comes from a source that is perfect in goodness and generosity, and that what comes from Him must follow the finest order. What comes from Him is either pure good, like the angels and beings like them, or something in which good outweighs evil, like jinn and human beings. Good comes from God directly, while the evils that accompany it come only indirectly. In this sense, people have said that God wills the disbelief and sins people commit, but does not approve of them.[^V06-664] Consider someone bitten by a snake whose life can be saved only by cutting off a finger. The person chooses the amputation in order to save their life. They want the life-saving result and approve of it; they choose the amputation, but do not approve of it in itself. This is the subtle distinction. This view is better than the first two, free of their worst problems, and closer to the truth for anyone who sees what lies beneath appearances. It is a middle way between compulsion and complete independence: the best course is the middle one.

A fourth group—the people firmly grounded in knowledge, the people of God—says that although beings differ in their essences, qualities, actions, and nearness to or distance from the First Reality, they are all gathered within one divine reality that contains every level and truth. This does not mean that God is a composite made up of all things. God is far above multiplicity and composition. It means that this divine reality, though utterly simple and one, sends its light throughout the heavens and the earth. No particle in the world of existence lies outside the encompassing power of the Light of lights. “He watches over every soul and what it has earned.” He is with everything, but not by being beside it; He is beyond everything, but not by being separated from it. “He is God in heaven and God on earth.”

This subtle and noble truth was discovered through spiritual practice and direct insight. We have also established it through proof, in a way that agrees with what is seen and known directly. Just as nothing in existence falls outside God’s activity, so no action falls outside His action. This does not mean that Zayd’s action does not truly come from Zayd. It means that the very action that truly belongs to Zayd also truly belongs to God. Judgment belongs to God alone; there is no power or strength except through God, the Most High, the Most Great. Every power is His power. In all His greatness and exaltedness, He takes on the actions of things; and in all His transcendence, He is present in every earth and heaven, as the Qur'an says: “He is with you wherever you are.”[^V06-665][^V06-666]

Once this is understood, it becomes clear that actions and existence can truly be attributed to a human being, just as hearing, sight, and the other senses, their powers, actions, and responses can truly be attributed to us. Zayd’s existence is real and belongs to Zayd, not merely in a figure of speech; at the same time, it is one of the ways the First Reality is present in the world. The same is true of Zayd’s knowledge, will, movement, stillness, and everything else he does. These truly belong to him, and at the same time they are actions of the Real in a higher and nobler way, suited to God’s oneness, without weakness, passivity, resemblance, or contact with bodies and impurities. Exalted is He above all that.[^V06-667]

Transcendence belongs to God’s oneness, in which everything else is overcome: He is the One who prevails, and there is no other beside Him. Resemblance belongs to the many levels of created things and their dependence on Him. All praise returns to His one reality, while the many acts of praise and worship belong to the created order.

God gives existence to everything that exists. Existence is pure good, and existence is what He gives. Evil and nonexistence are not created; neither are essences that have never received existence. A dog’s body may be impure, but the existence it receives from God is pure. A person may be impure in essence because of what they are, but not in the existence God gives them, which is pure at its source. Existence becomes mixed with absence and darkness as it moves farther from the source of generosity and light. Sunlight falling on filth does not lose its brightness or become foul-smelling; it only seems so because it falls on something foul. In the same way, existence, simply as existence and as an effect of existence, is good and beautiful. Evil and ugliness come from its incompleteness, or from its conflict with another good. Both come down to some kind of absence, and absence is not created. All praise belongs to God, the Most High, the Most Great.[^V06-668]

This is the heart of the fourth view. Understanding the soul and its powers makes it easier to grasp: every action of the senses and of the animal and natural powers is an action of the soul, while still truly being an action of those powers. This fourth view is greatly fruitful and profound, if anyone can reach it. It removes the difficulties surrounding the creation of human actions and makes sense of the saying of the Imam of those who affirm God’s oneness, ʿAlī: “No compulsion and no delegation, but a matter between the two.”

This does not mean that a person’s action is made up partly of compulsion and partly of independence. Nor does it mean that the action falls outside both, or that a person is free in one respect and forced in another, or that someone is forced while appearing to choose, as some have said. Nor does it mean that people have a little freedom and a little compulsion. It means that a person is free precisely in being dependent on God, and dependent on God precisely in being free.[^V06-669]

The saying “the best course is the middle one” has a deeper meaning here. Sometimes a middle state is a blend of two extremes, like lukewarm water: it is neither hot nor cold, though it belongs to the same range as both. This kind of middle state is still a mixture. But a middle state can also unite two qualities on a higher, simpler level, where they do not oppose or crowd one another. The heavenly sphere, as philosophers understand it, is an example: it has both heat and cold, each in a higher and simpler form than what we find in this world. In this analogy, the first view is like fire’s heat, the second like water’s cold, the third like lukewarm water, and the fourth like the state of the heavenly sphere.

You who seek a true understanding of things and hope to reach the world of holiness: do not be devoted only to saying God is unlike creation, nor only to saying He is like creation, nor settle for a confused mixture of both. Hold the belief of those who dwell in the sanctuaries of the realm of power. They are beyond both likeness and negation, and beyond mixing opposites. They belong to the divine unity that gathers all things. God is exalted in His nearness and near in His exaltedness. His mercy encompasses everything. No being, action, affair, will, or wish lies outside Him. That is why He says, “Every day He is at work,” and, “There is no private conversation among three where He is not the fourth, nor among five where He is not the sixth.” This also reveals the meaning of, “You did not throw when you threw, but God threw.” The statement both denies and affirms the act from the same point of view. Likewise, “Fight them; God will punish them by your hands” attributes the killing to the people and the punishment to God, though the punishment there is the very act of killing.

This is what I have to say about the creation of human actions, a question that has confused many minds. God is the source of right guidance and understanding.

One example can make this easier to understand: read the book of the human soul. It is a shorter version of the great book of the world, written by the All-Merciful, who has taken mercy upon Himself and written faith in your hearts. Reflect on the soul and on the actions of its powers. Then you will see that actions coming from human beings are also, in the very same reality, actions of God—not as the determinists say, not as those who claim complete human independence say, and not as the philosophers’ usual account has it. Consider the senses and powers of the human soul: God made them an example of His own essence, attributes, and actions. “And in yourselves—will you not see?” The Messenger of God also said, “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.”

The truth, seen with care, is that every action of a sense or power, insofar as it is the action of that power, is also the action of the soul. Seeing is certainly the action of sight, since sight either brings the visible image before us or receives it. Hearing is the action of hearing, since it either brings the sound into awareness or receives it. Neither can happen without bodily change. Yet each is also the action of the soul, which is truly the one that hears and sees. It is not enough to say, as the standard philosophical account does, that the soul merely uses its powers, as someone might hire a writer or painter. Hiring a craftsman does not make the employer the craftsman: someone who hires a builder need not be a builder, and someone who hires a writer need not be a writer. So merely using sight or hearing would not make the soul seeing or hearing.

But if we look within, we know that the soul itself is the one that perceives in every act of sense and awareness, and that it is the one that moves in every animal or natural movement attributed to our powers. We will explain later how the soul itself is the power of sight in the eye, hearing in the ear, grasping in the hand, and walking in the foot. Through it, the eye sees, the ear hears, the hand grasps, and the foot walks. This recalls the sacred saying: “I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, and his hand by which he grasps.”

Though the soul is one and separate from the body and its powers, no part of the body—high or low, subtle or dense—is outside it. Nor are the perceiving and moving powers, whether animal or natural, separate in their reality from the soul. The soul’s oneness is a different kind of oneness, known by those who see directly. It is a shadow of divine being: one encompassing reality that gathers all the identities of the powers, senses, and organs. When the soul’s complete reality appears, their separate identities fade into it, and their individual existence gives way to its one existence.[^V06-670]

At death, which is the lesser resurrection of every being in the small world of the human body, these powers fade in their scattered bodily forms. At the second blowing of the trumpet, they take on another life, a single and gathered existence through the soul. Then they rise and look on. The land of the body in the next life shines with the soul’s light, just as the whole world will shine in the great resurrection. The soul encompasses and rules its powers; they begin from it and return to it. The soul’s rising begins with God and ends with God. So it is with all beings: “From Him they begin, and to Him they return. Truly, all affairs return to God.”

#### Chapter 12 — Answering the remaining objections to eternal will, and some objections to created will

One objection is that if God’s will is as the philosophers and careful Muslim thinkers describe it—identical with His essence and with the motive behind His actions, namely, His knowledge of the most complete order—then the world must be eternal and His will must fail to happen at the time He wills it.[^V06-671] We have answered this in our discussions of the coming-to-be of bodies and physical natures, and of how events are connected to the divine reality.

Another objection appeals to God’s words, “He is not questioned about what He does.” If any of His actions were directed toward a goal or motive beyond Himself, someone might reasonably ask why He acted as He did. Why, then, do the Qur'an and the reports forbid this question? The answer is that they do not deny that God’s actions have causes or aims, as the Ashʿarites and their followers suppose. Rather, as we have already explained, they deny that God’s action as a whole has some final aim beyond Himself. Particular actions may still have nearer and intermediate aims.

For example, the broad back teeth are shaped to help us chew well. Good chewing serves the first stage of digestion, which serves the next stage, which in turn helps nourish the body in a way suited to its nature. That supports a healthy bodily balance, which allows the soul to reach its perfection. The soul’s perfection leads to the intellect’s development, first as a capacity and then in full, and finally to the active intellect. The ultimate aim is God, exalted above all. The same pattern appears in learning: grammar and language help us learn logic, which serves the theoretical sciences that are not merely tools. Taken together, those sciences are their own final aim, since they are the end of the sciences that serve them. But considered separately, some can be the aim of others: the study of nature, motion, and generation and decay leads to the questions of metaphysics.

Metaphysics, in turn, leads to the study of immaterial beings and divine things, and from there to the knowledge of God’s oneness, His attributes, and the origin and return of all things. As knowledge, this has its own end; as a way of being, its end is nearness to God. Its aim is to pass away into divine oneness and then abide after that passing. It has no further aim: it is the aim of all aims and the highest of all states.

So there is no further “why?” behind God’s action as a whole, or behind the last act in the order. But the particular, nearer, and intermediate actions do have aims and explanations, arranged in a chain that leads to the One who is the aim of every aim and the end of every longing, through His own true and utterly one essence.

Another objection says that if everything happens by God’s will and decree, then reason and religious law require us to approve of every decree. A sacred report says: “Whoever is not content with My decree, does not endure My trial with patience, and is not grateful for My gifts, let that person leave My earth and heaven and seek another lord.” Yet approving disbelief and wrongdoing is itself disbelief and wrongdoing. Reports from the Imams say that approving disbelief is disbelief, and the Qur'an says that God does not approve disbelief for His servants.

Al-Ghazālī and others, including Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, answer that disbelief is what God decrees, not the decree itself. It is the object of the decree, not the decree; so we approve the decree, not what is decreed.[^V06-672] Some Sufis, including the author of *ʿAwārif al-maʿārif* and Jalāl al-Dīn Rumi, accepted this answer. But skilled scholars, among them Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi in his *Naqd al-Muḥaṣṣal*, rejected it. He says that when someone says, “I am content with God’s decree,” they do not mean that they approve one of God’s attributes. They mean that they approve what that attribute brings about—what is decreed.[^V06-673]

The correct answer, according to him, is that approving something because it is God’s decree is obedience, not disbelief; it is disbelief only when approved for some other reason. Our honored teacher says that the distinction between decree and what is decreed does not solve the problem: considering something as decreed is just another way of considering the decree, while considering it apart from that relation is not considering it as decreed. The sound answer is that we must approve the decree as decree, and what is decreed as decreed. Disbelief, however, is not decreed in itself or directly. It is decreed only indirectly, as something that follows from many goods. We approve it only in that respect, not because it is disbelief. Disbelief lies in approving disbelief as disbelief, rather than accepting something because it is bound up with the many goods of the order of existence.

I say that “decree,” like “judgment,” can mean the affirmative or negative relation expressed in a judgment. That is a relation, and what is decreed is its object. Or “decree” can mean the intelligible image that includes such a relation. The same distinction applies to knowledge, power, will, and similar terms.[^V06-674] On the first meaning, if the decree itself is approved, then what is decreed must be approved too, because a relation follows its object. If someone says that a judge has issued an evil or unjust judgment, they mean the object of the judgment. There is no sense in saying that the judgment is good while what it judges is evil.

On the second meaning, God’s decree is the existence of all the things in this lower world as they exist in His knowledge: in a sacred, intellectual, and divine form, free of limitation, evil, nonexistence, possibility, and darkness. Every being in this world has a corresponding reality in that divine world: a positive reality that causes it and gives rise to it. There it is pure good, with no evil mixed in. The world of command is entirely good; evil is found only in the world of creation, where existence is mixed with absence and darkness. That is why the Qur'an says, “I seek refuge in the Lord of daybreak from the evil of what He has created,” placing evil on the side of created things.

With this distinction, the claim that we should approve God’s decree but not necessarily everything decreed makes sense. But Tusi’s point—that “I am content with God’s decree” does not mean that one approves an attribute of God—still needs an answer. God’s decree is not a quality added to Him. It is the source and reality of beings themselves.[^V06-675] Nor do we accept that being content with God’s decree means only being content with what He knew beforehand.

There is also a problem with the claim that approving disbelief as God’s decree is obedience, not disbelief. Since God’s knowledge is active, every positive aspect of anything in this world is also the very aspect by which it is known to Him.[^V06-676] Just as God and His knowledge of things are one without any difference in His essence or even in how we consider Him, so too a thing’s existence in itself and its being known to Him are one reality, with no difference between them.

Here is an example that may make the point clearer. If you picture someone as a disbeliever or imagine their face as black, that image does not make you a disbeliever or blacken the face of your heart. The image of disbelief in the mind is not blameworthy disbelief, and the image of blackness in the mind is not the same blackness found outside it. Keep that distinction firmly in mind, and do not stumble over it.

The source of an evil or ugly thing in the world of bodies need not itself be evil or flawed. Those who know the divine names explain that God’s majestic and overpowering names—such as the Avenger, the Compeller, and the All-Conquering—are sources of evils and harmful things, including disbelievers, devils, wrongdoers, and the levels of Hell and its inhabitants. His gentle and beautiful names—such as the All-Merciful, the Compassionate, the Kind, and the Subtle—are sources of good things, including the prophets, saints, believers, and the levels of Paradise and its inhabitants. They even say that Satan was created through the name “the Misleader,” citing his words, “Because You led me astray,” and “Because You made me err.”[^V06-677][^V06-678] What clashes in the world of separation, evil, and conflict fits together in the world of unity and good.

Another objection says that if God knows a human action and His will and decree apply to it, then the action must necessarily occur. If He does not know it and His will and decree do not apply to it, then it cannot occur. How, then, can a person have power over their own action?

The objection can be answered by showing that the same problem applies to God’s will for events that come into being in time, especially if His will is said to change over time.[^V06-679] But there is also a direct answer. Al-Rāzī, in *al-Muḥaṣṣal*, and those who follow him say that this objection applies to everyone; God is not questioned about what He does. We have already considered this response. In his critique, Tusi says: if this argument disproved human power and choice, it would also disprove God’s power and choice, since He knew from eternity what He would do in the future. His action in the future must be either necessary or impossible. He replies that knowledge follows what is known, so it does not make the known action necessary or impossible.

Taken at face value, this answer is not sound.[^V06-680] Knowledge follows its object only when the knowledge is passive and comes into being after the thing known.[^V06-681] God’s decree, however, is active knowledge: it is the cause of things’ existence, not something that follows them. Perhaps Tusi offered this answer on behalf of the Muʿtazilites, who say that things had a reality from eternity in their own right.

The right answer is that although God’s knowledge causes a human action to exist, it causes that action to occur through the person’s power and choice, which come before the action and are among its causes.[^V06-682] Necessity through choice does not cancel choice; it is what makes the action a chosen one. God’s essence is the active cause of everything that exists, and of its necessity. This does not remove the role of intermediate causes and conditions or the order connecting causes to effects. The same is true of God’s complete knowledge of everything, which is His essence in the case of simple knowledge, or follows from His essence in the case of detailed knowledge.

It is remarkable that this great debater sometimes let the truth show through, even though elsewhere he insisted on defending the Ashʿarite denial of cause and effect. In *al-Mabāḥith al-mashriqiyya*, he writes that, once examined carefully, the question of whether the world is eternal or created and the question of compulsion or free choice rest on the same point: whenever an agent’s power is only possible, its action cannot follow unless something else makes it happen. This is the key premise in both debates.

Since God’s power cannot be made necessary by anything separate from Him, it must be necessary through His own essence. And when His power is necessary through Himself, His action is eternal. A human being’s power, by contrast, cannot be necessary through the person’s own essence, since neither the person nor their power to act exists eternally. So human action must depend on God. It follows that human actions occur by God’s decree and measure.

Someone might object: if everything happens by God’s decree, what is the point of commands, prohibitions, reward, and punishment? And if God’s decree makes an action necessary or impossible, then our power cannot apply to it, since power cannot apply to what is necessary or impossible. Yet we know directly that we can act. This seems to disprove the account.

He answers that commands and prohibitions also occur by decree.[^V06-683] Reward and punishment follow from actions that occur by decree, just as bad food causes bodily illness, and false beliefs and wrong actions cause illness in the soul. The same is true of reward. As for power, an action’s being necessary does not prevent it from being something we can do. The action’s necessity follows from the necessity of the power; an effect does not cancel its cause. An action is not within our power only when it is necessary for some reason other than our power.[^V06-684]

He supports this by pointing out that people who hold this view say God must give reward and compensate people for suffering in the next life. If He failed to do what is required, that would imply either ignorance or need, both of which are impossible for God. So it is impossible for Him not to give reward and compensation; therefore, giving them is necessary. Yet this action is still within His power. So the kind of necessity described here does not prevent an action from being within the agent’s power.

This is closer to the truth than his other writings. The argument in *al-Muḥaṣṣal* that all acts of will must end in a necessary act of will, to avoid an infinite chain, does not reach this level. In his critique, Tusi says that before an action is linked to God’s decree, it either comes about without an intermediary or through one. The first does not show that all acts of will end in God’s will; the second does not rule out choice, since a chosen action is one that comes through power and will, whether these come from God directly or through another cause. Therefore, some actions occur by God’s decree precisely through the agent’s choice.

I say that there is a real difference between saying that nothing exists unless God is its cause, and saying that God alone is the cause of anything that exists.[^V06-685] The first is true; the second is not. As we explained in the previous chapter, the very respect in which an action is attributed to a human being is also the respect in which it is attributed to God. The action comes from the person in the very respect in which it comes from God.

This noble question—the oneness of actions—is among the greatest questions in divine philosophy. Though nearly all philosophers claim to have understood it, no one has reached it, as far as I can tell from reading their works, except someone to whom it has been revealed through certain proof and holy light. Tusi reports in his commentary on the *Ishārāt* that all philosophers agree that existence as a whole is caused by God. But who among those we know, whose books and words we have examined, has truly seen the oneness of God’s essence?

Another objection says that human will comes from outside us through causes that ultimately go back to God’s eternal will. So our will must occur whether we want it or not; God’s necessary will forces us into it. “You do not will unless God wills.” How can our actions be by our will if our will is not itself willed by us? If it were, there would be an endless chain of wills.[^V06-686]

The answer is that a person is a willing agent when their action follows their will,[^V06-687] not when their will itself follows another will. Otherwise, a person’s will could not be identical with their own self. A person has power when, if they will to act, they can act, and if they do not will to act, they do not act—not when they can will their own will. One could add that willing to will resembles knowing that one knows or the existence of existence: the mind can form these ideas repeatedly, without end, but the chain stops when the mind stops considering them. There is no chain of causes in the world outside the mind.[^V06-688]

Our honored teacher offers another answer. He says that when a chain of causes leads a person to picture an action and believe it to be good, desire for it arises. When that desire grows strong and settled, the will takes shape and sets the limbs and organs in motion. This will is one general state of longing in the soul. If we attend to the action itself, it is desire and will for that action. If instead we focus on the act of willing, the same state is desire and will for the act of willing. The same is true if we focus on willing the will, or on any further level the mind can consider in detail. Each detailed act of will is an act of will, but all are contained in that one general state of longing. Their order in thought does not prevent them from being one in the soul’s single felt state. This kind of division applies only to extended quantities and things that stretch through space. A distance, for example, cannot be divided into parts that are inherently earlier and later; its parts are earlier or later only by where they lie.

I reply, first, that the mind can analyze a thing into parts that it finds to be prior to the whole only when the thing really has distinct aspects in some level of reality. For example, even a simple quality such as blackness can be analyzed by the mind into a general element, color, and a distinguishing element, its power to darken sight. The mind treats these as prior to the specific quality and the distinguishing element as prior to the general one, even though all exist together as one thing. But where there are no such real aspects, dividing something into parts is only an invention of the mind.

Second, on this account, analyzing a will into many wills would make identical things gather in the same subject, which is impossible. They would not differ in essence, in what follows from them, in accidental qualities, or in the subject they belong to. It is also established that no individual of a single kind is the cause of another individual of that same kind, since neither has priority over the other in its own nature.[^V06-689]

Third, we can consider all the acts of will together and ask what causes them. If their cause is another act of will, that act would have to be both outside and inside the very same total group of wills, which is impossible. If their cause is something else, then the will is not free. That is the truth, and it is the answer to the objection.

The following passages support this view. Abū Naṣr al-Fārābī writes in *Fuṣūṣ al-ḥikam*: If someone thinks they act and choose whatever they want, ask whether their choice comes into being after not existing or has always existed. If it has always existed, then it has been with them since the beginning, and they are naturally fixed in that choice; it must come from something else. If the choice comes into being, it must have a cause. The cause must be either the person or something else. If the person causes the choice, either they cause it by choice—in which case the chain goes on without end—or it comes into being in them without their choosing, so that they are compelled in this choice by something else. The chain then ends in causes outside their choice, and ultimately in the eternal choice that has set everything as it is. If the explanation ended in another choice that came into being, the same question would arise again. So every event, good or evil, depends on causes that flow from the eternal will.

Avicenna says in the third section of the natural sciences in *The Healing* that every earthly event, including our choices and acts of will, comes into being after not existing. Every such event has a cause, and the chain of causes leads through movement to the circular movements of the heavens. Our choices, too, follow the movements of the heavens. The movements and pauses on earth form a stable, connected order that gives rise to our motives and prompts us to act. This is the decree God has set. Decree is the first, simple divine intellect, above all things, from which all things measured out branch forth.[^V06-690]

At the start of the tenth section of *The Healing*’s metaphysics, Avicenna says that all these things begin from nature, will, and chance. Nature has its source in the divine realm. Our acts of will arise after not existing, and every such event has a cause. Each act of will has a cause, but this chain does not go on without end: it is caused by external, earthly and heavenly events. The earthly causes lead back to heavenly ones, and together they bring about the will. Chance arises when these causes meet.[^V06-691] When we trace the chain back, we reach first principles established by God. God’s decree is the first simple arrangement; measure is the gradual unfolding of what decree sets in motion, as though it were the necessary result of a meeting of simple causes.[^V06-692][^V06-693]

This brings us to another objection. If everything exists through God’s knowledge, will, and decree, then every part of the order and every particle in the universe must necessarily exist in relation to His eternal will. How, then, can the sacred report say, “I have never hesitated over anything I was going to do as I hesitated over taking the soul of My believing servant”? This is a difficult problem for anyone who wants to reconcile rational principles with religious teaching. No scholar has offered a fully satisfying answer. Our teacher suggests that hesitation is caused by competing reasons that favor each side. The cause is named by its effect: the meaning is that taking a believer’s life is good for the order of existence but painful for the believer. In other words, an act may be good in itself and necessary for many goods, while also being bad for some beings. This is what the report calls hesitation.

I say that this answer does not resolve the problem; it makes it harder. It places a conflict within the act itself, between doing it and leaving it undone. If neither side is stronger, choosing one would be unjustified. If one side is stronger, and God knows that it is stronger, then the act must follow from that knowledge; there is no hesitation. The answer that came to me is this: as we have explained, the natural world continually renews itself through substantial change. Every assumed part of something that unfolds gradually is preceded by a prior potentiality in matter. That potentiality is itself the earlier part of the thing.

There are two ways to speak of what is possible. We may consider what a thing is, without supposing that it must exist or must not exist. Or we may consider a real readiness in matter for a change that has not yet happened. In neither case is the outcome necessary simply because it is possible. In anything that comes into being gradually—motion, according to most philosophers, and bodily nature, as we have argued—each earlier stage contains the capacity for the next. Such a thing exists in a changing and incomplete way: what is present gives way to what has not yet come. But when the cause that produces a new stage acts, that stage must come to be. What was merely possible has then become real through the readiness that preceded it.

With this in mind, the things in this world belong to God’s detailed knowledge. In one respect they are His knowledge; in another, they are what He knows. As knowledge, each is a kind of hesitation within knowledge; as something known, each is a possibility in existence. Human souls, especially those of believers, contain more of these possibilities and changes than other beings, because they pass through many states of existence: nonliving, plant, animal, and then the stages beyond these. At last, through a divine power that takes souls from their bodies and draws their forms from matter, they leave these changes behind and reach holiness and lasting life.

Just as the mind can understand a form apart from the matter in which it appears, the Angel of Death and his helpers draw souls and forms out of their bodies and matter. Such changes occur in the material world, and also in the heavenly souls that are imprinted with forms and make up the Tablet of Effacement and Confirmation. Any detailed image written in those souls may be erased or changed: their level allows for this. What cannot change is God’s essence, His real attributes, the world of command, His prior decree, and His eternal knowledge. The Qur'an says, “God erases what He wills and confirms what He wills; with Him is the Mother of the Book.” That is the clear Tablet, protected from erasure and change. It contains all the beings that differ and even oppose one another in the outer world, gathered in a single, intellectual, luminous reality, free of conflict and opposition. The Qur'an says, “Not a leaf falls without His knowing it; there is no grain in the darkness of the earth, nothing fresh or dry, that is not in a clear Book.”

So the changes, new events, and limitations found in individual beings and natural things do not sever their connection to the First Reality or the divine oneness. Nor do they compromise God’s eternal will, which is free of possibility, or His true and timeless knowledge, which is free of doubt and hesitation. These changes occur at levels through which knowledge and will unfold.[^V06-694]

Abū Naṣr says in *Fuṣūṣ al-ḥikam*: “Pass into oneness, and you will be struck with wonder at eternity. If you ask about it, oneness casts its shadow, and the whole comes into being. The pen writes creation on the tablet.” He also says: “The first knowledge is of God’s essence, and it is not divided. The second knowledge comes from His essence; when it becomes many, the multiplicity is not in His essence but after His essence. Not a leaf falls without His knowing it. From there, the pen writes on the tablet without end.” He says again: “Oneness beheld itself, then beheld power, and the second knowledge followed, containing multiplicity. From there, the world of command begins; the pen writes it on the tablet, and unity becomes many.”[^V06-695][^V06-696]

#### Chapter 13 — How to understand scriptural references to God’s hesitation and testing, and to a change in what is decreed

This also concerns the response to prayers and the rescue of people in distress, when these things occur alongside their requests and without the usual natural causes. Know that the divine reality has different levels, and the most beautiful names of God have their own manifestations.[^V06-697] We have discussed part of this in earlier stages of the First Journey.

In the layers of heaven, God has angelic servants whose level is that of souls, below the rank of the foremost and nearest beings. The latter belong to the world of command, which is free of change and renewal. Though the angelic servants are below them, all their actions obey God and are done at His command. They do not disobey Him in anything they do, will, imagine, desire, or inwardly incline toward. Whoever is like this acts in keeping with the Real and speaks truthfully: there is no desire in them that conflicts with God’s desire. Their will is absorbed in His will, and their choice in His choice.

Their obedience to God is like the way our senses obey the soul. They cannot act against what the soul wants, and they do not need to be commanded, forbidden, encouraged, or restrained. As soon as our rational soul intends a sensory action, the sense carries out that intention. The senses belong to a different level from the rational soul; they are lower in the inner world. In the same way, the angels in heaven obey God. Their very being makes them obedient to His command. With their inner hearing they listen to His revelation, and with their luminous hearts they feel His greatness. They are in awe of His beauty and majesty. They cannot rebel or disobey; they do what they are commanded. Their movements, stillness, thoughts, and images all arise through the Real and from the Real. This is the first principle.

A second principle: something can come about in a receptive body through its own capacities, through external causes, or through bodily arrangements. Heat, for example, may enter a body suited to receive it when an external source such as fire warms it. But something may also come about in a body without any material readiness or receptive capacity, through a higher cause and an inner source. A person’s body may become extremely hot because the soul pictures something frightening or stirs up anger, without any external source of heat. A person’s limbs may grow cold through fear, without a natural or external cause. And imagining something desired can move the limbs and produce moisture, even without the body’s usual fullness or natural preparation.

The matter of the world—the body of the great human being—can likewise be affected by the soul that governs it. Rare events may occur in the world without following the usual natural course or arising from the natural causes that ordinarily prepare for them. The earlier causes may even work against the result, or be suited to produce its opposite. This is why such events are rare and astonishing. This is the second principle.

Once these two principles are understood, we can say that every inscription in the heavenly tablets and records of destiny is also written by the First Reality, following the prior decree written by the Highest Pen in the Preserved Tablet. The heavenly records and tablets of destiny—the active angels’ hearts and the souls that govern the heavens—are all tablets of erasure and confirmation. The images written in their hearts, natures, and souls may disappear or change, because their level allows for change.[^V06-698] As we have explained in discussing the world’s coming-to-be and the renewal of nature, souls, and other powers connected to bodies, only God’s essence, His real attributes, the world of command, His prior decree, and His eternal knowledge are beyond change.

Scripture speaks of God as hesitating through these records and the angels who write in them. A report says, “I have never hesitated over anything I was going to do.” Scripture also speaks of God testing people: “We tested them with good and bad things,” “We test what you do,” and “Until We know which of you strive and remain steadfast.” The angel who records these divine decrees is noble, as the Qur'an says: “Noble scribes.” God dictates to that angel in a way suited to His care, which is free of change or coming into being. Without these receptive souls, where forms of will and knowledge can renew and earlier records can be replaced, everything would be fixed once and for all.[^V06-699] The flow of divine generosity would be limited to a set number of things, the path by which seekers rise from the lower world to the higher would be closed, and no one could draw near to God after the darkness of distance.

In short, without this, the stages by which every being returns to God would be impossible. Rational principles rule this out, and divine care does not allow it. So the renewal of knowledge and states in some angels—the noble scribes—is possible, not impossible or far-fetched.

When a prophet or saint connects with these records and reads what God has revealed and written in the heart, that person can tell others what they have seen with the eye of the heart and heard with the ear of the heart: the sound of those noble pens. Their words are true and direct, unlike the words of astrologers and fortune-tellers, who speak without certain, direct insight, relying instead on experience, guesswork, and the like.

When the prophet or saint connects to these records again, they may see something different from what they saw before—something unlike the earlier forms and unlike the natural causes then present in the heavens and on earth.[^V06-700] This is called abrogation, a change in what is decreed, or something similar. No heavenly or earthly soul can know it except through God, who keeps this knowledge to Himself. No natural cause brings it about, nor can it be inferred beforehand from the images and patterns already recorded. This is why reports from our Imāmī tradition quote Abū ʿAbd Allāh as saying, “God has two kinds of knowledge: a hidden, guarded knowledge that no one knows but Him. From this knowledge comes a change in what is decreed. He also has a knowledge He has taught to His angels, messengers, and prophets, and we know it.”[^V06-701]

Abū Jaʿfar is also reported to have said: “There are two kinds of knowledge. One is kept by God, hidden from every creature. The other He teaches to His angels, messengers, and prophets. What He teaches them will surely happen; God does not contradict Himself, nor do His angels or messengers. But from the knowledge He keeps hidden, He brings forward, delays, or confirms whatever He wills.”

In his book *The Beginning and the Return*, in a section on how earthly beings are governed and on kinds that are not preserved, Avicenna says that this care does not come from the First or from the separate intellects. It must come from a later source: either a soul spread through the world of coming-to-be and decay, or a heavenly soul.[^V06-702] Most people, he thinks, would say that it is a soul generated by the intellects and heavenly souls, especially the soul of the Sun or of the inclined sphere. It governs what lies beneath the Moon with the help of heavenly bodies and the light of the active intellect. In any case, this source of care must perceive individual things. Avicenna therefore thinks it is more likely to be a heavenly soul, able to imagine and sense events in a way suited to its own body.[^V06-703]

When an event occurs, this soul understands the perfection the event needs and the way to bring it about. Its understanding then gives rise to the corresponding form in matter. The soul that answers those who pray and ask for help may be this one. Avicenna says this seems likely: when the soul sees matter changing, it understands the good order and perfection that should be there. What it understands then comes to be.[^V06-704]

It may also perceive changes in the lives of those who dwell in this world and understand what must be done to remove a lack or evil and bring about good. The event then follows that understanding. Such a soul cares for every lack and evil that enters this world and its parts, so that good and order may follow. This care should not be confined to one thing and excluded from another. If a prayer is not answered or an evil is not removed, there is some hidden evil that we do not see, or divine care does not require its removal.

In another section, Avicenna describes rare events that come from this soul and even change nature. Since the soul’s understanding is followed by forms in matter, it is possible for a wrongdoer to die, a good person to be saved, or an unusual fire, earthquake, or other event to occur. A natural material may cool when it is hot, warm when it is cold, move when it is still, or become still when it is moving—not through its usual natural causes, but through the new natural cause that arises from this higher source. Animals or plants that ordinarily come from reproduction may also come about in other ways, with new forms unlike those of their usual parents. Such things follow from the understanding of this soul. We should not reject unfamiliar events in the world’s governance; rare and astonishing events have causes of the kind he has described.

Avicenna then tells a story he had heard. A physician attended the court of a Samanid ruler and was so trusted that he was invited to dine in the women’s quarters. No men entered there; female servants brought the food. One day a servant carrying the meal bent over from a sudden bout of gas and could not straighten up. The ruler asked the physician to treat her at once. The physician had no natural treatment that could cure her so quickly, so he turned to a psychological remedy. He first ordered that her hair be uncovered, but it had no effect. He then ordered that her belly be uncovered, still without effect. Finally, he ordered that she be uncovered below. As the women moved to do this, a strong heat rose in her body, dispersed the gas, and she sprang up, well again.

#### Chapter 14 — A fuller account of answered prayers and how to resolve the objection

Once you see clearly that all beings depend on God’s will, power, and care—His knowledge of the good in the order of things—you can also see that the parts of this order are connected and that effects follow their causes. Among the causes that bring events about is the presence of someone who calls on God and their prayer. Just as a person’s existence, knowledge, power, will, and choice are among the causes of an action, prayer, asking, persistence, and humility before God are among the causes by which a request is granted and what is sought comes about, by God’s permission and through His realm.

Prayer may reach the world of command and affect the hearing of the angels in heaven. As we said in the previous chapter, there is a soul-like being in the heavens that can be affected by earthly events and can in turn affect the earth: it warms what is cold, cools what is hot, moves what is still, stills what is moving, and changes one element into another, with help from above and support from the light of the intellectual beings. It is affected by the illnesses and calamities it observes in this world, unless they are balanced by what brings good and well-being. In its receptive soul and intellect, it forms an understanding of what must be done to remove evil and bring about good.

This being is not purely an active intellect, unaffected by anything, nor is it purely passive, unable to affect anything except through help from above. It acts on what is below it and is also affected by what is below it. So it may be moved by the prayers of people in distress and the cries of those in need, and may answer them by God’s permission, meet their needs, and grant their requests.

We have already said that only the universal intellectual world of command is wholly free of change and response to anything else. The principle that a higher being is not affected by a lower one applies only to what is higher in every respect, or to what is higher in the very respect in question. But a soul-like being—even one of the heavens—can be affected by some earthly events, especially by the noble rational souls among us. It may turn toward them, help fulfill their requests, and answer their prayers. This does not conflict with its being higher than them in other respects.

For this reason, the claim in Avicenna’s *Notes* that the heavenly beings cannot be affected by earthly beings needs qualification. He says that we may imagine that the heavens are affected by us when we pray and they answer, even though we are their effects and they are our causes. But, as we have explained, this is not always true. Do not follow those who know truth only through the authority of other people.

Now consider the objection: if what we ask for has already been set down in the eternal decree and written in the divine measure, why go to the trouble of asking for it? And if it has not been written there, why ask for something that cannot happen? The answer is that asking and praying are also part of the decree and measure: they are among the causes and conditions that bring about the very thing decreed.[^V06-705] Whenever God decrees that something happen, He also decrees its causes and conditions; otherwise, He has not decreed it. When God wills a thing, He prepares its causes. Among the causes of the thing we pray for are the caller’s prayer, humility, and need.

Prayer and remembrance are to the fulfillment of our requests in the world what thought and reflection are to the discovery of conclusions and knowledge in the mind. So prayers and invocations are channels flowing from the sea of decree and streams flowing from the rivers of destiny.

Avicenna writes in his *Notes* that a prayer is answered when its causes coincide in keeping with divine wisdom. The cause of someone’s prayer and the cause of the thing they ask for both come from God and meet at the right time. Could the thing have happened without the prayer? No: the same God made the prayer a cause for the thing, just as He made medicine a cause of recovery. A sick person does not recover without taking the medicine; in the same way, the requested event does not come without the prayer that is meant to bring it about. For a reason, God brings the prayer and the event together as He has decreed. Prayer is necessary, and so is expecting an answer. God causes us to pray, and our prayer becomes a cause of the answer, meeting with the event it was made to bring about.

Sometimes a person’s prayer is not answered, even when the goal they seek seems beneficial. The reason, Avicenna says, is that a beneficial goal must serve the order of the whole, not only what that person wants. What seems beneficial to the individual may not truly be so. In that case, the prayer is not answered.

He also says that when a pure soul prays, the First Reality may give it power to affect the elements, which then respond and change according to its will. This is one way a prayer is answered.[^V06-706] We can see something similar in our own bodies: imagining something may change the body in keeping with the soul’s state and images. A soul may also affect a body other than its own or another person’s soul, as in stories about the powers of imagination among Indian sages, if those stories are true. The first principles may respond to such a soul when it asks for something that benefits the whole order.

Avicenna says that no prayer is impossible to answer. It can be known to God, even if He knows it through the person who prays. Whatever is known to Him can exist, provided that no other known thing prevents it. For example, someone may pray for another person to perish, expecting that this will happen if their bodily balance is ruined. But God may also know from another cause that the person’s balance must remain sound. Then the prayer cannot be answered. “From another cause” means from the causes that keep the body balanced. If God knows through those causes that the body need not remain sound, there is no opposing cause, and the prayer can be answered. That is why no one should pray for another person’s ruin: God already knows that the prayer will be made, and whatever He knows can happen.[^V06-707]

Avicenna also says that the First Reality is the cause of all things known to Him and of their necessity, but in an ordered way. He is the cause of causes, and so the cause of what He knows. In one respect, He knows one thing before another; in another, one thing is the cause of His knowing another. For example, He is the cause of knowing the First Intellect, and the First Intellect is the cause of knowing what follows from it. So, although He is the cause of knowing the First Intellect and its effects, in a certain respect the First Intellect becomes the cause of His knowing its own effects. Prayer works in the same way. God is the true cause of the prayer and of the person who prays. Then the person’s prayer becomes the cause of God’s knowing that prayer. In this respect the person is a cause of God’s knowing the prayer, though in truth the person does not affect God; God is the one who causes it. This is the end of Avicenna’s account in the *Notes*.[^V06-708]

As we will explain later, the philosophers say that the souls of the heavenly bodies know the consequences, aims, longings, and motives of their movements. Everything that exists in this world—substances, qualities, forms, and matter—has its shape, arrangement, and measure set in another world between the world of pure intellects and intellectual forms, and the world of natural bodies and material forms.[^V06-709] In his books, including *The Healing* and *The Directives and Remarks*, Avicenna says that heavenly souls and higher beings know particular things. Our changing thoughts and acts of will have earthly and heavenly causes that meet and bring them about. The same is true of natural events that arise after not existing, and of events forced upon things. These causes crowd together and clash, yet their ongoing interaction forms an order beneath the movement of the heavens.[^V06-710]

When the first heavenly causes know their own effects and the way they lead to the next causes, they also know what follows from them. Heavenly souls have some power to act on particular meanings and to perceive particular images, as well as universal meanings and the separate intellects they resemble.[^V06-711] They can perceive events through the separate details of their active and receptive causes and what follows from them. These causes ultimately lead to a natural or voluntary cause that is decisive, not merely a passing desire.[^V06-712]

Every act of will comes into being after not existing and has causes that meet and bring it about. It does not arise from yet another act of will, which would lead to an endless chain, nor from the agent’s nature alone, which would make the will arise whenever that nature is present. Rather, a will arises when causes and motives come together from both earthly and heavenly sources and make it necessary. Nature, if it is already present, is a basic source; if it has newly arisen, it must also depend on earthly and heavenly causes.[^V06-713]

Avicenna concludes that heavenly souls know particular things, while what lies above them knows particulars in a universal way. The heavenly souls know them directly, or through what reaches a direct perceiver, or as things seen by the senses. They know what will happen and, in many cases, which of two possible outcomes is more fitting, more beneficial, and closer to pure good.

We have explained that the images understood by these causes give rise to the forms found here. If such an outcome is possible and there are no stronger heavenly causes opposing it, then it must come about—not from an earthly cause or an ordinary natural cause in heaven,[^V06-714][^V06-715] but through the influence of these events on heavenly things. Strictly speaking, they do not themselves cause the effect. Its cause is the heavenly source of its existence. When the heavenly source understands the first causes, it understands what follows from them, including which possible outcome is better. Once it understands that outcome, it brings it about, unless an opposing cause prevents it.

For example, heat might arise even though no earthly source is heating the body. The heavenly soul understands that warmth is good and produces an image of it. This is like the way a person’s thoughts may warm their own body. Or an earthly cooling cause may be present. The heavenly image of the good that comes from warmth can overcome it, just as an angry person’s thoughts can overcome the cooling effect in their body. Events of this kind are changes, not ordinary natural processes. They may be inspirations that reach the person who asks, or someone else, or a mixture of events that leads to a useful result. Prayer calls for this power as thought calls for understanding; both ultimately come from above.

This is not the source of heavenly images themselves. The First Reality knows all of this in the way proper to Him, and all events begin from Him, though they come about through intermediaries. Prayer and offerings can therefore bring benefit, especially in asking for rain and other needs. We should fear the consequences of evil and hope for the reward of good: this is a deterrent from evil, and its truth becomes clear through particular events. The heavenly sources understand this, so it must also have a real existence. If it does not happen, there is an evil we do not understand or another cause that prevents it; in that case, the other outcome is more fitting than the one we asked for.

Avicenna adds that we can see how things understood to be beneficial and useful come to exist in nature in this way by looking at the uses of organs in animals and plants. Each is made for its purpose, though no ordinary natural cause explains how that purpose was first set. Its source must be divine care. So we should accept that these things exist through the same kind of care.

He also says that the causes of our prayers, charity, injustice, and sin all lead back to nature, will, and chance. Nature has its source in the divine realm. Our wills arise after not existing, and everything that comes into being has a cause. Our wills are caused by earthly and heavenly events, which together bring them about. Chance occurs when these causes meet. When we trace everything back, we reach first principles established by God. His decree is the first simple arrangement; measure is its gradual unfolding, as the meeting of simple causes produces particular results. This is the end of Avicenna’s account.

I have followed Avicenna’s words closely because they contain much that is useful about how events that come into being are linked to God’s will, knowledge, and care, without attributing change, passivity, or a low purpose to Him. Still, his account needs to be completed in two ways.

First, he avoids saying that anything in the heavens can be affected by something on earth. Yet we have often heard of—and seen—rain, earthquakes, sinkholes, and similar acts of mercy or punishment occurring alongside the prayers of devout people and the requests of those in need. We also hear of events connected with prophets and saints that make it clear that some heavenly source and some of God’s angels respond to events among people on earth: they hear their prayers and turn toward answering them.[^V06-716][^V06-717][^V06-718] Several Qur'anic verses say this plainly and cannot be explained away. God says to Noah, “Build the Ark under Our eyes and by Our revelation,” and to Moses and Aaron, “Do not fear; I am with you. I hear and see.” To the final Prophet He says, “When We recite it, follow its recitation; then it is for Us to make it clear.”[^V06-719][^V06-720]

Second, Avicenna does not accept that the beings of this world exist in a higher world as immaterial forms, free from matter, limitation, possibility, evil, nonexistence, and darkness. He criticizes those who affirm such separate forms, including Plato, Socrates, and earlier masters of knowledge and wisdom. We have already established their existence through philosophical explanation and proof.[^V06-721] Avicenna should have explained[^V06-722] how events that come into being connect to the world of destiny and correspond to it, just as a perfect copy matches its model. He should also have explained how the world of destiny connects to the simple world of decree, matches it, and is one with it. He could not do so because he denied that the simple intellect is one with all the things it understands, and did not see that a truly simple reality must contain all things. Nothing in heaven or earth escapes it.

For this reason, Avicenna treats God’s knowledge of things as a collection of qualities and images residing in Him. He then says that, though they are qualities in Him, they do not affect or complete Him because they follow His essence rather than precede it. His perfection and glory lie in their flowing from Him, not in their existing for Him. You have already seen the limits and flaws in this account. God is the source of generosity and grace.

### Station Five — God is living

The life we know in this world involves awareness and action. For most animals, awareness means sensation, and action means movement through space, arising from desire.[^V06-723] These two effects come from two different powers, one that perceives and one that moves. But if a being’s awareness were higher than sensation—intellect, for example—and its action were nobler than directly causing movement—bringing things into existence, for example—then it would have an even better claim to be called living.

And if the very same simple reality were the source both of its awareness and its action, so that its awareness were itself its action and creative power, it would have an even stronger claim to that name, since it would be free of composition. Composition requires possibility and dependence: a composite thing depends on something else to hold it together. Possibility is a kind of absence opposed to existence, and death is opposed to life. The truly living being is therefore one whose powers are not assembled from parts.

We have shown that the Necessary Being is simple in reality, one in essence and attribute, and utterly one in power. His very understanding of things is their coming from Him. One simple reality is the intellect of all things and the source of all things. He therefore has a greater and more fitting claim to the name “living” than any other living being. He gives life to all things and gives existence—and the perfections of existence, such as knowledge and power—to everything that exists.

Consider an image of knowledge that arises in us and then leads us to make something. If the image alone were enough to produce the made thing—if the image itself were already the active source of what it represents—then our knowing would be the same as our power to make. We would be able to make whatever we know, and what we know would be one and the same as what we make. But that is not the case. We often know things we desire but cannot bring about.

Even for something we can make, such as writing, knowing what it is does not by itself make it real. We also need a fresh act of will, arising from desire. These together set the moving power to work. That power moves the nerves and bodily organs; the organs move external tools; and those tools act on the material we work with, such as paper or a board. So in us, knowledge is not itself power or will. Power begins with the source of movement. Nor is will itself power, or power itself action: power is the capacity to act and bring something about. The image first stirs desire and will; they move the power; the power moves the bodily instrument; and that instrument moves what it acts upon. Each of these movers is itself moved, because each is mixed with limitation, lack, and possibility.

If any one of them were complete and powerful in its own way, it would not need the others in order to act. If our mental image were vivid and complete enough, for example, it could itself give rise to the action and bring it about. As we have said, God’s knowledge of the most complete order is itself His will. His power is His essence understanding all things—not as something learned from them, but as the source of them all. This first intellect is also His eternal will, free of any aim beyond itself, and the existence of all beings follows from it, just as loving someone also leads us to love what belongs to them. God loves everything He creates because He loves His own essence. This is His will, free of every flaw. Anyone who believes otherwise has left the right path and misunderstood God’s attributes and names.

This is also how we must understand God as living: He is the one who perceives and acts. We have explained what perception and action mean; in Him, they are one reality, though we can speak of them under different descriptions.

As we have explained, God’s essential attributes all exist through one existence, and He deserves their names because their meanings truly apply to Him. His relational attributes also return to one relation. The same existence in Him is knowledge, power, and life; and when related to what He creates, it is called knowing, creating, providing, making, and willing. If you understand the principles we have set out, you will see that the first attribute of the Necessary Being is simply that He exists: pure existence, without any essence or limitation mixed into it.[^V06-724][^V06-725] Other attributes either mean this existence together with a relation, or this existence considered without something that does not belong to it. None of them introduces multiplicity into His essence.

When we call Him “one,” we mean this existence without any division—whether into measurable parts, real parts such as matter and form, or conceptual parts such as broader kind and difference. When we call Him “one and unique,” we mean that this existence has no partner in its kind or in its necessity. When we call Him intellect, knower, and known, we mean that this existence is free of the possibility of being mixed with matter and its attachments, while also standing in a relation to what it knows. This is the account found in *The Healing*.

In our view, the negative element is not part of the meaning of perception or understanding, even if it follows from it in reality. Perception means that something exists for, or is present to, another. Matter and material things are not present to themselves or to another in this way. Because they are bound up with their own limited existence, they remain unknown unless another image of them is present to the knower. The stronger the existence, the stronger the perception. The strongest forms of existence are the intellectual images, in degrees of strength, followed by images in imagination and then images in the senses. When existence becomes so weak that it takes material form, it is hidden from perception and cannot be grasped or made present to the knower.

When we call God “the First,” we mean that this existence is related to all things as their active source. When we call Him “the Last,” we mean that it is related to them as their final aim. When we call Him “powerful,” we mean that the necessary existence of everything else follows from Him.

When we call Him “living,”we mean this existence considered together with its relation to the intelligible forms of all things as their knower and maker.[^V06-726]

As for the life of every living thing, it is a way of existing: a being is alive when it can perform the actions of living things, which arise from knowledge and power. But some beings need an additional state before they can act in this way, while others do not.

The first kind includes living bodies. They become capable of life only after another state has come about in them. If a body’s existence were already the very same as its ability to perform the actions of life, every body would be alive. If that ability comes to a body later, it does not come simply because it is a body, but because of something else that distinguishes it. Nor can you say that this new state—the ability to act in a living way—is what makes the body exist at all.[^V06-727] That would apply only to body considered as a general kind, not to body considered as matter. We are discussing the essence of body itself, apart from what is added to it. The existence a body has simply as a body is not connected with animal life. That life is something added to it.

The second kind is what lies beyond bodies. In beings that are not bodies, their existence may itself be their capacity for life; in most cases, it must be so.[^V06-728] Separate substances and immaterial forms have this character: their existence itself is their life. They are not made up of matter and form, since their formal existence is not tied to something merely potential. Life in them is not something added that makes them alive; it is simply their being alive. Otherwise, the thing would have to become alive through the very existence it already has, which would make it depend on itself, or else on another existence. That would contradict the assumption that it exists apart from matter, where one state comes after another. And if we then asked about that added existence, we would be led into an endless chain. As we have explained in discussing existence, relation, place, and continuity, life and the like are ways of existing. The Necessary Being, whose reality is simple, has an even greater claim to have life identical with His existence.
