The meaning of “life” differs from the meanings of “knowledge” and “power.”Life is in a way their source. Many later thinkers, not distinguishing meaning from reality, have disagreed about whether life in God is another attribute added to perception and action. They then relied on scripture to establish that He is living, saying that reason alone does not establish this attribute.[^V06-729]

The truth is that life, like the other attributes of perfection, is a perfection of existence simply as existence. Whatever is a perfection of existence without being limited to a particular kind of nature, size, or number must belong to the source of existence and its giver. The source that gives existence and its perfections has the strongest claim to that perfection.

Still, the meaning of life is not the same as the meanings of knowledge and power. One can imagine a living being that, at a given time, knows nothing and performs no action. It would still be alive.[^V06-730] In us, life is the state from which awareness and action arise. The powers of awareness and action are like instruments of this state, which is their source. This cannot apply to God, who is far above different powers and inner instruments. His life, like His knowledge, power, and will, exists at the same level, as one reality with one identity. Yet the meaning of life differs from that of knowledge; both differ from power, and power differs from will. Proof establishes this perfection in God in a higher and nobler way, just as it establishes His knowledge, power, and will in a higher and nobler way.

One clarification: Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi says in his treatise on knowledge that people wanted to describe God by the better of two opposites. Since they called Him knowing and powerful, and found that anything without life could not be described by these terms, they also called Him living—especially since life is nobler than its opposite, death. This reasoning is flawed and incomplete. Not everything nobler than its opposite[^V06-731] can be attributed to God. Solidity is nobler than softness, motion than rest, and the sphere is the noblest of shapes. To attribute a perfection to God, it must be a basic attribute of existence as such. Otherwise, it cannot belong to Him.

The divine philosopher, when establishing that God is living, must first give a meaning of life that can be a perfection of existence in general. It must not depend on a special capacity or on belonging to one kind of being, nor may it bring multiplicity, change, or dependence on anything else. Once life is understood in this way, it can be attributed to the Necessary Being, the source of all other beings and their perfections. The giver of perfection has a greater claim to it than anything else.

Al-Tusi also cites a report from one of the Imams: “Was He called knowing and powerful for any reason other than that He gave knowledge to those who know and power to those who are powerful? Whatever you can imagine, however subtle, is a created thing like you and belongs to you. God is the giver of life and the ordainer of death. Perhaps tiny ants imagine that God has two horns[^V06-732] like theirs, and think that anyone without them is deficient. That is how people are when they describe God. God is the one to whom we turn for refuge.[^V06-733]” This report is noble and true. It points[^V06-734] toward the way we have established God’s attributes of perfection: He gives knowledge, power, and life, which are perfections of existence as such. When a perfection exists in a caused thing, it must exist in its cause in a higher and nobler way.

The report also says that human understanding cannot reach the full reality of the attributes we use of God. This applies not only to life but to all His attributes. The real attributes stand in the same relation as existence and necessity. Just as existence is necessary in God while the meaning of existence is shared by all beings, so there is knowledge that exists necessarily in God while the meaning of knowledge is shared by eternal and created knowledge. The same holds for power, will, and all the real attributes whose meanings apply both to God and to possible beings.

Yet there are people[^V06-735] who know the truth through the truth itself and know God’s attributes through His attributes, not by comparing them with created beings. They have gone beyond the ordinary state of the soul, left behind their separate selves, and awakened to the final reality. They see what others do not see.

### Station Six — God hears and sees

Our true religious law teaches—and this is among the basic truths of our clear faith, known from the Qur'an, widely transmitted reports, and the community’s consensus[^V06-736]—that the Creator hears and sees. Scholars have differed over whether hearing and sight fall under knowledge in general, meaning knowledge of things heard and seen, or whether they are two attributes distinct from knowledge.

Some theologians, including the leading Imami scholars among our own teachers and the scholar al-Tusi, as well as al-Ashʿarī and his followers, agree here with most philosophers. Those philosophers deny that God knows particulars or anything other than Himself in the specific, direct way in which such things are present to Him. These scholars therefore explain hearing and sight as forms of knowledge: hearing is knowledge of what is heard, and sight is knowledge of what is seen.

Others treat hearing and sight as two kinds of sensory perception. They do so either because they believe God has a body or directly contacts bodies—God is far above what wrongdoers say—or because they believe that, since God has no limitation or need, He can sense without an organ, even though our limited condition requires one. This claim is unclear. If a deeply learned sage made it, it might have a sound meaning. But many theologians and debaters have not seen that sensing by itself points to a limitation in both the one who senses and the thing sensed.

In short, either God’s hearing and sight mean sensory awareness in the ordinary sense, or they mean His general knowledge of things that can be heard and seen. The truth is that hearing and sight mean something different from knowledge in general. They are two specific kinds of knowledge beyond it. If their meanings can apply to everything that exists, simply as something that exists, without implying a body, change, or being affected, then we must affirm them of God.

You have already learned that sensation alone does not make a thing particular or individual. What makes it individual is its own distinct way of existing. Existence and individuality are one in reality, though we use different words for them. You have also learned that God knows external things as individuals: their own existence is their presence before Him and their being known to Him. His direct, illuminating awareness of things heard and seen goes beyond His general knowledge of them, even when that general knowledge is universal, as it is when He knows them through concepts.[^V06-737][^V06-738] Directly witnessing what is heard is hearing; directly witnessing what is seen is sight. So His hearing and sight cannot be reduced to knowledge in general. Indeed, it would be closer to the truth to say the reverse, as the Illuminationist master did: God’s knowledge returns to His sight, rather than His sight to His knowledge.[^V06-739]

#### A Throne-like clarification

What sight really is differs from what most people think. It does not happen when an image of the thing seen is imprinted on the eye and affects it, as natural philosophers say. Nor does it happen when a beam travels in a cone between the eye and the thing seen, whether that beam is a physical body or something else. And it does not happen, as the Illuminationist master held, because the soul gains direct awareness of a material image through the body and its position in relation to that image. We reject all these accounts, as we explain elsewhere.

In our view, sight happens when the soul brings an image into being and that image is present to it in the world of images.[^V06-740][^V06-741] This image is free of natural matter. The soul relates to it as a maker relates to what it makes, not as something that receives a quality and is perfected by it.

Sight still depends on the eye, on a natural image, on a certain position between them, on the absence of anything blocking them, and on other conditions. But these are not needed because sight is impossible without them; otherwise, we could not see in sleep. They are needed because the soul is weak at the beginning of its life and depends on bodily matter, both for its existence and for its perceptions.

The soul’s existence is not the same as the body’s, even though it depends on the body. In the same way, sight does not take place through the eye, even though it depends on the eye. As the soul can eventually do without the natural body after reaching a certain level of maturity, it can also come to perceive particular things visually without relying on the organs or their physical effects. We have explained this fully in our discussion of the soul. Anyone who looks at the matter fairly and clearly will see that sight can occur without an organ or its being affected, and without the soul itself being affected, because the soul makes the images it sees; it does not receive them from elsewhere.

Once we establish that a soul free from the natural world can perceive particular visual images through its own sight—which is identical with its own being—we can see that sight, in general, belongs to anything that exists simply as something that exists. It does not require a body, change, or being affected. Sight is a perfection, and lacking it is a deficiency. God is more worthy of this perfection than anything else.

The same is true of hearing. People commonly say that hearing happens when sound strikes or tears through the air, which must then ripple toward a cartilage organ lined with nerves. The moving air affects the soul, which wakes to the sounds, letters, and words and perceives them. But this account is not correct.

Some of the greatest sages have said that the heavens make beautiful sounds and noble melodies as they move. They say that Pythagoras rose to them himself and heard their lovely music with a subtle mind, then wrote the science of music.

We can see that hearing does not always require a natural organ or moving air because a person may hear a loud sound in sleep, such as thunder, and be deeply affected by it—no less than by a sound heard while awake. In sleep, the soul is connected to another world. Our discussion of speech will make the truth of this clear, God willing. Praise belongs to God, at the beginning and at the end. This completes the first part of Divine Philosophy. The second part follows in the Seventh Station, on God’s being the speaker, and on the truths contained in the discussion of speech and the Book.

We did our best to correct this text, comparing it with handwritten copies and copies corrected by experts in the field—may God reward their fine efforts. Thanks be to God, it appeared almost free of errors, except for a very few that escaped notice.

Ibrāhīm al-Amīnī — Fatḥ Allāh al-Āmidī

Shaʿbān 1386 AH.

## Notes

[^V06-1]: The opening praise uses many kinds of beauty in its words and meaning: for example, fitting the introduction to the subject and book, and pairing opposite or related ideas. It brings together God's being, attributes, actions, and effects; light, shining, rays, and appearing; bringing into being, creating, making, and forming; intellects, souls, bodies, forms, and readiness; and pages, writing, words, reading, revelation, and clear and hidden meanings. The same pattern continues in the lines of thanks and blessings that follow. Calling God the “Necessary Being” and saying that He shines with light also points to the different language used by Aristotle’s school and the philosophers of illumination. The first speak of God as necessary and true existence; the second call Him the Light of Lights and the self-sufficient light. — س قده

[^V06-2]: By calling the intelligences “lasting,” the author means that their essences are lasting, since their existence is a ray from God's own being. The same applies to souls. He says that the intelligences come from the ray of God's being, but describes the souls as coming from His attributes because intellects belong to the divine realm, while souls are also connected to the world of images. Another possibility is that “attributes” here means God's active qualities, which are the intelligences. — س قده

[^V06-3]: “Motions” means the stages by which beings grow toward perfection. “Causes” means earthly causes, since the author has already named the souls and bodies of the heavens. It is better to add new information than repeat what has been said. Also, “forming” usually means bringing something into being after matter and time already exist. The heavenly spheres are created directly; they are not formed from prior matter. — س قده

[^V06-4]: The Prophet's gift of words that gather great meaning reflects the way his revealed Qur'an gathers all the words of existence in the outer and inner worlds. His spiritual reality is the Universal Intellect, which gathers all the higher and lower intellects. The human intellects of this world were made from what remained of his clay. The letters Ya-Sin in revelation also point to the all-embracing nature of the final complete human being: the letter Ya points to the ten levels of descent and ascent, and Sin, whose written form and sound are balanced, points to the complete human being, whose knowing and acting powers are balanced. In this way, all ten levels of existence are gathered in the human being. — س قده

[^V06-5]: Here “general knowledge” means the broad study of existence and the broad sense of divine philosophy. It considers the Necessary Being and possible beings in general, as ideas. The more specific study of divine philosophy considers the reality named by the idea “Necessary Being”: it proves that God exists and studies His attributes and actions. A person may know some features of a thing while still doubting that the thing exists. For example, one may be certain that the maker of the world would be knowing, powerful, and willing, yet still doubt that such a maker exists. — س قده

[^V06-6]: The author says “fellow human beings,” not “offspring of the same species,” because he means people in the ordinary sense. He may also be hinting at the point established later: inwardly, humanity is one kind; outwardly, it appears as different kinds. — س قده

[^V06-7]: The early philosophers said instead that God's giving never stops, His words never run out, and His light never sets. The world depends on Him forever. But they did not deny that the world began or came to an end. Religious thinkers use the world's beginning to prove its maker, and its passing away to explain judgment and the reaching of final goals. So a seeker of wisdom should not say, “I learned one thing but missed many others.”

    They also did not deny God's power. They denied only that He must act, and affirmed that He can act or not act. They openly say that a maker can act by choice: many theologians hold that God has a distinct power, while philosophers say His power belongs to His own being. Nor did they deny God's knowledge of particular things. They rejected only knowledge that changes in response to events. They affirm knowledge of particular things through their causes, which does not change. Finally, they did not deny resurrection. They denied only that the present worldly state remains exactly as it is, since that would mean no change or passing away in the world to come. They hold that the body's reality remains, though it changes from a worldly body into a body fit for the lasting life. Anyone who reads this philosopher's books will see all these points. — س قده

[^V06-8]: “Whomever you will” means any human being. God's love and choice even include people who show His power through opposition, such as Abu Jahl and Abu Lahab. They are included in the reach of God's commands. God's commands show His will, and His prohibitions show what He does not approve. A saying from the Prophet points to this distinction: “God commanded Iblis to bow to Adam, but He did not will him to do so.”

[^V06-9]: “He returned with the two shoes of Hunayn” is an Arabic saying for someone who comes back disappointed and empty-handed.

[^V06-10]: “Open-heartedness” means having a broad spirit and not objecting to things simply because God made them. A narrow heart objects to the existence of a sinner, or even an unbeliever, whether or not it says so aloud. A sound nature means balanced bodily materials and a good basic temperament. Good judgment means a steady mind. “Taste” means a feel for spiritual knowledge; “unveiling” means direct insight into matters that reasoning alone cannot settle. The author also calls this insight intuition, as in matters about the heavens and similar subjects. — س قده

[^V06-11]: This is the key point. A saying passed down from the Imams puts it this way: “I knew my Lord through my Lord.” Someone asked a spiritual knower, “How did you know your Lord?” He answered, “Through what my Lord sends into my heart.” A poem expresses the same idea: “When the lover longed to see her but could not, / out of kindness she lent him an eye. / He saw her through it; / the eye that saw her was hers.” — س قده

[^V06-12]: True faith is certain belief. Belief in God includes belief in His attributes. Belief in the angels includes belief in the higher and lower intellects. Belief in the Books includes belief in the universal souls and the souls impressed on matter. Belief in the messengers includes belief in the intellects that rise toward God. It also includes belief in the return to God. — س قده

[^V06-13]: The verse denies that they know the source of things. To know an effect as an effect is to know its cause. The words “or the creation of themselves” deny knowledge of the return, since knowledge of the return rests on knowing the human soul. “I do not take those who lead others astray as helpers” means they have been left without help; “helpers” here is a part standing for the whole. — س قده

[^V06-14]: The principles for weighing claims may be logical or may concern the subject matter. Here the author means principles about the material of a proof, as in the art of demonstration; he has not yet discussed the forms of arguments. — س قده

[^V06-15]: This wording appears in the manuscripts. In *The Beginning and the Return*, the wording is better: “From the Living, the Self-Sustaining, to the living, the self-sustaining.” That better fits the way Arabic letters traditionally begin, with a sender and a recipient, such as “From Zayd son of Amr to Bakr son of Khalid.” — س قده

[^V06-16]: Abu Dharr was among those who went with the Prophet to Tabuk. As the Prophet rode on, Abu Dharr's camel fell behind. He took his belongings, carried them on his back, and walked after the Prophet. The Prophet stopped at one of the camps. Someone saw a man walking alone and said, “Messenger of God, there is a man on the road by himself.” The Prophet said, “Let him be Abu Dharr.” When the group looked closely, they said, “Messenger of God, by God, it is Abu Dharr.” *The Life of the Prophet*, vol. 4, p. 178.

[^V06-17]: This is the station of nearness through voluntary acts. In the higher station of nearness through required duties, Ali is described as God's receptive ear, seeing eye, and outstretched hand. — س قده

[^V06-18]: “Rough” means hard, the opposite of easy. Thorns are sharp growths from plants. “Ruggedness” means rough, uneven ground.

[^V06-19]: You may think that God is hidden from the intellect just as He is hidden from the eyes. If the senses, imagination, and estimation cannot point to Him, you may think that the intellect cannot point to Him either. Know that this kind of claim to honor God's greatness can end in denying Him altogether.

    Some people think they are protecting God's holiness, but in truth they deny Him. If they hear talk of knowing God's essence, attributes, and actions, they say that knowing His essence is meaningless because, as the philosopher himself says, God's essence cannot be fully grasped. They do not understand that there is another meaning here, and that this way of thinking belongs in a public sermon, not in a careful discussion. Even the theologian Fakhr al-Din al-Razi, known for raising doubts, did not doubt that knowledge of God, His attributes, and His actions is the noblest knowledge. He said only that there is doubt about whether existence is the same as God's essence or is added to it.

    This denier does not understand that knowing that God is the Necessary Being, or deciding whether His existence is His essence or something added to it, is knowledge of His essence. This does not mean that we fully grasp what God is. Who ever said that knowledge requires complete grasp? As for “intellectual pointing,” these people have a problem with their own position. Remembering God is itself a kind of pointing to Him. And if we claim to know Him through His attributes, those attributes are either the same as His essence or something other than it. If they are other, they depend on His essence.

    The answer, as we said before, is that we know God through a light borrowed from Him and through sight He gives us. In truth, only God knows His essence through Himself. His being one is Himself being one; the one who describes Him cannot make Him many. Intellectual pointing to His names is possible, but what we say of the name must be referred to the one named. The name has no existence as a name apart from the one it reveals. In one sense, the name differs from what it names; in another, it is that very thing. When the name is a tool for seeing what it names, then remembering the name is remembering God, not something else. The spoken and written forms of His names are ways He appears to us, and a thing's appearance is not separate from it. But if we treat the names as objects in themselves, rather than as ways of seeing God, then they are not God. They are created things, returned to you in their finest forms. — س قده

[^V06-20]: This passage has a plain meaning suited to philosophers and a deeper meaning for those who know through direct insight. In the plain sense, the many paths are the paths of existence, possibility, beginning, motion, and other such proofs. God's many perfections and aspects are His attributes. Different groups of thinkers follow different paths, and the path through existence is the strongest.

    In the deeper sense, “the paths to God are as many as the breaths of His creatures.” God's perfections and aspects are His most beautiful names and highest attributes. Every kind of being and every particular reality known to God appears under one of His names. For example, a king shows the name “Holy,” a heavenly sphere the name “Everlasting,” an animal the names “Hearing” and “Seeing,” a jinn the name “Subtle and Aware,” and a human being the name “God.” Each is drawn to worship the name it reveals. The human being, especially the complete human being, serves God and reveals the fullest name, the Greatest Name. This is the widest path, as the Qur'an says: “This Qur'an guides to what is most upright.” — س قده

[^V06-21]: This argument is still an indirect proof: it moves from one feature that follows from existence to another. First, it says that existence is a graded reality, with a pure and complete level and lower levels mixed with lack. Then it says that the pure, complete level must be necessary. When the author says that the path is the goal itself, and that it moves from God to God, then from Him to His attributes and from His attributes to His actions, he means that this indirect proof is closer than the others to a proof from the cause. Otherwise, God's essence cannot cause itself or its own attributes, since His attributes are His essence. Nor can reason travel from a thing to itself, or from a thing to attributes that are the thing itself. — May God preserve our teacher.

[^V06-22]: “People of truth” means those who are always truthful in what they say, do, intend, and promise, both with God and with other people. The author hints that other paths may involve a kind of untruth. A person who does not begin from the reality of existence—the clearest and broadest of all realities, first in the world and first in the mind—but instead begins from less obvious things such as coming-to-be, possibility, or motion, and treats hidden essences as settled facts, has not looked at what is most clear. The mind's nature is then bent, its sight clouded, and its words and inner thoughts untrue. It has not put things in their proper place. By contrast, someone who reasons from the reality of existence to necessary existence looks at what is real and places things where they belong. Even if the argument uses points others do not accept, it takes a longer route. — س قده

[^V06-23]: This will be explained shortly in the summary that follows. — س قده

[^V06-24]: Necessary existence brings completeness, and more than completeness, when compared with lower levels of existence. As we will see, a thing's completeness is itself together with what exceeds it, and what is simple contains everything below it in a higher way. Possibility means that a possible thing is empty in itself and receives its presence as a loan from something else. That is true of possible essences. For possible existences, it means a kind of dependence on the light of existence: they are linked to and upheld by the sustaining existence. This is all that oneness means here. — س قده

[^V06-25]: This is the reality that defeats the skeptic's claim that nothing is real. Anyone who is aware of anything is forced to affirm it. Reality cannot be denied, because even the claim that it is false depends on reality being true. If we suppose that every reality is false, then it would be true that every reality is false—which means that reality exists. Likewise, if a skeptic thinks that things are only imagined or doubts their reality, then it is truly the case that they are imagined or doubted. So reality exists even as the skeptic denies it. Since reality itself cannot be denied or destroyed, it exists necessarily in itself. The existence of the Necessary Being is therefore a truth that every person already knows; proofs merely draw attention to it. — May God preserve our teacher.

[^V06-26]: The word “either” divides the possibilities; it does not repeat them. So there is no need to assume an endless regress, as will become clear later. — س قده

[^V06-27]: Do not suppose that saying one level of existence—the level beyond all completeness—is necessary, while existence in general is not, would make God limited or contradict divine oneness. The other levels of existence depend on Necessary Existence itself, as light depends on its source. As we explained in the First Journey, created existences are nothing but their connection to and dependence on God; they are not self-standing things that happen to be connected to Him. Just as a thing's defining parts must be present for it to be what it is, and without them only its name would remain, so without Necessary Existence the other existents would have no reality or appearance. This is what divine oneness means: the Self-Sustaining One depends on nothing, while all things depend on Him. The author points to their dependence and essential connection when he says, “It has already been shown that existence, when it is caused…” — س قده

[^V06-28]: You might object: “Causation is essential, since existence is made real by a cause.” We answer: yes, but causation belongs to each particular level, not to existence as a whole, which remains present through all its levels. “Deficiency” can mean either the lower mode of existence itself, which is what makes existence graded, or the loss of that lower mode in comparison with a higher one. The second kind is not part of the lower level's reality: the lower level is existence, while what it lacks is nonexistence. In short, existence taken without restriction has no deficiency or limit. Every level shares in the reality of existence; its limitation comes from the nonexistence that accompanies it, or from its receptive capacity. So when the proof moves from the level of manifestation to Necessary Existence, it does not use something other than existence. The level of manifestation is itself existence, and its oneness is not numerical but absolute.

    I would add this: although the premises of this proof are lofty and contain profound ideas, spelling them all out at the outset is unnecessary. It can make the argument too subtle and hard to grasp. They matter in another context, such as a discussion of God's generosity. A clearer, shorter proof is this: once we have established that existence is fundamental, we see that existence—the very heart of reality—is unrestricted. It cannot be absent, since no opposite can accept its opposite. And unrestricted existence, which cannot be absent, must exist necessarily in itself. That is what we set out to prove.

[^V06-29]: For these thinkers, light too is one reality with different levels, marked by priority and lateness, richness and poverty, and other differences. All light belongs to this reality, from physical light to the lights of souls and the higher lights. The two methods are close but not identical. The way of existence, the true light, is broader because existence is manifest in itself and makes other things—the essences—manifest. The light of existence reaches even prime matter, as well as natural powers, bodies, and their qualities. In the Illuminationist account, by contrast, the lowest level is accidental light; bodies and other qualities are dark. The proof from the light of existence is therefore stronger, sounder, clearer, and more complete. — س قده

[^V06-30]: This objection confuses the reality of existence with the reality of an essence. Two essences do not become one: for example, the essences of a broader kind and its defining feature remain distinct. But in the reality of existence they do unite. This follows from the graded nature of existence, established by proof and discussed in the First Journey. In things that are not externally composed bodies, even the essences of broader kind and defining feature are one in their actual existence. In simple things, such as qualities, what they share—the color—is the very same existent as what distinguishes them, such as the way white scatters light to sight. The existence of broader kind and defining feature is one, and their being made real is one. A thoughtful reader can see that this is a special feature of existence. Number offers a sign of it: the unity that numbers share, and from which they arise, is also what distinguishes each number; it is its matter, form, outline, and limit. Unity is existence, or something inseparable from it. — س قده

[^V06-31]: One possibility remains: perhaps both features are required by the same reality. The objection is not answered until this possibility is ruled out. — س قده

[^V06-32]: In short, the problem arises only if existence is univocal, with exactly the same meaning at every level. But existence is graded, not univocal. The author does not answer the first part of the objection here, relying instead on what was established in the earlier discussion of existence. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-33]: The unity of existence is different: it is real and present in things themselves, not merely a unity of thought or a feature added to them. When we say it is “universal,” that applies to the description, not to the reality described. The reality of existence is what is real, not an essence. Nor can we remove the particular features from existence, because each level is made distinct by its own degree. In short, this reality has genuine, absolute unity, not numerical unity. — س قده

[^V06-34]: The speaker is the learned philosopher Qutb al-Din al-Katibi, in *Hikmat al-ʿAyn*. After noting that the concept of existence is self-evident and shared by all things, he makes this statement. — س قده

[^V06-35]: Calling an unrestricted essence a “thing” or a “universal” can be understood in three ways. First, it may be a special convention: there is no problem with using “universal” by extension for a nature that is neither universal nor particular in itself. Second, it may be naming something by what it will become in the mind. If this refers to ordinary minds, it is like the Qur'anic words spoken by Joseph's fellow prisoner: “I see myself pressing wine.” Third, it may be naming something by what it already is in a higher mind. This is like the Qur'anic command, “Give orphans their property,” where they are called orphans even as they reach maturity. The author seems to mean the second sense. “Universality in potential” means logical universality; natural universality, by contrast, is actual in it. — س قده

[^V06-36]: An essence can be fully examined by the intellect, which can analyze it by making distinctions. This hints at the opposite case: the intellect cannot grasp the reality of existence itself, so it cannot divide or analyze it in that way. We know existence itself only through direct awareness. What the intellect holds is the general concept of existence, which has the same kind of mental unity as other concepts. — س قده

[^V06-37]: As we have seen, in the reality of existence itself, what distinguishes one level is also what it shares with the others. Existence is so wide that no single level contains it. This is not true of essences, which are limited and subject to change. The author is effectively asking the Illuminationist: “What do you mean by light with different levels? If you mean existence, you regard it as only a concept. If you mean essence, essence in itself is neither existent nor nonexistent. How, then, can it be complete, intermediate, or deficient? And how can what distinguishes its levels also be what they share? If you mean something else, there is no third option: anything real is either existence or essence.”

[^V06-38]: Existence has many levels because what distinguishes each level is also what they all share. In this respect they are one; in the reverse respect they are many. That multiplicity does not conflict with their true unity. The same applies to the Light of Lights and the higher lights in the Illuminationist view: these have no essences mixed into their simple light. Each level differs from the others through its own simple reality, while sharing the same underlying nature. This is true even of physical, accidental light: each level is light without any darkness mixed into it, down to the seventh lamp in the seventh room. Qualities are externally simple, and their limitations come from what they lack. For Illuminationists, physical and immaterial lights differ and overlap in the same way, since all belong to the reality of light. These simple levels are distantly like the levels of motion in the philosopher's account. Even the slowest motion contains no stillness; slow motion is one continuous whole, just as fast motion is. — س قده

[^V06-39]: This answers a possible objection: if every level of existence differs from the others by its own simple reality, with no separate shared element, how can there be unity or divine oneness? The answer is that differences of strength and weakness, priority and lateness, and the like do not undermine the unity of existence itself. They confirm it, showing that existence is broader and more inclusive, with no negative element mixed into it. From the perspective of direct insight, no level of existence is like another along the ascending order, just as no form in the visible world is like another. This is expressed by the divine names “the One without likeness” and “the One.” As the saying goes: “In everything there is a sign / that He is One.”

    The kind of multiplicity that comes from distinct kinds and essences belongs to the receptive possibilities of things, not to existence itself. It is only a way of considering things, as is the basis for that distinction. The higher and lower levels in the chain of existence relate like a summary and its explanation, a whole and its parts, a brief statement and its detail, or a limit and what it limits. Existence is one kind of reality; its levels are not separate kinds or mere examples of one kind. When people speak of “individuals” of existence, they mean its levels and degrees. All these levels belong to one reality when compared with nonexistence, just as all degrees of light belong to one reality when compared with absolute darkness. Since the levels share this nature, we form one concept from them: existence. Without that shared nature, one concept could not arise from realities that were entirely different, and existence could not have a shared meaning. The contrary has already been proved and is self-evident. — س قده

[^V06-40]: Our teacher, may God preserve him, says: After dividing concepts into univocal ones, which apply equally to all their instances, and graded ones, which apply to them in different degrees, philosophers examined the matter closely and saw that concepts themselves cannot be graded. They then said that grading belongs to concepts derived from an accident's relation to its subject, since such a concept has no matching reality of its own. But this does not solve the problem: the difficulty remains that one concept cannot apply to its instances in different degrees, even when those instances are not individuals of the concept, as with relational concepts.

    The truth is that concepts themselves are never graded. Grading applies only to existence outside the mind. This is close to what the followers of Aristotle mean when they say that degrees of blackness, heat, light, and the like come from differences in how fully these qualities are present. The author brought their view close to his own but did not identify it with his view. The Aristotelians still say that one meaning predicated of things that differ in degree cannot be what makes those things what they are, nor can it be a part of their defining reality. An essence—what makes a thing what it is—does not vary, nor does any part of it. So the shared meaning must be an external feature, either necessary or accidental. This differs from the author's view that the reality of existence itself is one, while the differences of degree occur within that very reality which constitutes things. — May God protect him.

[^V06-41]: Nothing else is mixed into existence itself. The mystics say that when a thing goes beyond its limit, it turns into its opposite: endless visibility becomes hiddenness, extreme nearness becomes distance, and extreme poverty becomes wealth. So too, the endless multiplicity of the levels and aspects of existence—there is no mode of existence that is not one of its expressions—turns into true, absolute unity. Farid al-Din Attar of Nishapur puts it this way: “O boundless God, who else is there but You? / Since You have no limit or end, who else could there be? / Surely nothing has passed beyond the Boundless; / how, then, could anything remain?” — س قده

[^V06-42]: “Without limit” means that nothing can set a boundary to it. A thing's limit is something other than itself, but anything posited as other than the reality of existence has no independent reality. True existence therefore has no limit: it is absolute and unbounded. Whatever is proposed as a second reality turns out to be the first, since no boundary separates them. Strictly speaking, this “unboundedness” is stated as a denial, but the author phrases it positively as a teaching aid. He calls this an intermediate approach and uses it to make the discussion easier, as he explains in his treatment of cause and effect. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-43]: Knowledge is direct awareness. The level of existence beyond all completeness is aware of itself because it is not absent from itself. It is also aware of every lower level, because, as simple reality, it contains all existence below it in a higher way. This establishes that this level knows both itself and what is other than itself. — س قده

[^V06-44]: Its power is also established: existence is manifest in itself and makes other things manifest, so it is light. Light gives forth its radiance. When this giving proceeds with knowledge and choice, it is power. Will is established because existence is love for itself; life because a living being knows and acts; and speech because speech expresses what is hidden within. The highest level of existence expresses what is hidden, as in the saying, “I was a hidden treasure and loved to be known.” Hearing and sight are established because all existents are present to it, including what is heard and seen. The same applies to every attribute of perfection.

    But take care not to mistake the claim that existence is identical with knowledge, will, and power for a claim about the general concept of existence in our minds. That concept is plain and empty of these qualities; it is only a mental shadow of the reality of existence. The reality has other names and shadows in the mind too, such as unity, individuality, knowledge, life, and power. If an intellect illuminated by God turns toward the simple, all-encompassing reality itself, it will find that this reality is not empty of its perfections. The levels above completeness, such as the intellects, and those below it, such as souls, are real and present. They know themselves through their own existence. The forms known by souls are lights too. These existences are also wills and joys, each being drawn to itself and to its effects insofar as they are its effects. The same holds for the other attributes.

    An intellect not illuminated by God, especially one unable to keep its attention fixed, reaches only the separate existences of the visible world. It does not rise to the worlds of the spirits, the intellects, or the divine realm; it sees only in a general way. This is why limited human reasoning cannot grasp the attributes of perfection that belong to existence itself. Understand this and hold firm. — س قده

[^V06-45]: The principle is that when two possibilities are equal, neither occurs unless something external tips the balance. They claim this is self-evident, and denying it is mere stubbornness. That is why even the Ashʿarites reject a choice with no reason behind it. Since we see that possible things have in fact come to exist, as the author says, we conclude that the Necessary Being exists: otherwise we would face either circular reasoning or an endless chain of causes. — س قده

[^V06-46]: This is true when we consider how the concept runs through the things it describes: the concept “existent” is the face of real existence insofar as it is present in an existent thing. A thing's face is, in one respect, the thing itself, though what it describes may also include an essence that has existence. — س قده

[^V06-47]: The proof does not take the existence of a possible thing as already settled, as proofs from possibility, coming-to-be, and the like do. Those proofs treat existence as a given subject. The learned al-Qushji likewise says, “There is no doubt that something exists…” Existence is not foreign to God, as possibility and coming-to-be are. As the proof of the theosophers showed, the reality of existence is the reality of the Necessary Being; its concept is a way God is revealed to us, and God is its fullest instance. This is the approach of the theologians. — س قده

[^V06-48]: What fails to give certainty is reasoning from an effect to its cause, since knowing that an effect exists depends on knowing its cause. Reversing that argument would be circular. But reasoning from one general feature that has no cause—such as one of the features of existence itself—to another is a demonstrative proof and gives certainty. This is explained in the section on demonstration in logic and applies to the proofs used in first philosophy. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-49]: The initial conclusion might be that any existent is either necessary, part of an endless chain of possible things, part of a circular chain, or a possible thing whose essence favors its existence over its nonexistence. The author then establishes the stronger conclusion: certainly, some existent is necessary and some is possible. You might object that this proves only something about the state of the concept “existent,” not the existence of God. We answer that God's existence follows necessarily from it; the proof establishes it indirectly. — س قده

[^V06-50]: In truth, the second state follows necessarily from the first; it is not caused by it, nor are both caused by some third thing. They are general features that apply wherever existence is found, and neither has a cause. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-51]: First, the earlier proof would not be a purely indirect proof; it would include an indirect step. Second, the same issue arises in other proofs. For example, one might reason from the concept “coming-to-be,” which includes something that has come to be and therefore needs a cause, to another feature of that concept: it also includes something that did not come to be, namely the Necessary Being. This would establish something about the concept, not directly prove God's own existence. Likewise, from the concept “mover,” which includes something moved and therefore needing another mover, we might infer that it also includes something unmoved, the Necessary Being. Third, proving that a composite exists by showing that its parts exist is no weaker than the reverse argument, or than saying both depend on a third cause, the Necessary Being.

    The right way to understand the philosopher and those who follow him is that they are not proving God's transcendent essence by starting from the world's being made or composed. They are moving to the way God appears to things. God appears in Himself through Himself, and this is also His hiddenness from created beings. He appears to what is other than Himself through His action, in the forms where His essence and attributes are disclosed. This is what the sacred saying means: “I was a hidden treasure and loved to be known, so I created creation that I might be known.” A poem says: “You would not appear if I were not; / I would not exist if You were not.”

    Or “made” and “composite” may mean, with the passive sense, the world's reality as made by God: its real existence. “Maker” and “composer,” with the active sense, may mean the relational concept we form when the source of that making exists. The world's true made-ness is the outpouring relation itself, the light that shines upon the essences. God's true role as maker is the same outpouring relation viewed from the active side: His real bringing into existence. The first is the unfolded existence spread across essences; the second is that same existence as it depends on the transcendent God. This real made-ness and outpouring relation are causes of God's relational attributes, which exist only between two things. They do not cause His active outpouring relation, much less His transcendent essence, which exists through itself. — س قده

[^V06-52]: This is accepted in the second reply but denied in the first. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-53]: “Proof” here means proof in the philosophical sense: a proof from cause to effect, since existence as a whole—which includes both necessary and possible existence—has no cause. It does not mean “proof” in the broader logical sense, which includes both proof from cause and proof from effect. In fact, every proof used in philosophy, including proofs of God's existence, starts from an effect or a necessary feature of existence—such as its reality or its being a first cause—and moves to another feature, such as its being necessary in itself. The author's words “there is also a proof like one from the cause” acknowledge that such a proof exists but deny that it is truly from the cause. It must therefore be a proof from effect. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-54]: This has two meanings. One is the entailment and necessary consequence we described when explaining their words. The other is that, just as the existence of a substance is only “substantial” in relation to its essence, and an accident's existence is only “accidental” in relation to its essence, so too a proof of existence is only a proof “by relation.” — س قده

[^V06-55]: This is another explanation, different from the earlier one about what is true only indirectly. The earlier explanation accepts that the proof is from the cause, but says it proves either a feature of the concept “existent” or God's relational existence directly, while proving His independent existence only indirectly. The present explanation does not call it truly a proof from the cause but says it is like one—and greater than one. It is plainly not a true proof from the cause, since God is not caused. But deriving necessary existence from the reality of existence is greater than proof from cause, because every cause borrows its light from the light of existence itself. As the verse says: “The whole world exists by His light; / how could He be made manifest by the world?”

[^V06-56]: A collection can be understood in several ways. First, as a general idea that applies to each member separately; this does not consider the members together. Second, as all the individual things taken together, while treating the fact that they form a group as something separate. Third, as the whole group with its collective arrangement. Fourth, as that arrangement by itself. With this in mind, those who say “the collection of individuals exists as something over and above each one” mean the second or third sense, where the condition of being together is external to the parts. The fourth sense clearly has no existence of its own, since the arrangement is only an abstraction; otherwise it would need another arrangement, and the process would continue without end. The author disagrees with anyone who calls it self-evident that the sum of all individuals exists as something in its own right. The author's answer is correct, as anyone with sound judgment can see. He discusses this in the general principles. — س قده

[^V06-57]: As explained in the general principles, a cause that independently brings about a whole is, in fact, the cause of each of its members. That is what “independently” means here. — س قده

[^V06-58]: We already discussed this objection in our notes on unity and multiplicity. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-59]: This would also require that the part be the cause of itself and of its own causes. The author does not mention this here, just as he left out the need for the remaining parts in the earlier discussion, trusting the student's judgment. Apply the point there and this point here. — س قده

[^V06-60]: This means all the causes on which the effect depends, each one separately. It does not mean the whole set of causes on which the effect depends as a group, since that set is the effect itself, and dependence requires the dependent thing and what it depends on to be distinct. This definition fits the incomplete causes, which are parts of the complete cause. The definition of cause as “that on which an effect depends” applies only to causes short of the complete cause.

[^V06-61]: You might ask what purpose this distinction serves, whether it also undermines the proof, and how. If each part in an endless chain of possible things is treated as an independent cause, it must have a cause. The part would then cause itself and its own causes, which conflicts with what independence means. Causing itself as a complete cause might be assumed possible, but something cannot be the agent cause of itself and its own cause. In the objection, however, if the Necessary Being causes the whole, this problem does not arise: the Necessary Being has no cause, because there is no reason for Him to need one. Nor does it arise for anything above the final effect in a chain that ultimately depends on the Necessary Being.

    The answer is that the objection does undermine the proof if we accept its premise. It can choose as the cause of the whole the part above the final effect in the endless chain, together with that final effect. If you then say that all the parts above it are possible and ask what causes them, I reply: all those above the final effect form the higher part of the whole, and so on without end. As explained when this proof was used against an infinite regress, “independent” means that nothing in the whole chain is left without this part causing it, either directly or through its parts.

[^V06-62]: The fact that a whole ceases to exist when one of its parts is absent does not mean that the whole exists whenever that part exists; a partial cause works this way too. This claim also applies only to a whole that has real existence, such as a genuine compound. A merely conceptual compound has no existence of its own, and so cannot meaningfully be said to cease to exist.

[^V06-63]: In many cases, you accept that a real distinction in how we consider things is enough for cause and effect, even when the things themselves are one. For example, the defining feature causes the broader kind, especially in simple external things. The existence of the broader kind and defining feature is one, but that existence is a cause when considered in relation to the defining feature and an effect when considered in relation to the broader kind. Likewise, form causes matter, even though the author says their composition is a unity, not a joining of two separate things. The attributes of life, knowledge, and will are also considered in a certain order when speaking about the transcendent essence.

    The reply to the first example is that broader kind and defining feature are causes because, in reality, they are matter and form. They differ in how we consider them: the form is taken without restriction, while the matter is considered as restricted. Form and matter are distinct in themselves, and form contributes to the cause of matter. The reply to the second is that “one” does not mean that potentiality and actuality are identical, since they are opposites. Their opposition shows that a body includes matter and form. Rather, matter is pure potentiality and so is not fully determined; it can unite with actuality. Fully actual things, by contrast, are too distinct to unite in this way. They are also distinct because a form can exist apart from matter in the imaginal world, and matter can take on a different form. This is the author's view; we hold that matter and form are joined, not united. As for the third example, saying that one divine attribute comes before another is not sound, because the attributes are identical with God's essence. — س قده

[^V06-64]: What is meant by the two terms are the concepts “cause” and “effect,” abstracted from the realities of the cause and the effect. Those realities cannot be together in one respect, since that would make a thing prior to itself. Cause and effect are relational opposites, though the author holds that relational pairs such as knower and known can be together in themselves. He says that such togetherness is ruled out only when a proof establishes its impossibility, as with cause and effect. The wording here is somewhat loose. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-65]: You might object: “According to that careful thinker, the cause of the whole is not God; it is the whole itself.” We answer that “cause” here does not mean the complete cause; it means the agent. As noted above, God is the agent cause of the whole. You might then say that the two things are produced in order, which their view allows, so the first effect comes before the whole. But this is not so. At the level where the first effect appears, the whole appears too; the parts come together without a sequence or delay. — س قده

[^V06-66]: If they existed as two separate things, the Necessary Being would be part of something else. Yet He has no parts and cannot be part of anything. This is another of the objections against that careful thinker and his contemporary; the author points to it, along with other difficulties, in the word “among them.” — س قده

[^V06-67]: Not every case is like the identity of cause and effect, which would expose the argument's weakness because it does not identify the cause. It is like truth slipping out on his tongue. — س قده

[^V06-68]: Notice that his reply depends on how the mind considers something changing its reality. He therefore accepts that, when considered in detail, the Necessary Being and possible things have separate existences, each distinct from the other; but when considered generally, their existence is one. This plainly makes our way of looking at things affect reality itself. Yet at the end of the passage he says that a difference in how we consider something does not make reality different. He has undercut his own argument and brought down its foundation.

[^V06-69]: Essential priority is either sufficient to explain the result or insufficient. The connection between the conclusion and its premise holds in the first case but not in the second. In proving the Maker, however, the second case causes no problem. The author therefore sets out to disprove the first case.

[^V06-70]: Our teacher, may God preserve him, says: “This person seems to be one of the mystics. The phrase ‘it suited the people of debate’ suggests that he was not himself one of them. Most mystics hold that existence is fundamental. If this person's arguments were based on that view, none of the author's objections would apply; those objections assume that ‘existence’ means only the concept of existence. But, as I have shown from his words, he means the reality of existence itself, the particular reality, not the abstract concept in the mind. In fact, the four arguments reduce to two: one from circularity, and one from the path of the truthful, which does not rely on circularity or an infinite regress. The other three reduce to this last one because, as we have seen, his argument assumes that existence is fundamental and takes the particular reality of existence as its starting point.” — May he endure.

[^V06-71]: The phrase “something existent” has five possible readings: a particular existent considered as an individual; the nature of existence; each individual existent; all individuals taken together; or the whole collection considered as one whole. The phrase “bringing something into existence” has the same five readings. Pairing the two phrases gives twenty-five possibilities. In each case, the statement “the bringing into existence of something depends on the existence of something” has twenty-five possible readings of its own, making 625 combinations. You might say that “something existent” clearly means one particular individual, not the other readings. But the author himself applies the phrases to the natures of existence. The indefinite word “something” leaves the meaning open across these five categories, not merely across particular individuals. There is no need to restrict it to the nature, to one individual, or to another option: the proof works for each reading. — س قده

[^V06-72]: One premise is left unstated here, as it is in the other version: a possible thing must have a source. Calling this the path of the truthful depends on taking “existent” to mean the simple, luminous reality of existence, and “absolute” to mean its breadth and all-encompassing scope—not universality as used of concepts. — س قده

[^V06-73]: The aspect of existence reveals the aspect of necessity: whatever exists cannot be absent while it exists. Nor can an existent become absolutely nothing in every realm of existence, whether higher or lower. If the image of a thing disappears from the external world, it remains present in the realm of knowledge, in one or more minds. Even when something exists in only one level, such as the material world, it remains part of reality. It is absent from reality only if it is absent from every level of reality. A nature is removed only when all its instances are removed, though one instance is enough for it to exist. — س قده

[^V06-74]: This is like the witty poet's line: “The question of circularity arose between me and the one I love: / Had I not gone gray, she would not have turned away; / had she not turned away, I would not have gone gray.”

[^V06-75]: Absolute existence has different levels of priority, lateness, and other degrees, as explained earlier. Being a source or starting point is one of the features that arises at some levels. The author says “also” because he means absolute existence without restriction, not an individual level. The term “absolute” here means existence taken without a condition, as explained in the First Journey. So it is also true that this unrestricted reality has a source. “Absolute” and “general” may mean either the existential breadth of existence across all its levels, as the author and many theosophers understand it, or the indeterminacy of concepts, as Khafrī and his school understand it. Either way, the subject does not have numerical unity that would make it impossible for opposites to apply to it in different ways. — س قده

[^V06-76]: You might say that the whole collection of existents, considered as a whole, has no source. We answer: the major premise is also negative—the whole collection of possible things, considered as a whole, has no source. Two negative premises cannot yield a conclusion in the second figure of a syllogism. If instead we consider the existences of the individual things and their causes, both premises are affirmative, and two affirmative premises likewise cannot yield a conclusion in that figure. — س قده

[^V06-77]: The person making this argument seems to mean the version of the proof of the truthful we gave earlier, so the author's objection does not apply. Treating “existent as existent” as necessary would amount only to necessity given the predicate, if existence were something added to the thing's essence. But where the thing's essence is existence itself—the reality of existence—its necessity is eternal and does not depend on the predicate. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-78]: You may think that establishing this point is needed to complete all these arguments only in the third one, and perhaps in the fourth. The author's concern here is with certain ways those arguments have been framed: for example, taking “something existent” and “bringing something into existence” to mean either all individuals together or a whole with the condition of being together, rather than merely its parts; or relying on the most common case. — س قده

[^V06-79]: The conclusion is framed as a conditional, and its truth does not establish that the condition is true. It says, “If the Necessary Being exists, He is one and simple.” It does not define the general concept of the Necessary Being as one simple essence. The concept is not itself that essence; it means either what requires existence by its own nature, or what is in itself worthy of being called existent. — س قده

[^V06-80]: Cause and effect cannot belong to the same species, since things alike in kind should be treated alike. If one were a cause and the other an effect, one would be favored without a reason. And a body, considered simply as a body, belongs to one species. — س قده

[^V06-81]: The author does not label the other approaches as “the second proof.” Instead, he introduces them with phrases such as “the heavenly bodies as well,” “more nearly,” and “as in this method.” The proof from motion means reasoning from the need for both an agent that moves things and a final goal toward which they move. The first is clear. The second can be stated briefly: every motion is a kind of seeking. Sometimes the seeking is conscious and chosen, as in the soul; sometimes it happens without conscious choice, as in natural motion. Yet, as the Qur'an says, “There is nothing that does not celebrate His praise, though you do not understand their praise.” A reading of the verse says “they do not understand.” Since motion is a kind of seeking, it must be directed toward something sought, and every sought thing ultimately leads to the Necessary Being, the final goal of all inclinations, movements, longings, and desires. The divine names express this: “You toward whom all things turn,” “You whom all desire,” “You through whom there is neither strength nor power,” and “You alone are worshipped.” The Qur'an says, “There is no creature whose forelock He does not hold,” and “Everyone has a direction toward which they turn.”

    In more detail, the motions of simple and composite bodies are forms of seeking the human being, the crown of creation, and of moving toward it. Human beings in turn seek to take on the qualities of those above them. A person moves to become learned and refined; then strives to become a jurist; then longs to become a theologian; then works to become a divine philosopher—an intellectual world resembling the world outside. If this rare goal is reached, the person seeks to become God-oriented, a knower of divine things, capable and wise, a leader in both realms, and blessed in both lives, embodying God's qualities in knowledge and action. In short, a person of high ambition is not satisfied with what has been gained until the journey reaches its end. There must therefore be a being complete, indeed beyond complete, toward whom all turn, making every effort to come near. The seeker is satisfied only by taking on the qualities of the one who is the source and destination. In a sacred saying: “Son of Adam, I created things for your sake, and I created you for My sake.” — س قده

[^V06-82]: This rules out the idea that earthly things cause the heavenly bodies. The remaining possibility would be for one heavenly body to cause another, such as one sphere causing another, or the sun causing a sphere. The author rejects these alternatives to establish his conclusion.

[^V06-83]: This is because an effect does not exist at the same level as its cause.

[^V06-84]: The proof from the soul belongs to natural philosophy because the soul, considered as a soul, comes to be with the body and is one of the subjects studied in natural philosophy. The proof from motion belongs there too, since natural philosophy studies bodies as things undergoing change. Still, this proof may equally belong to metaphysics, and may even be preferable there. It rests more securely on knowledge of the soul, which leads to knowledge of God, as the saying goes, “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.” It also shows that the traveling soul is itself the path; when it becomes immaterial, it is, through its action, a manifestation of its destination. Nothing is a greater sign than the soul that has become immaterial and complete in knowledge and action. In these ways the soul is also a subject of metaphysics, which is why the author placed this proof here and gathered these insights and benefits.

    You might suggest a shorter proof: the body, form, quality, or soul comes to be, so it is possible and depends for its existence on a cause that is neither body nor bodily. A bodily cause acts through contact and position, but there can be no position relative to what does not yet exist; just as position cannot apply to an immaterial being, it certainly cannot apply to something nonexistent. The answer is that this shorter route would lose those insights. Also, the bodily cause may have a position relative to the matter that will receive the nonexistent form—for example, fire has a position relative to water, even though the heat that will arise in the water does not yet exist.

[^V06-85]: You might object that the soul is not immaterial from the start. The author says that it begins with the body and survives as a spiritual being. We answer, first, that the proof as stated by earlier philosophers assumes the soul is immaterial in its essence from the beginning, though not in its actions. Second, although the soul begins in the body according to the author, it becomes immaterial as it continues to exist. The proof therefore asks for the cause of this immaterial level and proceeds from there. — س قده

[^V06-86]: The qualification “as it comes to be” points to the soul's eternity in its inner reality: considered as a universal intellect, the soul exists eternally. In God's high realm of intellect, the separate universal intellect exists in both its essence and its activity; the souls that exist there have the same kind of existence. This view rests on the sound philosophical principles established in this and the author's other books: the primacy and gradation of existence, substantial motion, the possibility that members of one kind can be bodily or immaterial, and other principles. Plato's teaching about the soul's eternity should be understood in this sense, not as saying that the individual soul, as a soul, is eternal; individual souls cannot be distinguished in that way. — س قده

[^V06-87]: If “body” means what first appears to us—the bodily form—or body as the Illuminationists understand it, prime matter remains. For the Aristotle’s followers, prime matter is the simplest substance. It cannot be a cause, or every body would have a soul. Matter is purely receptive; it has no actuality and cannot act.

[^V06-88]: “Bodily form” here means the specific form. The author seems to reject this as a cause on another ground: a bodily cause needs a spatial relation. Otherwise the first problem returns: every subject and receptacle would have a human soul, since an independent cause is very close to and present with its effect. If bodily powers were causes of a human soul, then the causes would be the specific forms, bodily qualities, and plant and animal souls present in human bodies. The same problem would follow. But there is no such problem if one separate human soul causes another, or if a heavenly soul causes a human soul; those cases are ruled out by the need for a spatial relation. — س قده

[^V06-89]: If spatial relation were needed for matter to receive existence in its very essence from immaterial principles, then matter would stand between itself and itself. Whenever a bodily power affects matter through spatial relation, matter must first receive a position, and that position then enables the effect. If spatial relation were needed for matter itself to come from a separate principle, matter would have to mediate its own coming-to-be. That would make a thing prior to itself; what depends on an impossibility is impossible too.

    Also, a separate being has an outpouring relation to matter and bodies, not a relation that falls under a category of the physical world. How could spatial relation apply here? Things with position have no position relative to the higher separate principles, especially the First Principle. In this respect they are neither in time nor in place, just like a point or an instant.

[^V06-90]: This means the leading philosophers among them, who use “coming-to-be” to mean renewal within existence itself. It does not mean those who speak of an imagined time. — س قده

[^V06-91]: Mentioning circularity and an infinite regress in the theologians' proof is unnecessary. They accept no separate immaterial being besides God. So it is enough, by their method, to show that a created thing cannot be a body or bodily. They need not also appeal to circularity or an infinite regress.

[^V06-92]: The objection does not refute the argument. The agent's priority over its effect is established, but the recipient need not come before what it receives; the reverse may be true. Form is a co-cause of matter and is prior to it in every way, as the author himself says elsewhere. Perhaps “recipient” here does not mean a recipient in the technical sense, but an external subject that bears an accident, in keeping with the rule that a subject must exist before its attribute. Yet that rule establishes only that the two—the subject and what is established in it—exist together. The stronger claim that the subject must come first needs separate proof. Comparing essence to a subject, and existence to its accidents, is also mistaken. In reality, essence is the reverse of existence; its unity and its added existence in the mind come from the intellect's analysis. The external reality of existence itself is not something the mind can reach and analyze in this way. — May God protect him.

[^V06-93]: The point is that essence is only a mental consideration. It does not create a real duality in a possible thing, nor a real capacity to receive or be received. A merely conceptual recipient need not exist before what it receives, even though a real recipient does. So how could a merely conceptual essence create a real duality in God, and thereby a real relation of making that would require the maker to precede what is made? The author is not saying that God's essence really makes His existence but does not precede it; God's essence is purely conceptual and disappears into existence, even in possible things—and all the more so in God.

    This answers al-Tusi's reply in his commentary on Ibn Sina's *Pointers*, where he responds to al-Razi's objection about a recipient. Al-Tusi says that essence receives existence only when it exists in the mind, and cannot cause an external attribute while existing only in the mind. But once the intellect analyzes God as involving two aspects—essence and existence—it is also positing a distinction in reality. The intellect must then decide whether essence is the cause of existence, its recipient, or neither; and the same choices apply in reverse to existence.

    If essence is the cause, it must exist before existence, because to posit a thing is to posit what necessarily follows from it. Even a possible essence, though only a mental consideration, is receptive when examined in itself, and existence is what it receives. So if one posits an essence for a possible thing, one must also posit it as receptive and prior in substance to what it receives. The same follows if one posits a necessary essence for God: it must be the cause of His existence and prior to it. All these problems arise from positing two aspects, even if only by the intellect.

    If essence is receptive in the sense that a thing bears a quality—as four bears evenness—it would also have to be a cause, and the same problem follows. If it receives existence passively, it would be a possible essence, contradicting the claim that it is necessary. If it is neither cause nor recipient, there is nothing to unite essence and existence, leaving them separate as a stone lying beside a person. If existence is the cause or the recipient, the essence is not made real, since making real belongs to existence. If existence is a passive recipient, then essence becomes a changing quality added to existence. If it is neither cause nor recipient, the same separation follows. Most of these options also contradict the claim that what is necessary is existence and that essence is outside it.

    There is another argument, beyond the one given in the notes on the First Journey. Essence is limited and mixed, so if it were the reality of God, He would not encompass all things. Each essence is incompatible with another; without existence, essences would not share their natures. Real existence, by contrast, is unity and breadth, and does not conflict with essences. Every essence is an existent in the ordinary predicative sense. One more argument: every real thing is either existence or essence. All essences are alike in being neither existence nor nonexistence, and essence is therefore not fit for the divine presence: in itself, it accepts either existence or nonexistence. Existence alone cannot accept nonexistence. — س قده

[^V06-94]: This point does not concern the theologians' argument. Even if existence were an added feature, they say that existence is caused, not essence. The author says that existence is fundamental and essence secondary, which might suggest that God's essence is caused by His existence even though that essence is necessary. He answers that essence is not made real apart from that existence. But essence, as you know, is a reflection of limited existence. Strictly speaking, God's pure existence has no essence in the disputed technical sense. What the author calls the secondary, uncreated essence consists of concepts that express the divine names and attributes, which point to unlimited existence. This is not the “essence” at issue in the debate, the natural universal. — س قده

[^V06-95]: This claim is surprising: a possible thing's existence is an accidental feature of its essence and is caused. It is an accident of essence, not an accident of existence, as explained more than once in the First Journey.

[^V06-96]: “The” refers back to the earlier argument. There, the cause was sought for existence itself; here, it is sought for being qualified by existence. — س قده

[^V06-97]: This assumes the primacy of existence. On that view, God's essence cannot be a necessary essence that is caused to be qualified by existence; it cannot belong to the level of the divine essence. It would instead be one of the derivative features, like relational attributes or concepts of the beautiful names, as we have said. Also, existence is not something added to essence; it is what makes essence real. So saying that essence is qualified by existence because of existence is like saying “a real essence exists,” even though essence in itself is equally open to existing or not existing.

    Perhaps the speaker means to raise a third or even a fourth possibility, as in the later words, “and also for the investigator…” In that case, the alternatives would not form a strict division. If someone says that the supposed essence of God is simply a nature that does not resist nonexistence, or that unlimited luminous existence gives rise to an essence, we can allow those claims. But they would be separate arguments, not this one, even if they agree with its lofty conclusion. — س قده

[^V06-98]: You might object that this premise is not part of the argument. Someone who thinks existence is additional to God's essence may accept that God's existence depends on His own essence, while denying that it depends on anything else. Notice, however, that the argument's problem is not dependence but that something would have to precede itself. We answer that the author is addressing the circularity that seems to arise: the subject, essence, depends on its attribute, existence, while existence, insofar as it is an attribute, depends on its subject. — س قده

[^V06-99]: This assumes that existence is fundamental. If essence were fundamental, one could say that a necessary essence entails existence, so existence belongs to it necessarily, as evenness belongs to the number four. The claim that the Necessary Being is “what requires existence by its own nature” would then also be taken at face value. But both views are mistaken, because what necessarily entails existence must itself be existence or be surrounded by it.
