[^V06-100]: Notice that the claim that God's essence is necessarily qualified by existence because existence follows from it is comparable to saying that an animal is necessarily qualified as human because humanity follows from it. Perhaps the comparison is intended only in this respect: the qualification is analytical, and the necessity comes from the defining feature, which is like existence. — س قده

[^V06-101]: They failed to notice that necessity is being considered in relation to real existence, and that real existence is what makes essence qualified by existence. They also missed a basic point in the argument: even if God had an essence, and that essence were only a mental consideration, existence would not become an external accident. If it did, essence would become the subject that bears it, and would itself be only an analytical feature.

[^V06-102]: The author interprets Dawānī this way even though Dawānī explicitly calls God an existence that subsists through itself, because Dawānī strongly favors the primacy of essence and treats existence as only a mental consideration. A thing must be either essence or existence. If existence is only conceptual, then of these two, God belongs to the side of essence. In Dawānī's account, existence is only the source from which the concept of existence is drawn. To speak of God as an individual instance of existence would therefore depart from his view.

    If Dawānī wanted to avoid that departure, he could have used the wording of the careful investigator: God is pure existence, as philosophers say He is pure existent. This means that other things are like the letters alif and bāʾ—each is something existent—while He is simply existent, with nothing more to say of Him. His essence deserves to be called existent in itself, without qualification or cause. A possible essence, by contrast, deserves in itself to be called neither existent nor nonexistent. This account, too, is far from the truth.

    You might say that Dawānī treats existence as conceptual because a possible thing has no individual existence of its own; for him, a possible thing exists by being related to God's self-subsisting existence. We answer: if a concept has something corresponding to it outside the mind, even if there is only one such instance, then it is real, not merely conceptual. The sun is not merely conceptual just because there is only one sun.

[^V06-103]: This applies if “possible” means its existence. If it means its essence, then it refers to another aspect that does not resist either existence or nonexistence. But because essence is only a mental consideration, this aspect is an accident. — س قده

[^V06-104]: This objection is not accepted. First, if the Necessary Being had an essence, it would have a unique level that belongs to no other thing: the level of its essence, which is distinct from all other essences in itself. That essence would determine its level of existence, the highest level in the graded reality of existence. Its existence in other levels would mean only that every effect depends on it as the First Cause, not that it is necessarily present in those levels.

    Second, the critic may say that this feature belongs only to possible essences, not to essence as such—including an essence supposed to belong to the Necessary Being. Third, the claim that things have known forms in the higher principles is acceptable only of direct knowledge; what is known in that way is their existences, not their essences. As for knowledge through mental forms, whose objects are universal essences, the author's earlier account of how universals exist in the mind rules out such knowledge in beings that are immaterial in both essence and action.

    There is another way to show that God has no essence. If He necessarily exists in Himself, then His existence must hold under every supposition. Whatever existent we suppose, in whatever way we suppose it, He exists with it. Even if we suppose nothing, that too is a supposition, and He still exists. So His existence is unrestricted, not limited by any condition. If it depended on a condition, it would be absent when that condition was removed, contrary to the premise that it exists under every supposition. It is therefore absolute and unlimited. He has no essence: if He had one, it would be distinct from other essences, and would necessarily be absent whenever they were present, contradicting the claim that He exists under every supposition. So the Necessary Being has no essence. — May his shadow endure.

[^V06-105]: That is, even if the thing is present through another existence in an incidental way, as it is in our minds and in higher minds. Essences have earlier and later stages of presence. — س قده

[^V06-106]: Yet He has a level that they do not share. As the saying goes: “Praise be to God, whose proof is that no state is without His presence.”

[^V06-107]: If the Necessary Being is simple in every respect, the concept “necessary being” is predicated of the whole reality. If it were predicated of only part of Him, He would be composite.

[^V06-108]: If necessary existence is not something added to God, then there is no essence in Him that could make Him many. A thing does not become two merely by itself. How, then, could His particular existence—identical with His individuality—become many? — س قده

[^V06-109]: The questioner accepts that God’s particular existence is identical with His individual identity, but denies that this prevents another such identity. If each were simple in itself, why could two wholly different simple identities not exist? — س قده

[^V06-110]: Ibn Kamuna’s objection bears most strongly on two groups. The first holds that essences are fundamental and intrinsically diverse, while existence is only an abstract concept. If God’s reality is an essence in the same way possible things are, these thinkers can allow two simple essences to differ completely, just as the most general kinds do. They can also distinguish a general title from what it describes, and so avoid the claims that the two realities must be composite or depend on something else for their individuality. The objection also challenges those who deny any likeness between cause and effect—or between one existence and another—even if they say existence has one shared meaning. If causes and effects need have no likeness, why could two necessary causes not differ completely? The objection is easy to answer on the view defended here: existence is real, simple, luminous, and graded. Its common meaning relates to its reality as a universal idea relates to a real individual, not as an abstract concept relates to what it describes.

[^V06-111]: Our teacher says that Ibn Kamuna’s objection does not trouble most philosophers who treat essence as fundamental, since they still hold that God is pure, simple existence, with no essence to which existence is added. They have also proved God’s oneness on this basis. The objection does trouble those who treat existence as merely mental even in God, making existence a feature that follows from an unknowable essence. They cannot answer as above; they must say that one concept cannot be drawn from two realities that have nothing in common. But that would require a shared feature in those realities, and so composition in God.

[^V06-112]: Some mystics call God the source from which “existent” is drawn; the Illuminationists call Him the Light of Lights, and the Aristotle’s followers call Him real existence. The intended answer is that one concept cannot be drawn from realities that are wholly different in themselves. In the true account, the shared idea of existence is drawn from the unity that possible essences have through their relation to God: God’s existence is what makes the concept apply both to Him and to things. The same would have to be true of two supposed Necessary Beings. But this answer does not fit a view that denies the reality of existence. If “relation” means the real giving of existence, it names the very doctrine that view rejects. If it means only a relation between concepts, then it follows the multiplicity of the essences and cannot unite them. — س قده

[^V06-113]: “What makes a thing what it is”: for both supposed beings, this would be necessary existence itself. — س قده

[^V06-114]: Rather, the difference would come from something else. If one shared meaning became many through matter or other features, the Necessary Being would depend on something else to be an individual.

[^V06-115]: If the supposed beings had the same essence, they would differ in individuality; if they had different essences, they would differ in necessary existence, which is one shared meaning. — س قده

[^V06-116]: This seems to refer to the proof that God has no essence. If the nature of an essence, a part of it, or something inseparable from it required multiplicity, there could be no single instance of that essence; every instance would have to be many. But if multiplicity came from something outside the essence, the instances would be caused by that external thing. So a Necessary Being divided into numerically many beings would be caused.

[^V06-117]: This relies on a view attributed to the author: calling knowledge a “quality” is only a loose way of speaking. In reality, knowledge of each category belongs to that category. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-118]: Rationality is not a bodily quality or passive state that could define a substance. Strictly speaking, reason is the power to grasp universals; that grasp is a relational quality, and the soul’s receiving it is a kind of change. Sense perception, which grasps particulars, is likewise a quality and change. Motion, too, is an unstable accident and cannot define a substance. The author does not enter into these technical points because he is discussing the ordinary meanings of words. In ordinary speech, “rational” means a being that has outward speech or reason; “having” expresses a relation. — س قده

[^V06-119]: You might say this case differs from the first only in being broader or narrower. The reply is that this second kind of usage is unrestricted: the relation need not follow ordinary language or convention, since the truth of things is not bound by either. The author points out that ordinary speech often uses derived words to mean “related to,” as in words for a milk seller, date seller, or grocer. The Qur'anic phrase “I am not unjust to the servants” can also mean “I am not one who does injustice.” A derived word can even name the source itself. This supports the claim that a source need not be present in its subject in just one fixed way.

[^V06-120]: This refers to the broader meaning just described, so it does not repeat the question’s earlier point that “existent” is a general concept. — س قده

[^V06-121]: This is the distributive universal, not the universal considered as applying to everything at once. The thing that produces effects, or that in which existence is present, is the logical universal.

[^V06-122]: In essences related to existence, real existence does not inhere in them, but the shared concept “existence” does apply to them as a derived idea. This wider concept also applies to existence that subsists through itself.

[^V06-123]: If the concept of existence is considered as a title that carries its judgment into the reality it describes, then it has no separate recipient or source of action. If it is considered as a mental object in its own right, it has a mental source, the mind. Looking closely at the shared concept also shows that its instances and the realities from which it is drawn must be one: if two things had wholly different features, neither could qualify as an instance of the same concept without some common ground. — س قده

[^V06-124]: The objection concerns real existence being added to an essence in the outside world. A mental concept may still be analytically attributed to another concept, as one abstract idea applies to the thing from which it is abstracted. When philosophers say that existence is added to essence, they mean this kind of addition in thought.

[^V06-125]: Arabic sometimes forms words by analogy, as when one says “the clay turned to stone” or “the camel turned into a she-camel.” But this is an exception to the usual rule and should not be extended without clear evidence—especially to the common word “existent,” used of both God and possible things. More broadly, the author is discussing how words are formed from verbal sources. Such a source is normally an abstract meaning that can take different forms, unlike a fixed noun such as “iron.” — س قده

[^V06-126]: Earlier grammarians treated the verbal noun as the source from which derived words are formed. Later scholars instead located the source in the root letters shared by those words, while each word’s form expresses a different relation to that source. A derived adjective can express a relation to its source in several ways: directly, as striking relates to the one who strikes, or by an accepted kind of usage, as “iron worker” relates to iron or “sun-warmed” relates to the sun. If there is figurative extension, it lies in the meaning of the source, not in the grammatical form of the derived word. The critic’s examples therefore do not show that a rigid noun such as “iron” itself can be the source of a derived word; nor do they prove that the source must be literally present in its subject. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-127]: The word “existent” can be applied to existence itself, just as “white” can be applied to whiteness and “hot” to heat. The standard for a derived word is that its source be either present in the subject or real in it in some other way; one specific relation need not always apply. — س قده

[^V06-128]: One version of the text adds “and,” but that reading is not sound. The examples are meant to explain figurative extension. The “source” is whatever makes the derived word applicable, not necessarily the technical source from which a word is grammatically formed. — س قده

[^V06-129]: The blacksmith is related to iron so closely that it dominates his thought and work; this can be described figuratively as his having a share in iron. It does not mean that merely imagining iron makes someone a blacksmith. — س قده

[^V06-130]: The image of the sun is present in the warmed water; “sun” can also be used for the sun’s rays. — س قده

[^V06-131]: If everyday word use is too ambiguous to establish philosophical truths, it is especially unreliable in cases that could mean relation, figurative realization, or literal presence. The author first says not to infer truth from common expressions, then notes that even ordinary usage does not always require a source to be present in its subject. If we examine usage carefully, he argues, it supports the conclusion required by reason. — س قده

[^V06-132]: Dawānī's point, as I understand it, is not that the reality of existence is itself a word or an idea. The source from which a word is formed belongs to language: one word may be formed from another. The reality of existence is neither a word nor a concept. How could it literally be the linguistic source from which the word “existent,” or its general meaning, is derived?

    It is a source in a different sense: the real existence from which the mind draws the concepts “existence” and “existent,” and to which those concepts point. We speak in a similar way when we call a real defining feature the source of the feature expressed in a logical definition.

    Dawānī also says that the source of the word “existent” can be something that exists through itself. This makes sense if he is speaking of the real thing named by the expression. It can also make sense if he treats the expression as a way of attending to the real thing, so that a judgment about the expression points through it to reality. That is how a term works in a statement about a whole class, rather than a statement about a nature considered only as a nature.

    The concept and the reality it describes are different, but not so unrelated that we cannot pass from the first to the second. A thing's appearance is that thing understood through one of its faces; otherwise a judgment about the appearance could tell us nothing about the thing.

    Existence, then, has both a concept and a reality. The concept is a name and a way of looking; the reality is what the name reveals. Some statements, such as statements about how words are formed, belong to the concept. Other statements, such as “it exists through itself,” belong to the reality. This is why the author says in *The Beginning and the Return* that the fact that a derived expression is a matter of thought does not make the reality from which it is drawn unreal. — س قده

[^V06-133]: Dawānī may mean that real existence, considered as the source of the word “existent,” becomes a general concept. But God’s being a true individual instance of “existent” does not make Him a universal or a particular in the ordinary logical sense. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-134]: The reply is that knowledge of God is innate: God is so clear and radiant that His very fullness of light hides Him from us. Everyone knows himself through direct awareness, though this awareness has degrees, and direct awareness of oneself cannot be separated from awareness of one’s source. This kind of inborn knowledge is enough to explain our use of words for God. If God is also the one who gave words their meanings, there is no difficulty.

[^V06-135]: One possible answer is to interpret “source” broadly enough to include both God and possible things. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-136]: The critic says that sometimes “existent” is derived from “existence,” and sometimes from the relation to existence. The reply is that a derived word and its source must correspond in both sound and meaning. If “existent” means existence itself, it is derived from existence; if it means something related to existence, it is derived from that relation. The author may also use “source” to mean what makes the word applicable, not only a verbal noun. — س قده

[^V06-137]: “Blacksmith” consistently means someone related to iron, never iron itself, so it is not a close parallel to “existent.” Dawānī tries to give “existent” one broad meaning: either what produces effects or what has existence. But the first meaning has no trace of existence in it, while the second does not fit “existent” when it means merely “related to existence.” Real existence can be a subject in its own right, but then “existent” is no longer a general term shared by God and other things. The author’s point is not merely that common usage determines the meaning; it is that reason supports the ordinary meaning, unless proof shows otherwise. — س قده

[^V06-138]: This is a strong objection. If God is real existence, then existence is real; the fact that it has no many instances inhering in essences does not make it a mere mental idea. A nature is real if even one real instance exists. Some later philosophers try to say that possible existences are not themselves instances of existence, since the outside world contains only essences, and that God is an incidental instance. But this does not resolve the problem on the author’s terms.

[^V06-139]: Dawānī’s discussion seems inconsistent: he says that if existence is considered on its own it has no existence in something else, but elsewhere says it cannot exist apart from another. The issue is more than whether existence is fundamental; it is whether it is real at all. — س قده

[^V06-140]: As noted earlier, the argument confuses a concept with its individual reality. Calling God “particular existence” is only a technical expression for those who treat existence as conceptual.

[^V06-141]: The alternatives may concern whether the critic consciously distinguishes a thing’s being qualified by something from that quality’s inhering in it, or confuses the two. The author uses both expressions at times without distinction. More precisely, a quality’s inherence is the feature that makes predication possible; being qualified is the relation expressed by the judgment. A second-order concept may exist only in the mind while applying to something in reality, as with possibility or “thingness.” — س قده

[^V06-142]: A subtle case does not require a more obvious case in which the essence and its attribute have separate existences. To say that a quality is added to essence requires only that the essence exist; it does not require an existence separate from that quality. A quality added to existence, by contrast, requires an independently existing subject.

[^V06-143]: The author is distinguishing the mystics’ “unity of existence” from the view that existence and existent are one reality whose levels are many. On the first view, essences are real and many, while existence is a mental concept; beings are united only by being related to existence. The author argues that this does not yield the deeper unity of existence. On his own view, existence is a real, graded light: each particular existence is a level of it, and relations between levels express the one reality. The parts of the world are therefore united more deeply than by contact or dependence. At the highest level of the created world, the complete human being gathers the forms of all other kinds of being, as a final form gathers the earlier forms within it. All things depend on that final form for their full actuality, and it in turn depends on God, the One who sustains all. The changing stages of the world are gathered in an enduring reality, just as the same human being remains while bodily conditions change.

[^V06-144]: Those who say that a collection exists as one thing usually mean that all its members together exist, not that the arrangement of the members forms a separate reality. Some later scholars, including al-Lahiji, do treat the whole as a distinct existent; some proofs of God’s existence rely on that assumption. — س قده

[^V06-145]: Otherwise, one possibility would be preferred over another without any reason. — س قده

[^V06-146]: A cause must precede its effect. This applies to the whole understood as a single reality, not to all the members simply taken together. See al-Lahiji’s *Shawāriq*. — س قده

[^V06-147]: In this example the Necessary Being alone is the complete cause of the first effect. Once that effect exists, the pair is a whole; no further cause is needed to make the whole.

[^V06-148]: If the argument is only a trick, the author’s criticism stands. If it comes from a philosopher who sees God as necessary in every respect, it may mean that God is present at every level of existence and with every existent, yet has no separate essence at any one level.

[^V06-149]: The argument would be sound if it said that both unity and multiplicity are caused, rather than saying multiplicity is possible in relation to necessary existence. — س قده

[^V06-150]: Dawānī treats existence as a mental concept and holds that everything outside the mind is an essence. So he interprets “God is pure existence” to mean “God is pure existent”: since existence has no real instance, God cannot be existence. This is only a verbal dispute. In reality, a thing is either real existence or an essence; there is no third kind.

[^V06-151]: This approach reverses the true one. On the true view, an essence is existent by being one with a real level of existence, from which the concept of existence is drawn. The essence itself has no separate counterpart in reality; it is indeterminate and disappears into the existence that makes it real. Dawānī instead denies any real instance of existence and says that an essence becomes existent by being one with the concept “existent.” The two views are opposite, though Dawānī’s rejection of a real split between essence and existence may seem close to the truth. His view still leaves possible essences as the real things, and it cannot explain why they deserve to be called existent.

[^V06-152]: On Dawānī’s account, the concept of existence is shared and mental, while its application to real existence is either figurative or based on another use of the word. This would make God have existence twice: as His own reality and as an instance of the shared concept. But the same problem applies to Dawānī’s account, since he treats God’s real existence as an instance of the general concept.

[^V06-153]: The author is presenting the consequence of Dawānī’s view, not endorsing it. If God’s essence is not a real instance of existence, then saying He is an existent means only that an abstract concept applies to Him. But Dawānī also says that existence does not inhere in an essence. His own account therefore faces the same difficulty. — س قده

[^V06-154]: A particular human being is a nature of humanity surrounded by individuating features. The universal nature and its particular instance are not two separate existences. Likewise, “existent” applies to particular existence as an external predicate, not as a quality added to it. — س قده

[^V06-155]: The discussion of mental analysis here does not yet answer Dawānī’s second objection. It only shows that a particular existence and the general concept do not amount to two separate existences in God, as a possible thing can be analyzed into what does not resist existence and what does.

[^V06-156]: An essence, in the technical sense, is a bounded nature that can be thought of as universal or particular and that does not in itself require existence or nonexistence. Any limited nature is an essence. Existence, knowledge, power, life, and the divine names point instead to unlimited reality, so God has no essence in that technical sense. “Essence” can also mean more generally what makes a thing what it is or answers the question “What is it?” — س قده

[^V06-157]: The concept of existence relates to its real instance as an essential feature relates to the thing it describes: it reflects that reality, unlike a merely added quality. But the real nature of existence cannot be present in the mind without being changed into a concept. So the shared concept does not literally become part of the individual reality it describes. This is why Sadra can call existence a second-order concept while holding that existence itself is real.

[^V06-158]: These are the possible essences, which are dependent features of actual existence. — س قده

[^V06-159]: One might ask how Dawānī can say that “existent” is identical with a possible essence when he denies that a real or mental instance of existence is present in the essence. The strongest version of his view is that both God and possible things are sources from which the concept “existent” is drawn: God is that source through Himself, while a possible essence is such a source only because it has been brought into relation with its maker. But this relation is itself existence. What makes God and possible things existent is therefore the reality of existence, even if one avoids that name.

[^V06-160]: “Confusing kinds of predication” here is not the familiar fallacy of attaching an unrelated condition to a subject. The mistake is to confuse strict, meaning-based predication with ordinary predication. A concept is always itself in meaning, but it is not always an actual instance of itself. — س قده

[^V06-161]: The true subject of metaphysics is unrestricted real existence: existence in its full reach, not limited to any one nature. When philosophers call “unrestricted existent” the subject of the science, they mean the real nature as it appears in its instances, not the universal concept alone. The fallacy comes from leaving out that qualification. — مره

[^V06-162]: The argument also risks confusing what is actually present to the mind with what the mind could analyze if it were present. A thing may be analyzable in principle even if it cannot exist as a mental image. This is another case of treating an abstract description as though it were the reality itself.

[^V06-163]: The concept of existence is self-evident; its real nature is the light and manifestation itself.

[^V06-164]: The author uses blackness as an example of a simple reality that can be present in itself or in another thing. Blackness also evokes darkness, which helps suggest how all distinctions and traces of other things disappear within the simple, luminous reality of existence.

[^V06-165]: There are three levels of oneness: the oneness of necessary existence, already proved; the special oneness of real existence, which will be discussed later; and the oneness of God as the sole deity and creator, established here. The first does not by itself prove the third. For example, the Muʿtazilites held that human beings act independently through their own power; another view would say that God created the first intellect and then left it to act on its own. That is not the philosophers’ position. For them, God alone gives existence; separate intellects, souls, and natural causes are only means through which His generosity and giving reach things. Only what is free of potentiality can give existence. — س قده

[^V06-166]: In short, nothing comes to exist by itself. All things receive existence from God, both when they first come to be and for as long as they continue to exist. The act of bringing something into being follows from the reality of existence itself. — س قده

[^V06-167]: The sun analogy is only a starting point; God’s relation to things is not literally like sunlight. Sunlight spreads over surfaces and visible things, while the light of existence reaches everything sensed, imagined, conceived, and beyond. Sunlight touches only the outer surfaces; existence reaches into the depths of things and makes them depend on itself. Sunlight is not aware, while the levels of the light of existence are living and aware. Sunlight also fades and has other lights beside it; the light of the Real never fades and has no second.

[^V06-168]: The word *sayb* means “giving” or “gift.”

[^V06-169]: Grammatically, “alike in nature” describes the elements; “in different places” describes how they are situated. — س قده

[^V06-170]: They would be separated in the sense of occupying different places, but could not remain permanently apart after first being naturally joined. Otherwise there would be empty space in the worlds from which they came and into which they moved, ruptures and joins in the spheres, and heavy bodies moving outward. All these results are impossible.

[^V06-171]: “First” refers to the beginning of the separation, and “return” to its end. The argument concerns all worlds, not only their elements. A permanent division of two bodies of the same kind—for example, two suns or two spheres—would require permanent force, but the heavens are not moved by force. They would have to return to natural unity, which would also bring the earlier contradictions. Ordinary individuals of one kind in our world are different: each individual passes away, while the kind continues only through successive individuals. The argument also rules out a world naturally made of many separate individuals from the start: their like parts would have to be separate too, yielding multiplicity without any unity.

[^V06-172]: Natural place is essential to a body of a given nature and cannot vary. If the places themselves joined together, place would have to move and have another place of its own. Nor can what is the center become an outer edge. — س قده

[^V06-173]: Such a union is possible in itself, even if the physical arrangement of the world would prevent it from actually occurring because it would require empty space or a break in the heavens. — س قده

[^V06-174]: Avicenna argues that no different natural movement or sensible quality means there can be no naturally different bodily world. One might object that the same effect can arise from different causes—for example, heat from fire, anger, movement, or the sun. The reply is that this resemblance holds only at a broad level; on closer examination, the kinds of heat differ. Even “evenness,” which applies to four, eight, and other numbers, is an abstract feature with no separate instance in them. Abstract features follow the things from which they are drawn, and one concept cannot come from wholly different things precisely as different. — س قده

[^V06-175]: The author first divides straight movement into three kinds, then revises this to a two-way division: movement either toward or away from the center.

[^V06-176]: The nineteen qualities are nine familiar simple tastes, four active and passive tangible qualities, three qualities of hearing, sight, and smell, and three qualities sensed indirectly. It would be more precise to list the basic tangible qualities individually. The author’s point is that the natural world has an ordered governance; as the active intellect governs this world in the philosophers’ account, so a governing power oversees the elemental world. — س قده

[^V06-177]: If nature moved a thing into a higher kind before completing the lower kind, there would be a sudden jump in its qualitative or substantial development. The philosophers reject such leaps, as they reject a sudden jump in place. This is the principle that a lower possibility must be fulfilled before a higher stage can be reached; it applies to the ascending order of things. — س قده

[^V06-178]: A sense and what it senses are correlatives and correspond to one another. A rational soul therefore has every sense that is fully represented in rational being.

[^V06-179]: Indirectly sensed things include quantity and relation. Avicenna names the most common and clearest examples. Geometrical body is counted among bodily qualities, and boundaries are included in shape. Movement and stillness are mentioned after movement and its divisions: stillness is the absence of movement in a place where movement is possible. They may also be counted as qualities in their own right, or as general kinds listed after their specific kinds. — س قده

[^V06-180]: The author gives an argument about the world’s unity that can speak to several philosophical approaches. His own view goes further than connection or contact: the parts of the world are genuinely one in existence, where what distinguishes them is the same reality that they share. Every earlier form is contained as potential matter within the final form of the complete human being, which gathers the forms of the world. What is indeterminate exists only through what gives it determination, so the world is fully actual through this final form, which itself exists through God. In another way, the kinds of beings are gathered into their higher principles and ultimately into the light of the human kind. The world’s cycles and changes pass, while this deeper unity remains. — س قده

[^V06-181]: The argument relies on one kind of dependence: a body causes another body only with a relation of position, and position cannot relate a body to something that does not yet exist. — س قده

[^V06-182]: An intellect must act and so would produce a bodily shadow or effect in the world; a soul must govern a body. Either way, separate intellects or souls would imply other bodies, whose existence has already been ruled out. The author sets aside the first claim because it would make the argument only persuasive, not conclusive.

[^V06-183]: Parts can be classified in four ways. They may share one external existence and be parts expressed in a definition, such as broader kind and defining feature; or be distinct parts considered in thought, like mental matter and form. Or they may have several existences: parts separated by position, such as parts of size, or external parts like real matter and form. — س قده

[^V06-184]: One might object that broader kind and defining feature are parts of what a thing is, not of its existence. The reply is that “existence” here includes the thing’s being established as real. The priority meant is priority in the thing’s basic reality, not necessarily temporal priority or efficient causation. — س قده

[^V06-185]: Parts of size may be posterior in actuality, since the mind first has a magnitude and then divides it. But magnitude itself makes division possible; that is what is meant by its parts. A point or an immaterial thing does not contain such divisible parts. Also, if a body had parts of size but no parts in its definition, it would still have matter and form, and therefore broader kind and defining feature, contrary to the premise. — س قده

[^V06-186]: The proof that dimensions cannot be infinite is this: one body cannot be the cause of another body of the same kind in a way that makes one prior to the other. If the Necessary Being were an extended body and caused another extended body, neither would be prior by nature. Each would have to be both cause and effect, leading to an unending series of bodies. But dimensions cannot extend without limit. — س قده

[^V06-187]: “Nature” here means either a kind essence or real existence. If a quantitative part were necessary in itself, it would still be only potential as a part, since a part of magnitude exists only as divisible magnitude. That conflicts with being necessary in itself. The clearer proof is the earlier argument that God, as Necessary Being, cannot have any limiting boundary.

[^V06-188]: This and the following proofs rule out every kind of part, not only parts of size. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-189]: The dependence here is dependence for existence, not just dependence in the way parts support a whole. Even if the composite whole were called necessary, the possible part’s existence would depend on the other Necessary Being; since existence is prior to essence, the whole would depend on that being for its existence. That contradicts the premise.

[^V06-190]: You might object that many wholes are made of parts that are not themselves the whole: a human being is made of animal and rational, and a house of roof and walls. But in a definition such as “animal, rational,” the parts are naturally related to the whole; a house is made of material of the same kind as its parts. The case of necessary and possible is different: the aspects of being free of nonexistence and being open to nonexistence have no such shared nature. — س قده

[^V06-191]: The same objection can be raised here—that a whole depends on its parts while a possible thing depends on God for existence. The reply is the same as in the previous note. — س قده

[^V06-192]: First, if something simple in thought were made of matter and form in reality, it would also have broader kind and defining feature in thought, which contradicts its supposed simplicity. Second, a thing simple in reality need not be simple in thought: accidents, for example, can be analyzed into broader kind and defining feature. This distinction preserves the two directions of the argument: simplicity in thought rules out external matter and form, though external simplicity alone does not rule out mental parts. — س قده

[^V06-193]: The broader kind depends on defining feature to become a definite kind of being, whether in the higher intellectual realm or in our own thought. In the material world, things also need features that place them in a class and make them individuals. A broader kind is incomplete and vague; it has no separate “lord” or ruler of its own, and cannot be pointed to in thought as a complete thing.

[^V06-194]: Predication requires unity in existence and multiplicity in meaning. If the parts were pure existence, there would be no distinct meanings to predicate. If they were essences other than existence, there would be distinct meanings but no common existence in which they could be one. If they were a mixture, one aspect would be only unity and the other only difference; neither would allow the required predication. Ordinary broader kind and defining feature can be predicated because they have a separate, accidental existence in the mind. But God’s essence is pure existence, not a mixture of existence and essence. — س قده

[^V06-195]: “Dividing defining feature” includes a feature that classifies a species, a subclass, or an individual. The phrase covers the possible divisions of essence: a composite species such as human, a simple species such as prime matter, or a most general kind. — س قده

[^V06-196]: The simple species is mentioned to cover every possibility. In essences, it has no broader kind above it and no species beneath it.

[^V06-197]: The previous proof began by denying essence and its dividing features. This proof begins from the fact that the Necessary Being is the highest degree of existence, so He cannot be indeterminate and await a feature that makes Him definite. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-198]: The phrase “becomes definite as an existent” applies to both kinds of feature. A species does not need a classifying feature to be pointed to by the mind; the reference may instead be to the inner senses that imagine or remember particular instances. — س قده

[^V06-199]: “Nature” here means nature as realized in individual things. No one would think that the bare nature itself—the subject of a general proposition—is divided into individuals.

[^V06-200]: True oneness is when a thing is one in itself, with no second feature serving as the basis of its unity. Non-true oneness applies when several things count as one because they share something: Zayd and ʿAmr are both human, while horses and cows are both animals. Humanity and animality are true unities; the people or animals that share them are one only in this derived sense. True unity may itself be the Necessary Being's self-subsisting oneness, or a unity added to a possible essence.

[^V06-201]: To be fair, the thinkers who offer these examples mean us to attend to the ways they bring the subject closer, not to the ways in which the comparison fails. Consider the soul and the body. God is above all literal comparisons, but the example can still teach us something. The body lives through the soul, which sustains it and is its inward life, although the soul is not simply a thing located inside the body or standing outside it. In a far higher way, the world lives through God's sustaining presence. He is the source that holds together every life and every bodily form. He does not enter things as a body enters a space, nor is He outside them as one separate object is outside another.

    As the verse says, “In every thing there is a sign pointing to His oneness.” The highest examples can therefore reveal God's light and show how everything depends on the One. Seen in that way, all faces bow before the Living, the Sustainer. A thing, considered as a sign or face of its source, reveals that source through its own existence. God shows Himself through Himself and remains unlike what He creates.

    We must not carry over the ways in which the human soul differs from God. The soul needs the body in certain respects, is affected by it, and comes to be with it. None of those features belongs to God. For that reason, after using an example, some writers immediately correct themselves and pray, as the Persian verse puts it: “You are beyond all that I can imagine or say; dust upon my head and upon my comparison!”

    Still, the right response is not to ban all examples. Prophets used them, and the Qur'an does too, as in the verse that begins “The likeness of His light is as a niche.” There is a reason for this. Our minds, tied as they are to imagination, struggle to grasp meanings without giving them a picture. This reflects the correspondences between the levels of existence. When the intellect says that God is light—meaning an ever-living reality that exists through itself—the imagination pictures a dazzling ray. When it hears that He encompasses all things, it pictures a vast physical space. When it hears that He is above every other light, it pictures something literally higher, or the sun outshining the stars in daylight.

    A mind trained in careful proof need not accept these pictures as literal truths. It can reject the imagination's mistake while keeping the meaning that the example was meant to suggest. It knows that God's attributes are far beyond bodily pictures. But consider the person whose thought is still ruled by imagination. Such a person's pictures may be as confused as dreams, whereas the images used by disciplined reason and spiritual insight can be like truthful dreams: they invite us to move from the picture to the reality it points toward.

    If imagination were denied any examples, and the mind were given only abstract meanings, the two powers would quarrel. Imagination would refuse to follow reason. It would be like inviting guests to a table and feeding only some of them, though all need food. This may help explain the poet Rumi's saying that the images by which God's friends are drawn onward are reflections of the beautiful faces in God's garden. — س قده

[^V06-202]: The author himself is cautious about the analogy: the soul's relation to its body is not the same as a shopkeeper's relation to a shop. The analogy is limited and does not make the two relations identical.

[^V06-203]: The proof can be put briefly: anything from which some feature can be denied is made definite through both an affirmation and a negation. Whatever is formed from affirmation and negation is composite. By contraposition, what is simple in reality has nothing denied of it; it is all things. This is not ordinary predication, as in “Zayd is human” or “Zayd is standing.” Ordinary predicates include both their positive and negative boundaries, which would make a simple subject composite. Here, only the positive, existing perfections of things are present in the simple reality; it has every perfection and rules over every perfection. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-204]: To say that the perfectly simple reality “is all existences” is to give the reason in the description itself: it includes every perfection *because* it is simple. It is existence through and through, unmixed with any separate essence or absence. The word “as” in the passage means “because,” much as in the Qur'anic instruction to remember God because He has guided you.

    The point is *many perfections within one reality*. One level of existence, through its undivided reality, contains the perfections found across all other existences. It can do, in its one reality, what the many do in their different ways. The statement does not mean *one reality spread among many things*, as some readers mistakenly suppose. They hear that the simple reality is “all things” and object that stones and clay would then have to be Necessary Being. That turns the claim around. It is not saying that every particular thing is the perfectly simple reality.

    The spread of unity across the many belongs instead to God's all-embracing gift of existence, received by each thing according to its capacity. Spiritual writers have often described this second view. The first view—seeing all perfections gathered in divine simplicity—is the one the author regards as especially his own. He finds only a brief anticipation in Aristotle and says elsewhere that he has not found anyone who fully knows it.

    Yet writers on spiritual knowledge also speak of seeing the many within the one and the one within the many. Perhaps the author means that they have not made the same distinction rigorously. We should not assume that their language means something crude when other, clearer statements in their works may explain it. A verse of ʿAṭṭār's *Conference of the Birds* seems to hint at the point: all are before Him, yet He precedes all; all are seen through Him, and He is seen through all.

    Mīr Dāmād also writes that God is all existence and entirely existence, all beauty and perfection and entirely beauty and perfection; everything else is a flash of His light, a trace of His existence, and a shadow of His reality. This may mean that everything called “existent” gets that description from Him, whether we speak of His own existence or that of others. But that alone is not the complete claim at issue. The fuller argument and the careful closing of its difficulties remain, in this commentator's judgment, the author's achievement. — س قده

[^V06-205]: The “not being” that contributes to composition is not a separate existing thing. The deepest composition is a positive feature together with the denial of some perfection. Existence and existence are not two different kinds; the second aspect is absence, possibility, or a boundary. Even “impossible existence” has a kind of conceptual thinghood while remaining nonexistent. A possible thing likewise combines its essence, which is not itself existence, with the absence of other perfections. The word “Zayd” points to his existence, essence, and the perfections he lacks. Pure existence has no such lack or essence and is therefore not composite. — س قده

[^V06-206]: We are speaking about what exists in reality, not simply about an idea formed in the mind, although the author's wording might suggest otherwise. The Necessary Being has no foundation but pure existence. Lower levels of existence are different: what makes them the particular levels they are includes their limits, lacks, and absences.

    Someone may object that we cannot say lower existences are made up of existence and nonexistence, since whatever actually exists is still an instance of existence. That objection confuses unrestricted reality with its limited levels. What makes a limited being this particular being is not another positive existence added alongside existence. Its limitation is real as a boundary, not as an independent thing.

    Our honored teacher offers a simpler route to the result: God possesses every perfection found in anything else, and none of those perfections can be denied of Him. Necessary existence means that His existence cannot fail under any condition. Were it bounded, existence would be absent beyond the boundary, and so He would not be necessary without qualification. He must therefore be unrestricted. No way of existing, and no perfection belonging to existence, can be excluded from Him.

[^V06-207]: The same point appears in the first journey: negating “horse” of “human” is not pure nothingness, but a denial made of an existing subject. This answers the objection that a negative statement has no subject and therefore no aspect that could be compared with its positive aspect. In this context, a negative statement with an existing subject functions like an affirmative statement whose predicate is itself negated. — س قده

[^V06-208]: The immediate point is clear, but elsewhere in *The Four Journeys* and in *The Witnesses* the wording reads differently: “What makes a thing what it is differs from what makes it true to say that it is not another thing.” The conclusion does not follow plainly from that wording. The question is why a human being is not a horse, not why a human being is not human.

    The author explains the point in *The Secrets of the Verses*. The meaning of a negative statement cannot be exactly the same as the meaning of a positive one, even when each is said in relation to something else. What the statement refers to is distinct from the meaning of the relation itself. Restricting a statement by that reference does not change the underlying nature of affirmation or denial. If the affirmation of A meant exactly the denial of B, affirmation itself would become denial. A thing would not be itself, which is impossible.

    Put another way, suppose we deny the existence of ʿAmr of Zayd. If Zayd's own existence, considered together with its limits and lacks, were itself what made that denial true, it would be a combination of positive existence and absence. That is impossible in the case now being discussed. But if we denied ʿAmr's existence of Zayd's existence *simply as existence*, we would be making a thing other than itself: for in this argument we are considering existence as existence, not as one limited share of it. If it were only a particular share that was absent, the denial would rest on that share's limitation; that is the first case again. — س قده

[^V06-209]: “Unrestricted existence” and “restricted existence” here anticipate the mystics' terminology below. A kind's definition limits it to a narrow range of perfections; a possible essence is likewise a bounded mode of existence. If one existence included the perfections of a human being and a celestial being, it would not be the restricted kind “human.” — س قده

[^V06-210]: “A fully realized kind” means a kind complete in existence, not merely a concept with a complete definition. “Animal” is conceptually definable but remains an open, broader kind; further perfections can be added without losing the name.

[^V06-211]: Philosophers usually use “unrestricted” and “restricted” for mental concepts. Mystics use the terms for real existence itself, in the sense of a reality that has breadth and encompasses its manifestations. In this usage, the mystics' unrestricted existence is like the philosophers' “simple existence, which is all existences.” — س قده

[^V06-212]: This explanation begins from the fact that the unrestricted is the source of the restricted and contains the perfections of its effects. The earlier approach is, however, more far-reaching, for it also establishes true unity and other important conclusions. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-213]: The author gives another form of the argument. Rather than begin with “the simple is all existences,” begin with the claim that the source and final end of all existence is their perfection. A giver of good cannot lack the good it gives, and a thing's end is its own perfection. If the end lacked that perfection, reaching it would not be completion but a change into something else.

[^V06-214]: A long line loses no part of line itself when separated into shorter lengths; it loses only endpoints and points of division. Those limits are not lines, and points are a different kind of thing from lines. — س قده

[^V06-215]: The verse about water more directly illustrates unity spread through multiplicity than multiplicity within unity. “Living” is explanatory here, not restrictive: every thing has life and awareness in its own way. — س قده

[^V06-216]: The flow of existence through things does not mean that the divine source acquires the changing attributes of what it flows through. In this sense, its light shines through every reality, and nothing veils it except its own majesty.

[^V06-217]: Every thing glorifies God through His own praise: all existents reveal His names and attributes. Their praise is not merely evidence of a maker, as theologians often say; every good and beautiful existence belongs first to God and only then to a thing's particular form. The editor connects this with the saying, “I have not seen a thing without seeing God before it.” The Qur'an speaks both of creatures praising and of God praising Himself. “Understanding their praise” means awareness that they are aware; embodied people cannot attain this knowledge of knowledge unless they are freed from bodily veils. — س قده

[^V06-218]: The two examples point to a distinction between an attribute that is purely real and one that is real but necessarily involves a relation. Attributes, whether used of God or created beings, fall into four kinds.

    First are *negative* attributes: statements that deny something. Even a word without an obvious “not,” such as blindness or inability to read, can express the absence of sight or learning. With God, an example is holiness, understood as freedom from defect. Second are *purely relational* attributes, such as being the knower of an object or having power over it, when we mean only the relation itself. Third are real attributes that involve no relation and do not require one, such as life, necessary existence through oneself, or a knower's awareness of itself. Fourth are real attributes which are not relations in themselves but necessarily involve one, such as knowledge of another thing or power over it. Zayd's knowledge of the sun, for example, is a form or quality in his mind. It is not itself the relation between Zayd and the external sun, but it gives rise to that relation: he is *the knower of* the sun.

    We can now see why negative attributes are not identical with the essence of the one described. Otherwise the subject would itself be an absence. Someone may reply that the *reality to which* a negative description applies can still be the divine essence, just as God's real positive attributes are identical with it even though their meanings differ. But absence and negation have no independently existing instance. An existent thing cannot itself be an instance of nonexistence. It can only be the reality from which our mind draws a negative statement. A positive meaning, by contrast, can be truly realized in something that exists. And a general nature can exist through the individual that genuinely realizes it.

    Another objection is that God's negative attributes often deny a negation and therefore affirm something. The answer is that negation, considered simply as negation, still has no positive reality corresponding to it. Any positive force in the double negation comes from the existing thing that makes the statement true, not from negation as such.

    Purely relational attributes are likewise not additional parts of the essence. If they were identical with it as relations, the subject would be nothing but a relation. The same question and answer apply: a real being allows us to describe a relation, but the relation considered as a concept is not the being itself.

    How, then, can a negation or a relation count as an attribute at all? Because the mind does not require every true description to be a separate object existing outside it. The description must be grounded in how matters really stand. For example, “being a universal kind” can truly be said of human beings or horses even though universality is something the mind recognises rather than a physical part of either animal. Thus the real, positive attributes of God, both purely real attributes and those that imply relations, are identical with His essence. The negative and merely relational descriptions are not additional realities in Him. — س قده

[^V06-219]: Here the author means the concept of God's sustaining relation, under which all His particular relations to things can be gathered. God's true existence sustains unrestricted existence, which some call the all-embracing mercy. Unrestricted existence, in turn, sustains limited beings in a dependent way. Besides these realities, we can speak of *relational* sustaining power: an idea that gathers all the relations philosophers say are additional to the divine essence.

    Could the author's meaning be different? Perhaps the realities from which we draw these relational attributes all return to the true, life-giving relation just described. On that reading, they are its aspects, and it contains them as the many divine names and attributes are understood to be contained within divine oneness. Each name would be the one named, considered through a particular perfection, as the mystics teach.

    But this reading does not fit what the author says a few lines later: these relational attributes come after both the one related and the things related to Him. The true sustaining power does not come after what it sustains. It belongs to the very first level of reality and precedes everything through that precedence. Indeed, as the act of connecting one thing with another, it cannot simply be judged identical with the essence or an additional part of it in the way an ordinary attribute can.

    Finally, the sustaining relation spoken of as an attribute means *giving and upholding existence*. If we instead take “self-sustaining” to mean only standing through oneself, it would no longer be a relational attribute in the sense used here. — س قده

[^V06-220]: God's sustaining relation means that every other being, and every existing aspect of it, depends on Him for its existence.

[^V06-221]: The author's qualification concerns denying positive existence, not denying absences or deficiencies. Denials of lack do not multiply aspects in God; denials of one real perfection from another being do. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-222]: Avicenna's statement can be read as saying not that no negations multiply, but that not every negation multiplies. Denying a perfection may multiply aspects in what is described; denying a deficiency does not. Even if the sentence is read as a universal denial, it can mean that negations do not multiply in themselves, since nothing distinguishes absences as absences; any multiplicity lies in what is denied. The author, however, rejects the claim that such negations have no effect on the subject at all.

[^V06-223]: “Extended” refers to the body's spatial form, which is sometimes loosely called “body” because it is what first appears to the senses. In a composite body, extension is only one part, not the whole body. — س قده

[^V06-224]: This follows the Illuminationist view that intellects are pure existence without essences. Or it may mean an intellect's knowledge of itself: knowledge is plainly additional to the bare concept of intellect, since no essence, considered by itself, is either knowing or ignorant. — س قده

[^V06-225]: The apparent circle is that God's life, knowledge, power, and will are said to depend on His essence, while His essence is said to depend on these attributes for its perfection. The answer is that God's living, knowing, powerful essence is already presupposed; otherwise, the attributes could not arise from it. There is no dependence on an external thing. — س قده

[^V06-226]: God's essence is the source of the attributes, while the attributes are perfections of the essence. The objection makes the essence seem more perfect than itself by comparing it with what proceeds from it, and then comparing it with what perfects it.

[^V06-227]: Al-Farabi extends the usual proof of necessary existence to every perfection: if knowledge is not necessary in itself, it depends on another, which must itself be knowing, since a giver of perfection cannot lack it. Continue this reasoning until reaching knowledge that exists through itself. The same applies to power, will, and other perfections. — س قده

[^V06-228]: A perfection is not a mere absence, such as an essence or a thing's bare existence. Whatever is drawn from existence precisely as existence must be identical with existence, not something added to it. Since the Necessary Being is pure, affirmative existence, every perfection drawn from existence belongs to Him through His essence.

[^V06-229]: In their first approach, later thinkers missed the reality of existence and treated the attributes' identity as a sameness of concepts. In their second approach, which said that the same effects follow from God's essence as follow from the attributes, they missed the degrees of real existence. — س قده

[^V06-230]: Even if the later thinkers' proof succeeds, it shows only that God has no attribute distinct from His essence. That is not yet the same as proving that all His attributes are identical with His essence.

[^V06-231]: The argument assumes that an attribute is something different from the thing it describes. Without that assumption, the claim that it depends on a subject would need to be proved. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-232]: A created thing cannot be the source of God's attribute, since the effect depends on God, and the attributes through which God acts—knowledge, will, and power—are already required for its creation. That would produce a circle. The gravest dependence would be for God to depend on an effect of His own. — س قده

[^V06-233]: “May” here means “sometimes”: it is enough to show that an agent can act on something else. A universal claim would beg the question and would not follow from the proof. — س قده

[^V06-234]: If acting and receiving were one and the same aspect, then whatever acts in a given respect would also receive in that same respect, and vice versa. Prime matter receives but does not act; a substantial form acts but does not receive its own effect. The point is not that a thing can never both act and receive in different respects. — س قده

[^V06-235]: Another possibility is that one aspect is identical with God's essence and the other is a necessary consequence of it. The author does not mention this option, perhaps because it leads even more clearly to a problem. If the essential aspect gives rise to the other, the two relations of acting and receiving would have to come from different aspects; otherwise they would conflict. If the second aspect came from yet another necessary consequence, the sequence would continue without end; if it ended in another essential aspect, God would be composite.

[^V06-236]: Receiving through passive change means gaining a fitting perfection through another thing, with matter playing a role; water receives heat in this way. Receiving in the broader sense simply means being described by something that necessarily belongs to a thing; fire is hot in this way. A simple nature considered apart from matter can still be characterized by its necessary qualities. The aspect through which it has these qualities is the same as the aspect from which they follow. — س قده

[^V06-237]: This is a further point, not a conclusion of the preceding argument. If the attributes are necessary consequences of God's essence, they do not need a separate act that makes them exist; their existence follows the essence. — س قده

[^V06-238]: The proof assumes that any attribute depends on a subject in which it inheres. That does not apply to relational attributes, which are concepts the mind draws from relations and do not inhere in God as added qualities.

[^V06-239]: There is a problem with this claim. It describes an animal's power, not God's. God's power means that the act follows His knowledge and will. The author has explained this in his other books. Al-Khafrī was wrong to claim that two meanings of power must always go together: being able to produce an act, and being such that one acts if one wills and does not act if one does not will. I think this objection, and several of the objections before and after it apart from the first, come from later thinkers. The author reports them without naming their sources and does not answer them here because he has already settled the question elsewhere. He also says that he will return to it. Notice that he opens the first discussion with 'We say' and introduces his own later conclusions with 'I say.' This suggests that the disputes between those passages are reports of arguments from later scholars. — س قده

[^V06-240]: This objection distinguishes existence in itself from existence as an attribute in another thing, treating them as different kinds of existence. The reply that follows instead treats them as one reality and identity, with different external relations. — س قده

[^V06-241]: “The first” refers to the objection that an effect is necessary in itself but possible in its receiver. “The second” refers to the claim that the receiver does not undo the necessity given by the agent.

[^V06-242]: The reply is that the effect's existence in a receiver also depends on an agent. Its existence in itself is likewise possible and depends on a cause; in this way, both kinds of existence are connected to both necessity and possibility.

[^V06-243]: The point is not a technical side issue in the debate. The objector has denied a premise of the argument, so the author must establish that premise itself. He does so in a way that answers the objection rather than merely attacking a narrower supporting point.

[^V06-244]: The proof seems to assume what its opponent already accepts: that God's essence is not itself knowledge or power, even if those attributes always accompany it. The missing premise is that an essence entirely bare of these perfections and their opposites would have to be a defined essence, not pure existence. — س قده

[^V06-245]: The proof rests on three claims: God has attributes such as knowledge, power, and life; these are perfections; and a giver cannot lack what it gives. The first is established later, the second is self-evident, and the third is argued in the discussion of cause and effect. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-246]: If the attributes are necessary consequences of God's essence, He does not gain perfection from them; they always follow from Him and come from Him. The stronger point is that an essence lacking both the perfections and their opposites would involve a real potentiality to receive them. Real potentiality requires matter, which would make God bodily. — س قده

[^V06-247]: Even those who say essences are fundamentally real still treat an essence as conceptual when it is considered apart from its actual existence. Its necessary consequences are conceptual in the same way.

[^V06-248]: The attributes cannot flow from an essence that lacks them, because a giver of perfection must possess it; nor can they flow from an essence already identical with the highest attributes, because that would assume the point being proved. The argument would require a higher aspect in God, which is impossible.

[^V06-249]: This is a consequence of the proof, not the proof's direct conclusion. The argument establishes that God is existence, knowledge, power, life, and every perfection in Himself. It does not by itself establish that each exists on its own and that all are one. The consequence can also be turned into a separate proof of identity. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-250]: “Perfection” can mean a first perfection, whose loss removes the thing itself, such as the form that makes a soul what it is, or a second perfection, whose loss leaves the thing in existence, such as knowledge. The first sense is intended here, especially in all but the final clause of the sermon; the final clause may use the second sense. — س قده

[^V06-251]: The Imam's “sincerity” may have a higher mystical meaning: passing beyond the level where existence is considered with its names and attributes to the hidden oneness beyond all names. The commentator recalls a verse of ʿAbd Allāh al-Anṣārī: “None has made the One one; whoever declares Him one has denied Him.”

[^V06-252]: In pure spiritual self-effacement, no pointing is possible. As the Imam says in answer to Kumayl's question about reality, it is “the unveiling of the lights of majesty without a pointer.” Even a mental pointer would set a limit, because the pointer would assert its own existence alongside what it points to. — س قده

[^V06-253]: The word “therefore” seems awkward here. The statement “whoever knows what Necessary Being means” appears to refer to our understanding of a concept, not to direct knowledge of the reality itself. Three readings are possible.

    On the first, the opening describes the beginning of knowledge and the later phrase about the reality of existence describes its perfection. But this goes against the plain sense and does not work: the author explicitly says that the first kind of knowledge leads us to affirm that a Necessary Being exists.

    On the second reading, both parts concern direct knowledge rather than concepts. The word “knows” can suggest acquaintance with a particular reality, and “meaning” can refer to the reality meant rather than a general idea. This reading makes better sense of “therefore.”

    The third, and strongest, reading takes the opening as perfected knowledge through concepts, and the later passage as a step beyond that to direct awareness. The use of “meaning” supports this. Moreover, the author does not say that understanding the meaning of Necessary Being is the same as directly seeing that being. He offers a proof. Someone wholly absorbed in direct awareness would not need a chain of proof in the same way. The proof is quoted from an earlier knower; the later statement is the author's own.

    On this third reading, someone first understands the general concept of Necessary Being. When the mind considers existence as such, it finds that one kind of existent does not need to be related to anything else in order to be called existent: that is God. Another kind exists only through its dependence on something else; indeed, its existence is that dependence. Understanding this distinction leads the mind to affirm a Necessary Being, because dependent beings exist and need their source. The author then takes a further step: if knowing the concept can lead to affirming God's existence, how much stronger is direct awareness of existence itself?

    An objection remains. Direct awareness normally seems to concern either knowing oneself or knowing something that comes from oneself. God is neither the knower's self nor an effect of the knower. How, then, can one know Him directly? The reply is that one can know through the passing away of one's sense of separate self in what is known. No alien existence need stand between the knower and the reality of existence. What makes them distinct is not utterly foreign to what they share. Forgetfulness creates the distance; the passing away of self-centered awareness removes it.

[^V06-254]: “Perhaps” may emphasize or qualify the claim. In some essences, identity with knowledge can be explained through a mental form that exists in itself. But self-awareness in the soul or intellect cannot depend on such an essence-concept: the soul may know itself while overlooking every universal concept. — س قده

[^V06-255]: For created existences, especially immaterial ones, “what is it?” and “why is it?” may have the same answer, as Aristotle observes. — س قده

[^V06-256]: This concerns complete conceptual knowledge. Direct awareness requires no reflection or deliberation. — س قده

[^V06-257]: The author does not mean that one cannot form a mental supposition of an impossibility. He means that one cannot understand it coherently. Some impossibilities, such as an effect preceding its cause, can be imagined in a mistaken way; others, such as a thing being denied of itself, cannot be conceived without contradiction.

[^V06-258]: The presence of a thing includes the reality on which its existence depends. Just as understanding an essence brings its defining parts into view, the existence of an effect brings the source that sustains it into view. — س قده

[^V06-259]: “Angels and those who have knowledge” can refer together to the higher, separate causes and to all beings capable of knowledge. Another reading takes the angels as the first universal intellects and those who know as universal souls, the later spiritual principles in the order of creation.

[^V06-260]: The more fully an existent gathers perfections, the fewer its absences and losses, and the greater its simplicity and unity. — س قده

[^V06-261]: An objection might be made: the author often says that what distinguishes one thing is the same reality through which it agrees with other things. Why does he reject that argument here so sharply?

    The answer is that the principle belongs to existence, not to separate essences or the concepts used to define them. In the definition of a kind, the feature that distinguishes it from other kinds is not simply the general nature that it shares with them. The feature and the general nature are distinct in thought. Their *existence*, however, can be one, especially in a simple being. Here the proposed difference would be between the reality of existence and the mere standing of a concept. That is no more a shared way of being than saying that a limited thing is distinguished from existence by its limiting essence.

    Besides, the principle is helpful only if the higher level contains the positive reality of the lower level within itself. If the higher level lacks something that must then be added to it, it would be composed of what it possesses and what it lacks—or of a shared core and an additional part. That is exactly what divine simplicity rules out, as the cited sermon teaches.

    We have also seen that real existence is itself will, power, and the other perfections. If a supposed “existence” had to receive those attributes as additions, we would no longer be speaking of real existence without qualification. The comparison between the two would break down. There would be no shared reality on which to base the claim that the same thing both distinguishes and unites them. This is a matter of argument, not merely a choice of words. — س قده

[^V06-262]: The difference between created things and God does not mean that pure existence is deprived of existence. The difference is in their attributes and limits, not in their being cut off from the source.

[^V06-263]: The point turns on the difference between “fourth of three” and “third of three.” In the first phrase, the word “fourth” does not mean the same thing as in “fourth of four”; it describes God's presence with three others, not His place as a numerical unit alongside them. Existence is not a number or a part of a number. — س قده

[^V06-264]: Philosophers use this expression for the forms of things said to be present in God's prior knowledge and care. It resembles their saying that the order of created things corresponds to the divine order. The higher realm is described as wide and great because the forms of all things, bodily and immaterial, are present there as general forms free from matter and its limiting conditions. Their existence is through the all-embracing existence of God.

    The author uses the expression for the level of the divine names and attributes. That level is particularly vast, because there the differences of meaning can be as numerous as we please without dividing the underlying reality. It is called the lordly realm because, on one mystical view, the divine names and attributes govern the kinds of things. They are the sources even of the luminous exemplars that the Illuminationists call the lords of the kinds.

    On the philosophers' other account, it is called the lordly realm because it belongs to the divine side of reality, in contrast to our created world. Here matter, motion, and separation make things seem independent of one another.

    “He is all in His oneness” is a saying of al-Fārābī in his discussion of knowledge. In this setting it means that God's one, simple existence contains the reality of all His names and attributes, without becoming divided or losing its unity. — س قده

[^V06-265]: “States” and “manifestations” may mean the successive appearances of divine light in the material world, one after another, across past and future days. They may also mean the appearances of this light in each kind of being. Every such kind has its own world, with changes both in its substance and in its outward qualities. The rising of the light for it is, in this sense, a new “day.”

    Or the words may mean the real divine names and attributes. The concepts we use are names *of* those names; the real name is existence itself considered through one of its divine perfections. God is revealed to Himself through His essence, through His names and attributes, and through the places where those attributes appear.

    In the level where the names are one, each is present in every other according to its existence. Every reality there contains the meaning of every other. The “days” at that level are not separate earthly days. They are gathered into a single eternal presence containing all times, just as the Qur'an speaks of the divine command as one and as swift as the blink of an eye, or nearer.
