    This level of the names stands between two extremes: God's undivided oneness and the many possible essences that depend on His names and attributes. It connects possibility with necessity and multiplicity with unity. The gathered disclosure of the names is called mercy at the level of attributes. When God's command “Be” appears through His actions in the ordered and varied world, that is called mercy at the level of acts.

[^V06-266]: It is the divine names, not created things, that are unmade. The Muʿtazilites held that possible essences were fixed before existence; the author instead says that divine names are many meanings within the one real existence. — س قده

[^V06-267]: This mistake confuses a concept with what the concept refers to. Saying that God's attributes are identical with His essence means their real existence is one with His essence, not that their meanings and concepts are one.

[^V06-268]: The author describes God as knowing to show that His knowledge is active and sufficient for things to exist, so it also entails power. For God, power does not mean that an act might or might not occur, as it can for us. God's will is not an additional desire beyond His knowledge; it is His knowledge of the order of existence.

[^V06-269]: Names can be combined, as in “the Most High and the Great,” “the Knowing and the Aware,” and “the Hearing and the Seeing.” They can also be individual names for particular acts, such as being the creator of Zayd or knowing ʿAmr. — س قده

[^V06-270]: Examples include “the Holy” as manifested in angels, hearing and sight in animals, misguidance in devils, and gentleness in spirits. The whole world, from the earth to the highest lights, is like a hall of mirrors reflecting the one beautiful reality.

[^V06-271]: The manifestations of God's names are gathered in Him, just as the names themselves are one in their source. In the vision where opposites are reconciled, every severity contains beauty and every affliction its remedy. The sun's light overpowers the stars without denying that they have light; so God's encompassing mercy includes every name.

[^V06-272]: If existence were only a concept drawn from things, with no real existence of its own, the attributes would be distinct existents in keeping with their different meanings. To avoid that conclusion, later thinkers made the meanings identical. The author rejects this and holds that existence itself is real. — س قده

[^V06-273]: The unity of the attributes is the unity of God's existence; the many created forms are the manifestations of those attributes. In spiritual vision, opposing forms are reconciled in God. At a lower level, their unity can be seen in their dependence on the one divine source, as many reflections depend on one mirror.

[^V06-274]: The “image-like, luminous existence” is not mixed with matter. Weak existence, such as that of matter, is not called knowledge or knower, though the reality of knowledge is not wholly absent from it. — س قده

[^V06-275]: Material bodies are divided into parts, and their bodily forms are not all present to one another. But someone could consider them as processes of ongoing change in their very nature or as a whole whose parts are gathered in one unity, and on that basis say that knowledge runs through them.

[^V06-276]: The “thing known” here means the form directly known in knowledge that works through representations. A few lines later the author calls it “a pure, luminous existence of awareness.” His point in this principle is that what is immediately known is the known form, rather than the external thing known only through that form.

    He is not yet giving a complete division of everything that can be known, nor distinguishing all the kinds of knowledge. The difference between direct presence and knowledge gained through forms will come later.

    So it would be a mistake to object that a material thing known through direct presence can exist for the knower exactly as it exists in itself. Such a thing need not be a pure light, free from matter's limitations and from absence, as the author says of the form considered here. One might reply that God's knowledge of material things strips away the rules that govern matter. But that would not fit the plain point of the present passage.

[^V06-277]: “Known” is used broadly here, for all four kinds of knowledge. In the earlier passage it meant the intellect's universal object, in contrast to sensory perception.

[^V06-278]: An objection arises about the features said to accompany a person, such as Zayd, when he is understood intellectually. Are these features general ideas of shape, position, and the like? If so, they do not affect what Zayd is, for the Zayd understood as a universal is simply a human being. A general idea of shape is not an actual bodily shape. Whether we think of that idea together with the human concept makes no difference to its existence. What matters in bodily life is a shape or position actually belonging to matter, not the abstract concept of one.

    Or are these features meant as spiritual counterparts of bodily organs? Aristotle's description of the intellectual human being may suggest this. In such a being, the 'hand' is whatever enables action and the 'foot' whatever enables movement. If someone could act through intention alone or cross the earth without walking, that power would serve as a hand or foot in the relevant sense. Philosophers do not regard such spiritual features as obstacles to understanding.

    A third possibility is that the body's actual shape, position, and color accompany the knowing soul and what it knows. Yet the soul and its knowledge do not disappear when the body disappears, still less when its position, shape, or color changes. Those features cannot be essential to intellectual knowledge.

    The answer is a fourth possibility. The features of material individuals can be included in the intellectual reality to which those individuals belong, but in a higher and unified way. The many particular beings are expressions of one reality. They are, so to speak, the spreading rays of a light or the broad bases of cones whose points meet in a higher source. Their shapes and positions therefore have their grounds in that higher reality, not merely in detached abstract concepts.

    The human being understood by the intellect is one simple, immaterial existence. It gathers the truth of every human individual into a more complete unity. Its individuals express that unity within multiplicity. The author accordingly says, in his discussion of the soul, that to understand a human being intellectually is to grasp a human being free from any particular size, place, or position, without leaving out any of its defining powers or members. The intellectual human being has head, face, eyes, stomach, hands, and feet, but all are understood in a way shared by many individuals.

    Elsewhere he says that removing every accompanying feature is not a condition of understanding a thing. The intellect can understand a human being with all its features—quantity, quality, place, position, time, shape, and members—so long as these are understood in a general intellectual way. He compares this with divine knowledge of particular things.

    He does not mean only that the spiritual *meanings* of these organs are included in a higher unity. He also means that the many features appearing in individuals have their reality within it, although not as features confined to one particular body. The reference to God's knowledge of particulars makes this clear: not an atom in heaven or earth escapes that knowledge. The author's wider rule is to hold together God's transcendence and the scriptural language describing His closeness, without reducing God to a body. — س قده

[^V06-279]: The same point can be shown in two ways: if the mind can picture size and shape, they are not barriers to being understood; and if these images are present in the mind, they coexist with the intellect as things it understands.

[^V06-280]: Any mental image, whether a particular imagined image or a universal idea, remains universal in this respect: it could apply to more than one instance. A mental image taken from one particular thing is still not that thing's particular existence.

[^V06-281]: The first argument begins from the particular nature of self-awareness: if it were an acquired image, it would be universal. The second begins from presence: if it were acquired, we would refer to ourselves as “it,” not “I.” — ط مد ظله

[^V06-282]: The conclusion is that the soul has no essence separate from its existence and is not a substance in the sense of a defined kind. The author cites Suhrawardī's description of the soul and what lies above it as pure lights and existences. — س قده

[^V06-283]: This is another way of stating the second argument, and prepares the reply to the objection that follows.

[^V06-284]: This answers the preceding objection that the soul is built from universal conceptual parts such as broader kind and difference. — س قده

[^V06-285]: You might say that it would be more fitting to mention “substance” and “rational,” which are essential features, rather than concepts that apply only as additional qualities. But the answer is that the argument moves from the general to the particular, not from what is essential to what is additional. — س قده

[^V06-286]: The objection is answered either by saying that if the soul is free of its essence, it is even more free of matter, or by observing that forgetting the concept “immaterial substance” supports the claim that self-awareness is not conceptual. The soul can also be described by many perfections without being composed of them.

[^V06-287]: The images the soul handles may have universal content, as when thought divides and combines concepts. But because they are present to the soul that works with them, they are also particular, concrete forms. If the soul knew them only through other universal images, those images would be the objects it handled instead. — س قده

[^V06-288]: The objects meant here are the forms that the soul keeps and works upon, not the bodily organs used to receive them. This is clear from the contrast with the later words about people who have reached perfection. The point to be proved is that the soul is directly aware of its powers of perception and movement, rather than knowing them through an added mental picture. Yet the second, third, and fourth arguments, and much of the first, really show that the soul is directly aware of what those powers receive or produce—the forms through which it perceives. The fifth argument concerns the soul's use of the powers, not its awareness of the organs themselves.

    There is a quiet reference here to the principle that the knower and what is known become one. Remember, too, the earlier discussion of power and action: ‘power’ can mean the source of an effect. Heat, for example, makes things less dense, and cold makes them denser. In the same way, these forms are sources through which something becomes known.

[^V06-289]: The author means that advanced knowers may have a more complete, integrated direct awareness; everyone has simple, immediate awareness of the images in their own mind. — س قده

[^V06-290]: The argument can be set out as follows. Either we are directly aware of the disruption in the particular part of the body where pain occurs, which is the point to be proved, or we know that disruption through an image of it. If through an image, where is the image? If it is in the same affected part, two images of the same kind would occupy one place. If it belongs to the soul at the level of understanding, it would be a general concept, not this particular pain. If it is present in another power, such as sight, then anyone who had the same image in sight ought to feel the same pain. That does not happen. Looking at an injury may produce distress, fear, or another kind of suffering, but it does not produce the particular pain of the injured part.

    An objection remains. Why could one form not belong to the soul in its lower, touching power, or arise in a power associated with it, while the other belongs to the bodily organ itself? They would then be present in different ways rather than two things occupying one place.

    The answer is that at the level of touch the soul is precisely the touching power. That power works by spreading through and receiving impressions in the body. A form present in it is therefore present in the affected organ as well. The difficulty returns.

    Someone might object that knowledge requires the known form to be separated from matter. But that condition applies to knowledge obtained through a mental form, not to direct awareness of a thing through its own presence. The Illuminationists likewise hold that material bodies are directly present to the Light of Lights.

    Why, then, can we not know every bodily object directly, without lifting it into a mental form? Because direct awareness occurs when a thing is present to itself or when it is aware of what depends on it as its effect. Other physical things are neither ourselves nor effects produced by us. Our bodily powers and their conditions, however, belong to us in that intimate way. — س قده

[^V06-291]: The editor qualifies the claim: ordinary sensory pain is generally perceived through an image of a harmful quality in the organ. Because the soul is united with the organ, it also directly experiences that quality. Sensory perception can err and unfolds over time, so it is not simply presence-based knowledge. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-292]: This means the distress added by imagination when it joins frightening or painful ideas to a sensation, making the suffering worse. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-293]: The argument also avoids requiring the soul to bring an endless series of images into awareness at once. The earlier argument and this one share the same problem: each added image would itself need to be perceived.

[^V06-294]: The regress is impossible because it would require an endless chain of similar images, and because it conflicts with our direct experience. — س قده

[^V06-295]: The deeper problem is the infinite regress of one image being known through another. A mere collection of similar mental images would not be impossible, since mental images do not have the external effects of physical things. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-296]: The argument is called “from the Throne” because it addresses what makes an act voluntary. It also relies on the idea that knowledge of the powers and love for them are contained in the soul's knowledge and love of itself. This is why the soul can bring its powers into activity through its own presence. — س قده

[^V06-297]: “Then” marks an order of concepts, not a time sequence. Knowledge of the soul's powers may be contained in its self-knowledge, or be considered as knowledge of its distinct powers; either way, it does not come later in time.

[^V06-298]: “Concept” here means acquired knowledge, since the distinction between concepts and judgments applies to acquired knowledge. The soul uses its powers through an innate inclination that is already present to it, not through prior deliberation. — س قده

[^V06-299]: The evidence may show only that we fail to notice our awareness, not that the image itself is absent from awareness. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-300]: The three kinds of absence may be: the object does not exist; it exists but has no perceptive or luminous form; or it has such a form but that form is not present to this particular power. The wording is compressed, and the examples can be divided in more than one way.

[^V06-301]: Two things can be distinct only in meaning, as in a knower's awareness of itself, or distinct in existence as well, as in knowing something else. The latter connection may be causal, receptive, or descriptive.

[^V06-302]: The philosophers of Aristotle’s school confine direct awareness to knowing oneself and deny it in knowing anything else. They therefore treat God's knowledge of possible things as mental images and, in the author's view, deny the fullest kind of knowledge. — س قده

[^V06-303]: The commentator connects this claim to the author's view that even acquired knowledge is grounded in immaterial forms. Particular images belong to an imaginal world, and universal ideas to intellectual realities; both can have effects in their own order. Their distance from us is what makes them seem universal or indistinct. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-304]: The “lights” mentioned here are the higher principles of the material kinds of things. They give those kinds their standing, much as the soul sustains the body through its presence. They are also the fullness of those things: not merely the ideas by which we describe them, but the realities from which their existence comes and toward which it is directed. A thing's full reality must be united with it in some way. Otherwise it could not be its completion or its goal.

    The point is to prevent a misunderstanding. The author has said that material things, taken in themselves, cannot be direct objects of knowledge. Someone might conclude that the higher principles therefore do not know them. But the higher lights are directly present to themselves and are the complete realities of the material things that depend on them. To have those complete realities present is also to have their less complete forms present. This is not how an image in our mind relates to the thing pictured. Our image neither causes the thing to exist nor joins it in that close relation between many things and their single source. A mental image therefore gives us a weaker knowledge of its object. Even so, the thing is disclosed through the image, just as what we see indirectly is disclosed through the visible form immediately before sight. Nothing need be left out of that disclosure, but it is still not the same kind of knowledge as the higher presence. We can also understand the higher lights more broadly, as including material things within their own sources in a fuller and simpler way.

    I would go further. Material things are dark and not directly intelligible *to us*. In relation to the higher principles, and especially to the First Principle, they are directly known. This is knowledge through presence and action, even if it is not the same as God's knowledge of Himself. A material thing's presence in matter prevents *us* from grasping it fully, since our awareness does not surround matter and all it contains. But the One who encompasses matter knows it through that very presence. Indeed, the thing is present to the cause that necessarily produces it more fully than it is present to the matter that receives it. Even the thing's presence in its receiver falls within the knowledge of that cause.

    The divisions, absences, and passing away that keep us from direct awareness do not limit the Principle that encompasses everything. It is present in what has passed, what is present, and what is yet to come. It remains constant through all things that change and disappear. In relation to that all-embracing presence, separation does not mean exclusion, absence does not mean ignorance, passing away does not mean loss, and darkness does not hide what is there. A prayer says that no dark night, stormy sea, or enclosed darkness conceals anything from God: for Him, darkness itself is light.

    Existence, moreover, is what is most fundamental in everything. The way one existence differs from another does not set it outside the reality they share. So the material things need not first be stripped of matter before they can in any sense know their principles. The Qur'an says that nothing exists without praising God. Their knowledge is simple rather than the combined, reflective knowledge we ordinarily call thinking.

    There are, then, two senses of life. In the narrower sense, a living being can perceive and act: touch is the lowest kind of perception, and movement by choice is the lowest kind of action. This belongs to animals and higher beings. In the wider sense, life accompanies existence itself and, in truth, is one with it. Knowledge can likewise mean the special awareness that belongs to living creatures, or the deeper knowledge that accompanies existence wherever it is found. With this distinction, we can understand how the author denies that material things are themselves knowledge in one sense while still holding that existence and knowledge are inseparable in another. — س قده

[^V06-305]: Someone may object that everything, even an immaterial intellect, exists through another—namely God. All things depend on Him so completely that their existence in themselves is also their existence through Him. Would the argument then imply that nothing except God can know itself? No. ‘Existing for another’ here means existing *in* another as in a material subject. ‘Another’ means matter in the broad sense, including underlying matter and the subject in which a quality exists. The eye, for example, does not see itself merely because it is a power present in a bodily subject. Its dependence on the soul is a different matter. A second objection is that Sadra elsewhere holds sight, hearing, and the other powers to be immaterial.

    Should they not therefore know themselves? The principles quoted here are those of the earlier philosophers, whom Sadra follows at the beginning of the inquiry but not at its end. They treated these powers as lodged in matter. Even in Sadra's account, the powers depend strongly on bodily expressions; for this reason their perception requires the presence of matter or something like it. They are not immaterial in the complete way that intellect is. Still, someone who holds that a wholly immaterial sensible form would itself be sensing, sensed, and the act of sensing can also hold that a sensory power freed from gross matter knows itself at its own level.

    Its sensing belongs to the soul, since the soul gathers all its powers; the object sensed is the form disclosed by that power. The power makes the soul manifest, and the perceived form is the power's own disclosure.

[^V06-306]: The commentator corrects the claim that the philosophers used a faulty argument. They did not reach the conclusion by combining the two claims above; they established each direction separately.

[^V06-307]: “Can be understood” includes even a very general way of being understood, such as being known as existent, necessary, or possible. — س قده

[^V06-308]: The argument runs as follows. If a being free from matter were understood by something else, it would exist for that other knower. It would then seem to depend on something else as a subject in which it was present, contrary to the assumption that it was wholly free from matter.

    An objection: why must knowing it place it *inside* the other being? Could it not be known directly through presence? The answer is that this proof belongs to the philosophers in Aristotle’s school, who treat another being's understanding as the reception of its form. On their account the thing understood in itself must have the same existence as the thing present to its knower. Its very being would then be received in the other, which is the difficulty the proof exposes.

    A second objection: universal forms are present in our intellects and are called immaterial. Does this not show that an immaterial form can exist in a subject? Here the word 'immaterial' is being used in different senses. For those philosophers, a universal form is free from external matter and its bodily features, but it is still received in the soul as its subject. On the author's own view, understanding happens through the soul's encounter with the higher realities of kinds, or through union with them. It is not the reception of an image in a subject.

    But then, the objector continues, must a universal form know itself? That sounds absurd if we think of the concept of a horse. The reply is that a concept and its intellectual existence are not the same thing. The author does not say that the *meaning* 'horse' becomes a thinking person. He says that its luminous intellectual existence is united with the knowing intellect. At that level it is intellect and soul, and its existence is aware of itself.

    Another proposed proof appeals to a pair of related terms: knower and known. If a separate being is known in its own right, perhaps it must also know in its own right, because the two relations stand on the same level. The author has used an argument like this elsewhere to explain the union of intellect and what it understands. But the commentator doubts that the relation alone proves union. Cause and effect are also related, as are mover and moved. Their relations require them to stand in a corresponding order; they do not require them to be one being. If every related pair were one and the same, we would collapse opposites into a single subject in the same respect.

    Nor does saying that the known is 'known in relation to a knower' solve the difficulty. The concepts 'known' and 'knower' remain different. Their difference does not by itself prove that two existences are involved, but their connection does not by itself prove that there is only one. A further argument is needed. One possible approach is to understand the relation as an actual connection of presence and illumination, not merely as a comparison between two concepts. This would explain a kind of union in disclosure without reducing a higher being to a lower one.

    To be fair, this is probably the author's real intention. He is not relying on related words alone. His deeper point is that a directly understood, luminous reality has no existence or activity apart from its being disclosed. Even if we supposed that nothing else existed, its own existence would still be a form of understanding. With no other object present, that understanding would be understanding of itself. This differs from an effect, which cannot remain an effect if its cause is removed from consideration. A being known in itself does not require another knower in that way. In actual understanding, moreover, something known *in actuality* requires an intellect knowing *in actuality*. That is the proper force of the relation between them. — س قده

[^V06-309]: “Essence” here means individual existence, not a universal definition. In God, knowledge and every other perfection are His existence, since He has no defined essence apart from existence. In other separate beings, their perfections are identical with their existence, not with the concepts used to describe their kind.

[^V06-310]: The exception concerns complete direct awareness, which has degrees. A person may mentally represent a separate being without knowing its own self-awareness; only someone who directly witnesses it would know it in that way.

[^V06-311]: The example of someone healing themselves may be only a verbal objection: even when a doctor or medicine acts as an intermediary, the true source of the action may still be God. The note also explains the actual distinction between a healer and a patient: healing and being affected involve different bodily powers. — س قده

[^V06-312]: The commentator argues that every relation, strictly speaking, has two sides that are understood together, and so those sides differ in meaning. He refers the reader to the earlier discussion of relational opposition. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-313]: The author distinguishes between paired descriptions, such as “knower” and “known,” and the two reciprocal relations “knowing” and “being known.” The first pair need not involve a repeated relation or real opposition; the latter can be a relation the mind considers on both sides. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-314]: If two sides were opposed by their own nature, no separate proof would be needed to show they cannot coexist. Cause and effect, mover and moved, and ready and what one is ready for do not form such reciprocal oppositions as terms; their relation comes from dependence. Earlier and later are truly opposed, so they cannot be one and the same. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-315]: The chapter gives three proofs of God's self-knowledge: knowledge is a form of existence; every perfection found in an existent belongs to the Necessary Being; and a giver of perfection cannot lack what it gives.

[^V06-316]: This is a test for deciding whether something is a divine perfection. It must not require the special conditions of lower beings, such as matter, bodily extension, change, or composition. Some such conditions follow from others, but they can still be distinguished in thought.

[^V06-317]: “Complete knowledge” here means knowing the cause's own reality and what makes it the cause, not every feature and consequence of the cause. Knowing every effect would then be included in knowing the cause. Likewise, knowing an effect in all its details could reveal its cause. — س قده

[^V06-318]: If “causal aspect” meant only the relation between cause and effect, the claim would be trivial: neither side exists as that relation without the other. The author means what makes the cause bring about the effect. — س قده

[^V06-319]: For example, fire's basic nature is a cause of heat. The source of heat is present before the relation called “causing heat” and before the heat itself, so knowing that source reveals the effect. — س قده

[^V06-320]: The effect does not identify its specific cause. Its essence, possibility, or existence considered as related to its essence is too indefinite to do so; it points only to some cause. If we know the effect as a definite existence, we may know its constituents, but this is a kind of knowing it through its own reality, not through a consequence it causes. A specific cause, by contrast, brings about its particular effect.

[^V06-321]: The commentator holds that reasoning from cause to effect gives certainty. Reasoning from an effect to a cause gives certainty only when it proceeds from a common consequence with no separate cause of its own; otherwise it does not amount to a demonstration. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-322]: The author first says that knowing a cause is one way to know its effect, then adds that things with causes can be known only through their causes. The commentator says this applies even to immediate knowledge: the appearance of any effect already includes the real cause's presence, whether or not the knower notices it. He recalls sayings attributed to ʿAlī and al-Ḥusayn about seeing God through all things. — س قده

[^V06-323]: In a detailed account, God causes the many levels of existence in an ordered sequence, from the higher and nobler to the lower. In a more unified account, one act of divine outpouring encompasses all possible things at once, beyond time. Either way, knowing the one source entails knowing what proceeds from it. The author presents this as common ground among the different views. — س قده

[^V06-324]: This is directed at followers of Suhrawardi’s Illuminationist school, just as the preceding point addresses Aristotle’s school. If God's knowledge were identical with His act of creating, then His act would not be preceded by knowledge and would not be voluntary. A voluntary act, in the philosophers' account, is preceded by knowledge, desire, will, and power.

[^V06-325]: The ancient philosophers may have meant that God does not know other things eternally because no other things existed eternally. But that does not show that His eternal knowledge excludes them: the things themselves may begin in time while His knowledge of them does not. The author rejects the denial and says it is unworthy even of a wise person. — س قده

[^V06-326]: Most theologians hold this view, even if they avoid calling God's knowledge “universal” or treating it as something added to His essence.

[^V06-327]: This note attaches to the claim about things being known “in reality,” and points out that the sources describe two ways of speaking about their prior status. Some copies also give the total number of views as eight. — س قده

[^V06-328]: Another way to organize the views is to ask whether God's knowledge comes before things. If it does, it may concern all things, as in Plato's view, or only some, as in Thales' claim that forms exist in an intellect present to God. — س قده

[^V06-329]: The author adds that the Muʿtazilites themselves may not need a separate proof of God's knowledge, since they deny that His essence has attributes.

[^V06-330]: “Higher minds” here means the higher level of knowledge. But how is this view different from that of the Muʿtazilites? They say that essences have a fixed reality apart from *all* existence, whether outside the mind or in it. The view under discussion separates essences only from existence in the external world. Once we place them in higher minds, they have existence there. That seems no different in principle from our ordinary mental knowledge of essences that have no external existence.

    The reply is that both views go wrong if they assume that a thing's essence has a reality of its own alongside existence. Existence is what is fundamental. An essence is considered a “thing” through the mind's way of understanding existence, wherever the essence is found. There are not two independent realities—existence on one side and the essence's fixed being on the other. Even when our mind treats them as two, the difference is one made by thought. Removing existence in a definition does not give us an essence that stands on its own: its being understood still depends on existence. What appears as a receiver and something received is, in reality, existence considered in two ways.

    The author's immediate point, however, is narrower. The essences are said not to have their *own particular* existences, and he rejects the idea that their fixed standing can be wholly separate from existence. His answer will say that, although they lack their own proper existence, they are present through God's existence. If the point had merely been that an essence does not exist independently of existence, one could have replied that its presence through God is compatible with its having only a dependent, conceptual standing—just as when the thing has its own limited existence. We must not overlook that distinction. — س قده

[^V06-331]: The Arabic word translated here as “recoil” means to shrink away from or keep one's distance from something.

[^V06-332]: Ibn Arabi uses “Be” as God's word. In the *Nahj al-Balāgha*, God's word is also described as His act; that act is the existence that flows through things. — س قده

[^V06-333]: The reports about things praising God before their worldly existence describe them as existing for God, not as independent beings. Their praise in that state is their recognition that dominion belongs to Him. — س قده

[^V06-334]: This is the Ashʿarite position. In another discussion, Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī interpreted the claim that knowledge “follows” what is known as a claim that the two must correspond, with the known object setting the standard for that correspondence.

    Here, by what is known first, the author of the view means an essence considered simply as what it is, apart from being made what it is by some added act. A thing's essential features are not put together by a further maker. By divine knowledge he means the simple, undivided existence of those things at the higher level, an existence that has no separate individual features of its own. To say that knowledge follows the known is to say that this knowledge takes on the distinction belonging to the fixed realities. Considering what a thing is comes before considering whether it has one kind of existence or another: we understand what belongs to it in itself before what belongs to it through something else.

    The best answer combines both ways of speaking. What is known comes first as regards the distinct character that knowledge expresses. Knowledge comes first as regards undivided existence. And what is known comes after knowledge when it appears as a particular thing in the created world.

    But why would the fixed realities choose darkness, unbelief, or misery? Everyone seeks what is good and what makes them happy, in every realm. The reply is that at the level under discussion “evil” is evil only in what the word means, not as a harmful reality actually existing there. Evil, in its realized form, is what opposes a thing's existence and well-being, while good suits it. There is no separate existence at that level for such good or evil to act in this way. Every essence seeks an existence here in which its own nature and what follows from it can appear. It does not seek to have that nature abolished.

    Think of the letters *alif* and *dāl*. The straightness of alif and the curve of dāl belong to what makes each letter the letter it is in the writer's mind. When they are written, they must appear with those features. An alif is not asking to become a dāl, nor a dāl to lose its own shape. In the same way, the fixed realities appear according to the identities they possess. — س قده

[^V06-335]: Just as each fixed reality gives itself its own character, God's one command brings to light what is already implicit in those realities. God gives generously; each being appears according to its own nature.

[^V06-336]: The comparison is that God knows His essence, names, and attributes as they are, just as He knows the forms of those names—the fixed realities. It also invokes the principle that knowing a cause entails knowing its effects.

[^V06-337]: The author has argued that image-based knowledge belongs to minds that understand through concepts. In his view, separate intellects do not contain mental concepts in this way. The fixed realities therefore cannot be things existing separately through God's names; what exists in higher levels is the fullness of existence, gathered simply and completely. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-338]: “Common usage” here means the ordinary way theoretical thinkers use the phrase. The note is not claiming that philosophical truths can be established by popular opinion.

[^V06-339]: The author has now shown how his interpretation differs from the Muʿtazilite view. He will next show how it approaches the older philosophers' account.

[^V06-340]: This assumes the view, discussed earlier, that things are known in detail within God's essence. Without that premise, the Platonist would not accept the author's argument. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-341]: If the separate forms were known through other forms, the chain would continue without end. If each were known only through itself, God would not know it before it existed. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-342]: The union of intellect and intelligible has two senses. In God's simple, perfect knowledge, the One contains all intelligibles without division. In detailed knowledge, God's light is united with each known reality without leaving its own level.

[^V06-343]: The passage supports the claim that Porphyry held a serious philosophical view. It does not, by itself, explain how God knows things in detail before bringing them about; that issue will be considered next.

[^V06-344]: The forms are called God's “word” because they arise from His essence and disclose what is hidden within it. They follow His essence eternally; His prior will is His joy in Himself, which includes the will of all things as His effects.

[^V06-345]: The argument assumes that a thing may be known even when it has no mental or external existence, as the Muʿtazilites claim. The author must rule out that assumption before the argument is complete. — س قده

[^V06-346]: The objection assumes that God's eternal power must relate to each possible thing in its own external form. The reply is that the thing can be present in God's power through the form by which He knows and brings it about.

[^V06-347]: The criticism concerns the arguments taken together. To say that relations exist because there are real things from which we can understand those relations is hardly a strange invention. Many Muslim thinkers have said much the same thing. So that claim alone cannot be the reason for calling this view utterly absurd.

    Perhaps the criticism instead refers to the idea that relations are like “the fangs of monsters”: things imagined but not found outside the mind. Yet when the author calls their existence “assumed,” he does not mean that the mind is free to imagine them without any basis. He means something like the way we speak of things in a general statement that remains true whether or not we are thinking of any particular example.

    Once we consider a relation, it can exist as a distinct idea in the mind. Before anyone thinks about it, however, its existence in the world is simply the existence of the real things from which that relation can correctly be understood. Those things provide a sound basis for the idea.

    — س قده

[^V06-348]: Some descriptions, such as blindness, can be understood from one subject alone. Other relations, such as brotherhood, require two subjects. The relation between knower and known belongs to the latter kind.

[^V06-349]: God's power is actual, not a mere capacity waiting to act. The created effect may be potential before it exists, but this does not make God's power potential.

[^V06-350]: “From outside” means from beyond God's essence. Avicenna's account is that the forms arise from God, as an effect from its source, rather than being received from something external. — س قده

[^V06-351]: The author means an attribute that comes to a thing from outside, so that the thing already exists as a subject before receiving it. In that case, receiving the attribute requires a power to be affected; here that means matter. The resulting composition is therefore real, not merely a distinction made in thought.

    One might object that a separate intellect also receives existence from its cause. Considered in itself it may fail to exist; through its cause it exists. Yet it is neither a body nor made of matter. This is no counterexample. Strictly speaking, a separate intellect has no essence distinct from its existence. Even on the common view that it has an essence, its receiving existence is not an external addition to a subject that already exists. It is not such an addition anywhere, least of all in an intellect.

    Moreover, the intellect's capacity is only the possibility of its existing; it is not a material readiness to receive something. If God, whose reality is sheer existence, had such a receptive capacity, it would have to be a material capacity carried by matter; matter in turn requires a form. God would then be bodily. He is far above that. — س قده

[^V06-352]: “Universal” here means comprehensive and encompassing, not an abstract general idea that leaves out individuals. A single higher form can contain a whole kind and its individuals, as a species-form or a complete whole encompasses what falls under it. — س قده

[^V06-353]: The forms are not successive ideas that God thinks one after another. Their order is one of cause and effect, not time. — س قده

[^V06-354]: Avicenna considers whether the forms exist in God, in separate Forms, or in another intellect or soul. Each option raises the question of how God knows the forms themselves.

[^V06-355]: These beings are assumed to be free from matter. So placing them in the “divine realm” plainly cannot mean that they are parts of God's being or qualities added to it.

    It means that they take on qualities reflecting God's qualities, while the limitations that belong to possible beings become absorbed under the higher rules of necessary existence. Matter, movement, and their consequences are what make a thing seem separate from others. These beings are free from those conditions.

    That is why the author often says that the intellects endure through God's enduring existence and are without beginning through His beginninglessness. This does not contradict the spiritual teachers' claim that nothing besides God is without beginning, since the intellects do not possess an independent eternity.

    If that is how we speak of the intellects, which are God's acts, consider how much closer His own qualities must be. A quality is more closely joined to what it qualifies than an act is to its source, even when the act is one of creation without matter. There is therefore no basis for the complaint that no one knows what Avicenna means by the divine realm.

    — س قده

[^V06-356]: If the forms exist in another intellect or soul, that being and its forms are effects of God. God must know that source and what proceeds from it. If that knowledge differs from the forms' existence, another form is needed to know them, and the chain begins again.

[^V06-357]: At first it might seem that Avicenna's argument must concern the thing itself: its knowledge of itself would have to come before it, so that we could say the thing was known and then came to exist. Its form would then be in some other intellect or soul, and the same question would arise again without end.

    If the argument concerned only representations impressed upon an intellect, it might look equally damaging to Avicenna and to the view that the forms are present in God's own being. But on closer inspection, Avicenna's point is stronger. The forms under discussion would be things that exist in their own right. Their existence would therefore have to be preceded by knowledge. They would not themselves be merely known forms able to dispense with another act of knowledge.

    Forms impressed upon God's being in the rival account are, by contrast, already forms of knowledge rather than separately existing things. Avicenna has treated the separate forms and their place in the same way. His objection resembles the one the author raised earlier against those who accept separate Platonic forms.

    — س قده

[^V06-358]: The chain would either circle back on itself or continue without end. Both are impossible.

[^V06-359]: By “relation” the author means a relation as one of the categories we use in thought, and by “cause” he means causation considered through such a relation. He is speaking about the condition that comes with the relation, not the true relation in which being is given or the real, dependent causation through which existence flows. God's existence is necessary in every respect.

    A relation considered merely as a category is a concept drawn by the mind. It is plainly different from, and dependent on, the reality of the thing to which we apply it. We cannot treat such a concept as necessary in itself. Yet it may still truly describe a thing under one of its aspects. The aspect through which something is known is that very thing viewed in a weaker way. This is like calling a person “a father” or “a universal kind”: the description adds something to our way of thinking, not a new part to the person's existence. That is the sense in which relational attributes can be said to be “additional.”

    The passage also accepts the philosophers' language of intelligible forms as following from the divine essence, and answers an objection: would that not join possible things to God? Properly speaking, what is possible here is only the relational idea of God *being a knower*. The forms themselves are necessary through the necessity of the divine essence, eternal through its eternity, and enduring through its endurance. This is an even stronger claim than the statement that the First Intellect endures through God, rather than through some separate act that keeps it alive.

[^V06-360]: The causal order applies to the chain of separate intellects, not to Plato's “horizontal” Forms. The note suggests that Plato's Forms might still reflect, in a secondary way, the order of higher realities. — س قده

[^V06-361]: Avicenna distinguishes between a form that could be understood and a form that exists as already intelligible. The forms from God belong to the second kind.

[^V06-362]: “Agency” can mean either the relation that exists after maker and made exist, or the source that brings about the effect. God's real agency is the latter and does not depend on the effect's prior existence.

[^V06-363]: By “renewed reception,” the author means that a thing must receive a change from something other than itself. If a feature followed from a thing simply because of what the thing is, it would not be a new reception of this kind.

    By “through readiness,” he means that matter must take part, because matter carries powers that can become actual. Water receiving heat is a renewed change that depends on readiness. But the number four's being even is not a change that it receives in this way; nor is fire's being hot in the same sense. These features do not come from another thing acting through some material base. Even if we imagined four and fire apart from matter, their natures would still require those features. In such cases, saying that the feature “belongs to” the thing and saying that it “follows from” its nature amount to the same thing.

[^V06-364]: Bahmanyār's word “subject” can mean simply that the forms depend on God. It need not mean that God receives them as a changing subject receives an accident. — س قده

[^V06-365]: The author's point is that knowing God's essence and knowing what necessarily follows from it are not two events. The existence of the forms is bound up with God's knowledge of them.

[^V06-366]: Suhrawardī is considering several possible orders: the forms may come before their effects, after them, or together with them. The author says each option seems to create a problem unless one sees that knowing and existence are the same here. — س قده

[^V06-367]: Suhrawardī's argument would make God's essence, by itself, insufficient to produce external effects. The author accepts that forms are involved, but denies that this invalidates the account of the first intellect.

[^V06-368]: The first intellect can be established by several arguments, not only by the claim that one simple cause produces one effect. — س قده

[^V06-369]: A plurality of aspects does not license arbitrary effects. Each form is the source of what corresponds to it in the proper order. — س قده

[^V06-370]: If the forms are God's knowledge of things, then what exists in the world must match them. Otherwise, God's eternal knowledge would not be true.

[^V06-371]: The objection repeats the claim that having forms in God requires separate aspects of action and reception. The author restates it before answering a different point. — س قده

[^V06-372]: Suppose a feature necessarily belongs to something and comes from that thing's own reality, not from an outside source. Since the source gives rise to the feature, it cannot lack the perfection that it gives. The feature must therefore be present in the source's own reality, though it need not be present in the same limited way. The forms that belong to God's knowledge come from His very being. They are present in Him in a higher and more complete way. If their higher, unified level belongs necessarily to Him, how could that level involve either a possibility of not existing or a capacity waiting to be realized? Even at their lower, more distinct level, these forms have no independent standing beside Him.

    The intellects brought into being by God have forms belonging to His knowledge, but in relation to Him they are like words whose meaning depends on something else. They exist through God's existence and endure through His enduring reality. If even these created intellects have no independent being alongside Him, this is still more true of the forms that belong to His eternal attributes.

[^V06-373]: The objection says one form would cause both the first effect and another form. A similar question arises for the first intellect, which causes both a second intellect and the outermost sphere. — س قده

[^V06-374]: The forms follow from God's complete essence; they are not additions that make Him more complete. Their existence is an effect of His perfection, not a perfection He receives.

[^V06-375]: If even separate effects do not make God more complete, the same is even more true of forms that are inseparable from His essence. — س قده

[^V06-376]: Avicenna's question concerns whether the forms' existence makes God know them, or whether God's knowing them makes them exist. The second is the right account, because the forms come from His knowledge. — س قده

[^V06-377]: God's self-knowledge is not caused by the forms. It is identical with His essence and is already complete. — س قده

[^V06-378]: If the forms' existence caused God's knowledge and God's knowledge caused their existence, each would cause itself. The author says their existence and being known are one reality. — س قده

[^V06-379]: The argument continues the same point: if knowing and existence were separate, either would need to come first and cause the other. — س قده

[^V06-380]: God does not learn about things from the things themselves. He gives them existence, and knows them as effects that proceed from Him. — س قده

[^V06-381]: Avicenna considers three readings of the claim that God knows things before they exist: He knows them while wholly nonexistent; He knows them when they exist, by receiving knowledge from them; or He knows them through prior forms. The first makes a nonexistent thing knowable, the second makes God receptive to change, and the third risks an endless chain of forms.

[^V06-382]: Relations, as understood here, do not need a separate maker or a receptive subject. — س قده

[^V06-383]: Descriptions based on relations or negation do not add a real perfection to God. The problem would arise only if an independent positive quality were added to His essence. — س قده

[^V06-384]: The main issue is not whether God can be called the “subject” of something in a loose sense. It is whether the divine essence contains a real multiplicity. — س قده

[^V06-385]: Suhrawardī's objection concerns the idea that God is the subject of many real, non-relational attributes. — س قده

[^V06-386]: If receiving means undergoing a change that depends on matter, then it requires a material subject. — س قده

[^V06-387]: The same problem would arise if the received quality came from another thing and made its subject more complete. — س قده

[^V06-388]: The example of a body becoming white assumes that whiteness comes from outside. It does not show that a necessary consequence can be separate from the essence it follows.

[^V06-389]: The author means that a simple source may be both the source and the basis of what follows from it, without being divided into a giver and a receiver.

[^V06-390]: A human soul begins as an intellect in potential and receives forms over time. A being that is always fully actual has no such receptive stage; the very forms that follow from it may be both caused and present in it.

[^V06-391]: If this statement comes from someone who holds, like the author, that God's utterly simple reality contains all existence in a higher way, its meaning is this: God's greatness lies in knowing Himself, and in that same knowledge knowing everything else. His knowledge gathers all things into one without losing their distinct details.

    Those who explain divine knowledge through separate forms usually accept a simpler and more general knowledge in God's essence. The forms do not add perfection to Him. Rather, they proceed from His essence, which already possesses perfection at a higher level.

    If the statement comes from someone who does not accept the author's full view, it has another sense. God's greatness does not depend on knowing forms or external things merely as objects outside Him. It lies in His bringing things forth as intelligible realities, considered in their relation to Him as lights and knowledge. In that respect they depend entirely on Him; His perfection does not come from something other than Himself.

    An objection may be raised: do not all these thinkers already agree that God's simple essence contains knowledge of everything? Why present this view as the special insight of only a few? The reply is that a conclusion may follow from someone's teaching without that thinker ever noticing it—or even being willing to accept it if it were pointed out. The same implication might be drawn from ordinary statements that God is self-sufficient and knows Himself and others. But explicitly recognising and proving that His single knowledge is detailed, not merely general, is a further claim. The author therefore treats it as a discovery shared with a few earlier masters of philosophy.

[^V06-392]: “Receive,” “be completed by,” and “be described by” can each suggest a different problem: change over time, dependence on another, or added qualities. None applies to forms that arise from God's essence without making Him more complete. — س قده

[^V06-393]: Avicenna's followers do not all explain the point in the same way as the author. The forms are not divine perfections because of their meanings as concepts; they are God's detailed knowledge insofar as they arise from His own essence, without an outside cause or receiver.

[^V06-394]: The author reads Avicenna to mean that God's knowledge of Himself entails the existence of these forms. Since His self-knowledge is direct, it is also the source of their existence. — س قده

[^V06-395]: “An existence that can be understood” means an existence that has the capacity to be known. A separate intellect, by contrast, exists in a way that is already intelligible. — س قده

[^V06-396]: The relation is purely intellectual and dependent, not a physical relation between a form and matter or an accident and its subject. — س قده

[^V06-397]: If the forms exist from God through the same simple act by which He knows them, then they are not separate from Him in existence. This suggests a kind of union between knower and known, though the author says its exact nature is hard to grasp.

[^V06-398]: The sentence means that God is both knower and known because He knows Himself as the source of all things.

[^V06-399]: The fact that many forms come after God's essence and stand in an order of cause and effect does not remove the difficulty. The objection made by Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī concerns the claim that forms are qualities present *in* God's being, as some philosophers say when they describe them as imprinted in Him or as inseparable features joined to Him. Even if those forms make up an ordered series, if they dwell in God's being, then His being is the place in which many distinct things exist. If they do not dwell in Him but stand apart and come from Him, the account begins to resemble the separate Forms that these philosophers reject.

    Those Forms, moreover, do not stand in a causal chain; thinkers of the Illuminationist school place them side by side. An ordered series of forms would not, by itself, require two separate principles in God, one to bring them forth and another to receive them, provided the necessity of the forms came from His very being and not from an outside source. But that does not resolve the main objection. The created intellects and lower things do not divide God's reality precisely because they exist in themselves, not *in* Him. If they were placed in Him, He would become the bearer of many things. Nor can we say that an ordered series is not really many.

    Differences between higher and lower causes remain differences; the cause and what it causes cannot be simply the same thing.

[^V06-400]: God knows Himself, and knowing a cause includes knowing what follows from it. So He knows the whole. As the Qur'an asks, “Does He who created not know?” That is: does the Creator and cause of all else not know what He created? He is subtle and fully aware. He is free from matter; and, in the argument used here, every being free from matter knows itself. Knowledge of the cause therefore brings knowledge of the effect.

    When al-Fārābī says that there is no multiplicity in God, he may mean His knowledge of all things in one complete act. That follows because a reality wholly without parts contains everything in a simple way, without separate pieces. Differences remain only between the meanings of the divine names and what follows from them, which are the natures of things. If instead he means God's detailed knowledge that follows this complete knowledge, as in al-Fārābī's own account, the many objects of that knowledge are understood in their proper order, as the author explains.

    — س قده

[^V06-401]: God's knowledge of all things is implicit in His self-knowledge; His detailed knowledge of them follows from knowing Himself as their cause. — س قده

[^V06-402]: If divine knowledge is described as detailed, saying that knowledge of all things comes 'after' knowledge of God's essence is easy enough to understand as an order of explanation. If divine knowledge is one and undivided, 'after' must mean something else. We first establish God's essence and His knowledge of Himself, then use these to establish His knowledge of all things. Or, in thought, we first consider His essence, then His being knowing, then His knowing other things. None of these stages is a change in God.

    We find a similar order when reports speak of God knowing, willing, intending, measuring, decreeing, and bringing about. Mystical writers likewise speak first of the divine essence without distinction, and then of names and attributes. The order belongs to explanation, not to God's life in time.

    In the sentence under discussion, 'after His essence' describes *all the things* whose existence is subsequent to His, rather than serving as a claim about when His knowledge occurs. The statement says that His knowledge of those things, like His knowledge of Himself, is His very essence. Some copies of the *Fusus* omit the words 'His very essence.'

    The statement that 'all things are united in relation to His essence' can be read through either simple or detailed divine knowledge. It is especially plain on the first reading. On the second, the author's account of the order of causes shows how many things are related through one source.

    The better reading is that the Teacher is explaining why an infinite range of different things does not introduce multiplicity into God's essence or His knowledge. God's one, simple existence discloses things that are many and unlike one another, because they all depend on that one stable source. Their shared dependence joins what is otherwise scattered and brings what differs into relation.

    The expression 'He is all things in unity' seems to point to the principle that simple reality contains everything in a higher way. Someone might instead say that 'all things' here means the created world as one whole, since God's essence simply knows that whole. But this reading is strained, and it gives the pronouns in the passage different references. — س قده

[^V06-403]: The critic's claim that the First Effect is “in” God would prove something only if “in” meant that it inheres in Him as an accident. That is precisely what the author denies.

[^V06-404]: Avicenna argues that the forms exist through God's knowing them, and God's knowing them is not a second act added to that existence. If one were distinct from the other, the same circular dependence or endless chain would return.

[^V06-405]: This is not like the relation of physical things to God. Bodies remain because He preserves them and exist because He creates them. Their dependence is plain: matter and its features, including change, time, and place, reveal how limited and distinct they are. In the separate intellects, even these signs of limitation are almost lost. Their relation to God is closer still, although they are among His acts, not His attributes.

    The author has been challenged for speaking this way about the intellects. If their everlasting existence belongs to God's own everlasting existence, the critic argues, then their being would be His being, and His being theirs. One of the author's contemporaries, Shams al-Gīlānī, quotes his discussion in *The Treatise on Coming into Being* and objects that a being possible in itself would then become necessary in itself.

    This objection looks more like a discussion of ordinary words than a careful philosophical distinction. Those who follow the approach sometimes called the *taste of divine unity* hold that possible essences exist through their relation to the self-subsisting Reality. Since the relation does not exist independently, in reality everything that exists does so through God's existence. Their teachers explain this in works such as *Al-Taqdīsāt*. Some even say that Zayd's existence is Zayd's God. Yet they do not conclude that a possible essence has become a necessary essence. On their view, the essence remains possible, while the existence through which it is present is another matter. That is why they speak of multiplicity among existing things and unity in existence itself.

    Perhaps the critic also objects to the spiritual teachers' claim that the meanings of the divine names and attributes exist through the One they name, so that a name is the same as the named Reality in one respect and different in another.

    Here, likewise, the intellects have essences to which possibility belongs, while their existence comes from God. Essence and existence must not be confused. Each has its own proper account.

    Even if we adopt the author's own position, according to which existence is fundamental and has levels differing in strength, the separate intellects still have no independent existence beside His. Their existence is dependent, like the meaning of a word that cannot stand alone but needs another word to complete it. One line of poetry says of such realities, “We were exalted letters that had not yet been spoken.” They show or point toward the divine essence. When something is considered only as a way of pointing to another, it has no independent standing. That is the meaning of saying the intellects belong to the divine domain and exist through God. In one respect they are not God; in another they are not entirely other than Him. The same applies to one who loses separate self-awareness in the reality contemplated.

    How, I wonder, can critics accept the saying attributed to ʿAlī, the foremost of those who ascend through knowledge, “I tore off the gate of Khaybar through divine strength,” while denying this relation? And how can they accept the Qur'anic words, “You did not throw when you threw, but God threw”? — S. R.

    — س قده

[^V06-406]: Why does the argument speak specifically of what is first brought into being, when the same account also applies to things that are formed within the world? One answer is that the universal kinds found here are preserved as one individual succeeds another. Knowledge of particular things is then contained within knowledge of their universal kinds, as the philosophers in Aristotle’s school maintain. Another answer is that the whole of the world, considered without dividing it into separate things and in relation to the one God, is one thing issuing from the One. Scripture says, ‘Our command is but one.’ From this point of view, the whole is brought into being at once, without any prior matter or stretch of time.

    Matter, time, and place already belong to the whole we are considering; they cannot stand outside it as conditions for its creation. The argument is stated in universal terms because science deals with general statements. Compare ‘Every Necessary Being is simple’ or ‘For every Necessary Being, what it is and that it is are one.’ ‘Form’ can mean either what makes something actually exist or the form grasped by the intellect. — س قده

[^V06-407]: One may object: how can the forms be without limit? The kinds of immaterial and material beings, whether heavenly or earthly, seem finite. Nor does this account assign a separate form in God to every one of infinitely many individuals. Further, if infinitely many forms are ordered as causes and effects, there seems to be an endless chain of causes bounded at either end.

    There are several replies to the first objection. “Without limit” may describe their range or intensity by reference to God's unlimited light, rather than an independent infinity of forms. That is why the author says that they are eternal through His eternity. Or the basic kinds of substances and qualities may be finite while their combinations are beyond counting. There are only a few basic tastes, for example, yet their mixtures have innumerable varieties. A further possibility is that the forms of particular things exist in God, each with the features that make it a kind represented by only one individual.

    The second objection assumes that the entire limitless collection forms one chain of causes. It need not. On the view that forms exist independently as dominating lights, the causal order belongs to the higher lights, not to the equal-ranking forms called exemplars. Moreover, the order extends towards the effects; it does not require an endless series leading upwards towards the first cause. — س قده

[^V06-408]: The argument denies that God's essence is multiplied when He knows many things. The many things are known through one eternal source.

[^V06-409]: “The known things” here means things as they exist outside knowledge, not the forms by which they are known. — س قده

[^V06-410]: These philosophers accept that God's perfect, all-embracing knowledge is identical with His essence and comes before particular things. That knowledge is enough when the things known are themselves forms whose very existence is detailed knowledge of them. God's giving them existence is then the same relation as His knowing them.

    But they say that external material things are different. For them, the simple knowledge in God's essence is not enough to explain His detailed knowledge of things outside Him. The forms must therefore proceed from Him before those external things come to exist. The objection cannot be forced on the philosophers of illumination, since they hold that God's producing external things and His knowing them are one relation. The Aristotle’s followers disagree, relying on their rule that what is directly known must exist for the knower, not merely in matter.

    A clearer way to put their argument may be this: God's existence is pure oneness and simplicity, while the many things outside Him are, by comparison, pure multiplicity. To connect the two, they posit an intermediate level that joins unity and multiplicity. This is the level of forms impressed in knowledge. The author's words can be understood in that sense. — س قده

[^V06-411]: These forms are necessary consequences of God's essence; the essence does not cause them as a separate agent causes a separate effect. — س قده

[^V06-412]: The critic's either-or argument works only if “substance” and “accident” are both taken in the same sense. A mental substance can be a real accident in the external order, so the two descriptions do not exclude each other. — س قده

[^V06-413]: The forms are substantial in meaning but exist in God as dependent qualities of knowledge. Since they are causes of external things, their effects make them more, not less, suited to be real. — س قده

[^V06-414]: The author accepts that these forms are not perfections added to God. God's perfection is His own essence, which contains all that proceeds from Him in a higher way. — س قده

[^V06-415]: This passage has two purposes. First, someone might say that al-Khafrī only meant that God's knowledge of external things through forms is not perfect knowledge belonging to His essence. If that is so, why does the author twice speak of God's knowledge of the forms in a way that seems to contradict him?

    Second, one might grant that reading of al-Khafrī but still argue that God's knowledge of external things follows from the production of the forms. The reply is that God's knowledge of the forms is the very knowledge through which He knows the external things. In much the same way, knowing an image of the sun is a way of knowing the sun itself: the image is how we direct our attention to what it represents. We can distinguish what is known directly from what is known through it, but that does not give us two separate acts of knowledge or prove that one act comes after the other.

    Likewise, there is no difference here between knowing by means of the forms and knowing the forms themselves. We therefore need not force the word “by” in the passage to mean “because of”; it can simply connect “knowledge” with its object. — س قده

[^V06-416]: The comparison with Suhrawardī is only partial. Suhrawardī says the presence of things themselves is God's direct knowledge; al-Tusi instead makes forms in higher intellects or souls the basis of that knowledge.

[^V06-417]: The objection to this account has already been explained: material things do not become intellectual forms in themselves before they are abstracted from matter.

[^V06-418]: A consequence that follows from an essence follows it in both ways of existing. It cannot exist in the world while its essence exists only in the mind, or vice versa. — س قده

[^V06-419]: It would be better to direct the objection at two claims: that the forms impressed upon the intellect are effects of the Necessary Being, and that knowledge acquired through representations belongs to the qualities of the soul.

    If we grant both claims, those impressed forms have a kind of existence outside the mere act of thinking. In that respect they are real features, as other qualities of the soul are. Their necessary connection with God concerns what they are, as existing qualities whose existence depends on the Necessary Being. It does not arise simply because they are ideas in a mind, or because they have such-and-such a mental content.

    — ط مد ظله

[^V06-420]: The First Being cannot be present in a mind as a mental image. He is pure reality, not something that can enter any mind and become an idea. Nor do the consequences that necessarily follow from Him need Him to be an object inside our thought. On the argument being discussed, they must therefore be real things rather than merely mental things.

    But this reasoning can be challenged by our own mental forms. Suppose the forms in our minds were properties of essences simply as essences. They would have to belong to those essences whether or not anything existed—an obviously false result. Suppose instead that they were “mental properties.” Would our souls then have to exist *inside another mind* before those properties could belong to them? That is also absurd. The argument would leave only the claim that our mental images are external properties, which contradicts what it set out to prove.

    The answer is that all these forms are real in themselves, because they belong to a soul that exists in reality. A feature of a real being is itself real. We call them “mental” only in comparison with the other real beings they represent. In the case of God, philosophers use “mental” for forms said to be present in divine knowledge, and “external” for beings that have their own standing or are present in matter, where one thing is absent from another. Indeed, by the usual test of whether something produces effects, the forms in divine knowledge would have a stronger claim to be called real: they are understood as sources of the things that appear in the world. — س قده

[^V06-421]: One defence of the rule says that knowing a cause directly must bring with it direct knowledge of the effect. On that reading, the rule supports the view that God's knowledge is detailed and immediately present even while it is one complete act. It would not support the view held by Aristotle’s followers that He knows things through separate representations.

    But there is a difficulty with that reading. The rule was discussed by the philosophers in Aristotle’s school in the part of logic concerned with demonstration. They did not generally accept direct knowledge except in the case of a thing knowing itself. Moreover, demonstration works through ideas and judgments, not through immediate presence. If the rule applied only to direct awareness, the logical distinction between proofs that move from cause to effect and proofs that move from effect to cause would lose its purpose.

    Avicenna and other Aristotle’s followers therefore do not grant that a cause's knowledge of its effect, or an effect's knowledge of its cause, must be immediate awareness. Nor do they take this rule to establish that claim.

    — ط مد ظله

[^V06-422]: Direct knowledge of another thing can come through union with it or through being its cause and encompassing it in existence. The author's claim is that God's knowledge of created existences works in this second way. — س قده

[^V06-423]: One might object that, even if existence itself is what God brings into being, the rule that knowledge of a cause brings knowledge of its effects establishes only that He knows those effects somehow. Why must His knowledge be direct rather than through representations?

    The answer is that existence has no single fixed manner of being and no essence that could be represented by a separate image. An image represents the nature of an existing thing, not existence itself. To know a particular existence as such is therefore to know it by its presence. The author's point is that the rule used by everyone to establish *that* God knows other things also establishes *how* He knows them: directly, not through forms impressed upon a knower, as some thinkers maintain.

    A second objection follows. Earlier, the author seemed to criticize Suhrawardi, Nasir al-Din al-Tusi, and others who regard existing things themselves as God's knowledge. He suggested that they had overlooked a familiar principle: for something directly perceived, its existence in itself and its existence as perceived are one. Material existence, however, is normally concealed from perception, while knowledge requires presence and disclosure. Why does he now seem to endorse the view he earlier challenged?

    In the earlier passage, he was explaining why the philosophers who rely on mental forms had adopted their position. Their rule made it difficult to call material things directly present to God. The thinkers who defended direct knowledge might not have accepted that rule, or they might have held that material things are not hidden *from God*. His knowledge embraces matter as well as everything else. Things with position and direction remain what they are, but before Him they are all equally present, as though gathered into a point.

    Here the author's task is different. He uses the established rule about knowing effects through their cause to reject knowledge by impressed forms and to support knowledge through presence. The main purpose is to challenge the theory of representations, not simply to repeat an earlier proof. — س قده

[^V06-424]: These existences may be called God's knowledge because they are one with His knowledge in existence, and their being known is considered before their being made.

[^V06-425]: The author adds a fourth objection to mental forms: image-based knowledge belongs to souls that perceive through sense and intellect. Separate beings that are free of matter know through direct presence, not mental concepts. — ط مد ظله
