[^V06-426]: The author has briefly used a principle of his own—that existence itself is what is brought about, and that it is known by direct presence—to criticize the approach of the philosophers of illumination. He now turns to the proof that Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi gave for that approach, a proof he regards as persuasive rather than decisive.

    But the heart of al-Tusi's argument is sound: if two causes are one, their immediate effects must also be one. Otherwise, many things would come directly from one source. In this case, there would be the forms arising from God's knowledge of Himself, which is identical with His being, and then the things corresponding to those forms, arising from His being again.

    Perhaps the author took al-Tusi to be arguing merely from a likeness between cause and effect: because the causes are one, it would be fitting for the effects to be one as well. That is not the force of the argument.

[^V06-427]: The forms follow from God's essence without existing later than it. They exist and remain through His existence and permanence, rather than being separately produced.

[^V06-428]: God's knowledge must belong to His divine reality, not to a world outside it. If Plato's Forms were external beings, they too would need prior knowledge and the same question would repeat. Properly understood, the Platonic view can be brought close to the account here.

[^V06-429]: Al-Fārābī's reconciliation treats divine Forms as present in God without being spatial shapes or separate worlds. This avoids the claim that God creates blindly or at random, while also avoiding an external realm of images. — س قده

[^V06-430]: If the divine forms were not connected to God's knowledge, one might treat them as separate realities existing on their own. But that would not make them ghostly images or bodies occupying places outside our world. They could instead be the inward realities of this world, not another set of objects beside it. Nor would that alone require infinitely many worlds.

    One interpretation of the Platonic Forms, discussed in the First Journey, treats them as independently existing measurable images, one for each particular member of each kind. The Teacher's point is that the divine forms should be understood as forms of knowledge, not as extended figures. If they were figures belonging to matter, they would be bodies, and bodies need places. This would put other bodily worlds alongside ours. Aristotle objected to such worlds, among other reasons because of the empty spaces they would require, as in the problem of two spheres touching at a single point.

    An endless series would follow as well. Each bodily world would have to be preceded by knowledge of it. But if that knowledge were itself a collection of separate, measurable bodies, it would make another world in need of yet earlier knowledge. There would be no end.

    There is another possible reading. Suppose the divine forms were not intellects and forms of knowledge belonging to the divine realm, standing inwardly and above this world. Suppose instead that they were separate intellects occupying a place outside it, alongside it, while the natural kinds here were not their shadows or effects. Such intellects would still act and produce natural forms. Just as things in this lower world reflect higher realities, these supposed intellects would produce lower worlds. Since these forms would themselves constitute a possible world, that world too would need earlier forms of knowledge, and so on without end.

    The issue is not that each divine form must be a whole world and that there are infinitely many forms. Rather, each *world of forms* is supposed to be preceded by another such world. On this reading, calling the forms 'images' means that they disclose the things beneath them. Describing them as being in places outside the world is a way of saying that they stand alongside it rather than above it in the order of causes. The first reading, however, seems the clearer one.

[^V06-431]: Their argument says that God's essence is what distinguishes each thing, and that whatever distinguishes a thing is knowledge of it. The second claim is the converse of “knowledge is what distinguishes a thing,” so the argument needs that reversal to be valid. — س قده

[^V06-432]: The author says the argument as first reported has a different structure and does not rely on reversing the claim about knowledge and distinction. The wording may mean that the second argument is only a restatement of the first.

[^V06-433]: These thinkers focus on essences and general concepts. One concept cannot be another, so knowledge of one concept cannot be the same as knowledge of all concepts. If the focus is existence itself, however, one simple knowledge may contain detailed knowledge.

[^V06-434]: If they understood that this oneness is true unity, not the numerical unity of one thing set against another, they would see the point. Every thing has a face turned toward God and a face turned toward itself. These two faces are not combined as one independent thing is combined with another, but more as an object is related to its shadow. A Persian verse says, in effect: “You are a radiant sun, and I am the shadow it casts in the west; the shadow's coming and going depend on the sun.” When the sun of truth rises, the passing appearances fade and all faces bow before the Living, the Sustainer.

    We have already established, in discussing existence and essence, that a thing's existence is simply the reality of that thing. Its essence does not become real in any other way. Consider blackness. As a meaning, it is blackness; as something real, it exists. But the actual occurrence of blackness is not a second thing added to a blackness that already existed. What was supposedly awaiting this addition would itself have no determinate reality. The actual blackness is one instance of existence. Reality deserves the name “real” more fully than anything that becomes real only through it.

    The difference, then, is between qualities within one reality, not a separation between two independent things. As the Qur'an says of invented gods, “They are only names that you and your fathers have named, for which God has sent down no authority.” The objection that prompted these remarks may therefore be met by distinguishing a thing's true existence from the names and descriptions we attach to it. — س قده

[^V06-435]: The argument has addressed the problem on the side of the knower—how one simple essence can know many things. It now raises the problem on the side of the things known: they do not yet exist. — س قده

[^V06-436]: This refers to the Muʿtazilite claim that nonexistent things have a kind of reality before they exist.

[^V06-437]: The reply is that a thing can be distinguished by what makes it the thing it is, just as it can be distinguished by its mental form. The thing's relation to a receiver is possible; its relation to the source that brings it about is necessary.

[^V06-438]: The commentator objects that a cause and its effect must have some likeness or shared nature; otherwise, one cannot explain how one produces the other. — س قده

[^V06-439]: A mental form represents the thing known, either by sharing its nature or by resembling it. A cause is neither the nature nor a likeness of its effect. The relation between cause and effect is more complex when existence, rather than essence, is considered. — س قده

[^V06-440]: This seems to contradict the first reply, which said that what gives a thing its character distinguishes it. The commentator reconciles them by saying that the first reply concerns differences among the known things, while this answer concerns the absence of differences within the one act of knowledge. — س قده

[^V06-441]: If “human nature” means an essence with properties added to it, then the claimed inclusion is false. The better reading is the complete intellectual human reality, which gathers all the levels and perfections found in human beings. Its implications follow from its existence without coming later in time. — س قده

[^V06-442]: For example, one may understand divine light as present and manifest, while also imagining physical light, its spread, extension, and duration in time. The mind understands the spiritual meaning while imagination supplies a bodily picture.

[^V06-443]: This is mainly a criticism of how the view is worded, as the author hints by saying “what can be inferred from the surface of this account.” The deeper example of simple knowledge is not itself refuted. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-444]: This is a mistake. What exists only potentially is the *detailed* knowledge of answers when those answers depend on the imagination. If their images are not in the imagination, we may wrongly suppose the answers are absent from the intellect. And because the answers are not there as a number of separate details, we may suppose they are not there in a single, gathered form either.

    But the simple knowledge that has become a settled ability is already knowledge. Indeed, it is actual: the person does not need to acquire the subject again before answering. What is still potential is the display of that knowledge as separate, numerous answers—not its possession as a unified and powerful understanding.

    One can go further, although thinkers of this school may resist it. Knowledge may be more distinct and exact in that unified state. Distinctness follows existence, which is itself the power to appear and be known. The fuller an existence is, the more perfectly it can contain distinctions, even when these are not spread out in time. Different ideas appropriate to the settled understanding can be drawn from it. That understanding is stronger in existence than the changing images in the imagination. It creates and supplies those images. Because it produces them, the original writer may have mistaken it for a mere power to know rather than knowledge itself.

    The point is that even the second kind of human knowledge—the settled ability that a person cannot yet draw on because the needed conditions are absent—is actual knowledge. Having knowledge and attending to it are different things. So are having it in the imagination and possessing it in the intellect, and knowing things as a number of separate details and knowing them together as one. The soul is not exhausted by its imagination, not even in its highest capacity. An object can therefore be absent from imagination without being absent from the soul's simple understanding. — س قده

[^V06-445]: The author repeats the earlier questions because he believes the replies do not answer them.

[^V06-446]: Without some account of how God's knowledge relates to different possible things, His essence would either become identical with them or be made of parts. One possible account is that God's knowledge relates to existents as a definition relates to what it defines: an effect is an incomplete expression of its cause, while the cause expresses its effect in a higher way. — س قده

[^V06-447]: This view avoids saying that one simple being knows many different things in detail by saying it knows only the First Intellect in detail. But God's essence would still know two different things in detail: itself and the First Intellect. This brings back the earlier problem. — س قده

[^V06-448]: “At the level of His essence” means that the knowledge follows necessarily from God's essence; it does not mean that it is identical with the essence. The view differs from the theory of forms in God's essence because it combines general and detailed knowledge and gives each level of causes and effects its own degree of detail.

[^V06-449]: This is the earlier objection to forms present in God's essence: knowledge before creation cannot be something added to the essence.

[^V06-450]: The phrase may refer to another account of knowledge. Since the author did not list it among the recognized views, the note suggests that he may instead mean the theologians' account, in which divine attributes are considered in a causal order. — س قده

[^V06-451]: “Some earlier philosophers” may refer to Thales of Miletus. The author may instead have al-Tusi in mind, whom he elsewhere identifies with this view.

[^V06-452]: In the realized account, the First Intellect exists through God's existence and is eternal through God's eternity. In that sense, it might serve as God's knowledge. — س قده

[^V06-453]: The reasoning is: knowledge itself is necessary existence, and no essence is necessary existence. So no essence can itself be the reality of knowledge. The First Intellect has an essence; therefore it cannot be God's knowledge.

[^V06-454]: In mystical usage, “substance” means what is followed and depended on; “accident” means what depends on something else. The world's forms are dependent on the divine reality, so they are “accidents” in this sense, while God is the source they depend on. — س قده

[^V06-455]: Presence may be an essential feature of the thing that is present, but it is an active relation for the cause. An effect depends on its cause in its existence and detail. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-456]: The objection assumes that presence comes after the thing present. The reply is that presence may be drawn from the thing's existence and so be active knowledge in the realm of effects, not a later state in God.

[^V06-457]: A simple reality contains all things, and stronger existence means greater light and disclosure. The many names and forms differ in meaning, not as separate existences. A cause knows its effects more fully than they know themselves. — س قده

[^V06-458]: Knowledge is the condition in which something is present and makes other things present. It therefore belongs to light: light is its center and its true meaning. In God, the reality of light is complete. Indeed, God is the Light of Lights. All other lights—those that rule, those joined to souls, and those found in the material world—owe their light to Him. That is what calling Him the Light of Lights means.

    A being whose own reality is true light without limit must be present to Himself through that very reality. By shining upon what is other than Himself, He also makes all other things present to Himself. How could anything in the unseen or the visible world be hidden from Him? Why would He need separate pictures or forms to make their objects known?

    This is a strong argument if “light” means existence itself: the reality through which things are, which appears by itself and makes the natures of things apparent.

    — س قده

[^V06-459]: The forms we imagine can become as vivid as external things, or more vivid, while remaining our own knowledge. On this account, the world and everything we see are manifestations of the soul's light and are known through its relation to them. — س قده

[^V06-460]: “Illumination” points to two linked ideas: light makes things visible, and a cause has power over what proceeds from it. A cause therefore knows its effects through its direct relation to them, without needing their images to enter it. — س قده

[^V06-461]: God does not see through an organ, as human beings do. His whole existence is sight, hearing, and every other perfect way of perceiving.

[^V06-462]: The author rejects two kinds of image-based knowledge and one kind of direct self-perception. The only valid account is that the soul itself knows its bodily powers through its relation to them. A power cannot know itself through an inner image: that would either duplicate the image in one place or lead to an endless chain.

[^V06-463]: The point is not that sight proves all knowledge is a relation. The author first argues that sight works through an illuminating relation, then says God's knowledge can also work this way because His knowledge is described as sight.

[^V06-464]: The imagined form is drawn from the storehouse of images already formed in the mind.

[^V06-465]: The author has said that al-Tusi's view is nearly the same as Suhrawardī's. Suhrawardī applies illumination to bodies and bodily powers; al-Tusi says that God knows things through forms present in higher intellects and souls. The note explains how al-Tusi's account can be read consistently with that of Suhrawardī. — س قده

[^V06-466]: Every being free from matter is capable of understanding and can itself be understood. There is no barrier between such beings. Each therefore knows the others, whether they are above it, below it, or at the same level.

    This explains the statement that intellectual beings receive forms of things that are not their own effects. The point is not that God knows some things directly and others by receiving images of them. God knows both these beings and the forms within them through their presence to Him. Nor does it mean that an intellect knows its own effects merely through representations. The intended distinction concerns things that are *not* its effects.

    Such things include higher intellects that cause a lower intellect to exist, and intellects at the same level, neither of which causes the other. They also include, for each governing intellect, the material forms governed by *other* intellects. Each material form depends on the higher being responsible for it, not on the being responsible for a different form.

    Why does an intellect know these other beings? Nothing separate from matter is hidden from another such being. Moreover, every intellect knows the Necessary Being, and everything else is His effect. Knowledge of a cause entails knowledge of its effects. All created things must therefore be knowable to every separate intellect, whether they are above it or below it. What lies below it as its own effect is known directly through presence.

    Why are forms needed for things it does not cause? When the thing known is neither identical with the knower nor subject to it as an effect, its form must be brought before the knower. Thus an intellect knows higher intellects and those alongside it through forms, since it does not govern them.

    God, however, knows everything directly. Every being is either His immediate effect or the effect of one of His effects. The condition for direct knowledge of another—the complete power by which it is made present—is fulfilled in His case for all beings. He knows Himself as the cause of the first effect and thereby knows that effect. The first effect causes the second, which is also known directly through its cause, and the same applies throughout the order of existence. Similarly, each separate intellect directly knows what lies beneath it as its own effect, through its power to illuminate and govern it. The higher principles therefore know directly even the bodies that depend on them, all the more so the First Principle of all principles.

[^V06-467]: Because the First Cause has power over everything, it knows material things directly through the same light by which it brings them into being. — س قده

[^V06-468]: The separate intellects and souls know some things through their own forms; God knows those beings and their forms directly, without another form representing them. The account therefore aims to cover both universal and particular things. — س قده

[^V06-469]: This view does not actually refute God's prior care. It only fails to prove it. Those who rely on the view must offer another account of divine knowledge and care.

[^V06-470]: The objection is that “illumination” may be only a name for an ordinary relation. If existence itself is treated as only an idea, and essences are dark in themselves, then the relation cannot explain how things become known. — س قده

[^V06-471]: A thought can match or fail to match reality. This difference belongs to image-based knowledge, not to direct knowledge of a thing present to the knower. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-472]: The author allows that direct knowledge is not divided into concepts and judgments. His objection is that the illuminationist account makes all knowledge a relation, while knowledge is one light and perfection, not an empty relation.

[^V06-473]: The same objection applies to the previous point: perception has recognized forms, and this account must show where illumination fits among them. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-474]: Suppose the objection means that God, or any immaterial being, directly perceives particular material things, and calls that perception “sensation.” We can accept the implication and deny that it is absurd. Those who say that God's knowledge is a kind of sight do not shrink from this conclusion. Thinkers who say that God hears and sees but needs no bodily organs—including the author—must mean that every existent is directly present to Him. Visible, audible, and other particular things are among those existents. He therefore sees and hears them directly, not merely by knowing general descriptions of them.

    For us, particular things become present through bodily senses because we need them. For God, they are present through His own essence. As the reports put it, His whole being is hearing and His whole being is sight.

    But if the objection uses “sensation” to mean a bodily faculty housed in an organ, the implication fails. Direct perception of particulars is not the same as possessing a material sense-organ. “Perception” can sometimes be contrasted with abstract understanding or knowledge. The difficult case here is the material particular, which has position, extension, and quantity. An immaterial particular presents no comparable problem: understanding a separate intellect or an immaterial particular is an act of intellect. That is why it is said that every immaterial being knows itself.

[^V06-475]: The classification concerns what is known in itself. A body may be known through its image without being known as a body in itself, just as a visible thing is known through light or color rather than as an object of sight in every respect. — س قده

[^V06-476]: This refers to the author's view that perceptible forms are living, immaterial forms in the world of intellect. — س قده

[^V06-477]: The claim that each changing material thing has an enduring, intellectual form is one way to support the existence of Plato's separate Forms. If material beings were already directly known, the separate forms would not be needed. — س قده

[^V06-478]: A relation that is simply a category of thought may come after its two terms. An illuminating relation, which is grounded in existence, need not be later in that way. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-479]: The author says elsewhere that material bodies cannot be known in themselves because their parts and motions are hidden from one another. The note points out that this objection may be answered by treating their movement as one continuous whole. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-480]: This claim seems to conflict with the author's earlier suggestion that al-Tusi has a different view. The note says that his account may be closer to Suhrawardī's than it first appears. — س قده

[^V06-481]: The same problem applies to sight: the soul does not control external objects, so illumination alone cannot explain how it sees them.

[^V06-482]: The author distinguishes between God's active knowledge in creating things, which is true on this view, and His complete knowledge before creation, which it does not explain. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-483]: The author does not reject every kind of general knowledge of things. A simple being knows itself in detail and thereby knows all else in a general, unified way. What he rejects is an incomplete account that knows things only through their effects after they appear. — س قده

[^V06-484]: Two questions arise. First, the author elsewhere says that divine forms are separate intellects, but here says that the divine knowledge is the existence of things in God. He may be using “forms” in another sense or pointing to how closely each thing's intellectual existence is joined to its divine existence. Second, “reality” may mean the thing itself or the world of intellect. In either case, divine existence is the thing in its fullest and highest form.

[^V06-485]: In other words, this knowledge is independent as existence and light, but follows the known things in the ways their particular meanings appear within it. It is not created by them; it reveals what is implicit in the divine names and fixed realities. — س قده

[^V06-486]: “Keys” can mean the keys to a treasury or the treasuries themselves. The second meaning is supported by other verses, which speak of God's treasuries but not of keys to them.

[^V06-487]: These forms are not known by themselves, because they are absorbed in witnessing God. They are known only as His lights, and only God fully knows them. — س قده

[^V06-488]: This account treats God's oneness as the highest level of existence, without limit. Other levels are real but lower. In other philosophical and mystical accounts, only God's existence is fully real and all other existence is secondary. The names and attributes belong to a level below the essence, even though they are not things added to it.

[^V06-489]: If the broader kind were literally a part of the defining feature, the defining feature would also be a species, and the broader kind would have to belong to it in the same way. The author rejects this: the broader kind meaning is present in the form's existence, but is not a separate part of it. — س قده

[^V06-490]: At first sight, it is difficult to say that a distinguishing feature or a form is itself a kind of existence. Existence has no broader kind, no defining feature, and nothing corresponding to them. Also, saying that something is a kind of existence does not, by itself, mean it lacks an essence. Whiteness, for example, is a kind of being, but we can still say what whiteness is.

    Consider the real features from which definitions are drawn. A body's bodily form corresponds to its capacity to have three dimensions. The plant soul corresponds to growing. The sensing and moving soul corresponds to sensation and movement by choice. Surely each of these has a nature we can describe. Otherwise nothing would have an essence at all.

    The answer is that the author is speaking particularly about the defining perfections of substances such as rational souls and separate intellects. Following the Illuminationist philosopher, he often says that human souls and intellects are pure lights, or pure existences, without a separate limited essence. An essence, in this account, is a concept that describes an existence confined within limits. Intellects are not confined in that way; souls, too, do not remain fixed within a single limit.

    A little later he will deny that the kinds of animal life below humanity possess distinct, positive defining perfections of this sort. The differences used in their definitions are ways in which their more general nature grows stronger. These differences are not separate existences added to the animal, but they are not mere absences either: an absence could not help make something the kind of thing it is. The final positive perfection, on this account, is the human being's holy or rational soul, a way of existing rather than an additional essence.

[^V06-491]: The many meanings do not weaken simplicity. They show that one existence gathers more perfections, loses less, and shines more fully. — س قده

[^V06-492]: Otherwise the human being could not be the last and most complete kind, gathering the earlier kinds within it. Or it would have to be put together from features that stand alongside one another rather than forming an ordered whole. There is a distinction here between considering the human being and a horse as separate kinds, and considering how lower forms are gathered into higher ones. When we consider the earlier forms as distinct things, they are like matter in relation to the last form. When we consider what they share without separating them from what completes them, they are wider kinds, and the final distinguishing feature gives them their completed identity.

    This is one meaning of the saying, ‘The greater world is folded within you.’ Someone might object: how could a wider kind exist without its distinguishing feature, or matter without form, when the feature or form is what makes it complete? The reply is that denying a particular feature does not deny the more general reality. In the final kind, its last distinguishing feature completes all the wider kinds, and its last form completes all earlier material levels. This fits especially well with the view that the soul changes in its very existence until it becomes united with the active intellect. That intellect, by God's permission, brings natural kinds to their completion and gathers what is actual in them, just as prime matter gathers their possibilities.

    A simpler way of stating the point is that animal life may count as a complete kind among animals but, in the human being, is included within a fuller reality.

[^V06-493]: God is the source who gives existence to all beings directly, and through their existence gives rise to all the different kinds of things. One principle therefore requires that every form of existence be present in Him in a higher, simpler, and more complete way. Other principles require that He be the source of every meaning and concept, without those meanings existing within Him as separate, limited things.

    His knowledge of His own complete existence is thus the very same as His knowledge of all existences and all kinds. Because His existence is one and simple, that knowledge is also one. Yet it reveals all things distinctly. Existence is light, and the stronger that light becomes, the more clearly things can be disclosed. If different meanings are distinguishable through the weak, scattered lights of the world, they can be disclosed still more clearly in the single, powerful light of the eternal source. Their unity there does not erase their differences.

    That explanation follows the author's own principles. The explanation favored by some mystics instead says that God pours out the kinds of things through His names and the forms of those names. In that account their fixed realities are present in the divine names. As they put it, glory belongs to the One who joins unity to unity and multiplicity to multiplicity. That approach will be discussed later.

    An objection: this may explain God's direct and complete knowledge of things eternally, before they appear. But how can He already know them *as actual effects*, since they come into existence in time?

    The answer is that the existence spread through things is the appearance of one reality in many forms. The earlier account described many things contained in one source; this describes one source appearing through many things. These two levels are not separate realities. Created existence depends on its source like a word whose meaning depends on its connection to other words. It cannot stand alone, and whatever is true of it is grounded in that source. Seen as a whole, the existence spread through many things is still one light, while also disclosing each thing in detail. It belongs to the divine order and is prior to created things by the priority of its source, not by being an earlier event in time.

    Furthermore, the kinds present in divine knowledge are the very kinds that appear in the world. Their existence in knowledge is complete in a way that embraces the limited forms they later have. To grasp the complete reality is to grasp what appears from it. Once we understand how cause and effect belong together, the difficulty falls away.

    The question of divine knowledge is central to theology. Many other questions can be understood through it, especially the unity of God. The claim that the one, simple reality includes all beings in a higher way is, at heart, a claim about direct divine knowledge. — س قده

[^V06-494]: This repeats the principle that a truly simple reality is all things in a higher way. Since God is fully present to Himself and nothing is absent from His unity, His self-knowledge is knowledge of all things.

[^V06-495]: A concept represents an essence when it describes a limited kind of existence. Concepts of God's names and attributes, by contrast, point to unlimited existence.

[^V06-496]: If God's existence does not belong to these meanings as their particular existence, then they are not essences belonging to God. They are meanings that appear through His existence.

[^V06-497]: “Potential” here can mean strength or power, not a readiness to exist. The unified divine existence is the power and source through which all separate existences appear.

[^V06-498]: When the forms are separate intellects, three questions arise. First, why do these intellects exist at all? That is not the issue here. The answer is that their existence is an expression of God's generosity. As His acts, they come from Him and return to Him as their beginning and end.

    Second, why would God's knowledge of things need these intellects to carry the forms of other things, like books containing His knowledge? That question, too, is not the present one. The answer belongs to the account of the levels of knowledge. Because these intellects are actually immaterial, their knowledge is their very existence, even if it is not identical with what they are. An immaterial being knows itself and the things that come from it. The knowledge of one such being does not make the knowledge of another unnecessary. Each also counts as God's knowledge at the level of His acts, because its reality and what it knows are present to Him.

    The third question is the one intended here. If God's own essence is already simple knowledge of all things, and if that one knowledge is detailed as well as simple, what need is there for forms alongside Him or for separate Platonic forms and lights? The author has already said that those who place God's detailed knowledge after His essence have not understood the point. Why, then, must these additional forms themselves be detailed knowledge? The following argument is meant to answer this third question. — س قده

[^V06-499]: The word here is a verbal noun linked to its object. An objection may be raised: knowing a cause must involve knowing its effects, but why must that knowledge take the form of separate images? If knowledge of the effects is already contained in knowledge of the cause itself, the principle is satisfied even more fully. So the question remains.

    The reply lies in the author's words, “in the way in which it is its effect.” Knowing the cause means knowing each effect in its higher mode of existence, where it is present in its divine source. But it also means knowing that effect in its own mode of existence, as the actual effect that follows from the cause. On the account being discussed, this second kind of knowledge is through forms.

    There is another objection. If these forms exist together with the knower, we can see how knowledge is through forms. But what if they exist separately as intelligences made of light? On the Platonic account, they are themselves acts of knowing. The reply is that, although they are knowledge in themselves, they are also independent forms through which material things are known. Seen in relation to God, they are His lights and His knowledge: they exist through Him and are eternal through His eternity. Seen as His effects in their own right, they are things known. The same realities can therefore be forms of knowledge in one respect and objects of knowledge in another. — س قده

[^V06-500]: Simple knowledge follows from the principle that a truly simple reality contains all existences. Detailed knowledge of things as effects follows from knowing their cause. This lets the author explain how the different accounts of divine knowledge fit together.

[^V06-501]: This passage explains more than the immediate question. It helps us understand the realm in which things were first known, divine decree and destiny, and the descent of spirits without their leaving their own higher level or changing their essential nature. It also explains how they can ascend without the higher level having changed.

    The essence of a thing remains recognisable through its different appearances, and its ways of existing have one continuing source. So when a natural being descends to the lower level that belongs to its kind of existence, that descent can be described as the descent of its higher, divine or intellectual reality. Yet the higher reality has not abandoned its own level. When the natural being rises, its rising is truly a return to its higher source, because it has never been cut off from that source. The higher reality and the lower reflection are one truth understood at different levels.

    The difficulty and the value of this knowledge lie in holding both sides together. Earth and matter have no place in the world beyond matter, yet what appears here is connected to that higher world and can rise toward the heavens and angels. Conversely, the world of bodies cannot contain a soul in its full reality, still less an intellect. Such beings have no bodily position or direction, yet their influence comes from their own source into this world, fills it, and gives it order. The Qur'an speaks of God ordering the affair from heaven to earth, “then it rises to Him.” The movement is real in the lower being, without requiring the higher source to change places. — س قده

[^V06-502]: Things do not literally break away from God as moisture separates from the sea. They are related to Him through their existence. God's essential existence is a stillness in the sense that nothing is waiting to happen and nothing changes in Him. — س قده

[^V06-503]: “The power of the moment” means that each reality receives existence according to its own capacity.

[^V06-504]: The passage about reaching the heavenly bodies might seem to say that individual souls, after passing through the levels of earthly bodies, attach themselves to one celestial sphere after another and finally unite with the universal Soul and universal Intellect. Some thinkers have understood the journey that way. But taken literally it would involve a soul passing from body to body, which is reincarnation. We should instead read the journey as a continuous rise in being: the soul develops and changes inwardly, through changes in its very existence.

    On one interpretation, the “heavenly body” is the subtle spirit associated with the brain. Because it is refined and balanced, it resembles a celestial sphere; it carries the soul's powers, serves as the camp of its forces, and receives life. Or the phrase may refer to the enduring body of the world of images.

    A better interpretation, in my view, is that the soul is attached to a heavenly body as something can appear *through* a visible form without becoming that form's soul. Suppose a person has an intense attachment to a beloved image and can encounter it only through a reflection in water or crystal. That does not make the person the soul of the water or crystal. Nor does the soul's reaching the universal Soul and universal Intellect mean it must literally inhabit each heavenly body in turn.

    Skipping all those heavenly levels would look like a sudden leap, unless the soul had already advanced so far during earthly life that it no longer needed a body at all. The prophets may travel in that way, and there may be others. But someone still attached to the forms, pleasures, and habits of this world cannot pass beyond them without loosening those bonds completely. Such a person must turn away from constant concern with eating, drinking, and the other habits of bodily life, and cease clinging to sights, sounds, and passing particulars. — س قده

[^V06-505]: This rejects the claim that an effect needs its cause only to begin existing, but not to continue. The outpouring of existence does not stop.

[^V06-506]: In this usage, pure existence without any determination is the divine essence. When that same existence is considered through a perfection, it is a name: as self-manifesting and revealing all things, “Light”; as giving existence knowingly, “Powerful”; as revealing Himself and all things to Himself, “Knowing”; and as expressing the fixed realities, “Speaking.”

[^V06-507]: In the usual language of theologians, an attribute is a quality that exists in something else. — س قده

[^V06-508]: Theologians also use “essential attribute” for a feature without which a thing would not be what it is, or for a feature that makes similar things alike and different things distinct. — س قده

[^V06-509]: In mystical language, the attribute is the divine reality considered under a particular determination. The name is that reality together with the determination; the essence is the reality without that determination. These are not separate existences, so the names and attributes are not later additions to God.

[^V06-510]: “The essence with each predicate” means the one reality considered through each quality that applies to it. The note clarifies that the name and attribute refer to realized existence, not merely to an abstract mental idea. — س قده

[^V06-511]: “Belonging to” here does not mean that the fixed entities are effects produced by existence. It means they are present through existence, like matter through form. They are not independently created things or accidents added to existence. — س قده

[^V06-512]: This means the names and attributes as concepts.

[^V06-513]: These “fixed realities”—whether concepts of the divine names and attributes or the possible essences of things—are not independently existing realities. They are distinctions made in thought. They exist only by virtue of an existence that is not their own. If their being intelligible to God depends on their being present to Him, and that presence belongs to them only through something else, then strictly speaking it is their existence that is present and known in itself. We have simply returned to the argument that God knows all things because they exist for Him.

    Calling these concepts what is known only replaces what exists and is known in itself with what does so through something else. It explains nothing and may make the argument worse.

    Moreover, as has often been pointed out, essences and other general concepts belong to the way souls think. They are abstractions, and some arise from mistakes of imagination. Beings that are entirely free of matter, both in what they are and how they act, do not know by acquiring such concepts. Their knowledge is direct presence. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-514]: A thing's dependent, relational existence cannot stand on its own. God's knowledge of it in that mode is already contained in His knowledge of it in its higher, unified existence.

[^V06-515]: This appears to assume that a thing's divine existence and its possible existence are joined in some way, whether as intellectual, imaginal, or material existence.

[^V06-516]: This points to the soul's ascent after its descent into lower levels. — س قده

[^V06-517]: “Form” here means whatever makes a thing actual. If it meant only a mental picture, the term would not apply to direct knowledge of the soul, intellect, or God, since they know through presence rather than images.

[^V06-518]: Every existent reality is the ground for certain general meanings at its own level. The note applies this to difficult cases: even an impossible claim can be understood through its concepts, and fixed entities can have features such as possibility and being known without existing separately. — س قده

[^V06-519]: This means the many concepts that describe God's essence. — س قده

[^V06-520]: The wording should be understood as “difference in the possible beings' kinds of existence,” not simply difference in concepts. — س قده

[^V06-521]: The problem concerns different possible compositions, whose parts differ in existence. Such compositions need a cause to determine how they are arranged. — س قده

[^V06-522]: The thing that determines the shape is not the shape-receiving body's own nature. The body needs matter in order to take on one shape rather than another. — س قده

[^V06-523]: This refers to the Muʿtazilite view that God's attributes do not exist as separate qualities in Him, but that He still acts as a knowing and powerful being.

[^V06-524]: This refers to the Ashʿarite view that God has added attributes such as life, knowledge, power, will, speech, hearing, and sight. The author rejects their separate existence, though he accepts that the names differ in meaning. — س قده

[^V06-525]: “Nothing is general” means that things are detailed in reality and in God's knowledge, whether they exist in His divine knowledge or in the wider world of command and creation. One level of knowledge is unified but detailed within its unity; the other knows things as effects in their particular existence. The difference between these levels is the difference between unity and multiplicity. — س قده

[^V06-526]: The author says “it is possible” because the verse can also mean that the verses are made firm and then clarified. Both readings point to wisdom.

[^V06-527]: Divine decree is the all-embracing knowledge that follows the level of the divine essence. It has several forms. There is decree in a gathered state: the forms present in the Pen, meaning the First Intellect and, taken together, the ordered series of intellects. Their ordered connection makes them one whole. There is a first detailed decree: the forms present in the intellects considered separately, as a group on the same level. And there is a second detailed decree: the forms passed on to the universal souls through their intellectual side. These are also called the Preserved Tablet and the Mother of the Book. The term “Mother of the Book” can also refer to decree taken as a whole.

    Destiny likewise has two forms. One belongs to knowledge: the forms present in the celestial souls and in the world of images. The other belongs to things as they actually occur: the ordered stages in the record of existence itself. The author has set aside that last account in this book.

    Every kind of decree and destiny has a source from which it comes and something in which it is received. We must also distinguish a receiver we identify only by an act of thought from a receiver that exists in reality.

    The source of decree in its gathered state is divine care. Its receiver *as considered in thought* is the nature of the Pen. The forms are not, in the author's mature account, additional things lodged inside it. They are the fuller, simpler way in which the lower existences already exist in the Pen. They become distinguishable only when we think of the concepts and essences belonging to them, much as the fixed realities are linked to God's names. So the capacity attributed to the Pen in thought is also the capacity attributed to these forms. Their receiver in reality is the lower Tablets. On the different view of philosophers who picture forms as imprinted on a knowing subject, their real receiver is the subject in which the imprint occurs.

    The source of the first detailed decree is the Pen. Its receiver considered in thought is essence, while its real receiver is the Preserved Tablet—or, for those who accept imprinted forms, the subject that holds those forms. A similar account applies to the second detailed decree and to destiny. There is one important difference: the receiver of the forms of destiny is not merely identified by thought, because those forms are added to the things in which they appear. Thus the author's reference to the “places” of decree means both kinds of receiver—the Pen and the Preserved Tablet—while the real receiver of destiny is the Tablet of Erasure and Confirmation.

[^V06-528]: “Providential knowledge” is knowledge of the end for which an action is done. Every action has an end present to its agent. Since God is the end of His own actions, He knows the fullest perfection of every being and the best order in which they exist. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-529]: The author's account treats providence as God's own simple knowledge, which is detailed, active, and prior to things. “At the level of His essence” marks its priority; “in which beings are revealed” its detail; “the most complete order” its perfection; and “leads to their existence” its active role. — س قده

[^V06-530]: This means that the knowledge is present in God's essence, not outside Him as another necessary being. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-531]: In ordinary use, a judgment makes a disputed relation necessary: for example, a judge declares that an estate belongs to one person. Applied to existence, God's decree is the cause that makes each effect exist through its complete cause. God's universal decree is His active providence. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-532]: The account given by Aristotle’s followers calls the intellects God's decree because they are the place where the forms of things appear. On the author's account, the forms are not created effects but the higher, simpler existence of the things that appear below them. — س قده

[^V06-533]: “Destiny,” in this discussion, means the measure and arrangement of a thing, including the limits and particular features of its existence. In ordinary crafts we see how measurement works. A tailor measures cloth before cutting and sewing it. A carpenter measures wood to make a chair with a certain shape. A mould gives the material poured into it a particular size, form, and appearance.

    When we apply the idea to real existence, destiny refers to the particular qualities and effects that a thing receives from the causes involved in its coming into being. Each kind of cause, each condition, the place where it acts, and everything that prepares the way can affect the outcome. The surrounding factors also shape the thing's later properties and actions. Together they are like a mould determining how the finished object will be.

    Three conclusions follow. First, a predicted result may fail to occur if “destiny” is being considered in relation to only some of the causes. A partial cause does not guarantee what the complete set of causes would produce. Second, destiny in this particular sense belongs to the material world. Here there are many causes, and they support or obstruct one another. In a realm free of matter, the complete cause does not require matter, a bodily form, surrounding conditions, preparatory causes, or the removal of obstacles. Only the cause that acts and the purpose of its action remain; in a fully actual immaterial being these are united. Third, divine decree has a wider reach than destiny in this narrow sense. Decree applies beyond the material world as well.

    If we expand the word “destiny” to include every kind of limit, even the limits used to define a thing's essence, it can then apply to material and immaterial beings alike, just as decree does. Under that wider meaning it also does not fail to take effect. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-534]: The Aristotle’s followers understand these forms as images imprinted in a soul. The Illuminationists understand them as separate forms that exist on their own. — س قده

[^V06-535]: The intellects are many because they differ in rank and strength. They are one because their existence is shared and they do not contain matter, time, or place, which divide things in the lower world. — س قده

[^V06-536]: “Nūn” is linked here to the universal soul, which holds the possibilities of the world. The two bowls in the Arabic letter point to two kinds of possibility; the line joining them points to necessary existence. — س قده

[^V06-537]: “Spirit” and “Word” can refer both to the intellects' descent from God and to the soul's ascent back toward Him. The author holds that the soul can ultimately unite with the Active Intellect.

[^V06-538]: In the philosophers' usage, “rational soul” can include the intellect. Mystics describe seven levels in the human being: nature, soul, heart, spirit, secret, hidden, and most hidden. Here, the simple intellect is general knowledge, while the heart or rational soul is detailed knowledge.

[^V06-539]: The comparison is between two books, both of which are the soul's own record, and two fires. The fire in the later life is the fire of nature. It has three branches and contains, more intensely, the cutting, burning, and painful effects of the fire we experience here. Yet this is still described in bodily terms. It has none of the light and benefits that earthly fire can have.

    The comparison does not hold in every respect. On this account, animals do not undergo the moral pain that human beings undergo, because the states in question are at odds with the nature of a human being in a way they are not for an animal.

    This book of the soul is real, but every reality has a more outward and delicate expression. So, in the world of images after death, the wicked person has a written record, like an unrolled sheet surrounded by the fire of punishment. This follows the outward language of revelation. The author has explained the matter more fully elsewhere.

[^V06-540]: The sun is called the world's heart because it gives light and heat, which support life. The body's warm life-force and its powers of sense and movement depend on it, as a lamp gives off light. — س قده

[^V06-541]: A dictionary explains “the splendours of God's face” as the lights of His face. In a commentary on *The Philosophy of Illumination*, the phrase means the lights of the eternal essence. When the angels nearest to God behold them, they glorify Him, overcome by His majesty and greatness.

    Why is the number seventy thousand used? One explanation begins with seven levels in the human being, the subtle faculties mentioned in the previous note. It adds the nine heavenly spheres and the one world of elements, making ten. Existence is of one kind, though its levels differ in how fully their effects appear. Just as the earthly soul has seven inner levels, so do the heavenly souls. Seven multiplied by ten gives seventy. Each level is a place where a thousand of God's beautiful names are displayed, making seventy thousand levels and veils. Their existence is a veil of light; our concepts of them can become veils of darkness.

    It may be clearer to reverse the calculation: each of the seven levels contains ten elements. In this symbolic account the human being is formed from ten portions—one from the elements and nine from the heavenly spheres. Pride is linked with the sphere of the sun, anger with Mars, love with Venus, and so on. Each of the seven levels contains these influences in its own way and reflects the divine names.

    Another explanation takes the lights to mean separate intellects and bodies. On that reading seventy thousand expresses an immense number, not an exact total. The intellects corresponding to the kinds of earthly, marine, heavenly, and stellar beings would be beyond counting. Or we may say that the intellectual world gathers the ten bodily worlds and their seven levels into a higher unity, each level disclosing the divine names. God knows best.

[^V06-542]: The purpose is that the divine essence be known. The fixed entities and all that follows from God's names return to His attributes, and the attributes are His essence. — س قده

[^V06-543]: “Lost spirits” means spirits carried beyond themselves by love of God, with no awareness of where they are or of anything other than the Beloved.

[^V06-544]: The simple universal being contains all intellects in unity; it is not a separate collection with its own existence. — س قده

[^V06-545]: The author accepts that one simple existence can be many in unity and one in multiplicity. He does not accept the view that the universal sphere is merely a collection of separate spheres.

[^V06-546]: These “seas” are the three hundred and sixty divine names mentioned in a report attributed to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq. According to the report, God created a name that was not a spoken sound, a physical shape, a color, or anything that could be compared with a created thing. It was free of direction and limit, beyond the grasp of the senses and imagination. Though hidden, it was not covered by anything.

    God made it a complete word with four parts, none of which came before the others. He disclosed three parts because creatures needed them and kept one part hidden—the treasured name. He then assigned four supports to each of the three disclosed names, making twelve. For each support He made thirty names of action, making three hundred and sixty in all.

    The report gives examples of these names and qualities: the Merciful, the Compassionate, the King, the Holy, the Creator, the Maker, the Shaper, the Living, the Sustainer who does not sleep, the Knowing, the Aware, the Hearing, the Seeing, the Wise, the Mighty, the Compeller, the Supreme, the Most High, the Great, the Powerful, the Giver of Peace, the Giver of Faith, the Guardian, the Originator, the Incomparable, the Exalted, the Majestic, the Generous, the Provider, the Giver of Life, the Bringer of Death, the Resurrector, and the Inheritor. The list continues until the total reaches three hundred and sixty.

    They are called “seas” because of the abundance of existence and effects that flow from them. If there are three hundred and sixty active influences on the side of the source, there are corresponding ways of receiving those influences on the side of what is created. The comparison extends to the three hundred and sixty degrees of the heavenly sphere.

[^V06-547]: The sphere's movement should not be compared to a ship moving one way while its passengers move the other. Circular motion can occur while something inside the sphere remains still. — س قده

[^V06-548]: The speaker does not mean that Hell is literally contained within the celestial sphere. The point is that sense-perception reaches this limit. If a person sees the sphere and its contents as separate from God, rather than as signs of His names and attributes, they may approach the world of Hell. The seven gates of Hell are compared to the five outer senses and two inner senses, common sense and imagination. For those who rise toward God, the Footstool is a passage toward Paradise.

[^V06-549]: When we attribute knowledge, power, life, and similar qualities to God, we begin with qualities that we recognize as perfections in beings like ourselves. We then remove everything in them that involves absence, limitation, or weakness. What remains is a pure perfection, which can be truly attributed to God. He is pure perfection, lacking nothing that counts as a perfection. The real difficulty is to understand *power* in this way, whichever of its two definitions we use. Human power, with all its bodily and limited features, cannot be attributed to Him unchanged.

    Consider the first definition: the ability either to act or not to act. Here *ability* means that neither alternative is ruled out by the nature of the agent. The agent is not forced into inaction because acting is impossible, nor forced into acting because inaction is impossible. In short, the agent is not bound by either alternative. The definition does not say that power must be different from the one who has it. Our power is something other than our essence, but that need not be so in every case.

    Proofs have already shown that God's existence contains no absence, that He is unlimited, and that He brings everything into being through His own essence, not through something added to Him from outside. Since His essence is the source of His action and is itself unrestricted, He cannot be confined either to acting because not acting is impossible, or to not acting because acting is impossible. His power is precisely this freedom from limitation, and it is identical with His essence.

    Nothing above Him or equal to Him can force an action upon Him; nothing else exists independently of Him in that way. Nor can one of His acts force Him to produce that act or another, since every act comes after Him in the order of dependence and relies on Him in every aspect of its existence. It would be especially absurd to suppose that God first gives His action the necessity by which it exists, and is then Himself compelled by that necessity to perform it. This shows how He acts freely under the first definition of power.

    Consider the second definition: an agent who acts if He wills and does not act if He does not will. This connects acting with will. In us, will is a state of the soul. It follows our knowledge of a benefit that moves us to act—or, put more simply, an additional motive. We act because that motive is present and we know it. Our capacity to act is therefore tied to our knowledge of something that gives us a reason to act.

    Now suppose we affirm will in God's essence, taking it to mean knowledge of what is best. We have already established that God acts through His very essence, and that His knowledge is identical with His essence. What limits human agency—our separately acquired will and knowledge—is therefore, in God, none other than His unrestricted essence. The second definition also applies: He acts through will and through knowledge of what is best, knowing the thing together with the good achieved through its existence. His power means that His essence is the source of every good and every benefit.

    But a benefit that follows an action in the outside world is not a goal that God needs to reach or a motive that makes Him act. Rather, that benefit is present in His knowledge, which is His essence. It would make no sense to say that God's knowledge depends on an external result. The goodness of an action follows from the action; the action, in turn, follows from God as its source. Its later benefit therefore cannot act back upon Him, control His action, or compel Him. Nothing else can compel Him either. He is free without qualification.

    Under both definitions, then, divine power means that the agent is unrestricted. No necessity imposed by the act or its omission limits Him. By contrast, if power is taken to mean that an act is entirely undecided because the agent provides no determining cause, that would deny genuine agency and leave the act to mere chance. — Ṭ. M.

[^V06-550]: “Possible” can mean that supposing something to happen leads to no impossibility. This is possibility *in practice*, as distinct from possibility *in itself*. Consider the nonexistence of the First Intellect. Considered in its own nature, the First Intellect is a possible being: it does not exist necessarily through itself. But it is not possible, in the other sense, for it to fail to exist when its necessary source exists. To suppose its absence would lead to the impossible claim that God does not exist. God is far above that.

    If we understand God's freedom to act as possibility in itself, we meet the difficulty already mentioned. If we understand it as possibility in practice, the difficulty is greater. Some theologians have gone further still. Rather than saying that an act of creation could be interrupted, they say such interruptions actually occur across an imagined, endless series of times. On careful examination, all these claims are impossible.

    — س قده

[^V06-551]: The author turns the usual objection around. God is not less free because His will cannot change; His action is free because no separate cause or motive controls it.

[^V06-552]: Rāzī says theologians could allow an eternal world to come from an eternal cause, if the world were eternal. — س قده

[^V06-553]: Rāzī does not say that an eternal world could exist without a cause. He says theologians rejected an eternal cause and effect for a different reason. — س قده

[^V06-554]: Theologians treat a necessary link between cause and effect as a threat to choice. Some allow the effect not to follow; others say one option can be chosen even when both are equal.

[^V06-555]: “States” are lasting features such as knowing or being powerful. Some Muʿtazilites treat them as eternal and dependent on God, which makes their view more complex than Rāzī suggests. — س قده

[^V06-556]: The study of nature deals with bodies and things that change. The study of divine things deals with God and His relation to the world. — س قده

[^V06-557]: An agent can act by choice over an endless time. A cause that acts by necessity may still stop when a condition changes or an obstacle appears, as fire needs dry wood in order to burn it.

[^V06-558]: Natural motion has one necessary direction at a time. Circular motion is different: the moving body can approach and then leave the same position as it travels around. — س قده

[^V06-559]: To be “compelled in one's choice” means that a being is made a chooser and its action depends on causes or motives beyond its own independent power. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-560]: The discussion began with power, whereas Avicenna's words concern choice. The result is the same. Choosing an act means that it follows four principles: knowledge, intention, will, and power. Choice can be understood as the combination of these, rather than as one further thing.

    If a reason or motive guides the choice, then power acts through that motive too. In us, a motive is a branch of knowledge: it includes a judgment that doing the act will bring some benefit. And if intention and will both depend on that motive, the exercise of power must also depend on it, because they come before the exercise of power and intention is part of how power is defined.

    Intention and will can be distinguished in two ways. Intention concerns the nature of the result, while will concerns its actual existence. Intention can also mean a general resolve to act, while will means the resolve directed towards this particular act.

[^V06-561]: Avicenna is using “power” broadly here. In us, the ability to will is itself only potential; power in the strict sense is the ability to act if we will.

[^V06-562]: God's will is not a new intention that aims to create something. It is one with His eternal essence. — س قده

[^V06-563]: An intention to reach a goal cannot continue after the goal has been reached. This is why the author rejects intention as the right description of God's will. — س قده

[^V06-564]: If there really is a distinct state of the human soul between firm knowledge and action, and we call it *will*, then it is an intention: an inward movement toward acting, like the natural tendency of a body to move from one place to another. It is not simply desire, even an intense desire, as will become clear. Nor is it knowledge. Love, dislike, satisfaction, anger, sorrow, joy, and other states of the soul all involve awareness, but that does not make them identical with knowledge. If will is a separate state coming between our knowledge and what we do, it cannot itself be our knowledge.

    This also shows why removing the limits of human will does not turn its meaning into God's knowledge. The very ideas are different. By contrast, when we remove the limits of human knowledge, we can still keep its basic meaning: something being present to a knower. Even if we take away every feature that makes it a temporary human mental state, that meaning survives. In the case of will and knowledge, however, stripping away limitations cannot make two different meanings identical.

    If we attribute will to God after removing those limitations, we should therefore understand it as a quality shown in His actions, as we do with creating, bringing into being, and showing mercy. When an act has everything necessary for its completion, we can call it *willed* from the side of the act and say that God *wills* it from the side of its source. Similarly, what sustains a thing in existence is its provision: the thing is provided for, and God is the Provider. The Qur'an commonly speaks of will in this way: “We wished to favor those who were made weak in the land”; “Your Lord wished that they should reach maturity”; “Who could prevent God if He wished to destroy the Messiah?”; and “When He wills a thing, His command is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.” Other verses follow the same pattern.

    What the author and other philosophers say about an eternal will belonging to God's essence is true in what it intends to establish. They have arguments for it. Yet those arguments show that everything apart from God depends on His power—the unrestricted source of all good—and on His knowledge of the order of goodness. They do not show that the word *will*, once purified of human limits, means the same thing as His knowledge of what is best, or that its meaning can be applied to the knowledge that is identical with His essence.

    It has indeed been proved that God possesses every perfection of being. But that is not by itself a reason to single out *will* as a separate name among His essential attributes. The philosophers' point is sound in substance. The remaining dispute concerns which word properly expresses it and whether the meaning drawn from our experience of willing can be applied to God's knowledge. As you can see, this is largely a question about language. — Ṭ. M.

    — ط مد ظله

[^V06-565]: The author identifies God's will with His knowledge of the good order that flows from Him, not with a separate desire. — س قده

[^V06-566]: The created order follows from God and does not oppose His essence.

[^V06-567]: God's knowledge that the good order comes from Him is what the author means by God's will and approval. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-568]: If God's will is eternal and necessary, creation cannot fail to follow from it. Will is part of what we mean when we speak of power, which is why this issue is discussed sometimes under God's power and sometimes under His will.

    Once we understand that God's will is necessary, that it is His knowledge as active in creation, and that this knowledge is identical with His being, we cannot define His power by saying that either of two outcomes could happen. Some theologians speak of possibility when they consider the divine essence alone, and necessity when they also consider the will. But if God's attributes are His very being, considering His will does not introduce a genuinely separate condition. This is why the discussion of will belongs with the discussion of power.

    — س قده

[^V06-569]: Earlier, the author explained one way to define power: the agent is not bound by a need either to act or to refrain. This gives choice without making God change from one state to another.

[^V06-570]: We explained earlier that an act performed under compulsion can still be an act of will. The person who compels someone acts upon the knowledge that comes before the will, not upon the will considered by itself. Without willing, the act would not be the person's act at all.

    For example, if someone ties a person's hands and feet and throws that person off a roof, the fall is not the victim's action, whether free or compelled. If, however, someone threatens to kill the person unless they climb down, and they climb down, that descent is compelled but still willed. If they climb down without any threat because they decide to do so, they act both willingly and freely.

    The difference between a forced act and a freely chosen act is not the presence of will in one and its absence in the other. It is whether the act agrees with or goes against the person's own aims. That difference belongs to how the mind judges and gives weight to reasons for acting, even though the author's wording may seem to place it within will itself.

    — ط مد ظله

[^V06-571]: God's eternal knowledge does not turn a chosen act into an unchosen one. If the act became unchosen, God's knowledge of it would be false.

[^V06-572]: On the first interpretation, the argument proves that the world began, in the manner of theologians who treat the matter rather simply. On the second, it establishes God's power and freedom, as those theologians understand them.

    There is a further distinction. On the first interpretation, the freedom included in God's power is possibility *in practice*: it is supposed to allow a period in time when the act and the agent are apart. On the second, it is possibility *in itself*: the act is considered as something whose nature does not make it necessary.

    The author rejects both kinds of possibility when applied to God's bringing things into being. All that His power requires is the conditional statement already given. A conditional can be true even when both its parts are true, both false, one true and the other false, or when necessity and impossibility are involved. In this argument, the words introducing the condition concern the meanings of the statements, not separate events that must happen in time.

    — س قده

[^V06-573]: The note corrects the wording: the point is God's power or capacity to act.

[^V06-574]: Two problems arise if one says God must be able to will pure inaction: that would deny His role as the source of action, and the will cannot be directed to sheer nonexistence.

[^V06-575]: This argument defines God's power by a possibility that belongs to the created effect. The author says an attribute of God cannot be explained by a feature of what He creates.

[^V06-576]: If one will can select between equal options without a reason, the same question applies to that will: why this one rather than another? The answer either repeats without end or allows a choice for no reason. — س قده

[^V06-577]: If neither side is necessary, neither side is settled. The fact that one side occurs shows that a cause has made it necessary; without that, the effect remains possible. — س قده

[^V06-578]: Possibility here means that neither side is necessary, not that the two sides are equally likely. A preference that falls short of necessity does not end the effect's need for a cause. — س قده

[^V06-579]: A long chain of preferences still allows the opposite outcome, just as the first preference does. Unless every way for the effect not to happen is closed, it has not become necessary. — س قده

[^V06-580]: For the author, true existence must come from the necessary source. A mere possibility or preference cannot bring the “light” of actual existence by itself.

[^V06-581]: Human will is a strong desire that reaches the point of action. The action normally follows from it, and the will takes its particular identity from the thing it wills. — س قده

[^V06-582]: Will has no identity apart from what it is directed toward. The author also hints that will is a kind of knowing what is good and fitting.

[^V06-583]: God's unity is not the limited unity of one item among others. It is a real, all-encompassing unity that relates to every existing thing. — س قده

[^V06-584]: The will and what it wills come from one source, like existence and what exists. The note connects this to love: the higher loves what comes from it, while the lower is drawn toward the higher.

[^V06-585]: The author distinguishes the early Muʿtazilites from later theologians who say that an effect does not exist until its cause makes it necessary.

[^V06-586]: If a cause does not make its effect necessary, the result is no better than saying it happens by chance, whether there was no preference or only a weak one. — س قده

[^V06-587]: The word “or” suggests that the author meant to discuss al-Kaʿbī's view in more detail. That part may be missing from the source copy. — س قده

[^V06-588]: The author explains this saying in the lines that follow: it rules out a cause of God's essence, not a reason for a particular act.

[^V06-589]: Here “act” first means the thing created; then it means the act of creating. — س قده

[^V06-590]: The leading philosophers had also reached this account, as reports of their views in discussions of substances, in *Keys to the Unseen*, and in the author's *Treatise on Coming into Being* show.

    The author's explanation rests on change in substance itself, a view that sets his approach apart among Muslim philosophers. It gives a strong account of both sides of the question: the natural world begins again and again and never stands still, while God's gift of existence never runs out and His words are never exhausted. To claim that the world began in a way that required God's generosity to cease is, in this account, a cure worse than the disease.

    The author's teacher also defended a form of coming into being that is prior to ordinary time. This too is a serious view that tries to reconcile both truths. But the author's position, that the world begins in time, agrees more closely with the language of the revealed religions.

    — س قده

[^V06-591]: This is an explicit statement of the movement of substance itself. More generally, the teachers in question arrived at the view that the world continually comes into being through both direct spiritual insight and rational argument.

    Direct insight is a familiar part of their practice. The words of Ibn Arabi, quoted earlier in the discussion of motion, and those of Rumi and other teachers make this plain. They did not build their beliefs merely on repeating what others said. Rumi writes:

    > From the Giver, the gift comes fresh, and fresh again;
    > Yet in the body it seems to flow without a break.

    There are many passages of this kind. Sanāʾī of Ghazna also writes:

    > Spiders turn flies into dried provisions;
    > Those who know God celebrate two feasts in a breath.

[^V06-592]: The objection treats the possible thing's existence and nonexistence as though they were the same as God's ability to create or not create it. A thing may be possible in itself even when its existence necessarily follows from God's eternal act.

[^V06-593]: A thing's existence can be necessary through its cause while its essence remains possible in itself.

[^V06-594]: The necessity mentioned in the objection is necessity in itself. If the world's coming into existence were necessary in itself, there would be more than one being necessary in itself, which is impossible. Nor can this necessity simply be the necessity of God's own essence: the argument describes it as something understood in relation to another, whereas God's essence is not dependent on any such relation.

    Perhaps the intended claim is that the world's existence is necessary *through God's necessity*. The author earlier distinguished this from a thing being made necessary by His action. Or perhaps it means necessity in relation to God: as God is necessary in Himself, He is necessary in relation to the world as a whole, and the first thing that comes from Him is necessary in relation to Him.

    Another reply is to distinguish the two sides of coming into existence. In its relation of dependence on God, the world's existence is necessary, because the One whose existence is necessary lacks no perfection. Considered as the world's own existence, it is possible in itself. The same coming into existence can thus be necessary from the side of its cause and possible from the side of its effect.

[^V06-595]: This refers to Muḥammad al-Khafrī, who argued that power is the possibility of acting or not acting when we consider the agent in itself, while action becomes necessary when will is included.

[^V06-596]: The sound meaning of “a position between the two” is this: causing and bringing things into being depend on existence. Nothing acts before it exists.
