Every existence, whether of a cause or an effect, has a relation to God when considered apart from the separate essences to which we give names. In that respect, the effects of created existence are effects of God's existence. But existence also has a relation to the essences of created things; in that respect they have their own effects, just as they have their own existence.

    If we look at existence in its necessary dependence on God, we speak of all things being under His power. This is the oneness of acts grounded in the oneness of His essence and attributes. It is not the fatalist's account, which speaks of the oneness of acts while leaving the oneness of God's reality unexplained. An effect points us back to its source.

    If, instead, we look at existence as belonging to possible things, we speak of choice and action. A person who understands both sides does not erase either of them.

    Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī's distinction between first and immediate causes can be read in this way. We could even reverse the description while keeping its point: seen as God's, existence displays the action of the one who freely wills; seen as belonging to creatures, it shows their dependence on a creator whose power rules over them. The first explanation is easier to grasp. The second makes a subtler distinction.

[^V06-597]: Created agents may look as if they choose, yet their choices are shaped by motives and causes beyond them. This is not choice in the same way as God's.

[^V06-598]: Power and will involve knowledge and awareness; they are not blind forces.

[^V06-599]: Created things follow from God necessarily, not merely as something that could happen or is more likely to happen. Necessity does not rule out power or choice. — س قده

[^V06-600]: God's knowledge is active: it brings about what He knows. In this sense, it is His will. — س قده

[^V06-601]: The order flows from God through a sequence of causes; this does not mean that all things come into being at once. — س قده

[^V06-602]: A result fits the nature of its cause. This fit is part of what it means for an agent to will its action. — س قده

[^V06-603]: The body’s nutritive power assimilates food into the living body, much as a fire changes what it consumes. The author quotes Rumi: “When bread joined the human body, dead bread became alive and aware.”

[^V06-604]: One thing can exist in different ways: knowledge may be an inner quality, a kind of substance in the soul or intellect, or God's necessary knowledge of Himself. “One essence” here means one shared idea, not one essence belonging to several categories. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-605]: The phrase refers to ‘those meanings’: the soul gathers together the meanings that make a thing what it is. It does not mean that the soul gives those meanings their very foundation. A feature that distinguishes one kind within a wider kind does not, simply by doing so, create the wider kind. The identity of a thing comes from its form and its final distinguishing feature. The features that came before are gathered into this last one, in a simpler and more complete way. As the final distinguishing feature, it gives the thing its definite identity. As the final form, it brings the earlier material into its completed state: it acts as the source of its fulfillment and as the end toward which it develops.

    As the form proper to this kind of thing, it is also the source of the effects that belong to the kind. Because it contains the earlier levels in a fuller way, it can produce their effects as well. The earlier parts and features are therefore conditions that make this complete form possible, rather than separate sources of the finished thing's identity. This was explained in the First Journey.

[^V06-606]: One might object: if it is hard to understand what will is in general, it must also be hard to know any particular act of willing. After all, a particular instance is simply the general nature together with the features that make it this instance.

    The answer is that identifying something as a particular example of a kind does not require us first to know that kind's complete definition. We can begin by knowing what the word points to; that simpler question comes before the harder questions about its nature.

    There is also another answer. What we know directly from experience is that willing exists and occurs. What needs a definition is its nature. The same holds for the soul. We are directly aware of ourselves, although this awareness has degrees, yet it is hard to define the soul's nature. Thinkers even disagree over the broader kind to which it belongs: is it a substance, or something else?

    — س قده

[^V06-607]: If knowledge is a level of existence, defining it is difficult because existence itself is the most basic and familiar reality. — س قده

[^V06-608]: Some definitions confuse will with thought, judgment, desire, or inclination because these experiences often occur together.

[^V06-609]: The author's point is that knowledge does not always follow an event; some events follow from what is known. — س قده

[^V06-610]: Will and knowledge can differ because knowledge may be a more distant cause of an act while will is a nearer one. — س قده

[^V06-611]: Belief in benefit belongs to the knowing power, while will arises from the power that reaches toward action. They are not the same state.

[^V06-612]: “Cracking the fingers” means bending them until they make a sound. Even routine actions usually involve some desire: habit can be pleasant, and going against it unpleasant.

[^V06-613]: We may have a rational desire to take medicine while disliking its taste. The author argues that this means will and desire are not always identical. — س قده

[^V06-614]: When comparing will with existence, the author does not stop at existence. He also speaks here of goodness and, a few lines later, of pleasure, saying that existence is loved and enjoyed in every thing. This makes the comparison closer: it links acts of willing with what is wanted, and the levels of the two match one another.

    Natural existence is not only something loved; in this account, it also loves, because even natural beings have a simple kind of awareness. So just as there is will in the sense of *being wanted*, there is also will in the sense of *wanting*. Desire and love spread through things both as their being objects of love and as their loving in return.

    — س قده

[^V06-615]: The author treats every level of existence as a kind of awareness, though ordinary speech calls only the clearest, least limited forms “knowledge.”

[^V06-616]: Even natural actions such as nourishment and growth can be understood as the soul acting through its powers and instruments. — س قده

[^V06-617]: The discussion concerns the powers that lead to action. Theoretical reason is not one of these; it is more complete and not directed toward a particular bodily act. — س قده

[^V06-618]: When the faculty of judgment uses these representations to join forms in its own way, they belong to that faculty. When it uses them to combine imagined forms, they are counted among the lower spiritual realm.

    The comparison here makes another point. In the greater world, one outpouring passes through many levels on its way to lower things. At each level it takes on what is appropriate to that level, while remaining one outpouring. Likewise, will is one reality passing through the levels of the soul. As it descends, it becomes the tendency in the muscles to relax or contract, much as the natural impulse arises from powers that cause movement. It is one will running through all these levels, expressed differently at each one.

    — س قده

[^V06-619]: The author speaks of something “smokelike,” although the usual older medical term is the *vapour-like spirit*. He has already connected the two descriptions. The Qur'an, the Torah, and other texts speak of the heavens as smoke. This could be because the heavens are like a subtle, smokelike spirit in the body of the greater world; or because the heaven of the smaller world, the human being, is the vapour-like spirit.

    The “layers” of this spirit refer to three kinds recognized in the old medical account. The brain has a spirit concerned with perception and the nerves are its channels. The heart has the vital spirit, carried through the arteries. The liver has the natural spirit, carried through the veins.

    On this account, the spirit associated with the brain is finer and higher than that of the heart, and the heart's is finer than the liver's. Even within the brain, its rear part is said to be finer than its middle, and the middle finer than its front. The same ordering can be drawn within the front part. These are features of the historical theory, not present-day anatomy.

[^V06-620]: The author's elaborate reply may not be necessary. The objector has confused the meaning of a word with the reality to which it points. We can properly say that God knows everything, but not always that He wills everything in the same sense. This difference follows from what “knowledge” and “will” mean. It does not mean that God's knowledge and will are separate realities in Him.

    The same point applies to His other attributes. We call Him living, knowing, powerful, hearing, and seeing. Yet we do not usually say that He hears what is seen or sees what is heard, because the words have different uses. “Life” is another example: in speaking of God, it does not point to something outside Him as knowledge does. We cannot freely exchange these words just because they all describe Him.

    But if the mind looks beyond these different meanings to God's reality, each attribute belongs to His entire undivided being. He is powerful with the whole of His being, just as He is living with the whole of His being. In that sense He hears by what He sees and sees by what He hears. No part or side of His existence lacks a perfection found in another.

[^V06-621]: Evil, considered simply as evil, is a lack rather than a thing that exists. God does not will evil in itself, though some limited harms follow from greater goods. — ط

[^V06-622]: Another reading is that verses about God's approval concern what He approves in religious law, while this discussion concerns His will in creating. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-623]: The two replies answer different parts of the objection. The first denies the opponent's claim that God knows everything but does not will everything. It says that, just as His knowledge embraces all things, His will embraces everything that exists as good. The second reply addresses the claim that knowledge and will must be distinct realities: if God's knowledge is identical with His being, will must be an added attribute. Different meanings do not require different things. Hearing and sight, for example, can be distinguished from knowledge by what they concern without becoming separate additions to God's being. Knowledge concerns everything; sight concerns what can be seen. Similarly, will can be understood as knowledge of a thing insofar as it is good.

    The first reply may be preferable because the second could seem to concede that some existing things fall outside God's will. But whatever exists has goodness insofar as it exists, and God's will embraces it on that account.

[^V06-624]: This points to the ordered way that many different things, including events that occur in time, can follow from one simple cause and one will. — س قده

[^V06-625]: Times may be alike in kind while still differing in their individual identity. That difference can explain why an event occurs at one time rather than another.

[^V06-626]: The point is that God wills the time itself to begin, not that He wills it at some earlier time. Otherwise we would have to ask when that earlier time began, and so on. — س قده

[^V06-627]: The world is treated as one ordered whole, like a single great human being, whose many individual beings are parts.

[^V06-628]: The point is not that God is compelled, as if someone or something passed a judgment requiring Him to act. Rather, His act follows necessarily from the way He acts: He makes it necessary, so it becomes necessary and exists.

    The words “one who is required to act” are awkward here. Some theologians who reject necessity in God's actions nevertheless say that certain things are necessary *for* God, such as His doing what is best for His creatures. God is not compelled in that sense.

    Perhaps, then, the author means an action imposed by some force, as the word “upon” suggests. That would be the kind of compulsion applied to natural processes, unlike an action that follows necessarily *from* God's own knowledge, power, and choice. Philosophers use the second sense. Because the words sound alike, critics sometimes accuse them of teaching the first. That accusation is unjust.

[^V06-629]: The author argues that God's action through His own will is the fullest kind of voluntary action. God's will is not an added state; it is His essence.

[^V06-630]: “Specific relations” means the ways God's attributes and names relate to particular kinds and individuals He creates. These relations do not divide His essence. — س قده

[^V06-631]: Without those particular relations, no specific creature would be distinguished from another. The author therefore says that God's action is necessary with reference to a particular will or relation. — س قده

[^V06-632]: This is a good explanation, but it does not yet answer the earlier objection. The meaning of will is not the same as knowledge of what is good or suitable. In our own experience, willing does not mean knowing. The state we call will goes together with knowledge, just as love, hatred, contentment, and anger may accompany knowledge, but their meanings are not identical with it. Removing the weaknesses of human willing does not suddenly turn its meaning into knowledge. For that reason, I think will should be understood from the action it produces, insofar as the action comes from God's irresistible power, or from the gathering of the causes that lead to an act, insofar as those causes depend upon Him.

    We often call the preparation for an act ‘willing.’ If someone brings food, reaches for it, and puts it in his mouth, we say, ‘He wants to eat.’ Some have confused this kind of will, which is spoken of in relation to an act, with the very cause that brings existence into being. That is a serious error, for it would undermine the account of God's oneness. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-633]: The wording may be understood in another way. If the term is taken to mean an act in relation to something, it may refer to the one who acts or to what receives the action. The grammatical distinction matters here: being known is not the same as the thing known. The former is said literally of what is known; the latter can involve a figure of speech. This is a point about language, not yet the reconciliation sought here.

    The commentator's concern is to preserve God's freedom from change. Knowledge, understood as God's being knowing, is eternal at every level, not something that begins in time. The same is true of will. The levels sometimes called providence, the Tablet, decree, and destiny are eternal insofar as they are God's knowledge.

    A clearer reconciliation, in my view, is to distinguish two aspects of divine will. First, God's delight in His own reality belongs to His essence. Second, there is His love as expressed in action, through the giving of existence to all things. The latter is indicated by the saying that God created things through His will, and created the will itself. This is God's command, the word 'Be.' It also explains what some theologians mean when they say that God's willing is His command and His disapproval is His prohibition.

    The first aspect is identical with God's essence. The second appears in His acts. Insofar as it is related to changing things, it can be described as coming about with them; insofar as it is related to God, it depends on His necessary and eternal being. It is like a meaning that exists only through its relation to something else. The will expressed in action is the appearance of the eternal will, not something separate from it.

[^V06-634]: Practical reason is the human power that judges what should be done. It differs from theoretical reason, which seeks knowledge, and from the animal powers of appetite and anger.

[^V06-635]: Desire arises from a sense of both having something and lacking it: the desired thing is present in thought but not yet possessed. — س قده

[^V06-636]: The report's phrase “what follows” refers to the action that follows our inner resolve. The action is not itself the will. — س قده

[^V06-637]: “Hollow” can suggest either a being that is made of parts or one that works through parts and powers.

[^V06-638]: “Made up of parts” also suggests a being whose existence is built up through composition and realization, unlike God's simple existence.

[^V06-639]: These reports describe God's will as an act related to creation, not as an attribute added to His essence. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-640]: *Al-Ṣamad* means the Lord to whom people turn in their needs: He needs nothing, yet meets the needs of others. In Arabic, the word can also describe something solid, with no hollow inside it. That is why one of the sacred descriptions places it next to the words “He does not eat.”

    God's reality is without parts, yet contains every perfection and every good. No good that belongs to existence as such is absent from Him. He is therefore sufficient in Himself and the source of everything needed by any dependent being, whether living or not. In the world of visible forms, something solid and without a hollow can serve as a sign pointing towards this meaning. But God Himself is far beyond comparison with any bodily shape or solid object.

    — س قده

[^V06-641]: The “gaps” in possible beings stand for the absences and limitations that accompany their existence. — س قده

[^V06-642]: These gaps are not always sheer nothingness. A capacity that has not yet become actual is still a real capacity, though it marks an incomplete state.

[^V06-643]: If created things are willed for God's sake, this does not mean that God turns toward lower things as though He needed them.

[^V06-644]: The benefit of an act must suit the act itself. If the act is the ordering of the whole world—bringing new realities into being, forming created things, and arranging everything with the highest care—then its ultimate end cannot be less than the Necessary Being Himself. Nothing is more beautiful or more complete than He is. Someone who knows that beauty and perfection would make every action a means toward that one end, rather than seek a separate reward. Since God is the one who brings about and orders the whole, He must also be its final end. That end is for creatures to know Him so fully that the knower loses himself in what he knows.

    God's appearing to creatures in complete unity is not something separate from God: an appearance wholly separate from a thing would not be its appearance at all. Just as God's knowledge and His other attributes are one with His being, so His being known by others does not add something separate to Him. His own reality is the final end of every end. — س قده

[^V06-645]: The example concerns a will that is itself effective knowledge, without a separate motive or desire following it.

[^V06-646]: The perfections that created beings gain through knowing God do not give God a new perfection; He is already complete and remains the ultimate aim. — س قده

[^V06-647]: When an image arises in our minds, desire may follow it directly, with no second act of will between them. — س قده

[^V06-648]: The definition of power is complete without adding “whether or not the agent wills.” In us, power is potential; in God, it is fully actual.

[^V06-649]: The point is that God's action is complete and His power fully actual; His action does not arise from an unresolved possibility. — س قده

[^V06-650]: Where the text says that God has no definition, we might have expected it to add, “and so no proof can establish Him.” Instead, the argument speaks of both knowing what a thing is and judging that it is, because the expression “if it has a cause” applies to both.

    A complete definition contains the causes that make a thing what it is, and a complete proof contains the causes that explain why a statement about it is true. Definitions and proofs therefore share a concern with causes, as discussed earlier. The final words, “He is known through Himself,” stand where we might have expected “There is no proof of Him.”

    In other works the author gives three possibilities for what has no cause. It may be clearer than anything else; it may be beyond the reach of our understanding; or it may be known through its effects. Avicenna does not discuss knowledge through effects here, because he defines wisdom as complete knowledge, whereas that route falls short of it. Nor does he dwell on our inability to know God's innermost reality, since that point is already plain.

    — س قده

[^V06-651]: The reality of existence can be known only through direct awareness, not through a definition. For this reason, the Necessary Being can be known only by direct illumination. Every intellect and soul, through knowing itself directly, also has some direct awareness of Him, though not of His innermost reality.

    Calling this awareness a “conception” may be exact or may be a loose use of language, depending on whether we reserve “conception” and “judgment” for knowledge gained through ideas. The word may instead refer to the ordinary, self-evident idea of existence. When direct knowledge is said to come first, its priority means that what is known is more evident and known through itself.

    So when Avicenna says that God is conceived and known through Himself, he means that beings free from matter have direct awareness of Him, not that they comprehend His innermost reality. They know Him through their awareness of themselves, because dependent beings can be understood only through their causes. This is one meaning of the saying, “I have never seen anything without seeing God before it.”

    — س قده

[^V06-652]: Things receive what they need according to their capacity, as valleys fill with water in proportion to their size.

[^V06-653]: The argument is aimed at Muʿtazilites, who accept that pointless action is wrong; Ashʿarites do not accept this moral rule.

[^V06-654]: Jurists use the purpose of a law, such as deterring murder, when they infer rulings in cases not explicitly settled by scripture. — س قده

[^V06-655]: An act is pointless when it is the sort of deliberate act normally done for a reason, but no reason is present.

[^V06-656]: There are two kinds of final end to consider. The first is nearness to God, the highest end of all and the goal of every longing. Reaching it involves passing through the gate of all gates: the complete human being, called the gate to God. This will come to pass, for God's giving does not cease, His words do not run out, and matter does not simply vanish. Everything moves toward the human level and comes to its threshold; other things are created for the sake of humanity. The human being, in turn, travels toward God and is created for His sake. The second kind of end is the fulfillment proper to each thing under one of God's beautiful names.

    Things reach that end as well. All God's names are beautiful and all His attributes are exalted; things finally pass into the source of enduring life. This may help explain the reported saying, ‘Do not set me above Jonah son of Matthew,’ whose account of ascent differs from that of the Prophet. From this point of view, philosophers have said that even lives cut short reach their natural end and that what sounds out of tune has its place in the larger harmony. This shows why it is wrong to say that God destroys things before they can reach any fulfillment.

[^V06-657]: “Benefit” can be something that follows an act or something found during it, such as finding treasure while digging a well for water.

[^V06-658]: The author says the body has many distinct powers. For example, each muscle has its own powers of movement, and each organ has its own nutritive power.

[^V06-659]: All beings return to God through the perfect human being, who stands at the gate of divine unity. The return of the whole is like the return of each individual.

[^V06-660]: The Ashʿarites sometimes explain this through *kasb*, or “acquisition”: God creates an action when a person firmly intends it, and that is enough to make commands, reward, and punishment meaningful. They compare it to someone carrying a heavy load while another person merely places a hand beneath it. The author says this excuse is worse than the claim it is meant to defend. — س قده

[^V06-661]: It is said that the judge ʿAbd al-Jabbār entered the home of the vizier Ibn ʿAbbād and saw the theologian Abū Isḥāq al-Isfarāʾīnī there. The judge said, “Glory be to God, who is above indecency.” The theologian replied, “Glory be to God, in whose kingdom nothing happens except what He wills.” — س قده

[^V06-662]: The Qur'an asks, “Are many separate lords better, or God, the One who prevails?” A report from the Prophet calls the Qadariyya—the people who deny God’s decree—the Zoroastrians of this community. Each side accused the other of holding this view.

[^V06-663]: This includes Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi, as cited earlier. In this account, God creates a person’s power and will; these then bring about the action. God is the distant cause, and the person the immediate cause. The people firmly grounded in knowledge describe God as near in His distance and far in His nearness. Unlike the Muʿtazilites, al-Tusi also holds that an effect continues to depend on its cause in order to exist.

[^V06-664]: God does not will evil directly or for its own sake. It follows indirectly from a good that He wills. The terms “will,” “approval,” “love,” and “delight” are used here with a particular technical distinction. — س قده

[^V06-665]: The third view moves from multiplicity within unity; the fourth moves from unity within multiplicity. In the third, an action depends both on its immediate cause and, through that cause, on the cause behind it. These are ordered relations, so one does not cancel the other. The First Cause wills the action to come from a willing agent, so the action occurs by choice; it does not bypass that agent’s choice. In the fourth view, God’s action is directly present through His encompassing knowledge, while the action also truly belongs to its created agent. The two views therefore do not contradict one another.

[^V06-666]: The divine reality is the enduring source and ground of all things, not a part mixed into them or a separate source standing beside them. It is present to every action, though the source has aspects that do not belong to its effects. Existence first belongs to God; created things receive existence from Him, and their essences are considered afterward. Keep these levels distinct: when we look at unity, the effect is God’s; when we look at the many created beings, their actions are theirs. This does not make God composite.

[^V06-667]: Names for limited things and actions include the conditions that limit them, such as subject, matter, time, and place. These are aspects of imperfection. What is attributed to God is their existence, without these limits. For example, if you imagine someone eating bread or drinking wine, that person and their action depend on you as the one who imagines them. The action is called eating or drinking when attributed to the person; when attributed to you, you are said to have brought the person and action into being. The author does not say that you yourself eat bread or drink wine.

[^V06-668]: The prayer “All good is in Your hands, and evil is not from You” means that evil is not affirmed as a thing in itself. The author also alludes to the Qur'anic promise that the earth and heavens will be changed, along with the one who sees and what is seen, so that evil will no longer be attributed to pure good.

[^V06-669]: These lines point to the oneness of God’s essence, attributes, and actions. A person who affirms God’s oneness must first affirm the oneness of His essence and attributes, then the oneness of His actions. The Ashʿarites claim to affirm the latter, but, in the author’s view, they do so incorrectly: they treat created beings as independent existences while denying that their actions and effects belong to them, then assign those effects to God as their place of occurrence.

[^V06-670]: The soul's unity is a true unity, but only as a reflection of God's unity. It can know divine names that point both to God's difference from creation and to His nearness to it. At its lower level, the soul has the powers of bodily nature; at its higher level, it has the qualities of spiritual beings. Through all these levels it remains the one source of its powers.

    Because the soul is God's reflection and representative, it bears, in its own limited way, something of the qualities of the One it represents. It therefore has a level of hidden unity, where its many powers are gathered in a higher and undivided way. It also has a level of outward expression, where one soul acts through many powers without leaving its higher level behind.

    The note connects this with the two blasts of the trumpet. At the first, which brings death, the soul's powers cease to shine separately through the scattered organs of the body. At the second, which brings renewed life, those powers rise in another form. They no longer depend on bodily matter for their existence; they are gathered into one simple life through the soul itself. At the first blast their separate modes of existence pass away. At the second they endure after that passing, raised together in a higher existence.

[^V06-671]: The difficulty comes from tracing all things to God’s knowledge of the most complete order, which is identical with His essence, and from holding that an effect cannot be separated from its cause. Calling this knowledge “will” or “an attribute of action” rather than “an attribute of essence” does not resolve the problem.

[^V06-672]: Decree is God’s active knowledge; what is decreed is what He knows. Each has a property that the other does not. We may say that God’s knowledge of all things is one, but we cannot say that all the things He knows are one.

[^V06-673]: The author does not deny that decree differs from what is decreed, or that God’s prior knowledge differs from the things known. He questions whether the required contentment concerns decree in that sense. Since approval and disapproval apply to the same things, they concern the created event, not prior knowledge. The discussion therefore distinguishes approving disbelief as an event decreed by God from approving it as disbelief. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-674]: The earlier statement that acceptance of what is decreed comes down to acceptance of the decree assumes that “decree” means the act of decreeing. But that is not the meaning used by the thinkers under discussion. For them, divine decree means the existence of the forms of things at an earlier level. On this reading, they can approve of things in that prior state while rejecting their condition in this world. In their view, earthly existence is mixed with dark matter, extension, and constant change. It also contains things that are plainly blameworthy.

    The objection need not be answered through such fine distinctions, however. Approval and disapproval each have their own object. If someone asks how anything that exists can be disapproved of when existence itself is good, the answer is that disapproval of a shameful thing concerns its particular nature—or, more precisely, the lack of good found in it—not existence as such.

    The statement can also cover the decree as it appears in the world, a use associated with Mīr Dāmād. If “decree” is taken broadly enough to include destiny, then the actual existence of things falls within it. Their existence can be approved insofar as they are lights and objects of knowledge, turned towards God. They can also be approved as faint expressions of higher realities, wholly dependent on those realities.

[^V06-675]: Whatever meaning we give to divine decree, it is an attribute of God, who decrees. If it means His essential knowledge, it is an attribute of essence. If it means the existence of created events as related to Him and subject to His judgment, it is an attribute of action. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-676]: Al-Tusi’s phrase “not from this point of view” refers to created qualities and actions under the names that relate them to their subjects. Heat and cold, for example, are attributed to God as existences He creates, but as heat and cold they belong to fire and ice. God is not Himself hot or cold. The author’s three answers are each correct in a different respect. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-677]: This clarifies how the thing decreed may be disapproved while its divine source is not. The divine names behind things considered evil are themselves beautiful.

[^V06-678]: The Qur'an does not say, “Because You led me astray.” It does quote Satan as saying, “Because You made me err,” but that does not support the claim being made here.

[^V06-679]: The parallel argument applies to God’s will for events that arise in time: if God knew and willed such an event, it would have to be eternal; if He did not, it could not come into being. — س قده

[^V06-680]: The author may have in mind his later answer: divine knowledge applies to things as they are. So it applies to a chosen action precisely as something that comes from a person’s power and choice. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-681]: In *Tajrīd al-iʿtiqād*, al-Tusi says knowledge “follows” what it knows in the sense that the two must correspond. In the mystical account, knowledge follows things in their particular form and coloring, because knowledge itself is light without color. — س قده

[^V06-682]: This is enough to make the action voluntary. A voluntary action is one preceded by choice; choice itself need not be preceded by another choice. God’s decree contains both the person’s power, will, and choice, and the action that follows them. Just as your existence is truly yours even though God gives it, your choice is truly yours. To deny the qualities and effects of something while affirming the thing itself would be strange.

[^V06-683]: One answer is that commands and prohibitions are meant to bring about a deeper understanding and state of being, not merely to inform us. Another is that although God gives power for human actions, people also act by their own power, as already explained.

[^V06-684]: “Obligatory” here means that failing to do it would merit blame. This differs from “necessary” as opposed to possible or impossible, which is the sense that would rule out power. — س قده

[^V06-685]: The first claim allows for intermediary causes and for the two attributions to differ. The second denies this.

[^V06-686]: Strictly speaking, it is strange to call a will “human” if the person does not will it. The objection means that this account severs the connection between a person and their will, and more broadly between causes and their effects, leaving actions to follow only from God’s custom. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-687]: Since even a compelled agent may act with some will, “choice” would be a more precise word here than “will.” — ط مد ظله

[^V06-688]: This is what distinguishes the author’s possible answer from his teacher’s. The teacher says that one act of will is inherently prior to the next, and so on. But inherent earlier and later parts belong only to extended things, not to one simple state of the soul.

[^V06-689]: This argument is compelling if essences are primary, as the teacher holds, or if individual examples of one kind are considered alike. The author’s view is that existence is primary and graded: it is one reality in different degrees, so one degree may cause another. He also allows that members of one kind can differ, with some material and others immaterial.

[^V06-690]: “Decree” may refer to the intellectual images in the First Intellect, while “measure” refers to the impressions in the souls of the heavens. It may also mean the First Intellect itself, or the first intellectual act of knowing. — س قده

[^V06-691]: Chance is an event such as a falling stone striking Zayd as his will carries him beneath it. It is chance when judged from a limited perspective. From the perspective of all causes and the complete order, it is necessary. Possibility is likewise a feature of an essence considered by itself; in relation to existence and its cause, the event is necessary. Avicenna does not discuss forced causes here, since what forces something acts either by nature or by will.

[^V06-692]: “The first simple arrangement” is close to the ordinary meaning of decree. It is simple in two ways: all things exist through one simple existence, and each image there exists in a simple form, without the multiplicity found in the records of destiny. — س قده

[^V06-693]: Read “as though it necessarily produced.” The phrase “as though” is added because the gathering of causes is an abstract relation. Their existence is one; the meeting of their concepts gives rise to the specific forms and settings found in the records of destiny.

[^V06-694]: Some thinkers exclude these existences—which others count as forms of knowledge—from the levels of knowledge. The author therefore expands and completes his explanation through the Tablet of Destiny. — س قده

[^V06-695]: First, divine oneness knows its own essence; then it considers other things. For philosophers who affirm images in God, this second act is the forms of other things in His essence. Since divine knowledge is active and brings about what it knows, it is also called power. “The second knowledge followed” explains how the first knowledge gives rise to the second. — س قده

[^V06-696]: The note describes two stages of multiplicity: first, knowledge becomes detailed as it unfolds from its original unity; then the contents of knowledge become many as they are written in the tablet. The letters on paper were once one in the wet ink on the tip of the reed pen. The “Lote Tree” may also refer to the universal soul, the forms impressed upon it, or the great intermediate realm of divine names and attributes. — س قده

[^V06-697]: Divine agency has levels. The phrase about God’s beautiful names may explain the earlier point: in the mystical tradition, the divine names are like the archetypes or “lords” of kinds in Illuminationist philosophy. Created kinds are manifestations of those names.

[^V06-698]: “Heart” and “breast” are technical terms here. The breast can mean the imagination, which holds images that reflect subtle truths from the world of unity; an expanded breast is a luminous imagination. A constricted breast can mean a dark imagination that multiplies things into separate forms. — س قده

[^V06-699]: Without these changing records, there would be no successive beings or chain of events. Only fixed immaterial beings would exist in a vertical order. Some mystics therefore say that a complete world requires the creation of an imperfect world as well.

[^V06-700]: This account may seem difficult to reconcile with the view that heavenly bodies do not change inwardly. One way to understand it is that their movements and positions carry universal patterns that include their consequences. A heavenly soul can know the rules that connect certain movements with certain earthly events, and infer what follows as conditions change. A prophet or saint connected to such a soul may see some of these forms, but no human being knows every consequence of every heavenly movement. What is seen may therefore change or be incomplete. — س قده

[^V06-701]: The hidden knowledge comprehends all particulars, their causes, and whatever might prevent them. The knowledge taught to angels and messengers includes broad rules and some particulars. This does not conflict with the claim that God alone knows the unseen: any knowledge they receive comes through His power and knowledge. — س قده

[^V06-702]: Avicenna’s phrase “a soul spread through the world of coming-to-be and decay” is unclear. It might mean the collective of individual souls, an active source behind them, or a soul associated with an unchanging heavenly body. If the last, that soul may overlap with what mystics call the pole or helper of the world. The prophetic and saintly souls, which the earth is never without, must also be connected to the heavenly soul in their knowledge and action. The author’s wording may be an indirect way of referring to them. — س قده

[^V06-703]: This proposed soul is not a third option in addition to the two Avicenna listed. The preferred option is a heavenly soul. The author later argues that such a soul is not higher in every respect and may respond to some earthly events.

[^V06-704]: The cause’s knowledge is active: what it knows brings about what it knows.

[^V06-705]: Prayer and asking are also causes of our happiness because they remind us of God. God told Moses to ask Him for everything, even salt for his food. A Persian verse says: “Even if you pray and I do not grant your wish, / what I seek from you is your pleading.” If you ask for bodily health and do not receive it, the All-Merciful may know that your soul needs another kind of health. The soul begins as a simple capacity; what makes a thing fully itself is its form. There is no comparison between having the divine names and attributes become the form of your soul, and having passing worldly things become its form. Another verse says: “A house is not a heart if it holds / cattle, donkeys, fields, and property.”

[^V06-706]: The person’s will is contained within God’s effective will: the person gives up what they want for what God wants. God “does what He wills and chooses what He wants.”

[^V06-707]: The prayer can be known to God because the person intends to make it. But it does not follow that the person knows God’s full knowledge: another known cause may prevent the request from being granted.

[^V06-708]: All the forms of things in God’s realm come from Him. A maker who makes their own tools does not need anything outside themselves. — س قده

[^V06-709]: The first view described here belongs to the philosophers in Aristotle’s school; the second is the Illuminationist view. — س قده

[^V06-710]: The earthly causes form a chain beneath the chain of heavenly causes. When heavenly souls know the first causes, they can also know the later effects, since knowledge of a cause can lead to knowledge of its effect.

[^V06-711]: Heavenly souls are said to have the power to act and to perceive particular meanings and images, as well as universals and separate intellects. The discussion here concerns their knowledge of particulars. — س قده

[^V06-712]: “Not a passing desire” means that the will is decisive. Desire may become a firm resolve and then a settled decision, but a new obstacle can still prevent action. A will that is a complete cause does not fail to bring about its action. Avicenna says that particular events have natural or voluntary causes, and treats forced action as ultimately arising from one of these.

[^V06-713]: A heavenly soul may know a particular directly, through the common sense that gathers sensory impressions, or through something that reaches a direct perceiver. — س قده

[^V06-714]: The event is not caused only by natural causes. It may arise from an active conception and knowledge in the heavenly source. — س قده

[^V06-715]: “But” here adds a further point rather than correcting what came before. The recipient’s capacities and needs also contribute to what comes into being, though the true creative power belongs to the higher causes.

[^V06-716]: Avicenna may mean that the heavens are not affected by earthly things simply as earthly things. He may allow an effect from a higher earthly reality, such as the human being, who bears the greatest divine trust and for whose sake other things were created. — س قده

[^V06-717]: This discussion relies on older philosophical and physical assumptions: moving celestial spheres with individual souls, four elements, and other ideas later overturned by modern science. Setting those assumptions aside, the author’s general account can be restated in terms of three ordered worlds: matter, imagination, and immaterial intellect. Material events depend on the imaginal world, which in turn depends on intellect. A person who prays with persistence may become connected to the causes of what they need in the imaginal and intellectual worlds, and so affect material events. This can also explain miracles. Shared disasters such as floods and earthquakes may be understood through the unity of the whole world: the state of one part affects the rest, as illness in one organ disturbs the body. Human society also has a natural purpose and a divinely given way of life. Widespread injustice and wrongdoing can therefore disturb the larger order, which responds through consequences appropriate to the situation. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-718]: The response need not mean that the heavenly soul first learns of the event after it happens. It can mean that an awareness arising with the event completes its earlier awareness of the causes. We can know that we will move and also perceive the movement as it happens.

[^V06-719]: “Under Our eyes” refers to the heavenly souls; “by Our revelation” refers to the conceptions they receive.

[^V06-720]: The words “I hear and see” refer to God’s knowledge of particular events through the level of the heavenly souls. — س قده

[^V06-721]: Even if one questions the separate archetypes, this does not rule out forms of material things and their effects at higher levels. The imaginal world contains forms suited to material things, and the intellectual world contains what is in the imaginal world, each according to its own level. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-722]: Avicenna accepts particular images in the souls of the heavens and universal images in the intellects above them. But this alone does not establish that the real essences of things exist in those worlds. Philosophers in Aristotle’s school often treat the images as accidents or qualities. They also regard it as impossible for members of one kind to be substances in one world and accidents in another. Even the Illuminationist who affirms both luminous archetypes and suspended images, the author says, did not make clear whether the luminous forms are the archetypes of natural kinds or merely similar to them.

[^V06-723]: This is generally true, though movement in place, changes in quality, changes in quantity, and other processes also contribute to life.

[^V06-724]: Strictly speaking, pure existence is not an attribute added to God alongside knowledge, power, and life. The word “attribute” is used either by analogy or to refer to the concept without considering what it describes. The author’s point is that God is pure existence, without an essence or partner. “One” means that He has no partner of any kind. — س قده

[^V06-725]: The principles that existence is primary and one in different degrees do not allow us to treat existence as a quality in God like knowledge, power, or life. Pure existence corresponds to the concepts of essence and identity. Proofs for God’s essence are also proofs for pure existence. Knowledge and power, though identical with God in reality, have different meanings and are established after His essence.

[^V06-726]: The relational aspect is included in this definition of life because it describes the two results of life: perception and action. Life itself remains a real, essential attribute.

[^V06-727]: The question is what makes a body alive, not what makes it a body. — س قده

[^V06-728]: This applies to God and separate immaterial substances. But prime matter and bodies that remain connected to matter do not have this kind of existence.

[^V06-729]: These theologians believe that reason alone cannot establish life as an attribute of God. They rely on scripture, since they think life is a bodily quality arising from a physical mixture, while God is above bodies and mixtures. “Then” marks a shift to the next point. — س قده

[^V06-730]: Life differs from perception because we can conceive of one without the other. This does not mean that a living being can be entirely without awareness, including awareness of itself; it means that it can lack perception of particular things through the outer or inner senses.

[^V06-731]: One might object that al-Tusi gives two arguments: life is a condition for knowledge and power, and it is the nobler of two opposites. The author’s criticism seems to address only the second. But the first also depends on the claim that God is called knowing and powerful because these qualities are nobler, then argues that life must also be attributed to Him as their condition.

[^V06-732]: The word means a pair of horns or feelers, such as the antennae of ants and the horns of scorpions.

[^V06-733]: This means: know God through God, and know Him by the light He gives. — س قده

[^V06-734]: Do not read the Imam’s words as the Muʿtazilites do—as if God merely produces effects that imitate knowledge or power, while the attributes themselves are absent from Him. The report says that divine attributes are real perfections, present in God in the highest degree; they are not limited to qualities that exist in something else.

[^V06-735]: This explains the phrase “God is the refuge.” “People” here means those whose minds are mixed with imagination and limited forms of thought. Those who know God through His own light do not describe Him by comparing Him with possible beings; they witness what others cannot. The report should not be used to discourage sound reasoning or the search for knowledge. The Imam criticizes what people imagine about God, not intellect illuminated by God. Whatever the intellect distinguishes as a created object or concept is indeed created; but as a sign reflecting the truth, it may correspond to reality. — س قده

[^V06-736]: This may mean that these attributes have become so well known that they need no proof in God’s case, because what is certain in religion is like what is certain in reason.

[^V06-737]: The direct, illuminating awareness of all things is hearing and sight. This is what the Illuminationist master means when he says that God’s knowledge returns to His sight, and it also matches Mulla Sadra’s own words in *The Keys to the Unseen*. One might object that sight concerns light and color, while hearing concerns sounds and words. Does this support the theologians’ claim, made here and in the discussion of will, that will is an attribute added to God’s essence? The answer is that sight does require light and color in what is seen, and hearing requires that what is heard be words. But what is perceived must be suited to the one who perceives it. The light suited to God’s sight is the light of existence that lights up the heavens and earth; its colors are the colors of essences; and the words suited to His hearing are the words of existence, “Be!”, which branch into complete and incomplete words, as Mulla Sadra will explain. — س قده

[^V06-738]: As explained earlier, God’s direct knowledge means that all things are present before Him through their own existence. It includes every kind of quality—what is heard, seen, felt, smelled, and tasted—as well as substances, all the categories, and anything that falls outside them. All these are present to Him through direct knowledge. His knowledge is entirely direct; there is no basis for attributing knowledge through mental images to God. On this view, there is no reason to reduce His sight and hearing to direct, illuminating knowledge or to call them two special kinds of knowledge beyond knowledge in general. They can also be understood as His general knowledge of what is seen and heard. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-739]: The intended reversal may be to say that all knowledge returns to sight, if sight means direct, illuminating awareness, and to hearing, if hearing means witnessing all beings as God’s words.

[^V06-740]: What Mulla Sadra says about sight also applies to what the other senses perceive, such as smells, tastes, and textures. An inner sense gathers these impressions, as he explains later in his discussion of the soul. The impressions it gathers are all of one kind. They are not direct awareness of things as they exist; they are knowledge through mental images. One proof is that we can be mistaken about what we see, hear, and feel. It is not enough to say that sight is direct awareness because an image of the thing seen appears to the soul. Every kind of mental image—general or particular, sensed or not—appears to the mind. Knowledge through an image means that the thing’s essence is present in the mind, not that the thing exists outside it. — ط مد ظله

[^V06-741]: Just as the soul makes an image of a person, such as Zayd, when it sees him, it also makes images of his position, place, nearness, distance, and other features. It does not make them by choice or deliberate thought; God has made it do so by nature. These images belong to the imaginal world and have no natural matter, because what is perceived must exist for the one perceiving it. But their coming into being and their persistence depend on the presence of matter. This, together with their being perceived by the outer senses, distinguishes them from images in the separate imaginal world, which are perceived by the inner sense. Both kinds of images are free from matter to some degree and differ from material images, but the points above also distinguish them from each other. It may help to call sense-images that depend on matter the world of Hurqalya, and the other kind the Imaginal World. In older usage, however, both names could refer to separate suspended images, named by analogy with the imagination within us. Mulla Sadra and the natural philosophers both say that the direct object of sight is an image distinct from the image in matter. But they differ on where this image comes from. The natural philosophers say it exists in the soul’s powers as something received; Mulla Sadra says it comes from the soul as something the soul produces. They say the Giver of Forms sends the image to the soul’s powers; he says the soul produces it.
